# The Little Girl in the Giant Country

============================================================

# Volume 1: Thirty Days Since Xiaoman Arrived

## Part 1: First Meeting with Xiaoman

## Chapter 1: Arrival

July 14, 2026, 4:17 AM, Binhai Park, East Sea City.

The sea was just turning that color between black and deep blue. Streetlights still glowed, but feebly. The air smelled of salt and... sweetness. Like freshly baked milk bread, but amplified a thousand times.

Then the ground shook.

Not an earthquake. Footsteps.

You looked up and saw her.

She stood at the border where the shore met the city, roughly eighteen or nineteen meters tall. A light blue dress, its hem smudged with seawater and fine sand, long black hair falling to her waist. She looked eight or nine years old, tilting her head, examining this world — and you — with one enormous amber eye.

Her breath stirred a warm breeze that ruffled the leaves around you.

She spoke. Her voice seemed to come from far away, yet was terrifyingly clear:

"...Where is this?"

Her voice was like wind through a valley, carrying the soft texture of a little girl's voice, only magnified. You felt your eardrums cave inward slightly, then recover. She was speaking in a whisper — she instinctively knew to keep it "quiet," though for her this was already her limit.

She crouched down.

The motion took her five seconds. The ground sighed dully as her weight shifted. Now her face was only three stories above you. You could see her eyelashes — each one as thick as a broom, trembling slightly in the morning breeze.

She looked at you. Not looking down — at eye level, because she had rested her chin on her knees, folding herself into a small hill. Her pupils dilated in the dimness, like two amber moons, reflecting the streetlights and you.

"You... are so small."

She said it with no mockery, only a childlike, pure surprise. She extended one hand, index finger and thumb spread, slowly reaching toward you — the gap between those two fingers was wide enough to fit a small car. Her movements were slow, careful. Her nails were trimmed round, healthy pink.

"Can I... touch you?"

She asked, her voice as soft as coaxing a butterfly to sleep.

You said: "This is the beach, you can touch me."

The moment you finished speaking, her eyes lit up. Her mouth curved into a smile that showed a missing front tooth — probably lost during her giant-country teething. The gum was pink; to her it was a small gap, to you a two-meter-wide fissure.

"Really?"

Her voice rose with the pure joy of finally being permitted. She pushed her hovering hand forward, then stopped halfway, brow slightly furrowed.

"Will I... hurt you?"

She tilted her head, thinking seriously. Then she decided: she did not use two fingers to pinch you — that was too much like grasping an object. She chose one finger.

Her index finger descended slowly, like a flesh-colored drawbridge reaching down from three stories up. You could smell the faint, sun-warmed milk scent on her skin, mixed with sea salt. The fingertip drew nearer, blocking the streetlight above your head. The world dimmed.

Whoosh—

Her finger stirred a small gust of wind that tousled your hair.

Then, touch.

Not pressure — just a light contact. Her fingertip was wider and thicker than your entire body, warm, soft, with the give of living skin. You felt gently enclosed by a warm, breathing cushion.

Her fingertip rested on your head for three seconds.

"Ah!"

She cried out softly, quickly withdrawing her hand and covering her mouth — but her eyes curved into crescents.

"You're really so small! And... and warm!"

She swayed slightly with excitement. The ground hummed like a low-frequency speaker. Her dress hem swept the seawall with a canvas-rasping sound.

"At home, the smallest friend is still a little taller than me," she gestured — that "little bit" was three stories to you. "I've never touched anyone this much smaller than me! You're like... like..."

She searched for words.

"Like a talking little hamster!"

She was pleased with her metaphor, propping her hands on her knees and leaning her face even closer. Now her nose was only ten-plus meters away. You could see the fine down on her nostrils, each strand as long as a reed, swaying in the morning breeze.

"Little hamster, what's your name?"

She asked.

You said: "I'm Miaowu."

"Miaowu..."

She rolled the name on her tongue, producing a low, soft resonance like a cello string plucked. She smiled, revealing that two-meter-wide tooth gap.

"What a cute name! I'm—"

She was about to introduce herself when she stopped.

In the distance, the normal noise of the city waking at 5 AM: bus air brakes, steam from a soy milk stall, some early-rising old man practicing his voice by the river. No helicopters, no sirens — the government had shut down, civil servants had gone home, budgets frozen, but the city kept running.

But Xiaoman didn't know that. For an eight- or nine-year-old girl: unfamiliar place + strange noises = danger. Her breathing grew heavier, each exhale like a warm wind sweeping over your head.

She looked at you again, but her eyes had changed. No longer curious — the look of someone grasping at a floating log.

"Miaowu..." her voice dropped, trembling, "it's so noisy here. And... and I'm hungry."

As she spoke, one hand pressed against her belly. The wind pressure from that movement made you step back half a pace. Her stomach emitted a gurgle—

Not an ordinary gurgle. It was hollow, enormous, a resonance from deep within, like a large engine stuck and idling far away. The sound lasted five full seconds, the ground resonating at a low frequency.

"I haven't eaten since morning..." she said plaintively, eyes reddening, "at home, Mom would make me strawberry toast and a giant cup of hot milk by this time..."

A tear gathered at the corner of her eye. It was basketball-sized, hanging from her eyelash, refracting the morning light like a transparent amber about to fall.

The tear finally fell.

Plop.

It hit the sand, spraying a ring of sand mist, leaving a washbasin-sized wet pit at your feet.

She sniffled, looking at you, her voice as soft as a plea:

"Miaowu... do you know where I can get something to eat? And... I need to use the bathroom, but I don't dare move... I'm afraid if I move I'll crush those tiny houses..."

Beneath her dress hem, seawater rippled gently with her trembling. She was about to burst — whether from hunger, or from the other end of physiology.

---

## Chapter 2: Convenience Store

You said: "Sure."

"Really?!"

Her voice surged like spring wind through a valley, carrying pure, unreserved joy. She almost jumped — the ground shook, and she immediately forced herself to stop, covering her mouth, eyes wide.

"Sorry! I'll be gentle!"

She began moving toward the convenience store. Not walking — shuffling. She lifted one foot, hovered it in the air like playing "the floor is lava," then placed her sole down very, very gently—boom, still a little vibration, but much lighter than before.

She took three steps, taking two full minutes.

The convenience store's automatic door sensed her.

Ding. Welcome.

The door opened before her, then immediately realized something was wrong — its height only reached her ankle. She paused, then slowly crouched, bracing both hands on either side of the road, lowering her entire upper body.

Now her face was level with the convenience store's glass door, nose almost pressed against it. The space inside was fully visible: shelves like miniature model blocks, the checkout counter like a small drawer, the oden machine like a cup of steaming hot water.

A night-shift clerk stood behind the counter, half a rice ball in hand, mouth forming an O.

Xiaoman tapped the glass door gently with one finger — tap. Not loud, but the door hummed.

"Hello..." she whispered, her breath hissing through the door gap, scattering flyers near the frame, "can I buy something to eat?"

The clerk didn't answer. He just slowly stuffed the rice ball into his mouth, chewing mechanically, not blinking.

Xiaoman turned to look at you, confused: "Why isn't he talking? Is it because I'm too quiet?"

She took a deep breath, about to raise her voice—

You couldn't react. No time to speak.

"Hello—!!"

She raised her voice. Just a little — to her it was only from whisper to normal speaking volume, like answering a teacher in class.

But to you, to the convenience store, to this street—

BOOM.

The glass door exploded inward — not shattered, but the entire door tore off its frame, as if struck by an invisible fist, flying into the store and knocking over two rows of shelves. Glass cracked into a thousand radial patterns, then crashed to the floor.

The oden pot was knocked over, hot soup splashing everywhere. The cash register screamed an alarm. Cup noodles on the shelves toppled like dominoes.

The clerk collapsed on the floor, rice ball falling from his mouth. His ears rang, temporarily deaf.

Xiaoman herself was frightened.

"AHH!"

She covered her mouth, eyes huge, leaning backward—boom, her back hit the convenience store awning, the entire canopy flying off like a can lid, crashing in the middle of the road.

"Sorry sorry sorry!!"

She pressed her palms together and bowed frantically to the store. Each nod stirred a hurricane that sent leaves, flyers, and the clerk's apron flying.

"I didn't mean it! I just wanted to speak up a little!!"

Tears came again. This time two, falling simultaneously from both eyes, like transparent meteorites hitting the asphalt—

Plop! Plop!

Two washbasin-sized wet pits appeared, water splashing three meters high.

She cried louder: "I want to go home..."

Those four words, at normal volume, rolled through the morning street like a subwoofer. Windows in nearby buildings hummed in resonance.

In the distance, early pedestrians finally noticed.

An old woman walking her dog stood at the corner, her dog tucking its tail and barking furiously. A delivery rider on an e-bike stopped at the intersection, mouth an O under his helmet. A high school student in uniform had been waiting for the bus; now he slowly raised—no, he had no phone, he just stood there, gripping his backpack strap, staring.

Xiaoman kept crying. She crouched down, wrapping her arms around her knees, folding into a trembling small hill. Her sobbing was low and muffled, like a volcano erupting in the distance.

"I want to go home... I want Mom..."

The ground trembled with each sob.

The convenience store clerk finally snapped out of it and scrambled out the back door.

You stood there, ears still ringing.

Xiaoman saw you through tear-filled eyes. She sniffled and extended her hand — not to grab you, but laying her palm flat on the ground before you, like a giant, warm platform.

"Miaowu..." her voice dropped back to a whisper, thick with nasal congestion, "are you scared of me too?"

---

## Chapter 3: Not Scared

"I'm not scared of you, because this is just the natural you! I like the natural you!"

Your voice was thin, carrying the crisp clarity of a second-grader, rolling down the street like a fruit candy.

But she heard it.

Her pupils trembled slightly, as if a pebble had been tossed into those two amber moons. She froze, even her sobbing stopping. Tears still hung on her lashes, trembling, but didn't fall further.

"The... natural... me?"

She repeated it, then smiled — a relieved smile. She raised a hand to wipe her eye but stopped, looking down at you and asking carefully:

"I... my movements are too big, won't I blow you away? You're really small, smaller than my eraser."

"I'm Xiaoman," she said. "Full as in fullness."

She pushed her upturned palm closer. You stepped on it — her palm wasn't "a bed" to you, it was "an entire pink playground"; you ran three steps to go from her palm to the base of her fingers.

She lifted you up. You rose, the ground receding. The convenience store wreckage became Lego blocks, the delivery rider ant-sized. She placed you on her collarbone — a broad, pink landmass where you could lie down, with a faint salt-sea smell.

You sat on her collarbone, legs dangling six stories up, swinging.

"Miaowu," she whispered, and you felt the muffled resonance in her chest, "you're really so small. But I like you very much."

She wanted to cradle you there against her neck, but suddenly—

Her body stiffened.

Her stomach emitted a low, long rumble, like a broken washing machine idling in the distance. Her legs clenched, toes scraping white marks on the pavement.

"Miaowu..." her voice began to shake, "I... I really can't hold it..."

She tucked you gently against her neck, pressing you to her warm skin. Her hands gripped her skirt hem white-knuckled.

"What do I do..." her voice cracked, "if I... if I do it now... I'll dirty everything here..."

In the distance, the delivery rider took off his helmet and shouted: "Hey—! Do you, do you need help—?!"

The old woman pulled her dog back. The high school student stood frozen, backpack strap death-gripped.

You sat on her shoulder, six stories up, wind tousling your hair. You looked at your own small hands, then down at the toy-like city below.

You remembered the red plastic basin in your backpack — Mom had put it there that morning for a "water-saving guard" craft. It was big to you; you needed both hands to carry it.

You held it high like a little flag, calling up to Xiaoman's face:

"Use this! I pee in this! It's big enough!"

Xiaoman froze.

She looked down at the red plastic basin in your arms — to her, it was about the size of a bottle cap, or one of those shiny stickers on her nail.

Her mouth twitched, wanting to cry and laugh at the same time. The two mixed into a crumpled, near-breaking face.

"Miaowu..." her voice trembled like a leaf in the wind, "thank you... but... I'm a little bigger than you..."

Her stomach rumbled again, more urgently. Her body tensed, legs clamped shut, toes scraping the pavement.

"I really... really can't hold it..."

Tears welled in her eyes, but she fought them back because Miaowu had just said "I like the natural you."

In the distance, the delivery rider seemed to hear your conversation. He scratched his head, then turned and ran to his e-bike, rummaging in the rear box.

The old woman shouted: "Child—! Take her to the beach—! The beach—!"

The high school student hadn't moved, but his mouth went from O-shaped to — shaped, trying to understand the physics.

You held the red basin, sitting on the "pink landmass" of her collarbone, wind ruffling your hair. Xiaoman was trembling; you could feel it, because this "ground" was vibrating.

---

## Chapter 4: The Lawn

"Go to the beach! High tide will wash everything away!"

You pointed toward the sea, but Xiaoman didn't move. Her legs clamped tighter, body bowing like a drawn bow.

"Too late..." her voice seemed squeezed through her teeth, "I... I..."

She made her decision.

She placed you and the red basin gently, gently on top of her head — a warm plain with a forest of black hair, and the faint scent of shampoo.

"Miaowu, hold onto my hair! I'm going to run!"

"Run? Run where—"

Before you finished, she stood up.

BOOM—

The ground shook. Your field of view instantly jumped from six stories to eighteen! The wind suddenly roared, blowing you flat. You grabbed her hair with both hands — each strand as thick as a rope.

She ran.

Not toward the beach, but deeper into the park! Each step made the ground sigh, but you didn't feel much bumping because she was deliberately light-footed — nearly impossible for someone about to break.

"Over there... there's grass..." she whispered as she ran, and you felt the vibration above you, "grass... can cover it... right? Like a cat..."

She burst onto the great lawn deep in Binhai Park. Morning dew wet her ankles; grass blades swept her insteps like green velvet.

She stopped.

She faced away from the road, toward dense bushes. Her hands began untying her sash—

"Miaowu..." her voice was unrecognizable with trembling, "can you... not look..."

She gently lifted you from her head and placed you on a tree branch at the lawn's edge. The red basin was still in your arms.

Then she turned her back to you and crouched.

BOOM.

The lawn sank an inch.

You sat on the branch, holding the red basin, watching the light-blue hill slowly lower before you. The morning wind carried the smell of fresh grass, and...

In the distance, the delivery rider's e-bike approached. The old woman with her dog stood at the lawn's edge, watching from afar. The high school student had finally followed, standing under the tree, looking up at you — a second-grader sitting on a branch, holding a red basin, before whom crouched a blue hill that was weeping.

You did nothing.

You just sat on the branch, both hands holding the red basin, fingers unconsciously picking at the raised anti-slip ridge around the rim. The plastic creaked softly under your nails, so quiet only you could hear. Your inner left leg pressed against rough bark — itchy, then a little painful. There was a vertical crack in the bark with dried tree gum inside, like an amber, hard little scar.

The wind stopped.

Not completely — it just suddenly became very light, as if someone had turned the world's volume down one notch. Morning dew condensed on grass tips, one, two, then rolled down the slope with tiny taps like miniature glass beads on a jade plate. You even heard a cricket chirp from deep in the bushes, "chirp—chirp—", then that too fell silent.

Xiaoman's back tensed further.

Her shoulder blades protruded from the light-blue fabric like two small hills about to break ground. You saw the skin on the back of her neck — a vast pink-white plain, every fine hair standing on end, forming a thin golden edge in the morning light. Her breathing was shallow and rapid, each inhalation stirring the air and flattening the grass before her in waves, as if stroked by an invisible hand.

Her toes dug deeper into the dirt. Five pink-white toes like five giant stone pillars turned the black earth into furrows, exposing darker, moister soil beneath. Earthworms, startled, wriggled deeper, leaving only small silver-gray tails trembling in the air.

Pfft—

A very light, muffled sound came from below the "parachute."

Not a single sound — a sequence: first the short pop of a balloon pricked, then the heavy, warm thud of something hitting ground, then the splashing — shush—sssh———— like someone pouring a bucket of warm honey into an absorbent sponge.

It lasted a long time. You counted your heartbeats, thump, thump, thump; at the twelfth, the sound became the finer sss of soil absorbing water.

The sound of grass bending, the sound of soil drinking, and a warm breath rising from below the "parachute," mixing with the cool of morning dew to form a strange temperature difference. You smelled the bitter-green astringency of crushed grass, like the scent of Mom cutting a cucumber; then the earth's sweet-mud smell after rain; finally a stronger smell from inside her body — warm, fermented, like banana peels in a summer trash can left out all day, but not stinky, just very, very strong.

Your little nose wrinkled, but you didn't cover it or turn away. You just held the basin and sat on the branch, motionless.

Xiaoman's back arched sharply, like a bow pulled to its limit. Her hands death-gripped her skirt, knuckles white, nails scraping sss-sss on the fabric. Her forehead nearly touched her knees, long hair pooling on the ground among the grass like a black lake. A few strands, wet with dew from grass blades, lay flat on the ground, becoming dark, winding little rivers.

Her body trembled — a deep tremor as if underground springs were surging. You felt the branch beneath you shaking too, the bark crack rubbing against your pants, making a soft shush.

"Nngh..."

She let out a young-animal whimper, muffled in the darkness between her knees and hair, becoming lower and wetter. Her shoulder twitched, then a second, a third — each twitch made the sky above sway slightly in your vision.

Then, suddenly, she let go.

Not slowly — like a snapped string, bang, her whole body collapsed. Her spine went from a drawn bow to a soft, drooping arc. Her head dropped lower, forehead on knees, hair completely covering her face. Her hands slid from her skirt to the grass at her sides, fingers slightly spread like five tired white arch bridges.

You heard her release a long, trembling sigh—Whoosh—carrying the exhaustion of finally being released, like the last bit of air escaping from a pricked balloon. Her shoulders still trembled, but not with tension — with the shaking after crying, or after exhaustion.

Dew on the grass evaporated from her body heat, rising as an almost invisible white wisp from her skirt hem, glinting in the golden morning light, then dissipating.

The delivery rider still stood at the lawn's edge, one end of his blue tarpaulin trailing in the mud. He maintained his "should I hand it over?" pose like an awkward statue. His e-bike lay on the ground — probably fallen during the earlier vibration — front wheel still spinning, humming, rear wheel wedged by a stone, mirror tilted toward the sky, reflecting a fragmented blue.

The old woman faced away, but her dog didn't. The dog tilted its head, ears swiveling, front paw tentatively stepping half a pace forward, black nose sniffing quickly twice in the air, when the old woman yanked the leash and shushed. The dog blinked, confused, and licked its nose.

The high school student stood under the tree, staring at the ground. Several splatters of mud were on his uniform pants legs, radiating like a brown little flower. His fingers gripped his backpack strap, knuckles white, about as hard as you gripped the basin. His shadow was stretched long by the morning light, reaching under your branch. When you looked down, you could see the head in his shadow tilted slightly up.

"Whoosh—"

Another sigh. This one lighter, longer.

Xiaoman slowly turned her head.

Her movements were slow, like a rusty hinge. Hair pulled away from her cheek with a slight rip — a few strands stuck by tears and sweat peeled off, revealing pink skin below with finger marks. Her eyes were red-rimmed, as if painted with red chalk, nose red too, with a tiny snot bubble swelling and shrinking with her breathing.

The two-meter-wide tooth gap glinted in the morning light, gum pink, edges moist.

She saw you.

You held the red basin, sat on the branch, hair wind-tousled, expressionless — the blank face of a second-grader zoning out, eyes round, mouth slightly open, as if watching a cloud or an ant. Your left leg was numb, but you didn't shift because you'd forgotten. Your fingers still rested at the seventh fork of the white scratch inside the basin.

You didn't run. Didn't cover your nose. Didn't laugh. Didn't shout.

You just were.

Her mouth slowly curved up, then drooped halfway, then curved again. It was a crying-and-laughing expression, ugly, but terrifyingly real. The tear that had hung in her eye finally fell—

Plop.

It hit her own open left palm, splashing a small transparent spray that dampened the grass bits caught in her palm lines.

"Miaowu..." her breath carried heavy nasal congestion, like a tape recorder with a cold, "you... you're still here..."

Her skirt still lay spread across the flattened lawn like a blue lake. Grass blades around the lake held water droplets refracting tiny rainbows. A few sparrows, startled away, returned to distant treetops, tilting their heads.

You did nothing.

But Xiaoman seemed to think you had done the most important thing in the world.

---

## Chapter 5: Going Home

"I'm not scared of you, because this is just the natural you! I like the natural you!"

You said it again, voice thin, floating down from the branch.

Her pupils trembled slightly. She froze, even sobbing stopping. She raised a hand to wipe her eye but stopped, looking down at you and asking carefully:

"I... my movements are too big, won't I blow you away? You're really small, smaller than my eraser."

"I'm Xiaoman," she said. "Full as in fullness."

She pushed her upturned palm closer. You stepped on it — it wasn't "a bed" to you, it was "an entire pink playground." She lifted you, you rose. She placed you on her collarbone — a broad pink landmass.

You sat on her collarbone, legs dangling six stories up, swinging.

"Miaowu," she whispered, "you're really so small. But I like you very much."

She wanted to cradle you there, but suddenly her body stiffened again.

"Nngh... not done..."

Her voice carried a new, deeper panic. Her legs clamped shut again, knees knocking inward like pillars about to fall. Her hands came up from her sides, hovering, fingers opening and closing, opening and closing — she didn't know where to hold.

"More... more..."

Her face was redder than before, from forehead to neck, even the skin behind her ears turning ripe-tomato red. She turned, back to you, back to the road. Her hurried movement stirred wind that rattled the sycamore leaves; a few early-yellow ones drifted down, landing in your hair.

You saw her back trembling.

Not the shaking of crying — the shaking of holding it to the limit. Her shoulder blades were like two startled birds rising and falling under the light-blue fabric. Her long hair fell to her waist, ends swaying in the morning wind, brushing the bushes behind her and making them rustle.

"It's coming... it's coming..."

She muttered, voice low as if chanting a spell. Her toes dug into the dirt again, but this time not to hold it back, but—

Whoosh—

The sound came without warning.

Not drop by drop, not in streams, but as if someone suddenly turned on a giant faucet. The sound was broad, continuous, with powerful water pressure, like a storm-drain pipe pouring. You even heard the slap of liquid hitting the ground, then the shush of it spreading, like someone pouring a whole bucket of warm water on grass.

She'd death-gripped her skirt up to her waist — you saw her pale lower back and further... but you were only in second grade, not sure what it was, just thinking it looked like a waterfall falling from a cliff.

BOOM—

The moment the urine hit the ground, you felt the branch beneath you shake violently. Not an earthquake — sound waves through the ground into the tree trunk. You clutched the basin and saw a pale yellow, steamy stream rapidly spreading from her feet like an expanding map.

Grass blades were flattened, soil carved into small furrows that quickly filled and overflowed, becoming a glistening, warm pool. The pool's edge expanded at a visible rate, flowing toward the lawn's low point like a newly born stream.

The smell was stronger than before.

Warm, sharp, like a public restroom on a summer afternoon, like when you bring your nose close to the toilet at home — but amplified a thousand times. Your nose wrinkled. This time you couldn't help rubbing your nose with your shoulder, but you didn't turn away, didn't cover your nose, didn't jump from the tree.

You just watched.

Xiaoman's back trembled gently behind the waterfall. Her head was low, long hair covering her profile, but you saw her ears — red almost to bleeding, the small mole on her earlobe visible in the morning light.

"Sorry... sorry..."

Her voice was drowned by the water sound, but you read it from her lip movements. Her shoulders twitched. She was crying, but silently this time, only large tears falling into the expanding pool at her feet, making ripples — plop, plop, plop.

The pool reached the lawn's edge.

The delivery rider's e-bike rear wheel got wet from the spreading liquid. He jumped back, looked at his tire, then up at Xiaoman, his expression shifting from shock to absurd numbness. He still held the blue tarpaulin, its corner trailing in the mud, already damp and darkening from light blue to dark blue.

The old woman shouted: "Child—! Take her to the beach—! The beach—!"

The high school student stood under the sycamore. His shadow, once below you, was now lapped by the pool's advancing edge. He looked down at his shoes — white canvas, toe damp and dark. He didn't dodge, just sighed softly, so quiet it was almost swallowed by the water.

The water sound lasted maybe a minute, or two. You counted Xiaoman's back rising and falling; at the thirty-seventh, the sound changed — from pouring shush to pattering drips, then complete silence.

Only the soft sounds of residual water droplets on grass blades: tap, tap, tap.

Xiaoman still stood there.

Her skirt was death-gripped, fabric full of wrinkles. Her back still rose and fell gently, but slower. She slowly turned her head toward your branch—

Her face was streaked with tears, nose red, eyes swollen like peaches. But she looked at you, trying to smile — her mouth curved up then drooped, then curved again, uglier and more real than before.

"Miaowu..." her breath was hoarse like a broken trumpet, "I... I've... made everything dirty..."

The lawn beneath her had become a glistening lake. Morning light on the water refracted fine gold light, like scattered shards of glass. A few early sparrows landed at the edge, tilting their heads at the sudden "little lake," then hopping away, shaking droplets from their paws.

You sat on the branch, holding the red basin. Your left leg was completely numb, but you suddenly realized — your basin really was too small.

She held the crouch a few more seconds, as if confirming "is it really over?" Her fingers unconsciously dug at the ground, nails packed with black mud and grass bits. She looked around — no tissues, no leaves (too small for her), nothing. She bit her lip; that tooth gap glinted.

Then she stood up.

BOOM.

Her knees pulled from the soil, bringing up two chunks of turf. She didn't wipe. She didn't dare use her hands; they were already covered in mud. She tried pressing her legs together, hoping her skirt would wipe clean — but that just smeared the brown mud and dark-blue wetness wider on her legs.

She stood there, wind from behind chilling her calves. She felt something was wrong but didn't know what — until she looked down and saw her skirt.

---

## Chapter 6: The Skirt

You pointed at her skirt: "Your skirt... is dirty."

Your voice floated down from the branch, thin as a cotton thread dangling six stories, swayed by the morning wind. You pointed with your free hand — your finger tiny, pointing toward her skirt like a toothpick indicating a blue sky.

Xiaoman froze.

She followed your finger and looked down. The light-blue dress hem still hung below her knees, fabric crumpled from her death-grip, like a crumpled tissue spread out. She saw it — the hem edge had an irregular dark-brown band, the color of mud mixed with grass juice, like someone had dipped a brush in dirty water and painted a wobbly frame along the hem. A few spots were darker, deep blue — urine splashed and absorbed, darkening the fabric. Morning light on the wet band reflected a dull, non-glossy sheen, contrasting sharply with the dry light blue above.

Her fingers hovered, wanting to touch but not daring. Now she slowly crouched — boom, ground shook, ripples on the pool — and gently pinched a corner of the hem between two fingers.

The fabric unfolded between her fingertips like a giant, soiled map.

"It's really... dirty..."

Her voice was hoarse, with the nasal tone of someone who's been crying. Her thumb gently rubbed the dirty edge, immediately leaving a brown mud mark. She stared at the mark for a long time — long enough for the morning wind to dry her tears, leaving two tight white salt streaks on her cheeks.

Then her mouth suddenly downturned.

"Mom will scold me..."

She said it, with the distant grievance of the giant country, like the tone when you lose your favorite crayon at kindergarten. Her fingers still pinched the dirty hem, knuckles white, as if afraid the skirt would shatter.

"This dress... Mom just bought it... she said I'm growing up and should wear blue... like the sky..."

Tears came again, but not as many — just two, hanging on her lower lashes like transparent grapes. She sniffled, trying to wipe with the other hand, but that hand was also muddy; one swipe left a brown finger mark on her cheek.

"Now it's not like the sky... it's like... like..."

She couldn't find the words.

In the distance, the delivery rider finally moved. He carried the blue tarpaulin, stepping carefully around the reflective "little lake," and approached. He stopped about twenty meters from Xiaoman, looked up, and held up the tarpaulin.

"That... I have a cloth here... it's pretty big... want... want to cover up with it?"

His voice was broken by the wind, carrying the awkwardness of not knowing who to talk to or what to say.

The old woman faced away, but her dog didn't. The dog tilted its head, ears pricked, front paw tentatively stepping forward, when the old woman yanked the leash and shushed. The dog blinked and licked its nose.

The high school student stood under the sycamore, his white canvas shoes soaked. He pulled a pack of tissues from his backpack side pocket — normal-sized tissues, like small feathers in his hand. He looked up at Xiaoman, then at the tissues in his hand, and suddenly laughed — a helpless, self-mocking laugh. He stuffed the tissues back, then pulled out a bottle of water. He unscrewed it, took a sip, then held it up and shook it toward Xiaoman, as if to say: "Want water?" Though the bottle was probably only a drop to her.

Xiaoman didn't look at anyone. She still crouched, pinching the dirty hem, looking at you.

"Miaowu..." her breath fell from above, "are... are your pants dirty?"

You sat on the branch and looked down — your uniform pants legs did have a few mud splatters, kicked up when she crouched. The inside of your red basin had brown bark debris shaken from the branch.

You still held the small basin. Its rim pressed against your arm, a little painful.

---

## Chapter 7: The Natural You

"A little dirty too. But we can wash together — after I take my medicine."

Your voice was thin, floating down from the branch, half scattered by the morning wind. You said it naturally, as if saying "after breakfast." Your fingers unconsciously picked at the basin rim; the white scratch grew deeper.

Xiaoman heard.

Her ears twitched — not really, that micro-reaction when attention is suddenly caught. Her fingers on the hem stopped. She slowly raised her head and looked at you.

"Medicine...?"

Something new entered her breath — not grievance, not embarrassment, but worry. Her pupils contracted slightly, like two amber moons half-covered by clouds. She leaned closer, nose only ten-plus meters away. You smelled her breath — no longer the sharp hunger scent, but faint, warm milk, with a little salty after-cry.

"Miaowu, are you sick?"

Her voice was even lower, as if afraid to startle something. Her gaze slid from your face down to your small arms, legs, finally resting on the red basin in your arms — she probably thought it held medicine.

"Is it... because of me... you got sick from being scared?"

Her eyes reddened again, but this time not from the dirty skirt — from guilt. Her fingers released the hem, both hands twisting together on her knees, knuckles cracking. She lowered her head, long hair falling like a black curtain between you.

"Sorry... I always... always dirty things... break things..."

Her voice came from behind the curtain, muffled, as if from under a blanket. You saw her shoulders trembling, but she was fighting it, not wanting you to hear her cry again.

"It's not that. I have neurodevelopmental differences. I was born this way, that's why I take medicine. We need to wash clothes. If I don't take my medicine, it might take a long time to wash, until they fade."

In the distance, the delivery rider still held the blue tarpaulin in that awkward pose. He looked at Xiaoman, then at you, then made a decision — he folded the tarpaulin twice and flung it over his shoulder with a splash, like a blue cape, then turned and walked toward his e-bike.

The old woman with her dog took two steps toward the lawn edge, looking up at you on the branch, eyes narrowed as if watching a fledgling high up.

"Child," she called, her voice scattered by the wind, "where are your parents? So early, out here alone?"

The high school student still stood under the sycamore. He finished the water, crushed the empty bottle, and stuffed it in his backpack. Then he looked up at you and suddenly spoke — not loud, but clear:

"I know her. She lives in the apartment building below me."

He paused, choosing words.

"Her mother works the night shift. She wakes herself up for medicine every day, then goes to school."

He fell silent, unaccustomed to speaking so much. His fingers gripped his backpack strap, knuckles white, about as hard as you gripped the basin.

Xiaoman heard.

Her "black curtain" slowly lifted. She raised her head and looked at you. Her face still had tear streaks and brown mud marks, but her eyes had changed — from guilt to something softer, like slipping a hot water bottle under the covers in winter.

"Miaowu..." she said, "you're braver than me."

She slowly stood up. This time very slowly, afraid to shake anything. Her skirt fell; the dirty brown edge was obvious in the morning light, but she didn't pinch it anymore.

She extended her hand — not grabbing, but laying her palm flat like a giant, warm pink landmass, stopping below your branch.

"Come down. I'll take you home for your medicine. Then..." she looked at her dirty skirt, then at the reflective "little lake" on the lawn, and pulled an ugly, earnest smile, "then we'll wash the skirt together. I'll wash yours, you wash mine."

The morning wind rustled leaves on your branch. Your left leg was completely numb, but you suddenly felt light — as if someone had taken away a small stone from your heart.

The small basin in your arms was still warm, carrying your body heat.

---

## Chapter 8: The Happy Neighborhood

You set the basin on the ground with a clatter, the plastic bouncing half a turn on the concrete. You turned and ran.

First hundred meters.

Your sneakers slapped the concrete, arms pumping wildly like a small motor starting. Wind whooshed in your ears, bangs flattened. Your backpack slapped your spine.

3.6 m/s.

The pajama-clad auntie had just poked her head out when a blue-uniformed blur whooshed past her window. She blinked, and her spatula clattered on the floor: "...what was that?"

The scooter boy felt only a gust of wind blow his helmet sideways. He turned his head blankly, watching your receding back, mouth open: "...so fast."

After a hundred meters.

Your legs started aching. Like someone stuffed two water-soaked cotton balls into your calves, heavy and soft. Your lungs wheezed, like a hot wok on your chest. Your speed dropped — 2.5 m/s... 2 m/s... finally nearly dragging your feet.

Running through the alley, you were no longer "flying" but thudding against the walls. A cat jumped from the wall; you didn't even have the strength to look.

The apartment gate.

One hand on your knee, the other pulling the key from your neck, you clicked it open. You thundered up the stairs — not three at a time, but one by one, the banister creaking under your grip.

Third floor!

You crashed through the door, lunged at the dining table, grabbed the yellow medicine bottle — "Miaowu's medicine" — shook out two white tablets, swallowed dry. Glug. The pills rolled into your stomach like two small stones, bitter.

You charged into the bathroom, ripped off the scrub brush, dragged out the soap, tore off a piece and stuffed it in your backpack.

Turn. Downstairs.

Slower now.

Your legs were like two cooked noodles, trembling with each step. Your breathing was heavy, like a leaky bellows.

The alley.

You wheezed through, maybe 1.5 m/s, barely faster than a fast walk. Laundry on the balcony didn't even stir — you couldn't raise wind anymore.

The abandoned parking lot.

You nearly stumbled in, dropping to one knee on the concrete with a plop. Right hand braced on the ground; left hand dumped the brush and soap from your backpack.

"Whoosh—whoosh—whoosh—"

Your face was flushed red. Sweat dripped from your chin onto the ground. Your calves twitched like a small mouse jumping under the skin.

But you straightened up, raising the brush and soap, voice broken:

"Medicine... took it... brush... soap... brought them..."

Xiaoman crouched there, watching you, eyes round.

"Miaowu..." her breath carried heartache, "your... your face is so red... like a tomato..."

The high school student stood by the water pipe. He looked at you, then at the two shallow, wobbly footprints you'd dragged in the last few dozen meters.

He walked over, crouched, and pulled a water bottle from his backpack — the half he'd been drinking. He unscrewed it and handed it to you.

"...drink some water."

You shook your head, gently pushing it back.

"No water. Sit down and rest."

You plopped down on the concrete, back against a pillar. You set the brush and soap between your knees, hands hanging at your sides, palms up, fingers still twitching. The morning wind dried the sweat on your forehead, bringing a chilly, rusty smell.

Xiaoman shuffled forward. Her knees boomed half a meter on the concrete, stirring fine dust. She bent down, bringing her face near you — not ten-plus meters this time, but close enough to count the tears still hanging on her lashes, each a marble refracting tiny rainbows.

"Miaowu..." her breath was light as a feather, "your legs... are jumping."

You looked down. Your calf muscles were indeed twitching on their own, like two startled frogs under the skin. You placed a hand on them — your palm warm, calves burning hot, like touching a freshly baked sweet potato.

"It's okay..." your voice was hoarse, like sandpaper on wood, "just ran too fast... rest... and it'll be fine..."

You closed your eyes.

The world suddenly went quiet. The pipe's rushing receded, Xiaoman's breathing lightened, even your own heartbeat became a distant... thump... thump... thump... You felt the concrete's coolness crawl up through your uniform, like a cold hand stroking your burning back.

After maybe thirty seconds, or a minute.

You opened your eyes.

Xiaoman still held the pose, crouching before you like a giant, quiet pink statue. Her gaze moved from your calf to your face, then she did something—

She extended her index finger and gently touched the top of your head with her fingertip.

Not pressure — just a light contact. Her fingertip was warm, soft, with the give of living skin, like a giant breathing quilt draped over your head. You felt enclosed in a warm dome, even the water pipe sound muffled as if from underwater.

"Thank you..." her voice came from outside the dome, muffled and nasal, "for... running so fast... for me..."

The high school student stood aside. He looked at the half bottle of water pushed back, screwed the cap, and stuffed it in his backpack. He didn't speak, just walked to the pipe and turned the flow down from rushing to a gentle drizzle.

Your legs still twitched, but slower. You looked up through the gap between Xiaoman's fingertip and your head, and saw the sky — blue with a few white clouds like cotton candy floating above the abandoned parking lot.

You suddenly remembered something.

"Xiaoman..." your voice was still hoarse but clearer, "your skirt... still needs washing..."

Her fingertip still rested on your head. You heard her softly say "mm-hmm," the resonance traveling through the bones of your skull like someone gently tapping a small drum.

---

## Chapter 9: The Laundry Room

"Wait—you're so tall, I can't reach."

You suddenly straightened up, tilting your neck back, which cracked softly from the earlier sprint and lean-back. You looked at Xiaoman, at the light-blue skirt hem falling from her knees to the ground — to you it was an entire blue sky, spreading from six stories to your feet.

"You're so tall—" your tiny finger pointed at that sky, like a toothpick indicating a wall, "I can't reach it."

Xiaoman froze.

Her fingertip still hovered above your head like a pink cloud that had suddenly stopped. She looked down at her skirt, then at you crouching on the ground — your head only reached her ankle; when she crouched, her skirt spread about three basketball courts.

"Ah..."

She let out a soft, enlightened breath, like someone blowing from far away. Her ear tips turned red again — that embarrassed red, like when someone suddenly asks "did you finish your homework?"

"I... I can..."

She started thinking. Her fingers twisted unconsciously, making soft creaking sounds. Her gaze swept the parking lot — concrete ground, wall, water pipe, drain — finally landing on her skirt.

"I lie down?"

As she said it, her body began to move. She slowly leaned forward like a snow mountain collapsing—boom, hands on the concrete, palms spread wider than two cars. Her knees dropped too, with a boom that stirred dust; your hair fluttered.

Now she lay on the ground.

Like a giant, light-blue whale beached in the abandoned parking lot. Her cheek lay on the concrete, one amber eye facing you from about ten meters away. Her breath stirred fine dust on the ground, making small vortices at your feet.

Her skirt finally lay flat like a giant blue lake spreading infinitely before you. You could see the brown mud stains, dark-blue urine stains, grass-green juice — all spread on the concrete like a child's doodled map.

"Like this..." her voice came from the ground, muffled like from under a blanket, "can you reach it?"

You stood up and walked to her skirt edge.

The fabric under your feet was like a soft, gently undulating ground. You crouched and poked it—pfft—your fingertip sank into the light-blue fibers, like poking a giant marshmallow. You looked up; the skirt extended to her waist like a gentle blue hill; to climb it would take you about ten minutes.

You picked up the scrub brush.

The brush head was twice the size of your basin, with a long wooden handle you needed both hands to grip. You pressed it against a brown mud stain—scrub—bristles sank into the fabric like a pebble into a swamp.

"It seems..." you frowned, "seems like it's not doing much..."

You scrubbed ten times; the stain faded a little, but only an area the size of your fingernail. And across the whole skirt, there were dozens of such stains.

You set down the brush, hands on your hips, standing on the blue "ground" like a small, confused explorer.

The high school student walked over. He stood at the parking lot edge, looking at Xiaoman on the ground, then at you standing on her skirt. His mouth twitched, then he pulled out the water bottle and took a swig, as if steeling himself.

"I have an idea..." he said softly, as if afraid to startle something, "but... we need her to cooperate."

He looked into Xiaoman's eye.

"Can you... take off the skirt?"

The air suddenly went quiet.

The pipe's drizzle sounded distant. Xiaoman's eye on the ground blinked, lashes fanning wind that pushed you back half a step. Her ear tip — visible because her face was sideways on the ground — reddened at a visible rate, from pink to deep red, as if someone had splashed a whole bottle of red ink on it.

"Take... take it off?"

Her voice trembled like leaves in the wind, "here?"

Her fingers unconsciously dug into the concrete, nails scraping harsh sounds. Her breathing quickened, each exhale stirring dust at your feet into small tornadoes.

"I... I'm only wearing... this one..."

Morning wind blew through the abandoned parking lot, weeds rustling on the wall. You stood on her skirt holding the giant brush, looking up at her — her profile clear in the morning light, you could see the fine sweat on her nose, each drop marble-sized, rolling down her cheek toward the concrete.

"But..." her voice dropped as if telling a secret, "if I don't... Miaowu can't wash it..."

Her fingers stopped digging. She took a deep breath — the wind blew your bangs back — then slowly closed her eyes.

"Then... you guys... don't look..."

She began to move.

Not taking off the skirt, but rolling the skirt hem under herself. She braced one hand on the ground and used the other to clumsily roll the blue "lake" under her, little by little, like rolling a giant carpet. Her movements were slow and careful, each roll making a rustling sound.

She tried to cover her body with the rolled-up skirt.

But even rolled to the limit, the fabric only covered her waist and legs — her back, shoulders, and profile were still exposed to the morning light. Her skin had a warm, pink-white glow like a freshly baked giant loaf.

She pushed the rolled skirt cylinder aside with a gurgle, like pushing away a giant blue blanket.

Now she lay there wearing only a white giant-country tank top — a tank top to her, but to you like a giant white bedsheet with faint pink lace at the edges. Her arms and legs were exposed, pink-white, giant, living limbs spread on the concrete like a soft sculpture stripped of its shell.

She wasn't wearing underwear.

This thought came not as language but as visual confirmation. You saw the gap between the tank top hem and the concrete, saw the pink-white giant curve revealed there. You immediately looked away as if burned, staring at the mole on her elbow.

"Miaowu..."

Her voice came muffled from above her arm, as if rising from deep underwater. Her eyes were still closed, but her lashes trembled more.

"Are... are you inside there...?"

You scrambled out of the rolled skirt with a rustle, fabric scraping your back. You stood on the concrete still holding the scrub brush, heart pounding as if you'd just run that hundred meters.

"I... I can't move it..." your voice was thin as a breaking thread, "your skirt... is too heavy..."

Xiaoman's arm moved. She slowly lifted her face from the crook of her elbow, one amber eye looking at you, still holding mist like a sky after rain.

"Then..." her breath was hoarse, "then I... carry it under the water pipe myself...?"

Her fingers still dug into the ground but with less force. Her ear tips were still red, but lighter than before, like ink diluted in water.

"But..." her gaze moved to her body in only a white tank top, then quickly away, "you guys... really won't look...?"

The high school student still faced away, his uniform back damp with sweat like a dark map. His fingers gripped his backpack strap, knuckles white, like a stiff statue.

The pipe still drizzled; water pooled in the drain into a small clear stream reflecting morning light.

---

## Chapter 10: The Pool

"I don't know either." Then you quickly backed away a little, because water was overflowing.

Your small sneakers patted the concrete. You hopped back two steps, but the water still chased you, covering your toes, cold like standing on two water-soaked sponges. You looked down at your white sneakers — now dark gray, like two soaked little mice.

Xiaoman stood in the pool bottom, holding the giant dripping blue skirt in her arms, wearing nothing. Her long hair clung wet to her back and shoulders like a black, wet waterfall. Water droplets fell from her hair ends, plopping on the pool bottom and making ripples.

Her body was fully exposed in the morning light — pink-white, giant, water-beaded skin. Her backside was clean now; the white salt marks had been washed away to match the surrounding pink-white. But her ear tips were still red, red as if about to bleed.

Her left foot stepped back half a pace — not for balance, but to swish the skirt through the water again. When her heel came down, a tiny crack came from the pool bottom. Not the crisp snap of broken glass, but a duller sound, like a tooth biting into a grain of sand. She didn't notice. She was busy lifting the skirt hem from the water, water cascading out and drowning all fine sounds. The old tile embedded in the concrete cracked into three pieces beneath her heel — cracks radiating in three directions like a tiny white lightning bolt frozen in her footprint. Moss squeezed out from between the cracks, floated in the water, then sank.

"Miaowu..." her voice carried water echoes, "don't... don't look..."

You quickly turned back, small hands covering your eyes again. But your second-grade brain was still working.

"Soap..." you held the fist-sized soap out from between your fingers, as if offering candy, "here... and... you really have no change of clothes..."

Xiaoman looked at the soap. To her, it was only the size of a peanut. She extended two fingers and gently picked it up like a small bean. Her fingers were still wet; water dripped down her wrists.

"How... do I use this...?"

Her voice was confused. She tried pressing the soap against the skirt and rubbing — but it was too small. Her fingertip pressed and the soap went gliding between her fingers, falling into the pool water.

"Ah! It dropped!"

She cried out and bent to grab it — boom, another wave shook the pool, overflowing water rushing to your feet and wetting your pants again.

"I... I'll get it!"

You turned around — staring only at the water — and jumped into the shallow pool edge, bending to feel for the slippery soap. The water was cold, making you inhale sharply. Your small hands felt through the water, touching slippery moss, cold tile, then—

You found the soap.

"Found it!"

You held it up like treasure. But your uniform sleeve was soaked, dark blue fabric clinging to your arm like a cold layer of skin.

You looked up—

You saw her.

She stood in the pool bottom, holding the giant wet skirt, wearing nothing, long hair wet against her body. Her eyes looked at you, amber, carrying shy and urgent panic. Her body glistened with water droplets in the morning light, like a giant pink-white sculpture freshly washed by rain.

You froze.

Then you turned as if burned, flinging the soap toward her—

"Here! I'm not looking!"

The soap arced through the air and plopped into the water at her feet, making a small splash.

You faced away, hands death-gripping your eyes, heart pounding as if you'd just run that hundred meters.

"But..." your voice was thin as a breaking thread, "you really have no clothes... what do we do...?"

Xiaoman stood in the pool bottom, looking at the "peanut" soap at her feet, then at your small figure facing away.

Her eyes reddened.

"I... I don't know..." her voice cracked, "I... I only brought this one skirt..."

The faucet still rushed.

Water had overflowed the pool edge, pooling on the concrete as a glistening, expanding lake. Your shoes squelched; your pants legs were soaked, like two dark blue, heavy strips.

In the distance, the high school student stood by the wall, facing away. His white canvas shoes were wet from the overflow, but he didn't move. His fingers gripped his backpack strap, knuckles white.

Morning wind carried a warm, damp scent — from her, mixed with water mist, like mud after rain.

---

## Chapter 11: The Cover-Up

The old woman with her dog stood near the pool. She pulled a handkerchief from her pocket — a normal-sized, white floral one — held it up and waved it, then lowered it, her expression saying "I know this is useless but I don't know what to do." Her dog crouched at her feet, tilting its head, ears swiveling like two small radars.

The high school student still faced away from the pool. But he took off his uniform jacket, balled it up, and tossed it without looking toward the pool—pfft, the jacket landed on the wet concrete, splashing. To Xiaoman, it was probably only the size of a postage stamp.

"Everyone... everyone turn around..."

Xiaoman's voice rose from the pool bottom, muffled with water echoes and heavy nasal congestion. She stood in the pool bottom, clutching the wet blue skirt to her chest — barely covering from chest to waist, but shoulders, back, sides, and most of her hips were exposed. Her long wet hair clung to her skin like an irregular black curtain, but the curtain wasn't wide enough to cover everything.

She looked at the blue tarpaulin by the pool.

It lay spread on the concrete, two meters square, edges flapping softly in the morning wind. To her, it was only handkerchief-sized — no, smaller than a handkerchief, like a blue, crumpled tissue.

She extended a hand to reach it.

Fingers spread, water droplets on her fingertips, her fingers crossed the pool edge and landed on the concrete with a tap. She carefully pinched a corner of the tarpaulin — to her, that corner was only fingernail-sized — then lifted it gently.

The tarpaulin swayed between her fingertips like a small blue leaf.

She held it before her.

Silence.

"This..." her voice trembled like a candle in the wind, "it... it can't cover..."

She compared it to her chest. It barely covered one collarbone, or one ear, or—

She placed it on her head.

The blue tarpaulin spread on her head like a small blue roof tile, just covering her cowlick. Water droplets dripped from the corners onto her nose, then slid down her nose bridge.

"...is this right?"

She asked, her voice carrying absurd earnestness.

You stood by the pool, facing away, hands covering your eyes. But through your fingers you saw — you saw an eighteen- or nineteen-meter-tall girl standing naked in the pool, a handkerchief-sized blue tarpaulin on her head, looking as serious as if solving a math problem.

You couldn't hold it.

Pfft—

Your laugh leaked through your fingers, thin and crisp, like a fruit candy bouncing on concrete.

Xiaoman heard.

Her ear tips flushed red in a flash, from pink to deep red, as if someone had splashed a whole bottle of chili sauce. She took the tarpaulin off her head, clutching it in her hand, her mouth corners drooping.

"You... you laughed..."

Her voice carried a new grievance — not the earlier embarrassed, ashamed grievance, but the "I'm trying so hard and you're still laughing at me" grievance. Her eyes reddened, but no tears fell this time; she was fighting them.

"I... I know it's small... but... but..."

She couldn't continue. She pressed the tarpaulin against her collarbone — it was like a blue band-aid on her giant pink-white skin, covering about a thousandth of the area.

You laughed again.

But this time not mockingly — the laugh of seeing a big dog try to fit into a tiny cardboard box. Warm, soft, like cotton candy.

"Xiaoman," you said, facing away, voice thin with a smile, "that cloth is too small, it can only be a hat."

"...a hat?"

Her voice came from under the "hat," muffled. She paused, then slowly reached up to touch the blue tarpaulin on her head — it was still there, askew like a wind-blown blue roof tile.

Her mouth twitched.

Then she laughed.

Pfft—

Her laugh was low and soft, like a distant volcano snoring. But she was laughing — not the earlier crying-laughing expression, but really laughing. Her shoulders shook; water rattled off her wet hair in showers, hitting the pool bottom and making ripples.

"Then... then it'll be a hat..."

She pressed the tarpaulin on her head like fixing a tiny, tiny hat. Her face was still red, ear tips still red, but she wasn't crying.

She looked at your small figure facing away, your soaked pants legs and squelching sneakers, your fingers covering your eyes — between the middle and ring finger there was a very wide gap through which a round, peeking eye showed.

"Miaowu," she said, "your finger gap... isn't closed."

You snapped your fingers together, but it was too late.

"...you peeked!"

Her voice held no anger, only a helpless, soft reproach. She pulled the wet skirt higher in her arms to cover more.

"I... I didn't!" your voice came from behind clamped fingers, muffled, "I just... just looking at your hat!"

"hat?"

She reached up to touch the blue tarpaulin. It was soaked, clinging to her cowlick like a blue, crumpled scalp.

"...is it pretty?"

She asked, her voice carrying a resigned earnestness.

You peeked again through your fingers — she stood in the pool bottom, wearing nothing, holding a wet skirt, a handkerchief-sized blue tarpaulin askew on her head, dripping. Her face had tear tracks, redness, and a "whatever, this is happening" absurd expression.

"Pretty," you said, voice thin, "like... like a princess."

"...you're lying."

She said it, but her mouth curved up again.

In the distance, the delivery rider still faced away. His neck had turned from red to deep red. He cleared his throat loudly, as if steeling himself:

"Hey—! I have another one on my bike—! A bigger one—! A picnic mat—! Want it—?!"

The old woman with her dog watched this scene and shook her head with a smile — not mocking, but the smile of seeing a neighbor's child walk in adult shoes. She pulled out another handkerchief, blew her nose, then tied the dog leash to a nearby railing.

"I'll go see if there's anything else," she called, "I live nearby, I have bedsheets — big bedsheets —"

The high school student still faced away, but he turned his head.

Just a little. His profile was lit by morning light; you could see his mouth — curved up. His fingers still gripped his backpack strap, but his knuckles were no longer white. He bent down and picked up his uniform jacket, wrung it out — water splashed — then draped it over his arm.

"Once she's wrapped in something," he said without looking at you, "I'll help you wash the skirt."

Xiaoman heard.

She stood in the pool bottom, one arm holding the wet skirt, one hand steadying the askew blue "hat" on her head. She looked at the people by the pool — the delivery rider's back, the old woman's back, the high school student's profile, and you, facing away but with wide gaps between your fingers.

Her nose tingled.

But this time not from grievance — from something else, the warm, full feeling in your stomach after drinking a big cup of hot milk.

"You guys..." her voice rose from the pool bottom, wrapped in water sound, becoming wet and soft, "...why are you so good to me?"

No one answered.

The delivery rider was rummaging in his rear box, making clattering sounds. The old woman was walking away, her figure shrinking toward the Happy Neighborhood. The high school student was wringing his jacket, water dripping on the concrete.

You faced away, your finger gap still not closed, eyes sparkling through it at her.

"Because you're Xiaoman."

You said it, voice thin as a cotton thread dangling from the pool edge.

Morning wind blew through the abandoned parking lot, rippling the huge blue-green surface of the pool. Xiaoman stood in the water, the blue "hat" on her head flapping in the wind. The wet skirt in her arms trailed one corner to the water like a blue lily pad.

She looked at you.

Her eyes — those two amber moons — glinted in the morning light.

"Miaowu," she said, "your pants... are wet again."

You looked down. Your pants legs had been soaked by the overflow, dark blue uniform clinging to your legs like heavy, cold strips. Water dripped from the cuffs, pooling a small muddy puddle at your feet.

"...it's okay," you said, "they need washing anyway."

You paused and added:

"When your skirt is washed, we'll hang it up together."

The askew blue "hat" still dripped. Water ran down her forehead, to her nose, down her nose bridge, hanging on her upper lip edge, glistening like a transparent little mole.

She didn't wipe it. She couldn't free her hands — one held the skirt, one steadied the "hat."

But she smiled.

Revealing that two-meter-wide tooth gap, pink gum glinting in the morning light.

"...mm-hmm."

---

## Chapter 12: Hanging the Skirt

Granny Xiulan had walked away. The delivery rider was rummaging in his rear box. The high school student was still wringing the uniform jacket that no longer dripped.

You stood by the pool, pants soaked, sneakers squelching. You looked up at Xiaoman — she still stood in the pool bottom, holding the wet skirt, the askew blue "hat" on her head. A corner of the skirt trailed to the water like a blue, wet lily pad.

You suddenly thought of a question.

"Xiaoman," you said, "the skirt's washed, but it's wet. You need to dry it before you can wear it."

Xiaoman looked down at the bundle in her arms. She extended a finger and poked the wet fabric—pfft, her fingertip sank, and when she lifted it, a dark watermark remained.

"But..." her voice rose from the pool bottom, "I don't have a clothesline."

You looked around. The abandoned parking lot had no line, no rack, no dryer. Only concrete, wall, water pipe, drain, and a brightening sky.

You looked at the row of wall along the parking lot edge. It was about two hundred meters long, concrete, topped with broken glass — but one section had no glass, later repair, flat and wide, like a narrow runway.

"There!" you pointed, "On the wall! We can hang it there!"

Xiaoman followed your finger. That section of wall reached her knee — about sixty centimeters. To her, it was like a low, long step.

She carefully stepped out of the pool.

Boom—boom—boom—

Three steps. Water cascaded off her, leaving giant wet footprints on the concrete, each the size of a bathtub, full of water, glistening in the morning light.

She reached the wall, bent down, and shook open the wet skirt.

Whoosh—

Water droplets flew like a localized warm rain. You stood at her feet and got splashed in the face, cold, with soap scent. You spat out the water and wiped your face with the back of your hand.

Xiaoman spread the skirt on the wall.

The light-blue fabric unfolded along the wall top like a long blue runway. The footprints on the skirt — your footprints — were still there, darkened by water, like a trail of blue, wobbly little flowers along the wall.

Xiaoman stepped back and tilted her head.

"Hung up," she said, as solemnly as announcing a major event.

Then she looked down at herself. She still wore nothing — except the "hat" and three "band-aids" on her collarbone. White bedsheet, pink towel blanket, blue tablecloth, wet and clinging, edges slightly curling.

"I..." her ear tips reddened again, "I need to wait for the skirt to dry... before I can wear it..."

"Yeah," you said.

"Then... then I... like this now?"

She looked down at you. Her body in the morning light — that pink-white, giant, warm mountain — fully exposed. Her arms hung at her sides, fingers unconsciously twisting the skin on her outer thighs, leaving small red marks.

You thought about it.

"You can sit down," you said, "then you're not so... um... not so tall."

Xiaoman paused, then pfft-laughed.

"Sit down... and not tall?"

"Yeah," you nodded seriously, "sit down and... you're shorter. Shorter means less noticeable."

"...what kind of logic is that?"

But she sat down anyway.

Boom—

Ground shook. She sat cross-legged on the concrete, back against the wall, head exactly level with the wall top. Her wet hair fell on her shoulders, water dripping to the concrete, pooling a small ring beside her. Her hands rested on her knees, palms down, like a giant, quiet, pink-white statue.

Only this statue had an askew blue tarpaulin hat on its head and three differently colored "band-aids" on its collarbone.

The delivery rider finally found the picnic mat in his rear box. He turned around — then froze.

He saw Xiaoman cross-legged on the ground against the wall, "hat" on her head, "band-aids" on, a giant blue skirt spread on the wall beside her with a trail of tiny footprints.

He held the picnic mat to his chest, opened his mouth, then said: "...am I too late?"

The sound of the old woman returning came from the alley.

"Found it found it—!" She dragged a giant woven bag, rustling on the concrete. Her dog followed, carrying a corner of the bag in its mouth, tail wagging like a helicopter rotor.

She dragged the bag to the pool and upended it with a clatter — curtains, old bedsheets, a faded sofa cover, a peony-patterned summer blanket. Colors piled on the ground like a small fabric mountain.

"This curtain—" she shook out a dark green one, "used to be in the living room, then we changed it, never threw it away. This sofa cover— a little pilled but clean. This summer blanket—" she shook out the peony one, "my daughter-in-law bought it, too bright for me, kept it in the chest."

She spread all the fabric pieces by the pool, then looked up at Xiaoman.

"You pick. Whatever works, use it; whatever doesn't, use as rags."

Xiaoman looked at the fabric. The curtain — maybe cover one shoulder. The bedsheet — the other. The sofa cover — maybe a... scarf? The summer blanket — barely cover her knees.

All small. To her, all small.

But her eyes still reddened.

"Thank you..." She picked up the dark green curtain and carefully draped it on her left shoulder, "this... this can be a cape..." She picked up the bedsheet for the right shoulder, "this too can be a cape..." She wrapped the sofa cover around her neck — like a giant, pilled scarf. Finally she picked up the peony summer blanket and laid it over her crossed knees.

Now she looked like a giant princess in strange clothes sitting by the road. Or, like a giant cat wrapped in colorful fabric, sunbathing.

The delivery rider handed over the picnic mat. "This — for you — as... as a cushion."

Xiaoman took it and placed it under her crossed legs. "Thank you," she said, voice muffled and nasal.

The high school student stood by the pool, still holding the wrung-out jacket. He looked at layer after layer of fabric on Xiaoman — curtain, bedsheet, sofa cover, blanket, picnic mat — then suddenly laughed. Soft, but real.

"You look..." he said, pausing, "...warm."

Xiaoman looked down at the colorful layers, then looked up, showing that two-meter-wide tooth gap.

"...I am warm."

You stood at her feet, looking up at her. She wasn't trembling anymore. Her eye corners still had tear tracks, but her eyes were dry. Her hands rested on her knees, palms down, fingers slightly spread — soap suds from washing the skirt still clung under her nails, glinting white in the morning light.

You suddenly remembered something.

"Xiaoman," you said, "your front tooth."

Her hand came up to touch her lips, fingertip lightly touching the pink gum gap.

"This?" she said, "lost it during teething. Mom said the new tooth will grow, but it takes a long time." She paused. "At home, kids put lost teeth under their pillows, and the tooth fairy comes at night to take them and leave a coin. But my tooth is too big for a pillow."

She looked at you, expression serious.

"Miaowu, do... do you have a tooth fairy here?"

You thought. Your second-grade brain turned.

"Yeah," you said, "but the tooth you lost... the tooth fairy can't carry it."

Xiaoman blinked. Then she laughed — a surprised, caught-off-guard laugh rising from her stomach, shoulders shaking. The peony blanket on her knees slipped a little; she quickly pressed it back.

"...you're right," she said, voice smiling, "the tooth fairy would get flattened by my tooth."

In the distance, the skirt on the wall fluttered gently in the morning wind. The light-blue fabric was half dry; the footprints on the hem swayed like a string of blue, wobbly little bells. Sunlight slanted from the east through the wet fabric, casting a pale blue, watery patch on the ground.

Xiaoman looked at the light patch. She was quiet a few seconds, then said:

"The skirt is almost dry."

Her tone was calm, but hidden within was a tiny — just a tiny — barely noticeable disappointment. Because when the skirt was dry, she would put it on. And putting it on would end everything — the morning of washing the skirt, the pool with "band-aids," the little footprints on the wall.

"Almost," you said, "but not dry yet. The edges are still a bit wet."

You walked over and tiptoed to touch the hem edge on the wall. The fabric was cool and damp but no longer dripping. Your finger left a small dark-blue dent — another footprint.

Xiaoman watched your tiptoeing. Her eyes curved into crescents.

"Miaowu," she said, "the way you tiptoe... looks like a little hamster reaching for seeds."

"...I'm not a hamster," you said, still on tiptoe, "I'm Miaowu."

"Okay," she said, "a hamster named Miaowu."

"...not a hamster either."

"Then what are you?"

You pulled your hand from the skirt, turned, and looked up at her.

"I'm Miaowu."

She looked at you — your crumpled uniform, soaked pants, bangs wet and stuck to your forehead, the flush from the hundred-meter run still on your face. Your sneakers still squelched.

"Okay," she said softly, "you're Miaowu."

Morning wind blew again. The skirt on the wall fluttered; the footprints swayed. The sky was fully bright now, blue with white clouds like cotton candy above the abandoned parking lot.

In the distance, the city woke. Bus air brakes, soy milk steam, early risers practicing voices by the river. No sirens, no helicopters. The government still shut down, but the city ran.

Xiaoman sat cross-legged in the parking lot, wrapped in colorful fabric, waiting for the skirt to dry.

You stood at her feet, pants soaked, on tiptoe, waiting for the hem to dry.

Granny Xiulan sat on the concrete by the pool, back against the wall, eyes closed, mouth still curved. Her dog lay at her feet, chin on her knee, tail swishing.

The delivery rider sat on his e-bike seat, helmet pushed back.

---

## Chapter 13: Appetite

Your calf was still jumping.

Not the violent twitching from before, but slower, deeper, like someone gently drumming under the skin. You looked down at your left leg, uniform pants still wet, dark fabric clinging to your knee, rising and falling with each twitch.

You counted. One, two, three, four... at the seventh, you heard another sound.

Gurgle—

Not from your stomach. From above.

You looked up. Xiaoman sat cross-legged beside you like a pink-white mountain. Her stomach — that giant abdomen covered by the white tank — was making noise. Not a simple rumble, but deeper, longer, like a washing machine idling far away. The sound traveled through skin and air and arrived at your eardrums as a low vibration you could feel, like someone pressing the back of your head gently against a big drum.

Gurgle—boom—

It lasted three seconds. Four. Five.

Her hands pressed against her belly, fingers sinking into the skin. She looked down at you; the askew blue tarpaulin hat was still on her head, dripping a bead onto her nose.

"Miaowu..." her breath was hoarse, with a familiar weakness like a dying battery, "I... I'm really hungry..."

Her lips trembled. Not crying — hunger. You saw a white dry flake on her lower lip edge, the mark of dehydrated saliva. Her tongue darted out between her lips, quickly licking, then withdrew.

You looked around.

Granny Xiulan still sat on the concrete by the pool, back against the wall, eyes closed. Her dog lay at her feet, tail swishing but slowing, like a pendulum about to stop. The delivery rider sat on his e-bike, helmet pushed back, eating a sesame flatbread, crumbs on his blue tarpaulin shoulder. The high school student faced away but had turned half around, looking at Xiaoman, then at you, fingers unconsciously rubbing his backpack strap.

The skirt on the wall fluttered in the morning wind. The light-blue fabric was mostly dry — you touched the edge, cool and smooth, no longer damp. Only the thickest folds still held water; when you pinched, a few clear droplets squeezed out, landing on the concrete with a tap.

Xiaoman looked at the skirt too.

"It's dry..." she said, with something you couldn't identify — like relief, or... something else. She held the wall and slowly stood. Boom. Ground shook. The curtain, bedsheet, sofa cover, and blanket slid off her shoulders like a waterfall, piling at her feet.

She took the skirt down from the wall.

Whoosh—

The sound of fabric unfolding like a great banner in the wind. She held it before her, nose almost touching it — she was smelling it. You saw her nostrils flare, black nostrils (fist-sized to you) expanding and contracting.

"...it smells like soap," she said.

She started putting on the skirt. Not like a normal person, but like draping a giant blanket — head first, then finding the arm positions, arms through. Her movements were clumsy because the skirt was wet, fabric sticking to her skin with raspy sounds.

Done.

The skirt was still damp, light-blue fabric darker than when dry, like a post-rain sky. The brown mud stains and dark-blue urine stains on the hem had faded to near-invisible, only noticeable on close inspection, like a few darker clouds at the sky's edge.

She looked down at you. The blue tarpaulin hat had fallen off, lying at her feet like a blue leaf. Her hair was still wet, clinging strand by strand to her shoulders, water dripping onto the skirt and making dark circles.

"Miaowu..." she said, "I... I want to eat. And water. I'm thirsty."

Her voice was soft, but the physical fact of hunger still roared in her stomach. You could even see slight twitches in her abdominal muscles — through the wet skirt, that giant belly rose and fell with her breathing like a tide.

You stood at her feet. Your sneakers squelched, your calf jumped, your pants were wet. You didn't hold the basin anymore; only the scrub-brush wooden handle remained in your right hand, bristles carrying a few blue fibers from brushing the skirt.

You looked up at her. Her face was pale in the morning light — the pallor of someone too hungry. Her eyes — those two amber moons — looked at you with something you recognized. Not an order, not a request, but a waiting. Like at school when the teacher asks a question and you know the answer but aren't sure if you should speak.

You tilted your head back, neck cracking softly from prolonged looking up. You looked at Xiaoman, her face glowing white in the morning mist, wet hair clinging to her cheeks like black kelp.

"I'll buy you snacks!"

Your voice was thin, with the crisp clarity of a second-grader, like a marble on a porcelain plate. As you spoke, you transferred the brush to your left hand and reached your right hand into your soaked uniform pants pocket.

The pocket was deep — Mom had sewn it so you wouldn't lose things. Your fingers touched:

- A curled, sticky fruit candy (wrapper torn)
- A broken pencil stub with teeth marks
- A tangled blue-and-white jump rope (PE class, forgotten)
- Then, your fingertip touched something hard, square, warm.

You pulled it out.

It was a children's smartwatch, dark-blue silicone band with white cartoon kittens, their ears worn fuzzy. A fine crack ran along the screen edge — from hanging on the horizontal bar last month, a lightning-like crack from upper left to center. The screen was still on, showing 07:42, and three unread messages from Mom.

You poked the screen with your pinky (because your index and middle fingers still held the brush handle). The screen lit and jumped to the home screen: a yellow, round piggy-bank icon.

You poked the pig.

The screen changed. Green text appeared:

【Balance: $984.50】

Below the number: "Available for immediate use."

You held the watch above your head like a small glowing flag, shaking it toward Xiaoman's face. Morning light slanted from the east through the screen, casting a small shifting green patch on the concrete at your feet.

"I have nine hundred eighty-four dollars and fifty cents," you said, with no boast, just stating a fact like saying "I have two brushes," "I can buy a lot of snacks. Chips, chocolate, and jelly."

Xiaoman froze.

Her amber eye blinked, lashes fanning wind that pushed your bangs back. She looked at the small green-glowing screen in your hand, then at you.

"Nine hundred... eighty-four...?"

She repeated the number slowly, as if tasting each syllable. Her gaze moved between you and the watch, then her mouth started to curve up.

Not an ugly crying-laugh, but pure, childlike surprise.
