"Really?!" Her voice rose a little, then she remembered the convenience store glass door and clapped a hand over her mouth, eyes wide, watching you through her fingers. Her stomach rumbled again—gurgle—boom—but this time there seemed to be a hint of... anticipation?

"I can... eat snacks?"

Her voice rose like a kite string in the wind. She even unconsciously licked her lips — her tongue, towel-sized to you, passed between her lips leaving a wet trail.

In the distance, the delivery rider on his e-bike stopped eating. He tilted his head, looking at the green watch in your hand, then at you, his expression like seeing a talking hamster carrying a vault.

Granny Xiulan opened her eyes. Her dog lifted its head. The old woman squinted at the watch above your head, then smiled — that "kids these days" smile, but didn't speak.

The high school student walked over. He stood beside you and crouched so his eyes were level with yours. He looked at you and said softly:

"...nine hundred-plus, for snacks?"

His tone held no skepticism, only a gentle earnestness at converting. He looked up at Xiaoman, then down at you.

"But," he said softly, as if afraid to startle something, "her appetite... might not be enough."

You turned to look at him. Your small brain processed this.

"Why not enough?" you asked, "nine eighty-four fifty can buy a lot of bags. A big bag of chips, half a person tall. I've seen it at the supermarket."

You gestured with your free left hand, arms spread as if holding a giant invisible ball.

"Half a person tall," you repeated, "very big."

Xiaoman heard.

The light in her eyes dimmed a little. She looked at you, then at her own stomach — that giant abdomen still emitting low resonance. She extended a hand and gently pressed it to her belly; the wet light-blue skirt fabric dented deeply under her fingers.

"Miaowu..." her breath fell from above, with hesitation you didn't fully understand, "your snacks... are small?"

She extended her other hand, index finger and thumb spread, gesturing a distance — a two-meter-wide gap to you.

"At home," she said, "the cookies I eat... are this big."

Her fingers gestured. Then she thought about it and doubled the gap.

"No, this big. Like... like a bed."

She looked at you, her eyes carrying a distant, giant-country hunger, and a gentleness that didn't want to disappoint you.

"Your nine eighty-four fifty..." she said, "can it buy a cookie the size of a bed?"

Your small finger still poked the watch screen. The green numbers glowed: $984.50.

You'd never calculated what this was. To you, it was just a number, like your height or your student ID — an existing attribute, not "much" or "little." You didn't count when Mom deposited money. You just knew: swipe for the sausage at the school gate, swipe for vending-machine juice, the number went down but soon went back up.

But now, looking at Xiaoman — eighteen or nineteen meters tall, stomach roaring like a broken washing machine, tears basketball-sized, a cookie needing to be "bed-sized" to her — you suddenly realized the number might be much bigger than you thought.

You looked up at the high school student.

He crouched before you, uniform pants still wet. He looked at you, his expression shifting from "gentle" to mild shock.

"Do you..." he paused, "do you know how much money you have?"

You shook your head. You only knew the number on the screen.

"Nine hundred eighty-four fifty," you said, like reporting your student ID, "it's US dollars. Mom said it's US dollars. She said it's for going... going..." you thought for the word, "going abroad, it can be used."

The delivery rider jumped off his e-bike. Sesame crumbs still on his lips. He walked over and crouched, making his eyes level with yours — this put him only ten-plus meters from Xiaoman's toes, and he instinctively shifted sideways.

"Little friend," he said softly, as if afraid to startle something, "what does your mom... do?"

You tilted your head and thought.

"I don't know."

Granny Xiulan walked over too. Her dog followed, tail no longer wagging, just tilting its head at you.

"Child," the old woman said, "that watch in your hand — that's real nine-hundred-plus dollars?"

You held up the watch, screen facing her. The green numbers were bright in the morning light.

"It's real," you said, "I swipe it to buy things. One swipe, 'beep,' the door opens. Not a door — paying."

Xiaoman had been listening. Her tears still hung on her lashes, but she wasn't crying. She looked at you, at the small green-glowing screen, then did something—

She crouched down.

Boom.

Ground shook. She blocked the sun before you; your world darkened. Her nose was only ten-plus meters away; you smelled her breath — no longer the sharp hunger scent, but faint warm milk, and a hint of... anticipation?

"Miaowu," her breath fell from above, with a solemnity you'd never heard, "your money... how much bread can it buy?"

Her eyes — those two amber moons — looked at you. You saw your own reflection in her pupils, tiny, like a glowing green star.

You looked down at the watch. Your thumb swiped to "transaction history." A column of numbers appeared:

-$0.37  school-gate sausage
-$0.74  vending-machine juice
-$1.76  stationery-store colored pencils
-$6.62  bookstore picture book
+$52.00  Mom transfer (note: happy birthday)

Granny Xiulan leaned in to look at the watch screen, her reading glasses narrowed to two curved slits. Her dog pushed its wet black nose toward your wrist, sniffing twice, when the old woman yanked it away.

"Well," the old woman straightened up, "it really is real money. Child, your mom saved a lot for you."

The delivery rider stuffed the last bite of flatbread in his mouth, chewed twice, then stopped. He looked up at Xiaoman — her stomach making that broken-washing-machine sound, gurgle-boom, carrying a pained tremor — then down at the small green screen on your wrist.

"Little friend," he crouched, knees cracking on the concrete, voice as soft as handling something fragile, "listen to me. Nine eighty-four fifty, buying school-gate sausages, gets you over two thousand. But..." he pointed at Xiaoman's stomach, that giant abdomen rising and falling under the wet skirt, "for her, two thousand sausages... might just whet her appetite."

The high school student walked over and stood beside you. His white canvas shoes were still wet, each step making a squeak. He looked at Xiaoman, at her still-dripping light-blue skirt, at her hand pressed to her belly with white knuckles.

"How much does she eat per meal?" he asked Xiaoman, his voice like a feather on water.

Xiaoman lifted her face from her knees. Her eyes were half open, a basketball-sized tear hanging on her lower lashes, trembling, refracting the morning light like a transparent moon about to fall.

"I... I'm not sure..." her breath was like a leaky bellows from far away, "at home... a slice of bread... is this big..."

She extended her right hand, index finger and thumb spread, and gestured. A two-meter gap. Morning wind passed over her fingertips, carrying a warm gust that blew your bangs back.

"I eat three slices in the morning... and milk... two cups... the cups... are this tall..."

She raised her hand to above the wall. That wall reached her knee; the level she indicated was two Miaowus higher than the wall.

Granny Xiulan's dog whimpered, tail stopping, ears flattening back.

Xiaoman's stomach rumbled again. Louder, longer this time, like a freight train entering a station far away, wheel-on-rail sounds pulled very far. Her body swayed like a mountain in a mild earthquake, one hand bracing on the ground, knuckles scraping harshly on concrete.

"Miaowu..." her voice was light as a feather, with a dying-battery weakness, "I... I'm a little cold..."

Her wet skirt clung to her; morning wind raised fine goosebumps — to you, giant pink-white waves spreading from her arms to her shoulders, each bump the size of a small ball, glinting like pearls in the morning light.

The delivery rider suddenly stood and brushed off his pants. Sesame crumbs still clung to his blue tarpaulin shoulder.

"East Sea Farmers' Market!" he said, with the urgency of someone grasping a floating log, "that place just opened! Wholesale prices! Rice, flour, whole pork sides, baskets of vegetables! Cases of instant noodles!"

He turned to you, crouching, hands on knees, eyes level with a second-grader. An oil sweat bead glistened on his nose.

"But little friend, you have to think. Nine hundred-plus, retail snacks, gone in one meal. If you go wholesale... maybe it lasts two or three days."

The high school student shook his head. His bangs swayed in the wind.

"Going to the market..." he said softly, "how does she get in? Eighteen meters. Doors are only two meters. And... can she even walk there?"

He looked at Xiaoman's legs — those pink-white giant pillars, knees still trembling slightly.

Everyone fell silent again.

Xiaoman slowly lowered her head, forehead almost touching her knees. Her long hair fell like a black waterfall, half covering her face. Water dripped from her ends onto the concrete, pooling a small expanding puddle at her feet.

"I... I can eat less..." her voice came from behind her hair, muffled like from under a blanket, "it's okay... I've... been hungry before..."

Her shoulder twitched. Not crying — cold, and hungry. Her fingers sank deeper into the wet skirt fabric, nails scraping sss-sss on the light-blue fibers.

Granny Xiulan suddenly said: "My place!"

Everyone turned to look at her.

The old woman with her dog stood in the morning light like an old tree that had suddenly started talking. A chipped plastic hair clip with a faded red flower still pinned to her pajama collar.

"My daughter-in-law went home last week and brought half a pig! It's in the freezer!" She paused. "Three fifty-jin bags of rice! Two cases of instant noodles! A bucket of oil! Five jin of eggs!" Her voice echoed in the empty parking lot. "It was for a full-month banquet... then postponed. Everything's in my freezer and on the balcony."

She looked at you, at this second-grader, eyes narrowed.

"But child, those things... how many meals is that for her?"

The delivery rider calculated, fingers in the air: "Half a pig... maybe a hundred jin? To her... maybe... one piece of meat? Three bags of rice... a hundred and fifty jin... maybe... a few bowls?"

The high school student added: "Maybe... one meal? Two?"

Xiaoman buried her face deeper. Her breathing grew heavy, each inhalation stirring wind that swirled ground dust into small tornadoes at your feet. Her back rose and fell under the wet skirt like a storm-tossed sea.

You stood there, watch still glowing on your wrist. Green numbers jumped in the morning light: $984.50.

You looked down at your shoes. White sneakers had turned dark gray, toes still dripping, tap-tapping on the concrete. Your left leg still twitched, but slowly, like a sleepy animal. Your left hand still held the scrub brush, wooden handle dark with your palm sweat.

You looked up, tilting your neck, at Xiaoman — that trembling, wet, hungry pink mountain. Xiaoman's eyes showed through gaps in her hair, half open, amber, like two low-battery lamps. Her lips were dry, the white flake on her lower lip curling up like a small, withered leaf.

The wind stopped.

The abandoned parking lot went suddenly quiet. The pipe's drizzle sounded distant, the delivery rider's breathing lightened, Granny Xiulan's dog rested its chin back at her feet with a long sigh.

Everyone looked at you.

---

## Chapter 14: The Decision

You looked down at your shoes.

White sneakers had turned dark gray, toes still dripping, tap-tapping on the concrete. Your left leg still twitched, but slowly, like a sleepy animal. Your left hand still held the scrub brush, wooden handle dark with sweat.

You looked up, tilting your neck, at Xiaoman — that trembling, wet, hungry pink mountain. Her eyes showed through gaps in her hair, half open, amber, like two low-battery lamps. Her lips were dry, the white flake on her lower lip curling up like a small, withered leaf.

The wind stopped.

The abandoned parking lot went suddenly quiet. The pipe's drizzle sounded distant, the delivery rider's breathing lightened, Granny Xiulan's dog rested its chin back at her feet with a long sigh.

Everyone looked at you.

You looked down at the watch again. Green numbers still jumped: $984.50. Your thumb swiped to contacts — Mom's avatar there, a white kitten in a nurse's cap. You poked it.

Ring—ring—ring—

Three rings, then voicemail. Mom was on night shift and couldn't answer. You heard her outgoing message: "Miaowu, Mom's busy. Remember to take your medicine, remember to eat breakfast. Love you."

You hung up without leaving a message.

You let your wrist down and looked up. Your eyes were black, small, like two watermelon seeds set in a round face. Your face showed no hesitation or struggle, only the calm of a second-grader who has figured something out.

"Go to the farmers' market."

Your voice was thin but clear, like a marble on tile, bouncing once, twice.

"Wholesale buy rice, flour, meat, eggs. Buy as much as possible."

You paused, looked down at the watch number, then up and added:

"It's okay to spend it all. This is my money."

The delivery rider's mouth opened. Sesame crumbs fell from his lip onto his blue tarpaulin shoulder; he didn't notice. He looked at you like a talking hamster carrying a vault that had just made a decision an adult might not make.

Granny Xiulan's hand froze mid-air — she'd been reaching for a handkerchief to blow her nose, now forgot. Her reading glasses narrowed as she looked at you, lips moving but no sound. Her dog tilted its head, ears swiveling, tail slowly, one by one, beginning to wag.

The high school student crouched beside you, eyes level with yours. His white canvas shoes still wet, uniform jacket on his arm, shirt cuffs rolled to elbows showing pale, water-whitened forearms. He looked at you for a long time — long enough to count the fine droplets on his own lashes.

Then he smiled.

Not that "this kid is cute" smile, but another — as if he'd been waiting for an answer and you'd given it, though he himself didn't know what he was waiting for.

"Okay," he said, standing, knee cracking, "I'll help you calculate. I roughly know wholesale prices — my dad used to run a small eatery."

The delivery rider snapped out of it. He pulled his helmet down and clicked it under his chin, then ran to his e-bike.

"My bike can carry it! Rear box plus footrest plus front basket — about eighty jin! Two trips — no, three!"

His voice echoed in the empty parking lot, carrying the excitement of finally having something to do. He mounted the e-bike, turned the key, the dashboard lit with beeping electronic tones.

Granny Xiulan blew her nose, stuffed the floral handkerchief back in her pocket, and stepped forward with her dog.

"Those things in my fridge — half a pig, rice, eggs — bring those out too. It's postponed anyway, just sitting there. I'll go get my little grocery cart."

She turned to leave, then looked back at Xiaoman.

"Child," she said, her voice like sandpaper on wood but warm, "you wait. You won't go hungry."

Xiaoman slowly raised her head.

Her long hair fell from her cheeks with a whoosh, like two black waterfalls brushed aside. Her face was streaked with tears — cried, wind-dried, then freshly added. White salt tracks ran from eye corners to chin like dry riverbeds. Her nose was red; that two-meter tooth gap glinted.

She looked at you.

Her eyes — those two amber moons — looked at you. You saw your own reflection in her pupils: a small, crumpled-uniform, messy-haired second-grader, left hand holding a scrub brush, right wrist wearing a green-glowing watch, pants soaked, sneakers leaking, but standing straight.

"Miaowu..." her breath fell from above, thick with nasal congestion like a tape recorder with a cold, "that's your money... spending it all?"

Her fingers dug into the concrete, nails scraping soft creaking sounds.

"What about... buying sausages later? Juice later?"

Her voice trembled. Not from hunger, but another trembling — like when she stood in the pool and whispered "why are you so good to me."

You looked up, neck sore, but held the pose. Morning light slanted from the east onto your round, flushed face, still carrying the flush from the hundred-meter run.

You thought.

Your second-grade brain turned. You thought of the sausage — the school-gate sausage, sizzling on the griddle, brushed with sweet sauce, sprinkled with cumin, speared on a bamboo skewer, hot enough to make you switch hands. You thought of vending-machine juice, clanking down, cold and sour, your tongue turning orange. You thought of colored pencils at the stationery store, picture books at the bookstore, the "happy birthday" note on Mom's transfer.

Then you looked at Xiaoman — eighteen or nineteen meters tall, trembling with hunger, skirt still damp, face streaked with tears, a basketball-sized tear hanging on her lashes. Her stomach rumbled again, gurgle-boom, the sound from deep within her like a tired freight train entering a distant station.

You said:

"Sausages can wait. You're hungry now."

Your voice was thin as a cotton thread rising from the ground, swayed by the morning wind, but it didn't break.

Xiaoman's eyes reddened. Not just moist-eyed red, but deeper — as if someone spread a thin layer of strawberry jam inside her eye sockets, from inner corners to outer, then overflowing. Her lashes — that row of broom-thick lashes — trembled violently.

A basketball-sized tear gathered at her eye corner, refracting the morning light like a transparent moon about to fall.

Then she smiled.

Different from the earlier crying-laugh, different from the pool's "whatever, this is happening" smile. It was an expression she couldn't control — mouth curving up, revealing that two-meter tooth gap, pink gum glinting, but eyes still streaming, tears and smile mixing into a wet, crumpled, ugly face.

"Miaowu..."

Her breath was hoarse as a broken trumpet, but with a frequency you hadn't heard before.

"Do you... really want to spend your sausage money... on an adult who cries loud enough to shatter glass..."

You looked at her.

Your hand still held the brush, wooden handle darkened with sweat. Your calf still twitched, but you no longer noticed. You tilted your head, eyes round, mouth slightly open, face blank — the blank face a child makes when hearing a question they don't quite understand.

"But," you said, as if stating a fact everyone knows, "you're not 'an adult who cries loud enough to shatter glass.' You're Xiaoman. Full as in fullness. You said that yourself."

The basketball-sized tear finally fell.

Plop!

It hit her own crossed knee, splashing a wide transparent spray. Her peony blanket got a deep wet pit; water dripped down the petal edges onto the concrete. Her shoulder trembled, her stomach growled, her hair dripped, her skirt hem was still damp.

But she smiled. Not ugly now — a real smile, like sunlight through clouds.

"...mm-hmm."

She said it. One word, but its resonance traveled from her chest through the air and morning light and your soaked uniform and twitching calf, arriving at your eardrums as a soft, warm breeze.

The delivery rider had already started his e-bike, motor humming. He turned his head and called:

"I'll head to the market first! See which shop opens early!" Then he paused, his helmeted face turning to you, suddenly serious, "little friend, your watch — you'll need to swipe it. Nine hundred-plus buys a lot."

Then he twisted the throttle and the e-bike shot off, rear wheel splashing a small spray, then turned into the alley, taillights flashing twice and disappearing into the morning light.

Granny Xiulan had reached the parking lot exit. Her dog trotted beside her, leash dragging on the ground. She looked back and waved her handkerchief, fluttering like a small white flag in the wind.

"I'm going back for the cart and the freezer stuff! You wait — don't go away—!"

Her voice receded, swallowed by alley echoes.

The high school student stood. He shook out his uniform jacket, wrung it — no more water, but fabric still damp. He draped it over his left arm and pulled a palm-sized notebook and retractable ballpoint from his backpack. The cover was crumpled, corners curled, but he flipped to a blank page with practiced ease.

He crouched before you, clicking the pen out.

"Come, let's calculate. Wholesale rice is about... hmm, I've been a few times, I remember. Fifty-jin bags, about..." He said a number, then wrote a line in the notebook, small but clear handwriting, "flour is cheaper but more filling. Meat is expensive but lasts longer. Eggs sold in trays, thirty per tray — to her, maybe one bite."

He looked up at you.

"Your goal — fill her up. One meal at a time. No need for balanced nutrition, no need to think about tomorrow. Fill today first."

His tone was calm, like solving a math problem. But water droplets still hung on his lashes, his canvas shoes still squeaked, and his right thumb web had a faint pink old scar like a small, curved river.

Xiaoman sat behind you, cross-legged, like a mountain wrapped in a wet skirt. She watched the high school student's back, your tilted profile, the green-glowing watch on your wrist.

Morning wind blew from the east, stirring the ivy on the wall, rustling. Pool water settled, surface dropping a few centimeters, revealing a dark green moss line on the wall. In the distance, the city fully woke: bus air brakes, soy milk steam, some early riser's drawn-out ya—a——, like a rubber band slowly stretched.

You suddenly remembered something. You reached into your soaked uniform pocket and pulled out another thing.

The curled, sticky fruit candy. The wrapper was torn; its edge had faded in the water, revealing the pale yellow, translucent candy inside. A blue fiber clung to it — from the brush bristles when you were scrubbing the skirt. You plucked off the fiber and placed the candy in your palm.

The candy was small. To you, a normal fruit candy, about the size of your thumbnail. To Xiaoman... maybe a sesame seed — no, smaller than a sesame seed, like a grain of fine sand.

You tilted your head back and raised your hand high, palm open.

"Xiaoman. Here. A little something to hold you over."

Xiaoman looked down. She saw — in your pink, small palm, lay a pale yellow, translucent, slightly melted fruit candy.

Her lashes trembled.

She extended her right hand, index finger and thumb spread, slowly reaching toward you. The gap between those fingers was wide enough for a small car. Her movements were slow, careful — slower and more careful than any you'd seen. Her fingertip hovered above your palm, not pinching directly, but touching the edge of your palm lightly with her fingertip side, as if afraid to blow away the small candy.

Then she picked it up.

The candy was like a glowing, pale yellow grain of sand between her fingertips. She held it near her eyes to look closely — close enough to see the wrapper's creases, close enough to count the bubbles in the candy. Her pupils dilated then contracted, like a giant camera focusing.

She put the candy in her mouth.

You didn't see her chew. You only saw her lips close, cheek move slightly — to you, a pink-white hill rising and falling — then her eyes widened.

"...sweet."

She looked down at you. Her expression changed. Not crying, not laughing, but an expression you'd seen at kindergarten — when a child first eats ice cream, first tries pop rocks, first discovers such things exist in the world. Pupils slightly dilated, lips slightly parted, breathing delayed by half a beat.

"Miaowu," she asked, "what flavor is this?"

"Lemon. Lemon flavor."

Xiaoman extended her tongue between her lips and licked her upper lip. The candy had dissolved in her mouth, but the taste remained.

"Lemon..." her breath carried a distant, soft aftertaste, "we have lemons at home too. But not this sweet."

She placed her hands on her knees and looked down at you. Her eyes still held tear-mist, but beneath it something was glowing — not amber moons, but a finer light, like stars.

"...thank you."

The high school student clicked his pen and stood. He closed the notebook, cover crumpled and corners curled, but he held it like an important document.

"Calculated," he said softly but steadily, "nine hundred-plus, wholesale rice, flour, eggs, enough to fill her one meal, maybe a little left — what's left can buy cheap vegetables, or another bag of rice."

He looked down at you.

"To the market?"

You held up the watch; the screen lit. Green numbers still jumped: $984.50. Your thumb swiped to the payment page — under a yellow, round pig icon appeared: "Ready to scan."

You nodded.

"...but now there's a problem."

The high school student looked up at Xiaoman. She sat cross-legged in the parking lot, eighteen or nineteen meters tall, wet skirt clinging, hair still dripping. She looked back at the high school student, her eyes still holding the lemon candy's sweetness and the wetness from crying.

"How does she get there? Walking the road..." the high school student looked at the alley, where people and cars were already moving, "...it'll be gridlocked."

You followed his gaze to the alley, then at Xiaoman's feet — those giant pink-white feet, toes caked with mud and grass bits. Her sole on the concrete was longer than a car. You imagined her walking on the road: cars detouring around her feet like beetles, traffic lights only reaching her knees, bus roofs maybe brushing her skirt hem.

You turned to look into Xiaoman's eyes.

"How did you come here?"

Xiaoman blinked, lashes fanning wind that lifted the fine hairs on your forehead.

"I... I don't know," she said softly, as if recalling a blurry dream, "I fell asleep... and woke up here. At the beach, the sand was cool..."

She extended a hand and touched the back of her head — the wind pressure made you step back. Her fingers searched in her hair, as if looking for a switch, or an explanation.

"Can I... not walk on the road?"

She looked at you, then at the high school student, gaze moving between you. Her fingers stopped at the back of her head, pressing a strand of wet hair behind her ear, as if thinking about a very hard problem.

Just then, in the distance, came the rumble of rolling wheels.

Granny Xiulan returned from the alley.

She pushed a folding grocery cart whose wheels clicked on the alley's concrete like a small freight train. On the cart: a fifty-jin bag of rice — upright, taller than Miaowu, dark green woven sack printed with golden rice stalks — next to it two cases of instant noodles stacked crookedly, strapped to the handle with a bungee cord; on top, a red plastic bucket stuffed with eggs — each egg nestled in rice bran, pale brown shells visible.

Her dog followed the cart, carrying a frozen pig leg in its mouth — the leg was rock-hard, white frost steaming in the morning light. The dog's neck was stretched high, tail wagging like a helicopter rotor, making muffled whines as if to say "this is heavy but I won't let go."

The old woman pushed the cart into the parking lot; a wheel caught on a crack with a clatter, she muttered "damn road," then kept pushing.

"Coming coming—! The frozen pork hasn't thawed yet, hurry—!"

She pushed the cart to Xiaoman's feet and bent to undo the bungee, veins standing out on her hand but movements quick.

The moment she bent, she stopped.

Her hand stopped on the bungee hook; her eyes looked at the ground — at Xiaoman's shadow sitting cross-legged on the concrete, at those giant feet, at water dripping from the skirt hem.

Then she looked up at you.

"Wait," she said, "how does she get to the market? Walk there?"

She looked around — the high school student beside you, the delivery rider's e-bike gone, beyond the wall the city noise growing. A bus passed in the distance; its rooftop AC hummed; through the wall gap you could see a yellow light flashing on the roof.

"...can't walk the road," the old woman said decisively, like stating a law of physics, "she'd draw a crowd. Even without a crowd, her foot on asphalt — it's July, asphalt softens by noon. She'd leave a footprint with every step."

She undid the bungee and straightened, wiping sweat from her forehead with the back of her hand. Her hair was a mess from the wind, but her eyes were bright.

"Need to find another way."

Xiaoman sat there watching these people around her feet discuss how to get her there. The peony blanket still covered her knees, with a wet pit from the earlier tear. Her skirt hem was nearly dry now, only the thickest folds holding a little moisture.

She lowered her hand from the back of her head; a broken hair clung to her fingers, the strand glinting in the morning light.

"I..." she spoke, her breath soft as if afraid to interrupt, "I can try..."

Everyone turned to her.

"Earlier... those few times I ran over here, if I don't wear shoes, if I walk very, very lightly, the ground won't break." She paused, fingers unconsciously twisting the blanket edge on her knees, "on grass. The grass can... cushion."

She looked beyond the parking lot. Beyond was the alley, beyond the alley was the road, across the road more roads, and the farmers' market about two kilometers away.

But — she saw another way.

Binhai Park's great lawn connected to the beach; the beach connected to low reefs; beyond the reefs was an abandoned freight railway; beside the railway was the old East Sea industrial zone — and the farmers' market was at the edge of the old industrial zone, near the railway's terminus.

She raised her right hand, index finger extended, pointing that direction. In the morning light, her finger was like a giant, pink-white tower.

"When I came from the beach... I passed there. No houses, no roads, just grass and sand, and an old railway overgrown with weeds. I can walk along the railway."

Her fingertip trembled slightly in the morning light — not from hunger, but from the careful urgency of trying to prove she could be useful.

"When I'm near the market, I'll... I'll hide behind that big roof. You go in and buy."

She looked down at you, her gaze soft but earnest.

"I won't step on anything. I promise."

---

## Chapter 15: The Limit

The high school student closed his notebook, cover crumpled and corners curled, but held like an important document. He turned to walk toward the alley entrance, then suddenly stopped.

He turned back, brow slightly furrowed — not a "big problem" furrow, more like noticing a hidden condition in a math problem.

"Wait," he said softly but clearly.

You were about to follow Granny Xiulan's cart when you heard him and stopped. Your sneakers still squelched, water squeezing from the mesh with each step.

The high school student crouched, eyes level with yours. His white canvas shoes on the wet concrete, toes now thoroughly dark gray.

"Miaowu," he said, "your watch — does it have a spending limit?"

You blinked. The word turned in your small brain like a marble in a maze, finding no exit.

"What's a limit?"

"It means—" the high school student paused, searching for an explanation a second-grader could understand, "the most you can spend at once. Some cards can only spend a certain amount per day. If you go over, it won't swipe."

You looked down at the watch. The screen was still on, the yellow pig icon there, green numbers jumping: $984.50. You poked the pig; the payment page appeared, "Ready to scan" flashing. You poked the settings button — the little gear icon — and the screen jumped to a page of dense small text. You didn't know many characters; you frowned, slowly tracing your finger, recognizing one character at a time.

"...daily...transaction...lim..." your voice was thin, like reading aloud in class and stumbling on unknown characters, "...what's this character?"

The high school student leaned in to look. Water droplets still on his lashes, but when he narrowed his eyes they squeezed off.

"Limit. The limit's limit." Then his gaze moved to the number on the screen, and his pupils contracted slightly.

"...one hundred US dollars."

He said the number calmly, but you couldn't say why — the air felt suddenly heavier. Like blowing up a big balloon in PE class, halfway through, someone gently poked it — it didn't pop, but leaked.

"How much is one hundred dollars?" you asked.

The high school student stood and glanced at Xiaoman. She sat cross-legged in the parking lot, skirt spread on the concrete like a light-blue lake. Her stomach growled again — gurgle-boom — slightly quieter now, probably thanks to the lemon candy, but hunger was still written in her trembling fingers and pale lips.

"One hundred dollars," the high school student said, the ballpoint unconsciously drawing a line in the notebook, "can buy... two fifty-jin bags of rice. Or one bag of rice plus a tray of eggs. For her—" he looked up at Xiaoman, "—probably one breakfast. No, half a breakfast."

Granny Xiulan stopped. She'd been bending to lift the frozen pig leg from the cart — the dog still circling and whimpering — heard this and straightened, wiping sweat from her forehead.

"How much? A hundred?" her voice was like sandpaper on wood, "what's that for? That half a pig in my freezer is worth more than a hundred."

The delivery rider's voice came from the alley entrance. He'd ridden out a ways, but heard the conversation and turned back. The e-bike motor hummed, the rear wheel arcing back to the parking lot entrance.

"Spending limit?" he poked his head from his helmet, "then we're done. Wholesale market sells rice by the bag, fifty jin minimum. A hundred bucks is fine for retail, but wholesale — they won't break open a bag. Broken prices are different."

He took off his helmet and scratched the back of his head, hair flattened by the helmet like trampled grass. He looked at you, then at the high school student, then at Xiaoman — whose stomach rumbled again, longer this time, like a train slowly entering a distant station.

"I..." Xiaoman spoke. Her breath fell from above, so light it almost scattered in the morning wind, "I can... eat less..."

Her fingers dug into the concrete again, nails making soft scraping sounds, each carrying a suppressed carefulness not to trouble anyone.

"A hundred dollars' worth... is a lot too. Bigger than that candy just now... right?"

She looked at you, salt tracks still on her eye corners from earlier crying, but she tried to smile — mouth curving up, revealing that two-meter tooth gap. The smile was light, light as a feather on water, about to blow away.

You looked down at the watch. Green numbers still jumped: 100.00 USD daily transaction limit — you couldn't read all the English, but you recognized "$100" and "limit." You knew what "limit" meant from English class. No limit means no limit; limit means there's a limit.

There was a limit. Like an invisible wall between you and Xiaoman. You had nine hundred-plus, but could only spend one hundred per day.

You didn't frown, didn't sigh, didn't say "what do we do." You just stood there looking down at the number, left hand still holding the scrub brush, wooden handle growing darker with sweat. Your left leg barely twitched now, occasionally spasmning like a dying toy.

The high school student crouched beside you, fingers quickly writing in the notebook. The ballpoint scratched on paper; he wrote a few numbers, crossed them out, wrote more, crossed them out.

"One hundred a day," he said as if to himself, "first day buy rice — two bags, one meal. Second day eggs and vegetables — some nutrition. Third day more rice... but someone has to buy every day. And she needs to eat every day. Nine hundred-plus takes nine days to spend."

He looked up at Xiaoman.

"Can you stay here nine days?"

Xiaoman didn't answer. She looked at you; you looked at the watch; the number still jumped: $984.50. Morning wind blew your bangs, blew the nearly-dry skirt hem, blew rice bran off the eggshells in the red bucket on Granny Xiulan's cart.

In the distance, city sounds thickened. Bus air brakes, convenience-store door dings, some early riser's drawn-out ya — all mixing like a giant machine slowly starting.

But the parking lot was quiet.

You suddenly looked up. Your eyes — those two black, watermelon-seed eyes — looked at the high school student, at Granny Xiulan, at the delivery rider, then stopped on the person who'd been crouching at the alley mouth without speaking.

You asked a question.

"Then... can we spend the hundred today, and spend another hundred tomorrow?"

The high school student nodded: "Yes. Just a hundred per day."

"Then we come again tomorrow. And the day after."

Your tone was calm, as if saying "Chinese class today, math tomorrow, PE the day after." You looked down at the watch, then up at Xiaoman.

"Anyway, you're still waiting for the skirt to dry. Anyway, I still need to bring you breakfast. Anyway—"

You thought about it, tilting your head.

"Anyway, I have time every day. I'll come after school."

Xiaoman froze. Her fingers stopped on the concrete, nails still embedded in the white scrape she'd made. Her pupils — those two amber moons — trembled slightly, as if a very small pebble had been tossed in.

"But..." her breath trembled, "nine days... you come every day? Your sausage money... spend it all on me?"

She lowered her head, long hair falling to cover half her face. She stared at her hand pressed to her knee — that hand that had just picked up the lemon candy, bigger than a small car — and was quiet for a long time.

"At home," she suddenly said, voice soft as if rising from deep underwater, "I help Mom wash dishes. Each plate is as big as my bed. I can wash five at a time. After, Mom gives me a gold coin — this big—"

She circled a space with her index finger and thumb. To you, it was a giant opening, big enough for your whole body to climb through.

"I've saved thirty-seven gold coins," she looked up at you, tears welling again, "if I'd brought them... I could give them to you. We could buy lots and lots of rice. We wouldn't need to spend your sausage money."

Her voice grew smaller; the last words almost scattered in the wind.

"But I... didn't bring them."

She finished, lowering her head, hands twisting together, knuckles cracking. She was apologizing for not bringing gold coins — not apologizing for being hungry, not for dirtying the skirt, but for not having money to pay you back.

Granny Xiulan didn't speak. She just reached into her pocket, pulled out the floral handkerchief, and wiped her eye corner. Her dog looked up and licked her hand.

The delivery rider took off his helmet, looked down for a while, then put it back on and fastened it tightly.

"Nine days then nine days. Anyway, I pass by here every morning on deliveries. It's on the way."

The high school student stood, stuffed the notebook in his backpack side pocket, clicked the pen back and clipped it to his shirt pocket.

"I can help calculate the daily shopping list. The most filling combinations."

"Wait!"

Your voice popped out, thin and crisp, like a fruit candy hitting a tin roof. Everyone turned to look — Granny Xiulan bending to untie the cart's bungee, the delivery rider with one leg over his e-bike, the high school student who'd just stuffed the notebook in his pocket. Xiaoman sat cross-legged on the ground, stomach still making low rumbles, but her attention caught by your voice, lashes fanning a small breeze.

"I remembered," you said.

You held the watch before your eyes and swiped your thumb. Out of payment, out of piggy bank, out of transaction history — you kept swiping deep, to a screen no one had seen. The top had a gray bar with white text: 【Security Settings】. Below, a row of options: change password, fingerprint unlock, face recognition, payment verification... then, the second-to-last line.

【Daily spending limit】

You tapped in. A numeric keypad popped up, center a box showing "$100.00," beside it a green button labeled "Change."

"This limit—" your finger hovered above the green button, you looked up at the high school student, then at Xiaoman, "I set it myself."

The high school student froze. His ballpoint slipped from his fingers and clattered on the concrete, but he didn't pick it up. He looked into your eyes, mouth opening and closing as if wanting to speak but finding no words.

"You... set it yourself?" his voice was soft, a careful confirmation.

"Yeah." You nodded, as casually as saying what you ate for lunch, "when Mom gave me the watch, I tried every function. Alarm, step counter, weather, payment password, fingerprint unlock..." you counted on your fingers, pausing at the sixth, "then I saw the limit setting. So I set it. A hundred dollars."

You paused, tilting your head as if remembering why you'd chosen that number.

"Because a hundred a day isn't enough to spend. Setting it didn't matter... but now it does."

The delivery rider lowered the leg he'd lifted over the e-bike. His helmet was askew, sesame crumbs still unswallowed, but his gaze changed — no longer "this kid is cute," but a daze he probably didn't realize, watching an adult rule casually dismantled by a child.

"You can change it?" he asked, voice rough, "right now?"

"Of course," you said, "it's my own watch."

You pressed the green button.

A small window popped up: 【Enter current payment password to change limit】. You pressed your thumb to the fingerprint reader — that small blue-glowing circle in the upper-left corner. The watch vibrated softly, then a new input box appeared: 【Set new daily spending limit】.

Below it was an option, gray small text, directly beneath the input box.

You looked at that option. Then you looked up at Xiaoman — she sat cross-legged, eighteen or nineteen meters tall, wet skirt clinging, salt tracks on her face, stomach growling, a dry tear on her lashes. She looked at you, her amber eye reflecting your image and the faint glow from the watch screen.

You looked back at the screen. Your thumb hovered for a second above the words "No limit."

"When I set the limit," you said, voice thin as if to yourself, or explaining something important to everyone present, "it was for fun. I wanted to press every function. Alarm set for six-thirty, step counter to ten thousand steps, weather checked for a month without rain in East Sea City... the limit was the last function I tried. I set it and forgot. The high-school brother asked, and I remembered I'd set it myself."

You pressed "No limit."

The screen flashed, a green checkmark appearing, white text below: 【Daily spending limit removed】. Then it jumped back to the piggy-bank balance page. Green numbers still jumped: $984.50. But this time, a small line appeared beside the number: "No daily spending limit."

You let your wrist down and looked up at the high school student.

"Done. No more calculating a hundred a day. We can spend it all."

Then, before anyone could react, you added — still thin and crisp, like answering a question in class whose answer you already knew:

"Because this is my money. I'll spend it however I want. And Xiaoman is hungry."

---

## Chapter 16: Migration

When Xiaoman stood up, the ground shook softly.

Not the earlier boom, but lighter — like the sound when you set a large rock on grass and the soil sinks an inch. Her knees lifted from the concrete; the skirt hem was already dry, the light-blue fabric fluttering gently in the morning wind. She carefully stretched her legs, toes spreading and closing on the concrete, as if confirming the ground was still there.

"I'm ready," she said.

Her voice was still breathy, but steadier than before. The lemon candy sweetness probably still lingered in her mouth, giving her pale lips a little color. She picked up Granny Xiulan's cart items — half a frozen pig, three bags of rice, two cases of instant noodles, a bucket of eggs — one by one, and placed them on her skirt. The skirt hem was gathered up into a giant blue cloth bag; the food sat inside like a few scattered blocks.

"I'll carry them," she said, "so you don't need the cart. That little cart... its wheels make noise."

The high school student folded Granny Xiulan's grocery cart and leaned it against the wall. He stuffed the notebook in his backpack, clipped the pen to his shirt pocket, then mounted a shared bicycle he'd found at the alley entrance — basket a bit crooked, but wheels turned.

"I'll follow you on the bike," he said, "there's a dirt path beside the railway. I've walked it."

Granny Xiulan with her dog stood at the parking lot exit. Her dog still carried the frozen pig leg — later the delivery rider said it was a pig elbow, not a leg; legs were bigger — the old woman pulled it from the dog's mouth and placed it on Xiaoman's skirt. The dog whined in dissatisfaction but was quickly bribed by a small cookie from the old woman's pocket.

"I can't walk that far," Granny Xiulan said, thumping her knees, "old cold legs. I'll wait at home. When you come back, shout from below my building and I'll come down to help cook."

The delivery rider had already left. His e-bike left a shallow tire track in the alley, taillights flashing twice and disappearing. He said he'd scout the market, find which wholesaler opened early, where pork was cheapest. Before leaving he called back: "Message me when you arrive — no, I don't have your number — never mind, I'll ride back and find you!"

---

The abandoned freight railway started at the end of Binhai Park, beside a wasteland fenced with barbed wire. A faded blue sign hung on the wire, characters blurred, barely recognizable as "freight line" and "caution." When Xiaoman stepped over the wire, she was gentle — she first lifted the corner of her skirt holding the food, then raised one foot over, then the other. Her sole landed on the wasteland, flattening a swathe of foxtail grass, but not stepping on the tracks.

The rails were old. Two parallel steel rails extended into the distance in the morning light, rusted, weeds growing between the sleepers' gravel — foxtail grass, dandelions, clumps of purple morning glories. Morning glories still held dew on their petals; when Xiaoman's ankles brushed past, the dew droplets were flicked off, wetting the faded mud band on her skirt edge.

She walked along the railway.

Each step was slow. She lifted one foot, hovered it, knee bent, calf drawn back — like a giant egret pacing in shallow water. Then she placed her sole flat on the gravel between the two rails, toes first, then ball, then heel. The gravel made soft crunching sounds underfoot, but didn't shatter. She walked steadily, each step confirming the ground could bear her weight before shifting her center.

By the track was a row of abandoned factory buildings. Red brick walls, tin roofs, glass long gone from windows, rusted frames like empty eye sockets. Ivy climbed from the wall roots to the roof, wrapping the entire wall in a dark green waterfall. When Xiaoman passed, her shoulder brushed the ivy on the walltop — the vines were nudged by a giant hand with a whoosh, the entire wall of leaves trembling. A few leaves were lifted by her skirt, swirling in the air like green butterflies, landing on the rails, the gravel, her hair.

She didn't notice. She focused on the path below, lips slightly pursed, fine sweat on her forehead. Her stomach still rumbled, but less loudly now — no longer a "boom" of a train entering a station, but a softer gurgle, like a big cat purring in its throat.

Miaowu sat on her collarbone.

This position had become familiar. Xiaoman's collarbone was a slightly raised, warm "pink-white platform" to you, just big enough to let you dangle both legs on either side, hands bracing on the skin in front. Her skin was warm, carrying the clean scent of laundry detergent — residual soap from her skirt, mixed with her own skin smell, like sun-warmed bedsheets. Her pulse beat gently at your leg side, slow and steady, like a distant clock striking.

Morning wind blew from ahead, flattening your bangs. You squinted toward the railway's direction. The rails glinted in the morning light like two silver ribbons on green wasteland. In the distance, you could see the gray roofs of East Sea Farmers' Market — a row of giant rectangular concrete buildings, rooftops lined with dense exhaust fans like tiny black eyes.

The high school student rode his shared bike along the dirt path beside the tracks. The path was earth, washed by rain into shallow ruts, his wheels bumping over them. One hand held the handlebars, the other occasionally steadying his backpack — which held the notebook, ballpoint, and two bottles of water Granny Xiulan had stuffed in. His white canvas shoes pressed the pedals, toes now thoroughly dark gray.

He looked up at Xiaoman. She'd walked about five hundred meters, pace still steady, but he noticed her calves trembling slightly — not hunger trembling, but muscle fatigue, like someone who'd been tiptoeing too long.

"Want to rest a bit?" he called up.

Xiaoman stopped. She slowly turned her head; Miaowu on her collarbone felt her muscles rotate — that "platform" rotated about fifteen degrees beneath you.

"No need," Xiaoman said, her breath slightly panting, "almost there. I see the big building."

She pointed toward the market. The gesture was light, arm only to shoulder height, afraid to jostle Miaowu off her collarbone. Her finger in the morning light was like a pink-white slender tower, fingertip indicating the gray roofs.

She kept walking.

Another three hundred meters, and she passed a crooked-neck tree. An old scholar tree by the railway, trunk leaning, sparse branches, bark full of cracks. When Xiaoman passed, her skirt brushed the crown — the topmost branch was pulled down by the skirt, then sprung back with a snap, shaking off a cloud of yellow pollen. The pollen in the morning light was like golden smoke, landing on the rails, on her skirt hem, on Miaowu's hair.

Miaowu sneezed. A tiny sneeze, "ah-choo," like a kitten choked on pepper. Xiaoman immediately stopped.

"Sorry!" her voice was flustered, "is it the pollen... am I walking too fast..."

"No," Miaowu rubbed her nose, voice thin, "just itchy."

Xiaoman exhaled in relief. She raised a hand and gently touched Miaowu on the collarbone with her index fingertip — the movement extremely slow, like someone touching a butterfly on a flower petal. Her fingertip was warm and soft, resting on your head for a second, then withdrew.

"You have... pollen on your head," she said, "yellow. Like little stars."

Miaowu reached up and touched her hair, coming away with golden powder. She opened her palm and watched the fine pollen glint in the morning light.

"This is locust flower pollen," the high school student said below, stopping his bike and looking up at the crooked tree, "locust flowers are edible. Sweet."

Xiaoman's eyes lit up. She looked down at the tree — to her, just a small tree, crown only reaching her waist. But the high school student said locust flowers were edible, so she looked a bit longer.

"...too small," she finally said, with a hint of regret, "not even a bite for me."

She kept walking.

---

They reached the market.

From Xiaoman's perspective, the market was a row of giant gray buildings, but to Miaowu it was just a bigger wholesale market. The main entrance was a giant arched steel structure with a red banner reading "East Sea City Agricultural By-products Wholesale Market." The banner rattled in the wind, edges frayed, showing white fibers inside. Beside the entrance were rolling shutter doors, some already up, some still down. The open ones showed mountains of goods — rice sacks stacked to the ceiling, white woven bags printed with green rice stalks; hooks holding whole pork sides, red and white, swaying gently under fans; egg cases stacked like a wall, each labeled yellow with "fresh eggs" and the date.

The air smelled of raw meat blood, rice starch, water-evaporated vegetables, diesel forklift exhaust, mixing into a strong, unique wholesale-market scent. Someone shouted on a walkie-talkie: "Warehouse two, warehouse two, rice arrived yet—"; someone tapped a calculator, beep-beep-beep echoing; someone pushed a handcart down the aisle, wheels clattering on concrete.

Xiaoman hid behind the row of abandoned containers beside the freight platform. A dozen rusted containers — dark green, dark blue, rust-colored — like forgotten blocks. She crouched behind the largest, showing only the upper half of her face — two amber eyes peeking over the container at the market outside. Her skirt spread on the ground; the food — rice, pork, eggs — taken from her skirt and piled at her feet like a small food mountain.

"I'll wait here," her breath came from behind the container, "I won't move. I promise."

The high school student locked his bike beside the container. He pulled out his notebook, flipped to a blank page, and clicked the pen out.

"Rice, flour, eggs, meat. Start with these. We already have some meat—" he pointed at the half frozen pig at Xiaoman's feet, "but not enough. She can eat..."

He looked up at Xiaoman and paused.

"...a lot."

Miaowu climbed down from the collarbone — Xiaoman held her in her palm and gently placed her on the ground. Her sneakers squeaked on the concrete. She looked down at the watch; green numbers still jumped: $984.50. She looked up at Xiaoman — watching her from behind the container, eyes half-open, lashes still holding wind-tears (not crying, just wind), stomach gurgling.

"Let's go," Miaowu said, holding up the watch, "buy things."

She walked in front, wearing her crumpled wet uniform, pants still dripping, hair still carrying locust pollen. The high school student followed behind, notebook in hand. Granny Xiulan wasn't there (she waited at home), but her dog had followed — somehow slipping its leash, trotting beside Miaowu's feet, tail wagging like a small flag.

They entered the market's wholesale section.

---

The wholesale aisle was narrow — perfect for Miaowu, tight for the high school student. The rice-shop owner squatted at the entrance using a calculator, cigarette in his mouth, smoke rising slowly in the morning light. Before him, rice sacks formed a small mountain, each as tall as Miaowu, dark green woven bags printed with "East Sea Rice 50kg."

Miaowu walked to the foot of the rice-sack mountain and looked up. The owner didn't see her — he was head-down on the calculator, muttering "thirty-two times five-eighty, a hundred eighty-five sixty."

You walked to the foot of the rice-sack mountain and looked up. The owner didn't see her—head down on the calculator, muttering "thirty-two times five-eighty, a hundred eighty-five sixty."

"Hello."

A thin, crisp voice came from below the counter. The owner's finger stopped. He looked down and saw a second-grader standing before him—messy hair, crumpled uniform, wet pant legs, a bit of yellow pollen on her cheek. Behind her stood a high school student with a crumpled notebook; a dog tilted its head at him.

"...where are your parents?" The owner took the cigarette from his mouth.

"I'm buying for myself." Miaowu held up the watch, screen toward the owner, green numbers jumping in the morning light. "Can I pay with this?"

The owner looked down at the children's watch. He saw the number on the screen—$984.50—and squinted. He looked at Miaowu, then the high school student, then the dog.

"...what do you want to buy?"

"Rice." Miaowu pointed at the fifty-jin sack behind him. "This. How many bags until she's full?"

She turned and pointed beyond the wholesale area—toward the row of abandoned containers where Xiaoman hid, only the upper half of her face showing.

The owner followed her finger.

His cigarette fell from his mouth.

"Fuck—"

"Cough." The high school student coughed.

The owner swallowed the rest. He bent to pick up the cigarette, stamped it out on his sole, stood, and glanced again at Xiaoman—the giant crouching behind the container watching with one amber eye. She blinked; the wind from her lashes made rust flakes on the container drift.

"...that's your friend?" His voice was unnaturally calm—overdone, deliberate calm, like someone straining every vocal cord.

"Yeah." Miaowu. "She's hungry."

The owner was silent three seconds. Then he set the calculator on the counter, turned, and slapped the largest sack behind him—not fifty jin, but a hundred, standing taller than Miaowu, dark green woven bag printed with golden "special supply."

"This bag, a hundred jin. Wholesale price—" he named a number. "Enough for one meal."

"Not enough." The high school student opened the notebook. "She probably needs—"

He looked up at Xiaoman, did the math.

"—three bags. At least. Plus some vegetables and meat."

The owner's mouth opened. He looked at the high school student, then Miaowu, then the distant adult gurgling behind the container.

"...are you serious?"

Miaowu pushed the watch forward; green numbers still jumped.

"Serious."

The owner took a deep breath, then with the expression "today is probably the apocalypse but I've decided to keep doing business," turned and walked into the shop.

"Three hundred-jin bags, right? I need to transfer stock—only two here. Wait a second."

He went to the back and started calling. Miaowu stood at the door; the dog at her feet wagged; the high school student wrote a line in the notebook. Behind the container, Xiaoman's stomach rumbled again—but this time with anticipation.

Morning light fell on the wholesale aisle; forklifts beeped in reverse far away; someone on the walkie-talkie shouted "are the eggs here yet?"; the air mixed rice-bran dust with the iron smell of raw meat.

Miaowu looked down at the watch. Green numbers still jumped: $984.50.

She looked up at the next shop—a vegetable wholesaler, baskets of cabbage, potatoes, onions stacked at the door, boxes of eggs. The owner was a plump middle-aged woman sitting on the shelling edamame. She had seen everything—the rice-shop owner's cigarette falling, the giant girl behind the container—but she hadn't screamed or run. She kept shelling. Pods piled into a green hill at her feet.

She looked up as Miaowu approached.

"Buying eggs?" Her voice was rough, like sandpaper on wood.

"Yeah." Miaowu held up the watch.

The plump woman looked at the watch, then Xiaoman behind the container, then the thin layer of not-yet-dried locust pollen on Miaowu's face.

"Thirty a tray. How many trays?"

The high school student turned a page: "She probably needs per meal—"

The woman waved him off.

"Stop calculating. I have two cases of cracked ones in the back. Ugly but edible, cheap. You take them all, half price."

She paused, then added:

"Not that I'm generous. I'm afraid she'll eat my store if she gets too hungry."

But she said it with a corner of her mouth turned up—that "I'm telling a joke I find funny but I won't laugh" expression.

Miaowu nodded.

"Okay. All of them."

Her voice was thin but clear. The dog wagged; the high school student wrote another line. Far away, Xiaoman's stomach growled again—"gurgle-boom"—echoing under the wholesale market's steel roof like low thunder.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter17"></a>
## Part 3: Xiaoman Settle In

## Chapter 17: Procurement

By the time the vegetable-shop woman hauled out the two cases of cracked eggs, the rice-shop owner finished his call. He came from the back, phone wedged between ear and shoulder, muttering "three hundred-jin bags, yeah, now, transfer from the warehouse right away." His gaze swept the aisle—Miaowu at the vegetable-shop door, a dog wagging like a flag at her feet, a notebook-carrying high school student behind her, and eighteen-plus meters of giant girl peeking from behind containers with one amber eye.

He breathed deep, stuffed the phone in his pocket, and with the expression "the most absurd thing I've ever seen in my life is exactly this," turned to haul rice.

"Two bags in stock, third in twenty minutes." He heaved the first hundred-jin sack into the aisle and set it on the concrete with a thud, kicking up a white dust cloud. "You move these two first, I'll deliver the rest—where to?"

He looked at Miaowu, then the giant amber eyes behind the container.

"Deliver to... next to her?"

"Yeah." Miaowu nodded. "Just put it next to her."

"Okay." The owner asked no more. He glanced once more at Xiaoman—she blinked, the wind from her lashes blowing his hair back—then walked into his shop, shaking his head, muttering something that sounded like "the world makes less and less sense."

The plump woman stacked the two cracked-egg cases beside the rice sacks. Gray cardboard boxes printed with red "fresh eggs," the corner scrawled "CRACKED" in marker. She dusted her hands, hands on hips, looked up at Xiaoman, then down at Miaowu.

"What about vegetables? Rice and eggs alone won't do." She pointed at the baskets of cabbage, potatoes, onions. "Potatoes fill you, cheap, sold by the sack. Cabbage too—but cabbage is watery, not filling. Onions are cheap but a pain, need peeling."

The high school student turned a page; the ballpoint scratched. He wrote and did mental math, lips moving silently. Seconds later he looked up.

"Potatoes. Two sacks. One basket cabbage. Onions for now no—peeling takes too long." He paused. "Also oil and salt. No oil and salt, can't cook meat or vegetables."

The woman nodded and called into the shop: "Old Zhou—! Two sacks of potatoes! The biggest ones—!"

A muffled male voice from inside: "How big—?"

"The hundred-jin kind! Hurry!"

"Coming coming—"

A tall thin man hauled out two sacks of potatoes, thudded them beside the egg boxes. Earthen-yellow burlap, twine-tied, muddy potato skins poking through gaps. He went back for a basket of cabbage—bamboo, light green, leaves poking over the rim, edges slightly wilted but mostly crisp. Finally he carried out a five-liter jug of peanut oil and a five-jin bag of salt, set beside the cabbage.

"Salt and oil are shop stock, not wholesale." The woman named the oil price, then the salt price—cheaper than the supermarket.

"Okay." The high school student wrote it down.

Miaowu held the watch to the woman's scanner. The woman bent, aimed the scanner at the watch screen—the yellow pig payment page flashing. Beep. Paid. She straightened, set the scanner back, glanced at the transaction record.

"...nine hundred-plus." She said it without surprise, just "I see" recognition. She looked at Miaowu—cuff dusted with locust pollen, pant legs half-dry with a dark water ring at the knee—then went in and came out with a bottle of AD calcium milk, pressed into Miaowu's hand.

"Gift," she said. "No charge."

"Thanks." Miaowu took the bottle; cold glass, tiny condensation beads on the cap.

"Not that I'm generous," the woman added, mouth turned up, "a kid buying this much, you gotta give a gift."

She turned back to shell edamame, as if everything—the giant, the child's massive purchase, the free AD milk—were the most ordinary thing at the wholesale market.

The high school student closed the notebook, tucked it under his arm, and started moving rice. A hundred-jin bag he couldn't lift, only drag—he caught a corner, legs braced, pulled back, the sack hishing on concrete. Three meters and he was drenched; his shirt back dark with sweat.

Xiaoman saw this. From behind the container she extended a hand, fingers spread, and gently pinched the corner of the sack—to her, a hundred jin was probably just a small beanbag. She lifted it and set it by her skirt hem, gently, as if placing a block of tofu.

"I'll do it." Her breath fell from above, a little embarrassed. "You can't lift it... I can."

The rice-shop owner came out with the third sack just in time to see a giant hand descend from the sky and lift the sack away. He stopped, stood dazed for two seconds. Then he set the third sack in the aisle, turned back into his shop, and said without looking:

"Delivered. The third bag is free. Consider it—a gift for the young lady."

He said it calmly, like commenting on the weather. But from the doorway you could see him behind the counter, picking up the calculator, setting it down, picking it up, setting it down, as if confirming he'd really made that decision.

The delivery rider returned on his e-bike. His rear box held two live chickens—feet tied with straw, wings flapping inside, feathers drifting out gaps onto his blue tarpaulin shoulder. He parked at the aisle entrance, took off his helmet, hair soaked.

"Scouted around!" He panted. "The meat shop is at the very back—Zhang's is cheapest, whole pig sides twenty percent off. But—" he pointed at the chickens in his box, "—chickens are a better deal. Live chicken, slaughter free, basically free. Two chickens enough to—uh—" he looked up at Xiaoman, "—enough to whet her appetite?"

"Take the chickens," the high school student said. "Pork too. Lard for cooking, fat fills you."

He did the math in his notebook, clicked the pen back.

"After all this, nine hundred-plus should leave about a hundred. Leave it—might need to buy tomorrow."

"Buy again?" the rider asked.

The high school student glanced at Xiaoman. She sat by the container, turning the hundred-jin sack over in her palm as if curious why it was so heavy. Her stomach rumbled again—gurgle-boom—and her face immediately flushed, hand pressing her belly as if she hadn't heard.

"She'll be hungry tomorrow," the high school student said.

No one disagreed.

The butcher was a taciturn middle-aged man, apron oil-stained, fingers rough as sandpaper. He took half a pig from the iron hook, thudded it on the board, knife flashed, ribs, pork belly, hind leg—bagged separately. His knife work was fast, each cut precise. Between cuts he glanced at the giant behind the container; his hand paused half a second, then kept cutting, said nothing.

But when weighing, he shorted ten jin.

"Miscalculated." The high school student pointed at the scale.

"No miscalculation." The butcher didn't look up. "For the young lady." He pushed the bag to the board edge. "Next."

Miaowu held the watch to the scanner. Beep. The butcher glanced at the screen, then up at Miaowu—his gaze held on her face three seconds—then reached under the board and pulled out a lollipop, placed in her hand.

"For your sister... for you." His voice was rough as sandpaper, but the hand placing the lollipop was gentle.

Miaowu looked down at the lollipop—strawberry, pink wrapper printed with a white rabbit. She held it and the AD milk together; the bottle cold, the wrapper rustling.

"Thanks."

The butcher grunted and turned back to cutting.

Everything piled at Xiaoman's feet into a small food mountain—three hundred-jin rice sacks stacked; two sacks of potatoes, earthen-yellow burlap muddy; one basket of cabbage, edges wilted but green; two cracked-egg cases, cardboard damp from morning dew; half a pig divided into five plastic bags; two live chickens, feet still tied with straw, clucking softly on the rice sacks; a jug of peanut oil, transparent plastic golden in the sun; a bag of salt, blue "iodized" printed on the package.

The high school student turned to the last page—the shopping list he'd written. He read it aloud to Xiaoman:

"Three bags rice, three hundred jin. Two sacks potatoes, two hundred jin. One basket cabbage, about sixty jin. Two cases eggs, sixty total—some cracked but edible. Pork, half a side, about ninety jin—the owner didn't charge for ten. Two live chickens. One jug oil, one bag salt. Plus Granny Xiulan's half pig—" he pointed at the frozen pig leg still steaming cold at Xiaoman's feet, "—total should be enough for three meals. Four if careful."

He closed the notebook and looked up at Xiaoman.

Xiaoman said nothing. She sat cross-legged by the container, hands on knees, looking at the food mountain. Her eyes were wide, lashes fluttering with each blink, each one stirring a breeze that lifted white dust from the rice sacks.

Her lips moved, no sound. Moved again.

"...this... all for me?"

Her breath was light, light as a feather on water. She extended a hand and touched the top rice sack with her index fingertip—extremely slowly, as if touching a dream that might shatter. The fingertip sank into the woven fibers, leaving a shallow, giant fingerprint.

"Three hundred jin of rice..." she said, "at home... a bag of rice is this big—" she gestured, about pillow-size to her, "—but here there are three. Each one this big."

She looked up at Miaowu. Miaowu still stood by the food mountain, holding the AD milk and strawberry lollipop, face dusted with locust pollen, pant legs half-dry with a dark water ring at the knee, sneakers no longer squelching—the water had squeezed out.

"Your money..." Xiaoman said, "how much is left?"

Miaowu looked at the watch. Green numbers jumped: $736.03. She turned the screen to show Xiaoman.

"Seven hundred thirty-six and three cents left," she said, "enough for lots of sausages. But I'm not buying sausages."

"Why?" Xiaoman asked.

"Because," Miaowu tilted her head, thought, "because sausages aren't as important as you being full."

The high school student clicked his pen back and clipped it to his shirt pocket. The rider took off his helmet and wiped his brow. The butcher hacked bones far away, steady rhythm. The vegetable woman still shelled edamame, pods piling higher. The rice-shop owner stood in his doorway watching, calculator forgotten in his hand.

Xiaoman's eyes reddened. But this time she didn't let tears fall—she raised a hand and swiped her eye corner with the back of her hand, then lowered it. A wet streak glinted on her knuckle in the morning light.

"I..." her breath was hoarse, nasal, "I'll eat it all. Every grain. Not one grain left."

Her stomach answered—gurgle-boom—echoing under the steel roof, making the live chickens flap their wings.

The rider suddenly slapped his thigh.

"Alright! Let's go! Back to cook!" He mounted his e-bike, then looked back at the food mountain at Xiaoman's feet. "—can she carry all that alone?"

Xiaoman looked down at the mountain. With two fingers she gently lifted the three rice sacks and set them on her skirt. Then potatoes, cabbage, eggs, pork, chickens, oil, salt. Everything gathered in the skirt, the light-blue fabric bulging like a giant cloth sack stuffed with food.

"I can carry it," she said, "just... the skirt is a bit puffy."

She stood—boom, ground shook gently. She looked down at her skirt, swollen like a giant blue balloon. She carefully lifted the hem; the chickens clucked twice inside.

"Let's go," she said, her breath carrying for the first time an anticipation close to "being full," "go find Granny Xiulan. Didn't she say she'd help us cook?"

The high school student mounted his shared bike, stuffed the notebook in his backpack side pocket. The rider twisted the throttle; the motor hummed. Miaowu tugged the skirt hem—Xiaoman understood, bent down, cupped her hands, lifted Miaowu, and placed her on her collarbone.

That position was familiar now. Warm, steady, scented with laundry detergent. Miaowu tucked the AD milk under her arm, stuffed the lollipop in her pocket, then settled, legs dangling on either side of the collarbone, swinging.

Xiaoman walked back along the abandoned freight railway. Her steps were lighter than before—the lemon candy sweetness hadn't fully faded, the skirt was full of food, Miaowu sat on her collarbone, and ahead an old woman waited to cook for them.

Morning light was fully bright. Dew on the rails evaporated; morning glory petals fully opened, a vast purple carpet on both sides of the track. When Xiaoman passed, her skirt swept the flowers, petals lifted and landed on her hair, shoulders, hem—purple, tiny, like stars.

Miaowu sat on her collarbone, looking up at the sky. Blue, with white clouds like cotton candy. Far away, the city skyline glittered in the sun, glass curtain walls reflecting gold. Further, the sea was a thin blue ribbon merging with the horizon.

"Xiaoman."

"Yeah?"

"After you're full, will you go home?"

Xiaoman's step paused half a beat. She looked down at Miaowu on her collarbone—the small, messy-haired second-grader holding an AD milk bottle looking up at her.

"...I don't know." She was quiet a long time before answering, voice light as a feather on the rail, "I... I haven't thought about going home yet."

She paused.

"Because... I'm not full yet."

But as she said it, her fingers unconsciously touched the skin beside her collarbone—where Miaowu sat. Her fingertip traced a circle in the skin, circling Miaowu inside.

Miaowu didn't see. She was unscrewing the AD milk cap, pop, bubbles rising. She drank, then looked ahead. Granny Xiulan's building was in sight—a gray six-story apartment building, sheets hanging on the roof terrace, flapping in the wind.

Xiaoman quickened her steps. Her stomach rumbled again, but this time no pain, only anticipation.

Far away, Granny Xiulan stood downstairs in a white apron, tiptoeing toward them. Her dog darted from Miaowu's feet and galloped toward the old woman, tail wagging like a helicopter rotor.

"Back—!" The old woman's voice carried half a block, "where's the rice—?! Where's the meat—?!"

"All in her skirt—!" The high school student cycled behind, shouting back.

"Skirt—?!" The old woman paused, then laughed—a "I've seen everything absurd in my life and one more doesn't matter" laugh, "fine! In the skirt! Take it down! I have a pot upstairs—a canteen wok—my son used to run a restaurant—"

She turned into the building, then stopped and called back:

"You wait downstairs! Don't come up—the floor can't hold you! I'll bring the pot down—we'll cook in the parking lot—!"

Xiaoman stood downstairs holding her bulging skirt, watching the old woman bustle into the stairwell. Her mouth curved up, showing the two-meter tooth gap.

"Granny Xiulan..." she said, "seems like she really knows how to cook."

"Yeah." Miaowu sat on her collarbone drinking AD milk. "The instant noodles she makes will be delicious."

"I've never had instant noodles," Xiaoman said, with careful, expectant seriousness.

"Then today you can try." Miaowu. "She gave two cases."

Xiaoman's eyes lit. She lifted her skirt higher, gathering the food, standing waiting for Granny Xiulan to bring the pot. Morning wind blew from the sea, carrying salt and the distant soy-milk steam scent, stirring the purple morning glories on her hem.

Her stomach rumbled again—gurgle—but she didn't look down. She watched the stairwell entrance, waiting for the bustling old woman, waiting for the canteen wok, waiting for her first meal—full—in this tiny world.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter18"></a>
## Chapter 18: Cooking Smoke

Granny Xiulan's "canteen wok" was not a figure of speech.

What she carried down from the sixth floor was a cast-iron wok, 1.2 meters in diameter, 3cm thick at the bottom, two iron-loop handles welded to the rim—her son had grabbed it at a bankrupt canteen auction fifteen years ago when he ran a small eatery. It had been jammed in the stairwell storage closet, buried under old quilts, a broken fan, and a dead soy-milk machine; when Granny dragged it out, dust from the whole floor rose like a miniature snowfall in the stairwell light.

When she pushed the wok out the door, the high school student rushed to catch it. Its weight bent his knees; his sneakers squeaked half an inch on concrete.

"This wok... how heavy?"

"Eighty jin!" Granny stood hands on hips panting, the faded hair clip at her pajama collar askew. "My son used it to stir-fry—one batch fed eighty people! That young lady—is one wok enough? If not I have a small one, sixty jin!"

The rider poked his head from his e-bike, looking at the wok bigger than his rear box, and whistled.

Xiaoman crouched in the parking lot watching Granny and the high school student drag the big wok toward her. Her eyes were round; amber pupils reflected the wok's dark gleam.

"...it's so big." Then she realized how odd that sounded from her mouth, and her ear tips reddened.

"Big nothing!" Granny slapped the rim; the wok hummed. "For you it's not even a bite! Wait, I'll get the stove—!"

The stove was another outdoor high-pressure burner dug from storage, connected to an LPG tank, single burner, flame reaching half a meter. Granny directed the rider to unload the tank from his e-bike—his third errand today, the live chickens still clucking in the dark rear box. He rolled the tank under the wok, connected the hose, tightened the valve, movements practiced as if arming a bomb.

"How do you know all this?" the high school student asked.

"I worked in kitchens before." The rider didn't look up, tightening the hose fitting another turn. "Then delivery. Kitchens too hot."

Granny poured most of a jug of peanut oil into the wok. Golden in the morning light, it hissed when it hit the hot bottom, then she started throwing things—not "stir-frying" throws, but "land reclamation" throws.

Frozen pig hocks, whole, three, cracked from their ice shell with the back of a cleaver, thudded into the wok. Ribs, cartilage attached, laid in rows; by the third row the bottom was covered in meat. She called the high school student for cabbage—uncut, she tore it by hand, one cabbage into four, white stems and green leaves floating like boats on the oil. Potatoes went straight from the sack, clatter, fist-sized potatoes rolling into meat gaps, pushed back from the wok edge by oil pops. She didn't salt with a spoon—tore open the whole bag, poured a third in, stirred with a spatula. The spatula was her home stir-fry shovel, wooden handle, iron head, but in her hand it swung like a miniature oar.

"Water!" she shouted.

The high school student opened the hose from the parking-lot pipe; water roared into the wok. It hit the hot metal and exploded into white steam that rose three stories high, wrapping Xiaoman's knees. Xiaoman watched the steam roll over her hem, smelled the seared pork, the earthy potato skins, the sweet cabbage stems—these mixing with LPG mercaptan and morning mist into a cooking smoke she'd never smelled before, belonging to this tiny world.

"...smells so good." Her nose twitched; that broom-thick eyelash row trembled twice.

"Good nothing! Not done!" Granny stood by the wok, spatula stuck in it, steam frosting her reading glasses. "Need stewing. Until meat falls off bones, potatoes go soft, cabbage dissolves in the soup. Wait—at least forty minutes."

She pulled a folding stool from home and sat by the wok. Her dog lay at her feet, chin on her slipper, nose twitching at the meat smell.

Xiaoman sat cross-legged by the wok, hands formally on knees, watching the rolling water. Her stomach rumbled—gurgle-boom—louder than ever, making ripples on the soup surface. Granny's dog looked up, whined, lay back down.

"Soon soon." Granny poked a floating potato with the spatula.

Xiaoman swallowed. That swallow was ordinary to her, but to Miaowu crouching at her feet it was a deep "glunk" from above, like a stone dropped in a well.

Miaowu sat on the rice sacks.

Three hundred-jin rice sacks were her sofa. Stacked two high, topped with an old towel quilt Granny gave, she sat cross-legged, holding the AD milk bottle, the straw bitten flat. She'd drunk most of it; a little white milk remained at the bottom; she swirled the bottle to bring the last bit up.

The high school student sat on the sack edge beside her, notebook open on his knees, crossing off purchased items on the shopping list with the ballpoint.
