Each cross, a silent chant—"rice, done. potatoes, done. cabbage, done. eggs, done. pork, done. chicken..." He glanced at the clucking chickens in the rider's box. "Not slaughtered yet."

"Slaughter later." The rider crouched by the LPG tank, holding a cleaver borrowed from Granny's kitchen, sharpening on a stone with a scrape-scrape sound. "I worked in kitchens, I know how to kill chickens. But these two, to her—" he glanced at Xiaoman, "—maybe two popcorn chicken bites."

"Stew with potatoes." Granny didn't turn. "Small chicken, big flavor. Stew a soup, add cabbage, another meal."

Xiaoman listened to them discuss her meals like logistics for a small battle, fingers unconsciously twisting her skirt hem. The purple morning glories on the hem had wilted, sticking soft to the fabric like flattened stars.

She swallowed again.

"Granny Xiulan..." her breath fell from above, "should I help? I can... carry the pot. It's heavy."

"You sit." Granny's voice was crisp. "You're a guest. We don't have guests carry pots."

"But... it's me eating..."

"Because it's you eating." Granny turned to look at her, eyes slits behind reading glasses. "Can you cook? Can you chop vegetables? Can you stew meat? If not, sit. Just eat."

Xiaoman's lips moved, wanting to say "I help Mom wash dishes at home," but didn't. She just straightened her back, lifted her hands from her knees to her skirt, like a primary-school student criticized by a teacher but feeling warm inside.

Granny turned back and stirred the soup. It was turning white; meat chunks rolled in boiling water, potatoes split open showing sandy yellow inside, cabbage leaves half-transparent floating like green silk, ribs' meat slightly pulling from bone, pig hock skin trembling in the broth, the two chickens almost falling apart, drumsticks separating at joints and floating.

Xiaoman looked at the wok. Her pupils dilated, like two amber moons lit by sudden lamplight. Her lips parted; the two-meter tooth gap glinted in the morning light.

"Can..." her breath was thin as spider silk, "...can I eat now?"

"Eat!" Granny stuck the spatula in the wok, stepped back, wiped her hands on her apron. "Hot! Cold isn't good!"

Xiaoman reached. Her fingers hovered above the wok a second—she didn't know what to eat with. No bowl. To her, this 1.2-meter wok was probably an ordinary soup bowl.

She pinched the rim with two fingers. To her, like holding a small, scalding teacup. She carefully lifted it and brought it to her lips.

Then she drank a sip of soup.

The moment the soup touched her mouth, her eyes closed. Eyelashes trembled in the morning wind like reeds. Her cheek bulged—chewing, she'd bitten a potato. It dissolved in her mouth, sandy, warm, with the salty meat broth and sweet cabbage. She chewed three times, swallowed—glunk, that swallow like a stone in a deep well.

Then she opened her eyes.

"...hot." She spoke with a grievance of a burnt tongue not wanting to spit it out. "But it's so good."

Tears gathered in her eyes again. But this time not from hunger, not grievance, not embarrassment—the potato and broth were too hot, and something else. She sniffled, wiped her eye corner on her skirt hem, then drank again.

This sip was bigger. She tilted the wok; meat, potato, cabbage slid into her mouth. She chewed, her cheek rising and falling like a pink-white hill, a cabbage leaf on her nose she didn't notice. She swallowed, drank again, again.

Granny Xiulan stood on the ground looking up. Her hands stayed in the apron-wiping pose, forgotten. Her reading glasses reflected white steam and Xiaoman's big-eating face.

"Slow down," she said, suddenly less crisp, a little hoarse. "Don't choke. There's another pot."

"Another pot?" Xiaoman showed half her face above the wok rim, a smear of potato mash on her mouth.

"The chicken isn't stewed yet. Two cases of eggs left. Half a pig left. Three bags of rice." Granny counted on her fingers, stopped at the third because her voice trembled. "Plenty for you. All plenty. Eat slow."

Xiaoman set the wok down; bottom thudded on concrete. More than half the wok was empty, only a layer of golden broth at the bottom and a few potato fragments. She licked her lips—that tongue normal to her, but giant and pink to you, oily with broth.

"Granny Xiulan," she said, "this is the best stew I've ever eaten. Better than Mom's strawberry toast."

Granny turned away, back to her, blew her nose into the handkerchief. Her shoulders shook twice, then she stuffed the handkerchief back, turned, and wore her "no big deal" expression again.

"If it's good. Next pot chicken, add glass noodles—I have a big bundle upstairs. Noodles soak up broth, even more filling than potatoes."

The rider stood and dusted his knees. "I'll buy noodles. I know the cheapest at the farmers' market—wholesale, ten jin a bundle." He mounted his e-bike, twisting the throttle before his helmet was buckled, motor humming. "Wait! Twenty minutes!"

The high school student opened his eyes. He looked at the remaining broth layer, then at the cabbage leaf on Xiaoman's mouth, then pulled a pack of tissues from his backpack side pocket, pulled one, and walked over on tiptoe.

"...you have food on your face." He held the tissue above his head.

The tissue was a small white feather in his hand, probably postage-stamp-sized to Xiaoman. But Xiaoman lowered her head, carefully pinched the tissue between two fingers—to her like pinching a sesame seed—and pressed it gently to her mouth corner. The cabbage leaf was gone; the tissue stuck to her fingertip.

"Thanks."

The high school student grunted, walked back to the rice sacks, sat, opened the notebook. But he didn't write; he just looked at the crossed-off shopping list, ballpoint spinning between his fingers round and round.

Miaowu climbed down from the rice sacks, walked to the wok, and tiptoed to look in. A layer of golden broth remained, oil a thin film, a few potato fragments at the bottom like golden stones. She touched the rim—still warm, the rust and oil texture rough and warm under her fingertip.

"Xiaoman," she said, "you didn't finish."

"I'm full." Xiaoman said.

Her stomach gurgled softly—not hungry, but satisfied, that lazy low hum of food digesting.

"No, you're not full." Miaowu. "You're just embarrassed to eat too much. You said 'there's another pot' because you wanted to leave some for us."

Xiaoman's lashes trembled, then she turned her face away. Her lips pressed tight, but her ear tips reddened again—the embarrassed red of being found out.

"I... I didn't..." her breath was muffled, coming from the turned cheek.

Miaowu tilted her head. Then she reached in her pocket and pulled out the strawberry lollipop—the butcher's gift, pink wrapper with a white rabbit. She held it above her head like a small flag.

"For you. Dessert after the meal."

Xiaoman turned to look at the lollipop. To her it was probably sesame-seed-sized, pink wrapper glinting in the morning light. She extended two fingers and pinched the "sesame seed," held it before her eyes.

"...strawberry." she said.

"How do you know?"

"The wrapper has a strawberry on it." Xiaoman. "Small, but I can see it."

She put the lollipop in her mouth. It dissolved on her tongue, strawberry sweetness exploding like a small pink firework on her taste buds. She mmm'd, eyes curving into crescents.

"Sweeter than lemon."

Granny Xiulan watched this and pulled out the handkerchief again. But this time she didn't blow her nose; she just clutched it tight in her palm.

Morning wind crossed the parking lot, dispersing remaining steam from the wok, stirring ivy leaves on the wall, lifting the wilted morning glories on Xiaoman's skirt. Petals spun in the air, landed on Miaowu's hair—purple, tiny, like stars.

Xiaoman looked down at Miaowu, strawberry lollipop in her mouth, sweetness not fully dissolved on her tongue. She suddenly remembered something, extended a hand, and touched the top of Miaowu's head with her index fingertip—she'd done this several times, but each time as light and careful as the first.

"Miaowu," she said, "you asked what my home is like. I forgot to say—at my home, there's no you."

Miaowu looked up at Xiaoman. The fingertip still rested on her head, warm, soft, like a giant breathing quilt.

"But now there is." Xiaoman's voice was light, like telling a secret. "Now there's a home here. There's a parking lot, a big wok, a Granny Xiulan who stews meat for me, a tall brother who can calculate, a delivery man who gives tarpaulins, a dog named Huangdou..."

She paused, fingertip circling gently on Miaowu's head.

"And you. A little hamster named Miaowu. No—not a hamster. Miaowu."

Miaowu didn't answer. She just leaned the back of her head into Xiaoman's fingertip, like a cat being petted. Her hair warmed under Xiaoman's body heat; that faint sun-baked milk scent drifted up, mixing with the wok's meat and morning mist.

Granny Xiulan stood and tapped the wok rim with the spatula—clang, clear and loud.

"Alright alright! Stop standing! Next pot—chicken! Who'll bring the chickens? And the noodles—where's the delivery boy? Not back yet?"

"Three minutes only." The high school student checked his watch. "Fifteen more at fastest."

"Then start the chicken!" Granny hands on hips. "Add noodles when he's back! That young lady—can you eat more?"

Xiaoman took the dissolved lollipop from her mouth, candy shrunk a size, like a transparent pale-pink gem in the morning light. She looked down at the golden broth layer in the wok, then at the two plucked chickens on the ground, then nodded seriously.

"Yes. One more pot."

"Good!" Granny waved the spatula like a general commanding a small battle. "Second pot—fire!"

The high-pressure burner spat blue flame again, licking the wok bottom. Granny poured oil; it crackled. She lifted the chickens—the rider had chopped them into irregular pieces on the board—and dumped them in with a clatter. Chicken pieces rolled in hot oil, skin contracting from white to golden, searing aroma. She added water, salt, covered the lid, clapped her hands.

"Twenty minutes. Wait."

Xiaoman put the lollipop back in her mouth, hands on knees, waiting.

Miaowu climbed down from the rice sacks, walked to Xiaoman's side, crouched at her feet, sat beside Huangdou. Huangdou wagged and rested his chin on Miaowu's knee. She petted his head, then looked up at Xiaoman.

Xiaoman looked down at her, lollipop in her mouth, cheek slightly bulging, eyes crescent.

Morning light was fully bright. The sky above was pale blue, white clouds like cotton candy. The distant sea reflected broken gold; city noise—bus air brakes, soy milk steam, convenience-store door music—came from beyond the wall, but filtered through the meat and smoke of the parking lot, distant and light, like sounds from another world.

The rider's e-bike hummed from the alley, approaching, a big bundle of glass noodles in his basket, white noodles poking from the plastic bag, swaying in the wind.

"Noodles—!" he shouted, breathless and proud. "Wholesale price! Three bundles! Enough—?!"

Granny took the noodles, broke them by hand, lifted the lid and tossed them in. Noodles quickly softened and turned transparent in boiling soup, tangling with chicken pieces and oil, becoming a golden steaming chicken-noodle soup.

"Done!" She announced. "Second pot—eat!"

Xiaoman lifted the wok, brought it to her mouth, and blew on the steam—the wind from her breath blew Miaowu's bangs back—then took a big sip.

"...noodles are good." She said, voice muffled, mouth full. "Slippery."

"Sure are." Granny sat back on the stool, crossed her legs, wearing an "I knew it" expression.

Miaowu stood and tiptoed to look in. Xiaoman saw this, stopped sipping, pinched a small piece of chicken from the wok with two fingers—"small" to her, about fist-sized to Miaowu—and carefully placed it in Miaowu's open palm.

"For you. You eat too."

Miaowu looked down at the chicken in her palm. Still steaming, a strand of noodle and salt crystals on it. She took a bite—meat tender, fibers separating easily between teeth, skin slightly springy, rich with long-stewed aroma.

"Good." She said, chewing, muffled.

Xiaoman smiled. The two-meter tooth gap, pink gum glinting.

Granny watched them, one hand on her chin, the other unconsciously petting Huangdou's head. Huangdou wagged slowly, like a pendulum. The high school student sat on the rice sacks, notebook closed, just watching, mouth slightly curved. The rider crouched by his e-bike, wiping dust off his helmet with his sleeve, stopped when he saw Xiaoman carefully dividing a second piece of chicken for Miaowu—this time picking a boneless piece.

Far away, the city fully woke. But in the parking lot, time seemed stuck to the steam and meat in the big wok, moving very slowly.

Miaowu finished the chicken, wiped oil from her fingers on her pants, stood, and pulled out the children's watch. The screen lit; green numbers jumped: $736.03. She looked at the number, then up at Xiaoman's profile sipping soup—cheek bulging, steam beads on her lashes, wilted morning glories still on her hair.

"Tomorrow," Miaowu said, voice thin but clear, "after school, I'll come back. Take you to eat other things. The market has fruit, steamed buns—big buns, this big—" she spread her arms.

Xiaoman set down the wok, looked down at her. Noodles not fully swallowed, cheek still bulging.

"Buns...?" Her breath held an upward lilt.

"Yeah. Meat buns. And red bean buns. And custard buns."

"What's a custard bun?"

"It's sweet inside. Yellow. It flows out when you bite it."

Xiaoman's eyes lit. She swallowed the noodles—glunk.

"Then I'll wait for you tomorrow."

"Yeah."

Miaowu put the watch back on her wrist, looked at herself—crumpled uniform, dried mud on pant legs, sneakers now thoroughly gray-white. She should go home. She knew Mom would be home after night shift, would see her soaked uniform and empty AD milk bottle, would ask "where did you go."

But she didn't leave. She stood a while longer, watching Xiaoman finish the chicken-noodle soup, suck the last noodle from the wok bottom, then wipe her mouth on her skirt hem.

"I'm full." Xiaoman said. This time truly full—her stomach was quiet, rising and falling contentedly, like a pink-white calm sea.

"Then I'm going." Miaowu said.

"Yeah." Xiaoman. "See you tomorrow."

Miaowu turned and walked toward the parking-lot exit. The high school student stood and wheeled his shared bike over to walk her home. Granny started packing the stove; the rider tightened the LPG valve; Huangdou circled his feet. Xiaoman sat cross-legged in the parking lot wrapped in the peony summer quilt—slightly damp from steam, a golden oil stain on the edge. She watched Miaowu's back, the small crumpled-uniform figure receding, sneakers tapping on concrete.

At the exit, Miaowu turned.

"Xiaoman!"

"Yeah?"

"Buns—how many do you want?"

Xiaoman thought, then held up fingers.

"Ten."

"Okay!" Miaowu nodded, very serious. "Ten. I'll remember."


============================================================
<a id="Chapter19"></a>
## Chapter 19: Eye to Eye

"Ten."

Miaowu nodded seriously, memorizing the number. She turned toward the exit; the high school student wheeled his shared bike behind her; Granny bent to pack the stove; the rider tightened the LPG valve; Huangdou circled his feet. Xiaoman sat cross-legged, peony quilt slipping off her shoulders, strawberry lollipop sweetness still on her mouth, watching the small crumpled-uniform figure recede.

At the exit, Miaowu stopped.

Her sneakers squeaked on concrete—thin-soled old shoes making that only-old-shoes scraping sound. She looked down at her toe tips—gray, wet-dry-wet, the rubber edge split at the toe like a tired little mouth. Then she looked up at Xiaoman.

Xiaoman still sat there, eighteen-plus meters tall, a pink-white mountain. She saw Miaowu stop; her lashes fluttered, stirring a small breeze.

"What is it?" Her breath fell from above.

Miaowu didn't answer. She raised both hands above her head, palms up, fingers spread—the pose of reaching for something on a ceiling, or hugging the sky. Her fingers were tiny, like five pink toothpicks in the morning light.

"Xiaoman," she said, voice thin, "lift me up. Lift me to your height."

Xiaoman froze. Her fingers unconsciously twisted the quilt edge, crumpling the peony.

"Lift...?"

"Yeah." Miaowu kept her hands raised. "Put me in your palm, lift me to where you can see me. Not like before—before I sat on your collarbone and could only see your chin. I want to see your face."

She paused, tilted her head, and added:

"You see my face too. We look at each other."

Xiaoman's pupils trembled slightly. She slowly stood—boom, ground shook gently, the lid Granny had stacked slid off with a clang; she cursed "this damn floor" and bent to pick it up. Xiaoman didn't notice; she focused on Miaowu—standing at the exit, hands raised, head tilted back.

"But..." Xiaoman hesitated, "when I lift you, won't you be scared? It's high."

"No." Miaowu. "You put me on your collarbone earlier and I wasn't scared. Your hands are steady."

Xiaoman's ear tips reddened. That warm red that only comes with being trusted. She stepped forward, crouched—boom, a ring of fine dust rose from the concrete—and spread her palm on the ground before Miaowu.

That palm. Pink-white, wider than a car, palm lines winding like dry riverbeds. Her fingertip pads still held a faint oil sheen from pinching chicken, glinting in the morning light. The deepest line on the inner wrist—the lifeline—ran from thumb web to wrist root, like a pink, slender canyon.

Miaowu stepped on.

Her sneakers on Xiaoman's palm lines, sole ridges overlapping with Xiaoman's, like two rivers of different scales meeting. She walked three steps—from finger root to palm center—then turned, faced Xiaoman, and sat cross-legged.

"Okay."

Xiaoman's fingers slowly closed. Not gripping, but gathering—five fingers like five pink-white warm walls rising from all sides, circling Miaowu in the palm. Fingertip pads didn't touch her, only formed a loose, safe cage. Miaowu could feel warmth from Xiaoman's skin, like sitting by a just-extinguished heater.

Then, ascent.

Xiaoman's palm began to rise. Very slowly, slower than walking on the railway, like carrying a bowl of soup full to the brim. Miaowu felt gravity press her gently into the palm; the pink-white "ground" under her warmed slightly, Xiaoman's pulse rising through skin—thump... thump... thump...—clearer than on the collarbone, because the palm was farther from the heart, the pulse buffered by vessels, softer, longer, like someone beating a cloth-wrapped drum far away.

The ground receded.

Granny became a gray dot, her dog Huangdou a smaller yellow dot, the rider crouched by his e-bike looking up, helmet askew. The high school student stood at the exit, the crumpled notebook still in the bike basket, head tilted up, morning light on his face, expression unreadable.

Five meters. Ten. Fifteen. Eighteen.

Before Miaowu, Xiaoman's face appeared.

Not the chin-nostril-lash-shadow view from below, but a complete face, directly facing her. Xiaoman held her palm at eye level, close enough that Miaowu could see the finest skin texture.

Her eyes. Those two amber moons, directly before Miaowu, pupils slightly dilated from proximity, like two amber stones lit through by moonlight, reflecting Miaowu's image—a small, cross-legged, messy-haired second-grader surrounded by pink palm background, like Thumbelina on a giant flower petal. Miaowu could see the iris texture—rings of amber fibers radiating from the pupil, like a wind-ruffled lake.

Her lashes. Each one broom-thick, growing from the lid edge, slightly upturned, tips pale brown. Fine steam beads clung to them from the meal, like morning dew on grass blades, refracting eastern sunlight into a ring of tiny golden rainbows.

Her nose tip. Pink, a few faint freckles on the bridge from sun exposure, color like a drop of coffee stirred into milk. Nostrils flared slightly with each breath; each exhale sent a warm breeze blowing Miaowu's bangs back, like standing before a giant, quiet, breathing fan.

A cabbage leaf still clung to her nose tip—splashed there while sipping, unnoticed. The leaf had wilted, soft against the side of her nose, green faded to translucent jade, edges slightly curled.

Her lips. Slightly dry, the white flake on her lower lip still there but better than morning, softened by two pots of hot broth, dry skin curled at the edge like a white petal about to fall. A faint oil sheen at the mouth corner—leftover from wiping with her skirt, glinting gold in the morning light.

The tooth gap, more obvious from straight ahead. A two-meter gap, pink gum, edges moist, like a licked pink candy. The teeth on either side were white and square, with faint ridges on the biting surfaces.

Xiaoman looked at Miaowu too.

She saw—not the vague impression of "a child," but Miaowu herself, at this distance, every detail enlarged to where she could see.

Her hair. Not black, but dark brown, fine golden halos on strands in the sunlight. Bangs blown messy by her own breath, a few stuck to her forehead, damp with sweat not yet dry. Two whorls on top—one center, one left—hair standing up at the whorls like a tuft of wind-bent dandelion.

Her eyes. Not pure black, but dark brown, pupils like two washed obsidian, reflecting her own image—a giant face too close. When Miaowu blinked, upper and lower lashes met then separated, fast as a butterfly flapping once.

Her cheeks. Round, fleshy, a faint crease on the left—probably from the woven sack texture when she lay on the rice, slowly fading from deep pink to pale. A yellow locust-pollen grain stuck to the right cheek, tiny, like a golden mole.

Her mouth. Turned up. Not a polite smile, but real, bubbling-up happiness, corners lifting, pushing the cheek flesh up into round apple arcs.

Her clothes. Dark blue uniform, the top button undone showing a white tank edge. Cuffs frayed into pills, dark blue mixed with fine gray fibers. A small golden oil stain on the left cuff—from eating chicken, spreading in the fabric as a small irregular circle, dark edges, light center, like a miniature sun.

She sat cross-legged in Xiaoman's palm. Hands on knees, back straight, head tilted up, eyes wide, mouth slightly open. No fear on her face, only pure, focused calm, all attention on "right now."

They looked at each other.

No one spoke. The parking lot was quiet. Granny forgot to pick up the lid, crouched on the ground looking up, hand frozen mid-air. The rider's mouth hung open, chin strap undone, swinging in the wind. The high school student stood at the exit, one hand on the handlebar, the other unconsciously gripping his backpack strap—knuckles white, the same force as on the lawn, under the sycamore, in every bewildered moment. But this time he wasn't gripping fear, but something else.

Xiaoman broke the silence. Her lips moved; the voice was almost just lip contact, but Miaowu was so close that every syllable was clear.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah?"

"You have me in your eyes."

Miaowu blinked. She leaned forward to the palm edge, hands braced on Xiaoman's fingertip—that index finger pad, wider and thicker than her whole body, pink, soft, warm. She craned her neck like a bird poking from a nest.

"You have me in your eyes too." she said. "Two of me. One in the left, one in the right."

She held up two fingers, pointed at Xiaoman's left eye, then right.

"This me has messier hair. This me has pollen on her face."

Xiaoman laughed—a soft, low laugh, but close, and Miaowu felt a warm humid gust from directly ahead, carrying strawberry lollipop sweetness and chicken broth salt. She wasn't blown away because Xiaoman instinctively held her breath when laughing, muffling it in her throat.

"Miaowu." After laughing, Xiaoman's lashes lowered halfway, her voice lighter. "You're small. But your eyes are big."

"Because I'm looking at you." Miaowu spoke like stating a fact everyone knows.

Xiaoman's lashes trembled again. She moved the palm a little closer—ten centimeters to her, a big step closer for Miaowu. Now Miaowu was half a meter from Xiaoman's face. She could see her reflection in Xiaoman's pupils change from "two small me" to "two big me," could see the leaf veins on the cabbage leaf on Xiaoman's nose—each vein thicker than her own hair.

"Miaowu." Xiaoman said. "I used to be afraid of 'close.' At home, when I leaned near a flower, the flower scattered from my breath. When I approached a bird, my shadow scared it off. So I walked slowly, spoke quietly, kept far from everything. But..."

Her voice caught. She swallowed—glunk, Miaowu felt the palm ground shake gently.

"But you aren't afraid of me. I lift you this close and you aren't afraid. You're still looking at me. Not peeking, but looking straight."

"Because you're Xiaoman." Miaowu. "You're not a flower or a bird. You're a person. I'm a person. People looking at people is normal."

People looking at people is normal.

These words from a second-grader, thin as a glass bead dropping in a porcelain bowl, bouncing once. Xiaoman heard them, lips moving, no sound. She looked at Miaowu—cross-legged in her palm, head up, pollen on her face, oil on her clothes. Her eyes reddened again, but this time she didn't let tears fall. She just picked up the red basin from the ground—Miaowu had taken it from her backpack earlier and set it by the rice sacks; Xiaoman saw it and gently lifted it with her free hand's fingertips, placed it beside Miaowu.

The little basin was only nail-sized between her fingertips. Red plastic, raised anti-slip rim, a white scratch on the inside bottom—Miaowu had picked it with her fingernail that morning, running from the bottom to the rim, stopping at the seventh fork.

"Your basin." Xiaoman said.

"Yeah." Miaowu picked it up and held it. The rim dug into her arm, same as this morning.

"You said this morning—'use this! I pee in this! It's big enough!'" Xiaoman said, imitating Miaowu's tone, voice thin, tail lifted. "You were afraid I was in a hurry. You thought your basin could catch it."

"Couldn't catch it." Miaowu shook her head seriously. "Later I knew. Even your tears couldn't be caught. One tear is bigger than my basin."

"But you still held it up."

"Because that's all I had." Miaowu looked down at the red basin in her arms. "I didn't have anything else then. I don't have anything else now. But I have you."

She looked up at Xiaoman.

"You're bigger than the basin. Bigger than tears. You're big at everything. But your eyes—your eyes are the same size as mine."

Xiaoman blinked. A tear finally slid from her eye corner—plop, hitting her own crossed knee, adding a wet pit to the peony quilt. But she was smiling. The two-meter tooth gap, pink gum glinting.

"Miaowu." Her voice held a crumpled tenderness of mixed crying and laughter. "Tilt your head down a second."

"Hm?" Miaowu lowered her head.

Xiaoman's index finger moved. That index finger, longer than Miaowu's whole body, nails round, healthy pink, slowly rose from behind Miaowu. Then, the fingertip landed gently on Miaowu's head—not pressing, just touching. Warm, soft, with living skin's elasticity, exactly like that first touch at Binhai Park that morning. Only this time, Miaowu wasn't on the ground looking up, but sitting in her palm, face to face, eye to eye.

The touch lasted five seconds.

Xiaoman's fingertip stayed on Miaowu's head, warm, soft, like a breathing quilt. Miaowu didn't dodge or close her eyes; she just sat quietly on that pink-white palm, head up, looking at Xiaoman. Morning light leaked through Xiaoman's finger gaps, golden lines falling on Miaowu's hair, uniform, the red basin in her arms.

Then Xiaoman slowly withdrew her hand. Fingertip hovered a moment in the air, not knowing where to put it, then settled back on her knee, sinking slightly into the peony quilt's folds. The peony was already crumpled by her fingers, its outline askew.

Her lips moved, about to say something—

"Burp—"

The belch came without warning. Rising from her stomach, exploding at her throat, carrying hot chicken-noodle soup, starch from stewed potatoes, and the residual sweetness of melted strawberry lollipop. Air rushed from her mouth, warm and sudden, straight toward Miaowu in the palm.

Miaowu's bangs blew all the way back, eyes reflexively squeezed, her whole person swaying slightly in the palm like a small grass bent by a sudden warm wind. The red basin in her arms slid an inch back from the gust, clunking against her knee and stopping.

Xiaoman froze.

Her ear tips reddened at visible speed—from pink-white to deep red, as if someone splashed chili sauce on her ears. The hand still hovering above her knee flew to her mouth, fingers spread, knuckles white, as if trying to grab the escaped belch and stuff it back. Her eyes widened; amber pupils reflected Miaowu's small, middle-parted-bangs image.

Air was quiet about two seconds.

"Sorry sorry sorry—!"

Her voice burst muffled through her fingers, carrying the embarrassment of wanting to crawl into a crack in the ground. Her other hand flailed in the air, not knowing whether to shield Miaowu or cover her own face, finally hovering, fingers spreading and clenching, spreading and clenching.

"I didn't mean to—that belch ran out on its own—I drank soup too fast—sorry—I—"

Her eyes were already reddening. Not from grievance, but because she felt the belch had ruined the good moment. She'd finally lifted Miaowu to her eyes, finally seen each other's faces, finally touched her head with her finger—and then she'd belched a loud, chicken-broth-flavored belch right in Miaowu's face.

But Miaowu didn't cover her nose. Didn't laugh. Didn't say "it's okay" and turn away.

She just used her free hand to push the bangs back onto her head—the hair was disobedient, took two tries to stick to her forehead. Then she rubbed her squinted eyes, blinked, squeezed out the tiny moisture blown in. Her face was calm. Not the "I forgive you" calm, but the calm of someone who never felt this needed forgiving.

"You burped." Miaowu said. Same tone as saying "you have a petal in your hair"—a simple, unjudging observation.

Xiaoman's fingers moved away from her mouth a little. Her lips showed through the gap, the two-meter tooth gap half-visible. Her eyes were still red, but no tears fell. She looked at the small second-grader in her palm, hair middle-parted from the belch, waiting for her next words.

Miaowu pushed the bangs back—the disobedient hair took two tries—and set the red basin steady, bottom clunking against her knee. Then she tilted her head up, expression serious and calm, like reciting something she was certain of.

"It's okay. Because this is the most natural you. I like the most natural you."

She finished. Then looked up at Xiaoman, eyes wide, lips slightly pressed, waiting for Xiaoman to—like that morning—stop crying after hearing these words.

Xiaoman froze.

Not because the words were wrong—they were good, warm, that morning they'd made Xiaoman cry harder but laugh truer. But now, after the belch, after being lifted to eye level and looking at each other so long, something inside her went "glunk." Not moved. The feeling was still there, but beneath it, another layer had appeared.

These words were exactly the same as on the lawn that morning. Exactly the same as at the convenience store door. Not one word more, not one less. Tone, rhythm, pause positions—all identical. Like a tape played repeatedly, each time same quality, same pitch, same speed. Even the tail of "natural"—that light upward lilt at the end—was identical to morning.

She wasn't saying a sentence. She was playing one back.

Xiaoman's fingers stopped on her knee. She looked at Miaowu—still looking up, eyes bright, face calm, mouth slightly curved. That was an expression waiting for confirmation: Did I say the right thing? Are you okay? Did you stop crying?

"Miaowu." Xiaoman's voice was soft, as soft as asking "are you scared," but with a new carefulness.

"Yeah?"

"What you just said—"

"It's okay, because this is the most natural you, I like the most natural you." Miaowu said it again. Not one word more or less. Speed, rhythm, that upward lilt—all reproduced. As if there was a record in her head; someone pressed play, and it spun.

"Right, that sentence." Xiaoman moved her face a little closer, close enough to see her own reflection in Miaowu's pupils. "Miaowu, who taught you that sentence?"

Miaowu blinked.

"The teacher said it. Mom said it. The doctor said it." She spoke as flatly as reading a menu. "They kept saying it. I remembered."

"Did they say it to you?"

"Yeah. To me." Miaowu looked down at the red basin in her arms, fingers starting to pick at the rim. "I used to do unnatural movements. Shake my legs. Wave my hands. Not look people in the eye. The teacher said, Miaowu, just be natural. Mom said, relax your shoulders. The doctor said, come on, make a natural face for the doctor. They kept saying it, so I remembered. Once I remembered, I could use it."

You looked up at Xiaoman, expression not seeking praise, not expecting credit, only the calm of someone who's handed in a test and waits for grading.

"This morning you were scared, so I took it out for you. Just now you were scared too, so I took it out again. Same words should work the same way. This morning you stopped crying after using it. Now you've stopped crying."

Xiaoman said nothing. She looked at Miaowu—the cross-legged second-grader in her palm, holding the red basin, hair middle-parted, face without any sense of "I just said something impressive." Miaowu was being kind the only way she knew—taking out remembered words and handing them to whoever needed them, like handing a band-aid. You didn't necessarily understand what "natural" meant, but you knew these words made you feel better when people said them to you, so you saved them for next time someone needed them. The way you saved words was the way you saved the basin—Mom said bring the basin, you brought it. The teacher said be a water-saving guard, you did it. Someone needed to hear a sentence, you played it. It was your way—simple, direct, no explanation, and not yet able to understand what the sentence itself meant.

Xiaoman looked at you.

You sat in her palm, holding the red basin, bangs still middle-parted from the belch. Eyes bright, face calm, mouth slightly curved. That was an expression waiting for confirmation: Did I say the right thing? Are you okay? Did you stop crying?

Her fingers curled gently behind you. Nails scraped an extremely faint white mark on her own palm, like a snowflake on pink-white sand. She looked at you—at the stray hairs not yet settled on your forehead, at the yellow locust pollen on your cheek, at the red basin warmed by your body heat—the white scratch on the rim still there, from bottom to rim, stopping at the seventh fork.

She didn't cry. Her eyes reddened but no tears fell. She just raised her other free hand, both hands forming a loose warm shell, gathering you in the middle. Not gripping, not holding, but gathering—like a giant pink-white bird's nest, you the small egg inside.

"Miaowu." Her breath fell from above, light, light enough only for the distance between you two to hear.

"Yeah?"

"You don't need to play it back anymore."

You blinked. Lashes fluttered twice, like butterfly wings opening and closing in slow motion. Your mouth parted slightly, head tilted, that expression on your face—not sad, not confused, but the blankness of a child hearing a sentence not quite understood, brain rearranging word order.

"Play back?" You repeated the word, like tasting an unfamiliar candy.

"Taking out remembered words to use. Like playing a recording." Xiaoman's thumb circled gently behind you, not touching you, only drawing a warm breeze in the air; you felt a very light warm wind on the back of your head. "This morning you said 'this is the most natural you.' Just now you said it again. Every time the same. Not one word changed."

You looked down at the red basin. The white scratch on the bottom had been picked deeper by your fingers; a curled flake of white plastic lifted at the end; you pressed it back with your nail. It lifted again. Press again.

"Because..." your voice was thin, like a stretched cotton thread, "because I don't know what to say."

You looked up at Xiaoman. Her face was directly before you, like a giant pink-white moon. Two small yous in her pupils.

"When you do unnatural things, I say that sentence, and you're okay. You stop crying, you smile. So that sentence is right. If it's right, I can use it again."

You paused. Your fingers stopped on the basin rim.

"I don't know what 'natural' means. The teacher said, Miaowu, relax your shoulders. Mom said, walk more naturally. The doctor said, make a natural face. But no one told me what 'natural' looks like. So I remembered what they said. What they said was useful, so I wrote it down and used it next time. I wrote down a lot of sentences. 'It's okay.' 'You're great.' 'I understand how you feel.' 'You can do it.' 'It'll be fine.'... a whole boxful, bigger than my basin."

You gestured with your fingers—the "boxful" you gestured about as wide as your shoulders.

"When I don't understand class, when I can't solve a problem, when other kids won't play with me—I take out the remembered sentences. I take them out, say them, and they're not angry anymore. Or they leave. Either way is better than me saying nothing. Because if I say nothing, they keep looking at me. Keep looking in my eyes, as if waiting for me to do something. But I don't know what that thing is."

You set the red basin on your knee, both hands on the bottom. Your fingers trembled slightly—not cold, not tired, but the trembling of finally taking something kept inside for a long time and laying it out in broad daylight.

"But this morning," you said, voice light as a secret just discovered, "I said 'this is the most natural you.' You said 'the natural me.' Then you smiled. Not the kind of smile people give me—real. Your front tooth is missing, your eyes become crescents when you smile, your face crumples, but it's pretty. Then I thought—I thought—"

You looked up at Xiaoman. Your eyes were bright, dark pupils holding fine light, like two watermelon seeds soaked in starlight.

"I thought—maybe this time I get it. Maybe 'natural' is—just how you were this morning. Hungry, you groan. Scared, you cry. Can't hold it, you can't hold it. Full, you burp. Not hiding. Not pretending. Not playing back what people say. When you burped you didn't say sorry first; you did it, then said it. Saying 'sorry' after burping isn't really sorry. Real sorry is before the burp. You didn't. So your burp was natural. You were natural in front of me."

Your voice grew smaller, the last word almost scattered by the morning wind. You looked down and started picking at the white scratch on the basin rim; your nails scraped shush-shush on the plastic, like a small mouse gnawing wood.

"Am I right?"

Xiaoman didn't answer.

She just moved the warm nest her two hands formed a little closer. Now you were twenty centimeters from her face—twenty to her, half a meter to you. You could see the leaf veins on the cabbage leaf on her nose, the curled edge of the white dry skin on her lips, the two small yous in her pupils—middle-parted hair, pollen on face, red basin in arms, looking up at her.

Her lashes lowered halfway. That broom-thick lash row like a translucent curtain, covering half her eye. Behind the curtain, amber light swayed—not tears welling, but the pupils themselves trembling, like two small stones dropped into two moons, ripples not yet dispersed.

Then she spoke. Voice came from behind half-lowered lashes, muffled, hoarse, carrying an emotion you didn't yet understand—later, when you grew up, you learned that emotion was "being truly understood by someone."

"Miaowu."

"Yeah?"

"You're right. All right."

Her mouth curved up. Not the crying-laugh, not the "whatever, this is happening" laugh from the pool, but a smile you might not even recognize yourself—because no one had ever smiled at you like that. Very light, very slow, like a flower slowly opening its petals—not blown open by wind, but deciding itself to open.

"You don't need to play back other people's words. What you say is good. Everything you just said—good. Not 'right,' but 'good.' Words Miaowu said herself."

She lowered the hands cupping you. Not putting you back on the ground, but bringing them to knee height, letting you still sit in her palm but slightly farther from her face—so she could see your whole self, and you could see hers.

Then she did something.

She extended her right hand before you, index finger and thumb spread—the pose you'd seen, that morning at Binhai Park, the same hand she'd reached out with. Only this time, she wasn't going to touch you.

With two fingers, she gently pinched the edge of the red basin in your arms.

The movement was slow, slow enough to see every tiny vertical ridge on her nails—pink, healthy, edges round. Her fingertips didn't touch your hand, only pinched the rim, then gently, gently took the basin away from your arms.

You looked down at the red basin. It was between Xiaoman's fingertips, like a red sesame seed. The white scratch you'd picked countless times was still there, the anti-slip rim glistening with your palm sweat. You looked at it, wanting to reach for it, but held back.

Xiaoman set the basin on her knee—on the crumpled peony. Then she brought her free hand back, cupping behind you again.

"Now," she said, "no more basin. You have nothing to pick at."

You looked down at your hands. Empty palms, fingers slightly curved, white plastic flakes from the basin rim still on your nails. You opened and closed your fingers, not knowing where to put them.

"You don't need to pick at things." Xiaoman said. "When you talk, your hands don't have to do anything. You can just talk. Your words are enough."

You looked up at her. Your mouth opened a little, then closed. Your fingers finally settled—not because you made them, but because your attention was caught by what Xiaoman said, and your fingers forgot to move.

"...just talking is enough?"

"Enough."

"But what I say is simple. I say 'you're hungry,' I say 'you burped,' I say 'you have me in your eyes.' Those are very, very simple words. Kindergarten kids say them too."

"But kindergarten kids don't say them sitting in my palm." Xiaoman's lashes fully lifted. Her eyes looked at you fully—not looking down, not eye level, but that fully attentive gaze putting all focus on one person. "Many people talked to me today. Granny Xiulan said 'wait, you won't go hungry.' The delivery man said 'I'll buy noodles.' The high school student said 'I'll calculate for you.' They're all good to me. But none of them said 'you have me in your eyes.'"

She paused. Morning wind blew from behind her, sweeping her long hair to her chest, a few strands falling over the palm edge like black willow branches.

"Your words are simple. But simple words, when you say them, are yours. Not the teacher's, not the doctor's, not anyone else's. They're what you saw—you saw you in my eyes, you said it. You saw me burp, you said it. You saw me hungry, you said it."

Her thumb circled gently behind you. Air circled the back of your head, warm, tickling.

"These simple words—are what I most want to hear in this world."

You looked up at her. Your face was expressionless—that blank face of a child receiving information seriously. But your eyes moved. Your pupils slowly traversed her face, from left eye to right, from nose tip to the oil sheen at her mouth corner, from stray hairs on her forehead to the small mole behind her ear.

Then you looked down at your empty palms.

You set your hands on your knees. Both hands, palms down, fingers slightly spread. Beneath you was Xiaoman's palm skin—pink-white, warm and soft, pulse beating slowly and firmly under the skin. You didn't pick at anything. Your hands just rested, five fingers quietly against that pink-white "ground."

"Xiaoman."

"Yeah?"

You looked up.

"Your burp tasted like chicken soup."

Air was quiet one second. Then Xiaoman laughed. The laugh of being caught off guard, of being teased, bubbling up from her stomach. Her shoulders shook, the palm "ground" bobbing gently under you like a boat passing a small wave. She covered her mouth—but laughter leaked through her fingers, huffing air blowing her bangs up, revealing a smooth pink-white forehead.

"Miaowu—!" Her voice burst through her fingers, with laughter and pretend protest. "I'm saying something important—!"

"I know." You said, face still that calm, serious expression. But your mouth—curved up a little. Not played back. Yours. "You said 'your words are enough.' So I tried saying one of my own. My own words are—your burp tastes like chicken soup. And a little strawberry."

Xiaoman lowered her hand from her mouth. She looked at you, eyes crescent. The two-meter tooth gap fully visible, pink gum glinting. A tear finally spilled from her eye corner—not sliding from the middle, but squeezed out from the corner by laughter, glinting like a small transparent pearl in the morning light. She didn't wipe it. She just smiled at you, hair messy from her own laughter, the cabbage leaf on her nose finally blown off, spinning down.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"Do you know what you just said?"

"I said your burp tastes like chicken soup."

"Before that."

You thought. "I said I wanted to try saying my own words."

"Right." Xiaoman extended her free hand, her index fingertip landing on the top of your head—the gesture she'd done three times. Only this time, her fingers stayed on your head longer than ever. Three seconds, five, eight. Long enough that you felt the warmth of her fingertip traveling through your hair into your scalp, then into your brain, making your whole body feel warm.

"You did it."

She said these four words without laughter, without tears, only a solemnity you'd never heard. You weren't sure what it was, but you remembered it. The way you remembered useful words—you remembered her tone, the warmth of her breath, and the slight trembling frequency of her fingers on your head.

Later, when you grew up, you learned that solemnity was called "being seen." But now you just sat in her palm, cross-legged, her hand on your knee, her fingers on your head, no basin in your arms, your fingers not picking at anything, quiet as an egg just placed in a nest.

Morning wind blew from the sea, through the abandoned parking lot, stirring ivy leaves on the wall, lifting the last wisp of steam from Granny Xiulan's lid, disturbing the stray hairs on your forehead. You closed your eyes, then opened.

"Xiaoman."

"Hm?"

"Tomorrow when I come, I won't bring the basin."

"Why?"

"Because," you said, tilting your head, "the basin is for catching tears. But you don't cry anymore. I don't pick at things anymore. The basin can stay home and hold erasers."

Xiaoman's lashes fluttered. Then she nodded, very seriously, as if responding to something important.

"Okay. Hold erasers. What color is your eraser?"

"White. And one is pink. The pink one erases cleaner, but one corner has been bitten by me."

"You bite your eraser?"

"Sometimes. Not on purpose. My teeth just want to bite things."

"Kids at my house bite erasers too." Xiaoman. "My little sister. During teething she bit every eraser in the house. Mom's eraser, sister's eraser, her own eraser—all covered in rows of little teeth marks."

You widened your eyes.

"You have a sister?"

"Yeah. Two years younger. She's called Xiao Gu. Gu as in grain."

"Xiao Gu..." you rolled the name on your tongue. "Did she lose her front tooth too?"

"Not yet." Xiaoman touched her lips with her finger, pressing lightly on the two-meter gap. "But soon. She wrote last time that one tooth is wiggling. Has been for days. She pushes it with her tongue and it wobbles back and forth. She thinks it's fun. Mom says don't lick it, it'll grow crooked. She still licks."

"Letters?"

"Yeah. When I'm here—no, when I'm home, she went to Grandma's. Grandma's is far, over a mountain. So she writes me letters. On very, very big paper—for you, one sheet is about—" she gestured, "—this big. Then folded into a small square, under my pillow."

Her lashes lowered halfway, her fingers sliding from her lips to her knee, overlapping the peony on the quilt.

"When I woke up, there was no letter under my pillow. Because I didn't wake up at home."

You looked at her. Her lashes trembled slightly. Her fingers unconsciously traced the edge of the peony petal—the exact same gesture as your picking at the basin rim.

You stood up.

Xiaoman's fingers curled slightly behind you, as if afraid you'd fall. But you just stood up, walked three steps in the palm—from center to the base of her thumb—then stopped. Before you was the inner side of her thumb, pink-white skin, fine texture, a shallow soft crease where the finger bent. You spoke to that crease, voice thin but clear.

"Xiaoman. Do you know what you just said?"

Xiaoman's lashes lifted. She looked at you—the small figure standing by her thumb, head up, hands behind your back, expression as serious as a teacher asking a question.

"...what?"

"You mentioned your sister. You mentioned Xiao Gu. You mentioned her licking her tooth. You mentioned her letters. When you spoke—" you paused, searching for a precise word, "—when you spoke, your hands weren't twisting the quilt. Your toes weren't digging the ground. Your ears weren't red."

Xiaoman paused. She looked down at her own hands—quiet on her knee, fingers slightly spread, the peony not twisted, just lying between her palm lines. Then at her toes—ten toes flat on the concrete, slightly spread, nails pink, a few sand grains on them, but not digging. Her ears—she raised a hand to touch her earlobe. Warm. Not hot.

"I..."

"When you talked about Xiao Gu," Miaowu said softly, "you were natural."

Xiaoman's pupils trembled slightly. Then she placed her hand on her knee, palm up—the hand that had been cupping behind Miaowu now lay quietly before her like a pink-white warm stage. Miaowu still stood by her thumb, looking up.

Xiaoman looked at Miaowu. At the stray hairs not settled on her forehead, at the yellow locust pollen on her cheek, at her hands behind her back—the hands that had been picking at the basin rim now quietly clasped behind her waist.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"You're not picking at things either."

Miaowu looked down at her hands. Her fingers moved slightly behind her back, then she brought both hands out, held them before her, palms open. Palms empty, white plastic flakes still on her nails, but fingers quiet—not shaking, not grasping, not picking.

"...really." She said, with small surprise, as if just noticing a fact she'd missed.

"You forgot to pick at things while talking too." Xiaoman said.

They looked at each other.

In Xiaoman's palm, Miaowu stood by the thumb, both hands open before her like two newly found quiet shells. Xiaoman sat cross-legged on the ground, hands on her knee, the peony quilt's folds slowly unfolding in the morning wind, like a real flower opening in slow motion.

The parking lot was quiet. Granny Xiulan had sat back on her stool, holding the white floral handkerchief, but not wiping her nose, just gripping it, watching them. Her dog Huangdou lay at her feet, chin on his front paws, ears swiveling, tail occasionally wagging. The rider crouched by his e-bike, helmet on his knee, his expression not shock or awe but something he probably couldn't name—like watching a story he'd long wanted to see. The high school student stood at the exit, leaning against the crooked-neck tree by the wall, holding the crumpled notebook but not opening it, watching them with a quiet focused gaze, like observing in a lab a chemical reaction he'd studied for years that finally happened on its own.

Granny Xiulan stood and dusted her pants. Her movement was light—abnormally light for a bustling old woman. She said nothing, just walked over, picked up the big iron lid, and set it gently on the wok.

Clang.

Soft, but enough to break the parking-lot silence.

"Alright alright," Granny said, back to them, wiping her hands on her apron. "Sun's high already. Young girl—shouldn't you be going home? Your mom gets off night shift and doesn't see you, she'll worry."

Miaowu looked up from her hands at the sky. The sun had fully risen from the east, golden light flooding the parking lot. Her watch lit: 07:57. Mom finished at 7:30, subway home took about forty minutes, should be almost home now.

When you pushed the door open, the living room clock showed 8:06.

You kicked off your sneakers. Soles hissed on the floor—gray-white mud, parking-lot dust mixed with pool water, dried into a thin crust, scraping the tile like sandpaper. You crouched, put the left shoe in the bottom of the shoe cabinet, toe out, exactly one shoe-width from Mom's white nurse shoes. Right shoe in, same spacing. You stood, looked down at the gray-white shoe print on the tile, stared ten seconds, then turned into the bathroom.

Uniform off. Pants first, then top. Folded. Laid on the washing-machine lid. Socks rolled into balls, stuffed in the mesh bag. Changed into yesterday's uniform—the pill cuff still there, hard, smelling of laundry detergent. Then into the kitchen.

On the counter, Mom's prepared tray from that morning. Yellow mug, handle right. White pillbox, three compartments, tablets already in each. Whole-wheat toast, individually wrapped, transparent bag printed with a wheat stalk wearing a chef's hat.

You stood before the tray, motionless.

This was the morning routine. Morning routine: wake, toilet, brush teeth, change, take medicine, eat toast, leave. But it wasn't morning now. It was the end of morning—the sun was high, bus air brakes and soy-milk steam outside. The morning routine had expired. Expired routines couldn't run.

You picked up the pillbox and placed it in the far left corner of the counter—where Mom kept the vitamin bottle, outside the tray's range. Toast stayed in the tray, untouched. Mug handle right, exactly as before.

Then you walked to the sofa and picked up the picture book.

Little Bear Bubu Goes to the Beach. The cover worn white, a water stain from a knocked-over cup two years ago in the lower right corner. You tucked it under your arm, walked into the bedroom, climbed onto the bed. Propped the pillow against the headboard, sat cross-legged, opened to page one.

"Little Bear Bubu went to the beach. He brought a red bucket."

Your voice was thin, flat. You didn't need to look at the words—you'd read this book enough times, every character etched in your brain, like "it's okay because this is the natural you," a fully playable sentence.

Page two.

"Bubu built a sandcastle. The castle was tall, taller than Bubu."

Your eyelids drooped once. You blinked hard, pulled them back, kept reading.

"The waves came. The waves washed the castle away. Bubu was sad."

You paused. Your finger pointed at the bear's eyes on the page—two blue oval tears beneath. You stared at the tears a few seconds, then turned to page four.

"Bubu's friend came—"

You didn't finish. Mouth still open, but no sound came out.

Not forgetting the words. You were sleepy.

Your body was calling you to sleep. From 4 AM to 8 AM, you'd sprinted a hundred meters, hauled rice sacks, climbed a tree, sat on a six-story collarbone in the wind, stepped in the pool, scrubbed a skirt bigger than yourself with a brush. Your eyelids were heavy as the medicine ball pressed to your chest in PE; each blink was like pulling a rusty roller door—once down, you didn't want to pull it up.

But your brain kept turning. It said: the picture book isn't finished. Evening routine: finish the book, put it in the drawer, lights out, sleep. It wasn't evening, but the morning routine was expired, and you didn't know which routine to run. The routines were scrambled. When routines scramble, the brain automatically jumps to the most familiar one. For you, the evening routine was most familiar—it had never been skipped. So you had to finish the book. If you didn't finish it, you couldn't put it in the drawer. If it wasn't in the drawer, you couldn't sleep.

Your body's drowsiness fought your brain's "must." You sat on the bed cross-legged, eyelids slitted, fingers still on page four. Your mouth moved, wanting to keep reading, but only a soft breath came out—"Bubu"—then cut off.

You swayed. Not side to side, but forward—upper body slowly leaning, forehead nearly touching the book cover, then snapping back, eyes wide for a second as if telling yourself "I wasn't asleep," then sinking again.

But you didn't stop turning pages. You could turn with your eyes closed—pinch the corner, flip, press flat with your palm. Page five. Six. Slow, but correct. On page seven, you read—"The whale said, whoosh—" then cut off again.

The book slipped from your fingers, landing cover-up on the quilt. Little Bear Bubu stood at the beach, holding the red bucket, looking at a distant whale.

You picked it up, reopened. From page one.

"Little Bear Bubu went to the beach. He brought a—brought a—brought a—"

Your voice was a stuck tape, circling one word. You frowned, tapped your forehead as if to unstick it. Two taps, no luck. You breathed deep, closed the book, closed your eyes, lips moving silently—memorizing. Stuck at page four. Backed up, started over from page one. Stuck at page four again.

You were using memory to force yourself through the routine, like a runner forcing the last lap.

Just then, the door lock clicked.

Key turning. The low creak of the security door opening. Then Mom's slippers on the doormat—soft, half the volume of normal, as if afraid to wake the world. Keys dropped into the glass bowl on the shoe cabinet—ding.

"Miaowu?"

Mom's voice from the entryway, slightly hoarse after night shift.

You didn't answer. Eyes still closed, lips still moving—still reciting the book.

When Mom walked into the bedroom, this is what she saw: you cross-legged on the bed, uniform crumpled, hair damp with sweat on your forehead, a pale-yellow pollen streak on your cheek. Eyes closed, body swaying slightly forward and back, lips parting and closing, emitting faint unrecognizable breaths. A worn-edge picture book open on your knees, cover up, Little Bear Bubu holding a red bucket.

Mom didn't say "why aren't you sleeping." Didn't say "what are you doing." She just took off her nurse shoes at the bedroom door, walked barefoot to the bed, and sat. The mattress dipped; your body tilted, but you didn't open your eyes.

Mom reached over—didn't touch the book, didn't touch your shoulder. She just laid two fingers lightly on your wrist—thumb inside, index outside, at the pulse.

She felt your pulse. Fast, but steady. Not sick, not scared—it was like someone who'd run a long time finally sitting down, heart not yet slowing. Beneath the pulse was a finer tremor, not heartbeat, but calf muscles twitching—your calf pressed against Mom's outer thigh; through the uniform pants she felt that muscle going thump-thump-thump, like a small frog trapped under skin.

Mom released the pulse, moved her hand to your forehead. Back of the hand—cool, not hot. She brushed the stray hairs on your forehead aside, revealing the faint vein beneath your brow—overnight kids have more visible veins. She stared at it two seconds, then looked at the yellow pollen on your cheek. Her fingertip gently rubbed; the pollen crumbled, golden powder sticking to her finger. She rubbed her fingers; the powder fell onto the sheet.

"Miaowu." Mom said again, voice low but clear.

Your lips stopped.

Not gradually, but suddenly—as if someone pressed pause. Eyes still closed, but lips stopped moving. Your body leaned toward Mom, shoulder against her upper arm, head sliding into her shoulder socket. That bone was familiar to you—Mom's shoulder socket curved to fit your temple, the position you'd slept in since childhood. Mom didn't embrace you; she just sank her shoulder a little lower, letting you settle more securely.

Then Mom looked down at the open picture book on your knees. She recognized it—Little Bear Bubu Goes to the Beach, you'd brought it home from kindergarten two years ago, read countless times, spine worn white, cover water-stained. She reached and picked it up, closed it, set it on the nightstand—not in the drawer, just on top, cover up.

Your body moved. Eyes opened a crack, glanced at the book on the nightstand, lips moved, wanting to say "put it in the drawer"—but before sound came out, Mom's hand landed on your back. Not patting, but kneading. Palm from your neck down along the spine, stopping at your waist, then pushing back up.

"Close your eyes." Mom said.

You closed them. Not because you obeyed, but because that hand had found the right place—the two muscles beside the spine, tensed all morning in the parking lot, now softening under Mom's push and knead.

You heard Mom's heartbeat. Slower than yours. Her breath passed over your head, smelling of hospital hand soap and subway-platform wind. She didn't speak. When Mom judged silence needed, she was silent. She just kneaded your back, from neck to waist and back, like smoothing a crumpled paper.

Your calf twitched again.

Mom didn't open her eyes, but her hand immediately moved from your back to your hardest calf muscle. Thumb pressed, circled, released. Pressed, circled, released. You felt the muscle jump twice under her fingers, then slowly settle, like a frog whose back is held finally giving up.

Her fingers were cool. Night-shift nurse's hands, washed too many times, a dry thin callus on the pads. The callus against your skin felt different from anyone's—not smooth, slightly rough, with the astringency of antiseptic. This touch was more familiar to you than any voice. It didn't ask you questions, didn't need answers. It was just there, on your calf, circling and circling.

Your breathing was finally pulled slow by hers. Not self-adjusted, but her chest against your shoulder rising and falling like a giant warm pressure regulator, turning your frequency from fast-forward back to normal. You felt your body growing heavy—not the post-run soreness, but the heaviness of sinking into the mattress, into cotton, into something you were certain wouldn't disappear.

Mom didn't put the picture book back in the drawer.

She didn't notice the drawer rule, or she noticed but judged something more important. Mom's judgment was always like this—hundreds of decisions a day at the hospital, which patient first, which wound debride first, which family member needed to wait five minutes. At home, her judgment didn't clock out. She judged that what you needed now wasn't routine-correctness, but physical contact. Routines could wait until after waking; physical contact couldn't. A nurse knows some windows are only minutes—miss them, and the person retreats into their shell, taking ten times longer to pry open.

So she didn't get up. She left the book on the nightstand, cover up, Little Bear Bubu holding the red bucket. Then she gathered you a little closer, chin resting on your head. Her breath passed through your hair, warm, even, carrying a night of unbroken exhaustion and the relief of finally letting go after night shift.

Your finger moved. Before you realized, your hand had grabbed the hem of her nurse uniform. The fabric was crumpled in your fist, the blue hospital logo distorted by your grip. You didn't remember when you reached. Maybe when she kneaded your back, maybe when she pressed your calf, maybe earlier—when you heard her keys drop into the glass bowl with that ding.

You fell asleep.

Not the slow sinking sleep, but the sleep of holding your breath underwater and finally breaking the surface for that first breath. It was your body's surrender before your brain—your body judged, earlier than you, that it was safe now. Mom was here. Routines could wait. The picture book could wait. The drawer could wait. The whole world could wait.

Xiaoman drank the last of the second pot of chicken-noodle soup. She set the iron wok down, bottom thudding on concrete, then with her fingertips picked up the last clinging strand of noodle from the wok bottom and put it in her mouth. The noodle had stewed transparent, soft, soaked with chicken broth salt. She chewed twice, swallowed. Then wiped her mouth on her skirt hem. The brown mud ring on the hem had faded from washing, now marked with a small golden oil stain leaking from her mouth while sipping. She looked at the stain, rubbed it with her finger, it didn't come off.

She was full. Her stomach was filled, belly slightly rounded, a faint bulge visible through the light-blue dress. She leaned against the wall, sat cross-legged under the awning, the peony quilt folded beside her knee. Morning wind from the sea, cool, drying the fine sweat on her forehead from eating.

Then she was thirsty.

Not "a little thirsty"—the kind where after eating too much salt, the body shouts "water." Those two pots of soup were too salty. When Granny tore open the whole bag of salt and poured it in, salt grains had rushed directly from the bag mouth into the wok; some hadn't fully dissolved and stuck to the ribs. When Xiaoman ate a rib she bit into one—so salty her eyes squeezed—but didn't spit it out, chewed and swallowed. Now all that salt was in her body, circulating in her blood, pulling water from her cells. Her lips dried; her tongue felt astringent passing over her mouth, like licking a sun-heated stone. Her throat was dry too; swallowing felt the esophageal walls rubbing against each other, lacking lubrication.

She stood. The peony quilt slid from her knee, piling at her feet. She walked out from under the awning; sunlight was just spilling over the wall, not hot yet, but much brighter than dawn. Thin steam rose from the parking-lot concrete; last night's dew was drying. She walked to the faucet in the corner. An orange hose was attached, used that morning to wash the skirt, still connected. She turned the tap—the metal handle was only nail-sized between her fingertips; she pinched it with thumb and forefinger and turned gently.

Water gushed out, glinting broken silver in the morning light. She cupped both hands together. The cupping made a basin-sized hollow, but water still leaked through her finger gaps—the gaps too wide, water always finding its way. She crouched, recupped her hands, adjusted her fingers, pressing middle and ring fingers tighter. Water pooled in her palm into a small trembling lake. She brought her lips to the edge of her palm. Lips touching the thenar skin; water flowed from her palm into her mouth—cool, with residual iron taste from the hose and the night's cool not yet fully gone.

When the first sip slid down her throat, she felt her throat say "thank you."

She kept drinking. Second, third. Each swallow—glunk, glunk, glunk—deep sounds from her throat depths, like stones falling in a well. Some water leaked from the corner of her mouth, down her chin to her neck, into her collar. The collar edge dampened a small spot, light-blue fabric darkening, clinging to her collarbone. She didn't care, kept drinking. On the fourth, she stopped to breathe—crouching to drink, she'd unconsciously held her breath, then suddenly realized she was oxygen-starved. She wiped her mouth with the back of her hand; water slid from her wrist into her sleeve, making her shiver.

She cupped another handful. This one she drank slower, small sips, as if tasting water. Water had no taste—just water itself—but to her tongue recently occupied by salt, that "no taste" itself was comfortable. She finished the second handful, cupped a third. Halfway, she suddenly stopped, swept her tongue around her mouth—gums, palate, root—salt still there but much diluted. Throat not dry, esophagus not astringent, lips no longer sticking.

She tightened the tap, stood.

Then she stood there, looking at her spread hands. Palms wet, palm lines filled with water, glinting in the morning light. Water droplets still hung from her fingertips, falling one by one to the concrete, each making a dark dot that quickly evaporated. She wiped her hands on her skirt—the faded brown mud ring on the hem dampened by water, darkening a little more. She looked down at the wet mark, pressed it with her fingertip, leaving a shallow fingerprint on the fabric.

She walked back to the awning, sat again. The peony quilt was still piled on the ground; she bent, picked it up, shook off dust, folded it, set it by the rice sacks. Then she leaned against the wall, back of her head on the cool concrete, closed her eyes.

Now her body was comfortable. Stomach full, throat moist, bladder not yet signaling, intestines quiet. Her body was in a "full and digesting" phase. This feeling wasn't "great" but "replete"—a heavy, sleepy satisfaction. She yawned. Mouth wide, showing the gap and pink gum. After the yawn she smacked her lips; her tongue tasted a faint bone-soup flavor rising from her stomach, mixed with garlic pungency and potato starch sweetness.

Then she burped.

"Burp—"

Not loud, but sudden. It came from the top of her stomach, carrying leftover stew—rib salt, potato starch sweetness, cabbage sweetness, and the coolness of the water she'd drunk. Air rushed from her throat; her lips popped softly. She covered her mouth corner with the back of her hand, instinctively looking left and right—no one. Granny was upstairs, clinks of spatula on iron wok from the stairwell, probably making her own breakfast and thawing ribs for noon. The rider had left on deliveries; his e-bike still at the entrance, empty rear box open, an unfinished water bottle and a folded blue tarpaulin inside. Huangdou lay on the steps below Granny's building, tail wagging, sunning himself, ears occasionally twitching at passing flies.

After a while, another—"burp—." Lighter, almost just lip movement. This aftertaste added something: glass noodles. Not the noodle taste itself, but the faint starch smell of noodles absorbing broth, swelling in her stomach, then releasing. Her tongue slid across her mouth; touching her palate felt a thin film—the residue of oil and starch gelatinized in the broth. She rubbed her tongue with a finger, a small white coating on her fingertip. She looked at it, then wiped her finger on her skirt.

Then another—"burp—." This lighter, so light she didn't notice. But her body did. Each burp shrank her stomach's volume, gas expelled, making room for digestion. It was a slow comfortable release. She shifted—uncrossed her legs, knees sliding half an inch forward, leaving a faint gray mark on the concrete. The peony quilt slid from the rice sack, piled at her feet, corner on her ankle. She didn't pick it up because another sensation grabbed her attention.

Heat.

Not sun heat—morning wind still cool, the awning's tarpaulin blocked the rising sun, she sat in shade. Not quilt heat—the quilt was piled at her feet, not on her. It was the thermic effect of food, rising from inside. Those two pots of high-protein high-fat stew were decomposing in her stomach; the digestive system needed lots of blood. Blood surged to her stomach and intestines; she felt her upper belly warm, like a small stove burning inside her. This heat spread from core outward, to chest, neck, face. Her cheeks flushed—not embarrassment flush, but digestive vasodilation. She pressed the back of her hand to her cheek; hot. She turned the hand over, pressed the cool side, felt better. The back of her neck sweated too. Fine beads seeped from hair roots, dampening stray strands, sticking to skin, tickling. She reached and lifted her hair, fanned the back of her neck with her palm. The fan wind was small, but better than nothing. When morning wind came, neck sweat evaporated, giving her goosebumps. Cool and heat existed simultaneously, like two currents alternately on her skin.

Then her stomach growled again. Not hunger. A signal that digestion was advancing—the stomach's chyme being pushed into the small intestine, air and liquid moving through the gut, making a deep dull "gurgle." She placed a hand on her belly; through the skirt she felt a faint vibration. The stomach's outline was a bit smaller—food moving down. She spread her fingers, pressing different spots. Lower left a little bloated—not pain, just gas moving through the gut. Normal. This much food, this much water, digestion working at full capacity, gas was a byproduct. She gave a small burp, releasing the last air in her stomach. Then she closed her eyes and let her body handle the rest.

She leaned against the wall, half-asleep, consciousness like a leaf floating on water, pushed back and forth by waves. The food in her stomach was being decomposed layer by layer, gas accumulating in her gut bit by bit. This gas was deeper, at the transverse colon's bend—a natural curve where gas easily stalled, forming a tiny, repeatedly ignored pressure point.

That pressure point was slowly growing.

Her body realized first. Not consciousness—her consciousness was still drifting in the giant country's garden, telling Xiao Gu "don't lick that tooth"—but her abdominal wall muscles, without her control, made an infinitesimal adjustment: lower left belly tightened slightly, as if making a path for the gas. Then—

Pfft—pfft.

Two sounds. Half a second between them, like two bubbles released in sequence from the same exit. First muffled, low vibration, absorbed by the layers of skirt fabric, leaving only a deep blurry tail. Second shorter but crisper—not sharp, but like a finger flicking a thin leaf, a snap, then immediately dissipating in the awning air.

This sound she heard even in her half-asleep state. Not with ears—but with her body. She felt the extremely fine vibration as the gas left, not sound but touch, like someone far away gently flicking the ground she sat on. Her lashes trembled. Same as before, ten toes curling slightly on the concrete—an involuntary movement, the body's instinctive response to release.

The small air under the awning stirred. Morning wind immediately poured in from the tarpaulin edge, pushing the stirred air out, replacing it with new sea-smelling cool air. Then brief quiet. Her gut didn't move for those few seconds, as if resting—the smooth muscles that had just finished a round now relaxing, waiting for the next signal.

But digestion didn't stop. Chyme in the stomach was pushed bit by bit into the duodenum; new gas continued forming in the ascending colon. In less than a minute, her stomach gurgled softly—not the loud hungry gurgle, but a deeper, duller sound, like liquid and gas moving together in the gut. The new gas advanced along the ascending colon toward the transverse. Not a big burst, but scattered, tiny, like a string of small bubbles queuing through the same bend. Her thigh unconsciously shifted half an inch—this loosened the skirt pressed under her knee, making the exit less tightly covered.

Those bubbles were pushed to the end by peristalsis. They didn't come out one by one in order, but crowded together, finding the exit at the same instant. Purrrr—a fine long string, like a small balloon pushed underwater blowing bubbles. Lasted a bit over a second, pitch rising and falling, because her skirt was still pressed under her knee, but the loosened corner made the airflow irregular, the sound circling several times between fabric and skin before emerging. After this string ended, her calf moved slightly. Not cramp—relaxation. That calf muscle had been in an unconscious micro-tension, now suddenly loosening, knee sliding half an inch, leaving a faint gray mark on the concrete. The peony quilt slid farther from her feet, corner on her ankle, swayed twice in the wind.

Then she felt a light diffuse emptiness in her lower belly. That spot had been a bit bloated—not pain, just the fullness of gut walls stretched by gas—now gone. The string of bubbles carried away most of the backed-up gas; this region of gut temporarily calmed. But soon new gas gathered deeper—in the descending colon. This time finer, like very small bubbles clinging to folds in the gut wall, not moving, just sitting quietly. Until her body shifted. She shifted lightly—just moving her weight from left hip to right, shoulders changing angle against the wall. But this squeezed the descending colon. The tiny bubbles were compressed into a smaller space, then simultaneously found the exit. This time not a string—one.

Pssh—soft, thin, like the friction of a folded paper slowly unfolding. Not a "pfft" plosive but a "sss" fricative—the airflow channel too narrow to vibrate, just lightly rubbing the skin at the exit. This sound was so soft she had no reaction. Lashes didn't flutter, toes didn't curl, breathing didn't change. But her body felt it—the small patch of descending colon stretched by gas now deflated, pressing tight against surrounding tissue, quietly waiting for the next filling.

She kept sleeping. Or not sleeping—drifting. Digestion continued, gas continued forming.

She kept sleeping. Or not sleeping—drifting. Digestion continued, gas continued forming. Consciousness like a leaf on water, pushed by waves. Food decomposed layer by layer in her stomach; gas accumulated bit by bit. Urine slowly filled her bladder—the water she'd drunk, the soup, the salty liquids that kept making her thirsty, filtered by her kidneys, drop by drop gathering into a warm expanding lake.

She dreamed of home in the giant country.

In the dream she was in her own bed, the bed large, spread with light-blue sheets, the color of her dress. By the pillow was Xiao Gu's letter—very big paper, folded into a small square. Mom called from downstairs: "Xiaoman, wake up, breakfast is ready." She rolled over, face into the pillow. The pillow smelled of sun-baked milk and lavender. Her bladder was bloated, but it was okay—home had a big toilet, white, pink lid, she could lift it herself.

She stood, walked to the bathroom. Wooden floor, thudding underfoot. She pushed the bathroom door—

A gust of cool wind woke her.

Not home's wind. The abandoned parking lot's morning wind, carrying sea salt and iron. Her consciousness was pulled from the giant country's floor like a rooted radish, still carrying moist earth and the pain of breaking off.

She felt it.

Bladder. That warm lake had reached the shore, the shoreline pressing against a switch she didn't want to acknowledge. Her body in half-sleep made a judgment: get up and find a place, or keep sleeping?

She chose to keep sleeping.

Not on purpose. Too tired. Her muscles protested, her consciousness was on strike, her whole body said "five more minutes." She rolled over—in the dream rolling, in reality just shifting weight from left hip to right—the peony summer quilt piled by her hand, its corner brushing the concrete. Her legs parted slightly, knees scraping half an inch on concrete.

Then she relaxed.

Not all. The unconscious, slow, piece-by-piece release of sleep. Like a tiny hole poked in a balloon, air not rushing out in a "pfft" but leaking out in a "sss—" fine, steady. Her pelvic floor relaxed the first line of defense under cover of dream, then the second, then—

She didn't feel the start.

She only felt warmth.

A warm, continuous liquid rising from inside her body, flowing out between her parted legs. Not a jet, but overflow—like a cup filled too full, slowly flowing over the rim, spreading along the tabletop.
