The liquid was transparent, with a faint filtered water scent, temperature slightly higher than her skin, so she felt the warm current spreading on the concrete like an expanding warm map.

Her lashes trembled.

But eyes didn't open. Consciousness wavered between "wake and face it" and "keep escaping" for one second, chose the latter. She was too sleepy. Too comfortable. That warmth reminded her of the bathtub at home, of Mom drawing her bath, of Xiao Gu shouting outside the door "hurry up, I need to pee." Her lips moved, emitting a mumbled dream-talk: "...wait..."

Then more liquid flowed.

This time she felt it. Not pain, but emptiness. The bladder contracted like a slowly wrung sponge, each drop leaving taking a little pressure, bringing a little relief. Her breathing deepened slightly, shoulders dropping from their tight arc, forehead against the wall, concrete's coolness coming through skin, contrasting with the warmth beneath her.

The liquid pooled on the concrete. Her hips sat in the center of the pool, warm liquid spreading outward from beneath her, no fabric barrier—she wore only a white tank top, between her legs was pink-white giant skin, liquid directly touching concrete, absorbed, spread, pulled by gravity to lower ground. Morning light on the water refracted fine light spots like scattered glass. The pool kept expanding, spreading from under her to all sides, over the peony quilt piled at her feet—the lower half soaked, color from bright peony red to dark heavy brown-red.

Her toes moved slightly in the liquid.

Not conscious. Reflex. Warm liquid flowing between toe gaps, bringing a strange infantile sensation. Her toes spread and closed, leaving shallow wet marks on the concrete. Her heels were wet too, skin slightly whitened from soaking, like fingers in water too long.

Then she fully relaxed.

The moment her bladder emptied, she let out a sigh—not a waking sigh, but the satisfied sigh of sleep, rising from deep within. Her lips parted, exhaling warm air carrying faint mushroom glass-noodle soup flavor from her stomach. Her head tilted, cheek against the wall, mouth corner slightly moist—not saliva, but water vapor from her breath condensing on her lips.

She fell asleep. Truly asleep. Not drifting, but sinking.

The peony quilt piled by her hand, lower half in water, corner soaked, color dark as wet old newspaper. Her white tank top hem hung at her thigh roots, splashed wet in a small patch, clinging to skin, cool. The concrete beneath her had become a large dark wetland, area expanding, slowly evaporating in morning wind, releasing a faint warm ammonia smell. Her body in the center of the wetland, pink-white, giant, quiet, breathing evenly, belly rising and falling with each breath.


You woke at 12:50.

Not by alarm. Your children's watch was set for 6:30 AM, but that time had long passed. You were woken by your own heartbeat—thump-thump, thump-thump, like someone gently knocking in your chest, reminding you "time to get up."

You opened your eyes.

White ceiling, a water stain shaped like a crooked-neck rabbit. You stared at it three seconds, counted its edge folds—seven. Then you turned your head toward the nightstand.

The picture book was there. Little Bear Bubu Goes to the Beach, cover up. Little Bear holding the red bucket, looking at a distant whale.

You didn't get up immediately. You lay in bed, listening to home sounds. The fridge compressor hummed in the kitchen, stopping every twenty minutes; when it stopped you heard distant car sounds from outside. Mom's room was next door, door closed, but you could hear her breathing—slow, deep, like a fan on its lowest setting.

She was still sleeping. Night-shift nurse's sleep is fragmented, but she was in her deepest patch now.

You pulled back the covers. Very gently, like peeling tofu skin. Your feet touched the floor—cool, tile three degrees colder than your soles; you shrank, then stepped firm.

Your uniform was still on the washing-machine lid. You walked over, picked it up, sniffed. Soap smell, and a little iron smell of parking-lot concrete. You wrinkled your nose, then put it on. Pants first, then top. Buttoned to the top—you usually didn't, but today you wanted to.

You walked into the kitchen.

The tray was still on the counter. Yellow mug, handle right. White pillbox, three compartments, tablets already in each. Whole-wheat toast, individually wrapped, transparent bag printed with the wheat stalk in a chef's hat.

You stood before the tray, motionless.

This was the morning routine. But it was afternoon now. The routine had expired.

Your finger hovered above the pillbox for a second, then you picked it up. White round tablets with a score line. You poured two into your palm. Dry swallow. They rolled into your stomach like two small stones, bitter.

You didn't eat the toast. You put it back on the tray; the transparent bag rustled.

Then you turned toward the door.

Sneakers in the shoe cabinet bottom. Left, right. You crouched, slipped your feet in—the shoes still a bit damp, parking-lot moisture not fully dry. You tied laces. Left over right, right over left, pulled tight. Bow.

You stood, hand on the doorknob.

You stopped.

Your finger scraped the doorknob. The metal's coolness rose from your fingertip, like someone gently biting your bone. You thought of Xiaoman's finger—that index finger, wider and thicker than you, pink, nails round. When she touched your head, it was the same temperature.

You let go, ran back to the bedroom.

Pulled colored pencils from the drawer. You chose red, blue, yellow. On a white paper you drew a small yourself, in crumpled uniform, messy hair. Then beside it a giant girl, light-blue dress, long black hair, missing a front tooth.

Both had suns over their heads. Yellow, round, rays as short lines—you drew twelve.

You pressed the drawing under the pillbox. One corner lifted; you pressed it flat with your palm.

Then you returned to the door, turned the knob.


The alley sunlight was bright. The 1 PM sun was white, shining on concrete, the ground reflecting like a blurry mirror. You squinted, bangs stuck to your forehead with sweat.

You ran.

First fifty meters fast. Sneakers slapped the concrete, arms pumping, backpack smacking your back. But your legs were still sore—the morning's 100-meter sprint lactic acid not fully metabolized, calf muscles like two cooked noodles, heavy and soft.

You slowed. Not walking, but a drag-run slower than running. Breathing heavy, like a leaky bellows.

But you didn't stop.

You crossed the alley, through the parking-lot entrance, around the wall—

You stopped.

You smelled it.

Not sea salt. Not iron. Not the leftover aroma of those morning stews.

It was a strong, warm smell, like banana peels left in a summer trash can all day. But not stinky. Just strong. Very strong.

Your nose wrinkled. You knew this smell. You'd smelled it when you put your nose close to home's toilet. But much weaker.

This was much stronger.

And much larger—not from one spot, but from the whole parking lot, from under the awning, from the wall roots, from every crack in the concrete, steaming up.

Your steps slowed.

You walked step by step. Turned the wall corner into the parking lot.

You saw it.

Xiaoman leaned against the wall, sitting, but her head tilted. Cheek on the concrete wall, a dried drool mark at her mouth corner, glinting in the sun. Her lashes were motionless, like a row of wind-blown reeds frozen mid-air.

She was asleep.

Her dress was still damp, light-blue fabric clinging to her, darker than dry, like a post-rain sky. The brown mud ring and dark-blue urine stains on the hem were very faint now, but still there, like a few darker clouds at the sky's edge.

Then you looked down.

You saw the ground.

That light-brown, irregular map with white salt crystals at the edges. Spreading from beneath Xiaoman to all sides, over the peony quilt corner piled at her feet—the lower half in water, color dark as wet old newspaper—to the wall root, blocked by the wall, pooling into a shallow dried-river shape.

Sunlight slanted under the awning, shining on the mark, reflecting a dull light.

You stood still, looking three seconds.

Then you kept walking.

Step by step, to Xiaoman's feet.

The smell was stronger. Warm, acrid, mixed with sea salt and iron, like a lonely, private scent wrapping you entirely.

But you didn't back away.

You sat down.

Back against the wall, in Xiaoman's shadow. Cool. Your sneaker toes just touched the edge of the mark—white salt crystals, rough, like dried shells.

You tilted your head up to look at Xiaoman's face.

Her ear tips were pink-white, not red—because she was still sleeping, didn't know she was seen. Her breathing was slow; each exhale stirred a breeze that ruffled the stray hairs on your forehead. Her stomach didn't growl anymore, rising and falling quietly and contentedly, like a pink-white calm sea.

You reached out.

Not to touch Xiaoman.

To touch your own sneaker laces.

You untied the left lace, retied it.

Bow.

Left over right, right over left, pulled tight.

Then the right.

Same routine.

Done, you placed both hands on your knees.

Your fingers weren't picking at anything.

You waited.

Far away, at the parking-lot entrance, the rider's e-bike was still there. Empty rear box open, an unfinished water bottle inside, and a folded blue tarpaulin. Wind blew from the sea, through wall cracks, stirring the water bottle, surface rippling.

No one.

Only the smell, steaming quietly and continuously in the 1 PM sun.

You sat in Xiaoman's shadow, head tilted up to look at her face.

Her lashes moved.

But she didn't wake.

Just dreamed something—her lips moved, emitting a mumble, like saying a distant place's name.

You didn't wake her.

You just sat there, smelling the smell, looking at the map, waiting.

Waiting for your friend to wake up.

You sat there, smelling the smell, looking at the map.

Your nose stung a little. Not the kind that makes you want to cry, but the ammonia kind, like someone brushing a very fine feather across your nasal cavity. You blinked; a thin film of mist gathered in your eyes, but you didn't wipe it. Mist blurred vision, but you didn't want to blur—you wanted to see Xiaoman's face clearly.

Her lashes moved again.

This time not trembling, but quivering—like butterfly wings in slow motion, from root to tip, a wave passing through. Her nostrils flared slightly, lips parted a crack, exhaling warm air carrying faint mushroom glass-noodle soup flavor from her stomach.

Then her eyes opened.

Not all at once. First a slit, amber light leaking through, like two curtains parted a crack. Her pupils dilated in the shadow, like two moons half-covered by clouds, reflecting your image—small, sitting at her feet, head up, no expression on your face.

She blinked.

Blinked again.

She saw you.

Her ear tips flushed—from pink-white to pink, pink to deep red, like someone splashed a whole bottle of red ink on her ears. The change was fast, faster than you could count to three. Her lips opened, closed, opened, closed—like a fish pulled onto shore, looking for water.

"Miaowu..."

Her breath was hoarse, with post-nasal congestion from oversleeping, like a tape recorder with a cold. Her fingers unconsciously scratched the concrete, nails scraping shush-shush on the gray cement—the same sound as on the lawn, by the pool, in the parking lot that morning.

"You... why are you here?"

Her eyes moved from your face to beneath her—that light-brown map with white salt-crystal edges. Her ear tips reddened further, almost bleeding. Her fingers dug harder, gray cement dust packing under her nails.

"I... I didn't..."

She wanted to explain. To say I didn't mean to, I was too tired, I thought no one would come back. But her lips trembled, the words stuck in her throat like a string of caught bubbles.

You didn't speak.

You just looked at her. At her ear tips, at her fingers digging the ground, at the faded mud and urine stains on her skirt. Your face was still blank—the blankness of a zoning-out second-grader, eyes wide, mouth slightly open.

Then you spoke.

"It's okay because this—no no!!!"

"What did you dream about?"

Her lips stopped. The fingers digging the ground stopped too. Her pupils trembled slightly, like someone tossing a very small stone into those two moons.

"...what?"

"You just talked in your sleep." You said, voice thin as a cotton thread rising from the ground, swayed by the afternoon wind. "You said 'Xiao Gu, don't lick.' Who is Xiao Gu?"

Xiaoman's lashes lowered halfway. That broom-thick lash row like a translucent curtain, covering half her eye. Behind the curtain, amber light swayed—not tears, but something else.

Then she smiled.

Not the crying-laugh, not the "whatever, this is happening" pool smile, but a real, soft dream-borne smile. Her mouth curved up, showing the two-meter tooth gap, pink gum glinting in the afternoon sun.

"My little sister." She said, her breath carrying a distant, warm resonance. "Two years younger than me. Called Xiao Gu. Gu as in grain."

Her fingers lifted from the concrete to her knee. Gray cement dust still under her nails, but she wasn't digging anymore. Her ear tips were still red but paler, like ink diluted in water.

"She's teething, always licking that wiggly tooth. Mom says licking it makes it grow crooked. She doesn't listen. She hides behind the curtain licking it, thinks no one sees. But the curtain moves, she thinks she's hidden well."

As she spoke, her shoulder trembled slightly. Not crying, but laughing—the involuntary laugh of thinking of her sister. Her fingers twisted the skirt hem on her knee—the exact same gesture as your picking at the basin rim.

"I dreamed about her." She said. "Dreamed she was behind the curtain licking her tooth, the curtain going flutter-flutter. I was outside the door, wanting to tell her 'Xiao Gu, stop licking,' but the doorknob was too high, I couldn't reach. Then I woke up."

She looked at you, eyes crescent.

"Then I saw you. You're much shorter than the doorknob. But you can reach me."

You didn't smile. Your face was still blank. But your mouth—curved up a little. Not played back. Yours.

"Where is she now?" You asked.

"At Grandma's." Xiaoman. "Grandma's is far, over a mountain. She writes me letters, on very big paper, folded into small squares, under my pillow."

Her lashes fully lifted. Those two amber moons looked at you, reflecting your image—small, sitting at her feet, hands on knees, fingers not picking at anything.

"When I woke up, there was no letter under my pillow." She said, voice light as a feather on water. "Because I didn't wake up at home."

Neither of you spoke.

The smell was still there. Ammonia's acrid, warm, fermented scent mixed with sea salt and iron, continuously steaming up from the concrete beneath you. But neither of you cared. It became background, like distant traffic, like the fridge compressor, like a quiet natural presence.

Your finger moved on your knee. Not picking, but drawing—circling a small circle in the air, like drawing an invisible basin.

"Tomorrow," you said, "I'll take you to eat buns. Custard buns. They flow out when you bite them."

Xiaoman's eyes lit. The two-meter tooth gap flashed in the afternoon sun, like a pink-white small canyon.

"Custard buns..." she repeated, voice holding careful expectant seriousness. "Yellow? Sweet?"

"Yeah. Sweet. It flows out."

"Flows out..." her tongue poked between her lips, licked her upper lip, leaving a wet trail. "Then how do I catch it?"

You thought. Your second-grade brain turned.

"With your hands." You said, then held up both hands, palms up, like two small pink shells. "Like this. Cup them."

Xiaoman looked at your raised hands. Her pupils moved slightly in their sockets, from left to right, from your fingertips to your wrists, to the pills on your uniform cuff.

"Your hands are very small." She said.

"Yeah."

"Smaller than my fingernail."

"Yeah."

"But you said 'cup them.'" She said, her voice holding something you couldn't name, soft as cotton candy. "You said your hands can cup them."

You tilted your head. Your bangs blew back in the afternoon wind, revealing the faint sunline on your forehead.

"Because custard buns aren't as big as your tears." You said, tone like stating a fact everyone knows. "Tears I can't catch. But custard buns, I can."

Xiaoman's lips moved. No sound. Her eyes began to redden—from the corners, like someone dropping a drop of pale strawberry juice, slowly spreading. But she didn't let tears fall. She just raised both hands from her knee, held them before her, palms up.

Her palm. Pink-white, wider than a car, palm lines winding like dry riverbeds. A faint oil sheen from pinching chicken still on her fingertips, glinting in the afternoon sun.

She looked at her palm, then at yours.

Then she did something.

She brought both hands together—not clapping, but cupping—like two giant pink-white shells, forming a warm semicircular dome above your head. Not touching you, just hovering, about three meters above your head.

"Like this?" She asked, breath falling from above the dome, muffled, like from under a blanket.

You looked up. Her palms showed a warm translucent pink in the afternoon sun, shadows in the palm lines like river tributaries converging on the palm center. Her fingers slightly bent, nails pink, round, with a thin white edge.

"Too big." You said.

"Then..." her fingers adjusted in the air, the dome shrank a little. "Like this?"

"Still too big."

She shrank again. Now the dome was only a meter above your head; you could feel warmth from her palm, like sitting by a just-extinguished heater.

"Like this?" Her voice held the focus of finishing a math problem.

You thought. Then you stood.

Your leg was numb—sat too long, concrete's coldness climbing through your uniform pants, like a cold skin on your bones. You hissed, stamped your foot, then walked three steps forward.

To Xiaoman's thumb.

You turned, back against her thumb base—that thumb taller and thicker than you, pink-white skin forming a slight warm curve behind you. You raised both hands above your head, palms up, like that morning at the parking-lot exit.

"Like this." You said. "Your palm here. My palm here. The custard bun goes in the middle."

Xiaoman froze. Then her eyes curved into crescents.

"...you're in my hand." She said.

"Yeah."

"But you said 'middle.'"

"Yeah." You nodded seriously. "Middle. Not your palm center, not my hand back. Middle. We cup it together."

Her dome slowly descended. Not clapping, but covering—like a giant pink-white cloud, slowly descending from the sky, stopping ten centimeters above your palms.

Her palm faced your palm.

Ten centimeters of air between.

In those ten centimeters of air, ammonia smell, afternoon sun, your two body temperatures slowly mingling.

"Like this?" Her breath was light as a feather.

"Yeah." You said. "Tomorrow like this. Buy ten custard buns. You cup five, I cup five. Then we eat together."

Xiaoman didn't speak.

Her palm hovered above you, ten centimeters. You could feel her pulse through the air—thump... thump... thump...—slower than yours but deeper, like a distant clock striking.

Then her thumb moved.

That thumb, thicker than your whole body, nails round, healthy pink, slowly reached from the side, gently touched the top of your head.

Not pressing. Just touching.

Warm. Soft. With living skin's elasticity.

Same as this morning, as noon, as every time.

But her palm didn't cover down. She just let the thumb rest on your head, the other nine fingers hovering in the air, like the skeleton of a giant pink-white raining cloud.

"Miaowu." She said.

"Hm?"

"Your hands are really small."

"Yeah."

"But you said 'together.'" Her voice carried a resonance you'd never heard, like a cello string plucked. "You said 'we cup them together.'"

"Because," you said, tilting your head, "because one person can't cup ten. Two people can."

Her thumb stayed on your head a long time.

Long enough to count every fine vertical ridge on her nail—pink, healthy, edges round. Long enough to feel her fingertip's warmth traveling through your hair to your scalp, then to your brain, making you warm all over.

Then she slowly withdrew her thumb.

The dome rose, the cloud dispersed, afternoon sunlight fell on your face again.

"Okay." She said, voice light but clear. "Tomorrow. Ten. Together."

You lowered your tired arms, sat back at her feet, back against the wall. Your sneaker toes still touched the edge of the mark—white salt crystals, rough, like dried shells.

But this time, your fingers didn't circle in the air.

They just rested on your knees, quietly.

Xiaoman settled back too. She adjusted her posture, straightened her tilted head, back against the wall, both hands on her knees, exactly the same posture as you. Her dress was still damp, clinging to her, light-blue fabric lightened by the afternoon sun, like an old sky washed many times.

Her ear tips were still red. But much paler than before.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"The map..." her voice lowered, like telling a secret. "You saw it. You smelled it. You didn't leave."

You didn't answer.

You just sat there, looking at the rider's e-bike at the exit, looking at the unfinished water bottle in the empty rear box, looking at the jumping light spots leaking through wall cracks.

"Because," you said, voice thin as a glass bead dropping in a porcelain bowl, bouncing once. "Because tomorrow we eat custard buns together. If I left, I couldn't eat them."

Xiaoman laughed.

The laugh of being caught off guard, of being teased, bubbling up from her stomach. Her shoulders shook, but no sound—she was holding it in, like that morning at the convenience store, muffling laughter in her throat.

But her eyes curved into crescents. The two-meter tooth gap fully visible, pink gum glinting in the afternoon sun.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"Your nose is red."

You raised a hand to touch your nose. Fingertip touching your nose tip, a little hot, a little sore—ammonia irritation. You rubbed it, making it redder, like a pinched cherry.

"It's okay." You said. "Tomorrow it'll be fine."

"Tomorrow?"

"Yeah. Tomorrow we eat sweet. Sweet makes the nose stop being red."

Xiaoman looked at you. Her pupils held two small yous—red nose tip, messy bangs, a yellow locust-pollen grain on your uniform cuff, hands on your knees, fingers quiet.

"The day after tomorrow?" She asked.

"The day after tomorrow..." you thought, your second-grade brain turning. "The day after tomorrow we eat salty. Salty makes the nose red again. Then sweet again."

"Sweet again?"

"Yeah. Sweet, salty, sweet, salty." You said, like stating a universal law. "Keep eating. Then the nose won't always be red, and won't always not be red. It'll be just right."

Xiaoman didn't speak.

She just raised both hands from her knee, held them before her, palms up, like two giant pink-white shells.

Then she looked at you.

You understood.

You also raised both hands, palms up, like two small pink shells.

Her palms stopped ten centimeters above.

Your palms stopped ten centimeters below.

Ten centimeters of air between.

In those ten centimeters of air, ammonia smell, afternoon sun, your two body temperatures slowly mingling.

This was the middle.

Not her hand back, not your palm center.

The middle.

You held them up until your arms ached, until the sun set, until ivy shadows on the wall stretched very long, until distant evening bus beeps, until your watch vibrated gently—5 PM, Mom should wake up.

You lowered your hands.

"I have to go." You said.

"Yeah."

"See you tomorrow."

"See you tomorrow."

You stood, legs numb, stamped your foot. You turned toward the parking-lot exit.

At the wall corner, you looked back.

Xiaoman still sat there, both hands held before her, palms up, like two giant pink-white shells. Her eyes looked at you, amber, reflecting your image—small, standing at the wall corner, messy hair, red nose.

Her lips moved.

No sound came over. But you knew what she was saying.

"Middle."

You raised one hand, palm up, like a small pink shell.

Then you ran off.

Sneakers slapped the concrete, arms pumping, backpack smacking your back.

But this time, your legs didn't ache.

Your nose was still a little red.

But your mouth—curved up a little.

Yours.

5:23 PM.

When you reached the parking lot, the sun had already set. Ivy shadows on the wall stretched very long, like black rivers flowing from wall top to ground. Your sneakers slapped the concrete, but this time you didn't run too fast—legs still sore, like someone stuffed two wet cotton balls in your calves.

You turned the wall corner and stopped.

Xiaoman still sat there. Same as afternoon, back against the wall, cross-legged. But her posture changed—not relaxed sitting, but curled. Her spine was bowed, like a drawn bowstring released, sagging. Her hands held her stomach, fingers sinking into the light-blue dress fabric, knuckles white. Her head hung above her knees, long hair falling like a black waterfall, half-covering her face.

Her stomach—that giant abdomen covered by the light-blue dress—was making sounds.

Not "gurgle" hunger. Deeper, longer, like a washing machine running empty from far away. The sound came from deep within her body, through skin and air, arriving at your eardrums as a low-frequency vibration you could feel, like someone pressing the back of your head against a big drum.

Gurgle—boom—

Lasted three seconds. Four. Five.

Then stopped. One second pause. Then again.

Gurgle—boom—

Louder. More urgent. Like the washing machine wasn't running empty but had caught something, struggling desperately.

You walked over. Step by step, to Xiaoman's feet. You looked up at her—the trembling, hungry pink mountain. Your face had no expression. You just stood there, watch still lit on your wrist, green numbers jumping in the sunset.

"Xiaoman." You said, voice thin as a cotton thread rising from the ground.

Xiaoman raised her head. Her eyes were amber in the sunset, like two moons stained by afterglow. But no light inside—dark, like covered by clouds. Her lips trembled, the white dry skin on her lower lip curled up, like a small withered leaf.

"Miaowu..." her breath was hoarse, with a dying-battery weakness. "I... I'm so hungry..."

Her fingers sank deeper into her stomach, nails scraping shush-shush on the fabric. Her body trembled—not cold trembling, but hungry trembling, a deep vibration from below, like underground springs surging.

"I haven't eaten since morning..." she said, voice light as begging. "Those two pots... were breakfast... now... it's afternoon..."

Tears started falling. Not one by one, but strings—like broken beads rolling from her eye corners, down her cheeks, gathering into a bigger drop at her chin, then plopping on her knee. The light-blue dress dampened a large area, darkening, like rain suddenly falling from the sky.

"I... I forgot..." her voice softened with tears. "I forgot you all need lunch... I thought... I thought two pots were enough... I thought..."

She couldn't continue. Her shoulders twitched, each twitch making the sky above sway in your vision. Her stomach rumbled again—gurgle-boom—this time longer, like a freight train entering a distant station, wheel-on-rail sounds pulled very far.

You stood there, watching Xiaoman cry.

Your face was still blank—the zoning-out second-grader's blankness, eyes wide, mouth slightly open. Your finger moved on your knee. Not picking, but drawing—circling a small circle in the air, like an invisible basin. But halfway, you stopped.

You lowered your hand. Placed it on your knee. Quiet.

Then you thought. Your second-grade brain turned.

Not thinking "I'm hungry too." You wouldn't think that. Your stomach wasn't growling—or it was, but you couldn't hear. Medication like thick cotton muffled the body's internal signals. You didn't know what hunger felt like. You only knew: morning routine was medicine and toast. Afternoon had no routine. Afternoon was blank.

But Xiaoman was crying. Xiaoman's stomach was growling. Xiaoman said "I'm so hungry."

These you could see. Hear. Know.

"Xiaoman." You said.

"Yeah..." her voice came from behind tears, muffled, like from under a blanket.

"You ate two pots this morning."

"Yeah..."

"It's afternoon now. Afternoon is lunch."

"Yeah..."

"You didn't eat lunch. So you're hungry."

Xiaoman paused. Her fingers lifted from her stomach to her knee, gray cement dust still under her nails. Tears still fell but slower, like easing rain.

"...yeah."

"Granny Xiulan said," you said, voice thin as playing back a remembered sentence, "she has more glass noodles upstairs. A big bundle. Noodles soak up broth, more filling than potatoes."

Xiaoman's eyes lit. But only for a second, then dimmed.

"But... Granny already made me so much... I can't ask for more..."

"Granny said, 'you're a guest.'" You said. "A guest is someone who comes to your house to eat. The host cooking for the guest is right. She also said, 'just eat.'"

You held up the watch, screen toward Xiaoman. Green numbers were harsh in the sunset.

"My money's still there. Seven hundred thirty-six and three cents. Can buy noodles. Buy eggs. The fat lady's eggs, cracked, cheap."

You paused, tilted your head.

"But," you said, "the market closed. The fat lady went home. Can't buy now."

Tears welled again. One by one, hanging on her lashes, like transparent grapes.

"Then... then what..." her voice shook like a leaf in the wind. "I... I'm really so hungry..."

You looked at her. At her red ear tips, at her fingers digging her knee, at the faded mud and urine stains on her skirt.

Then you looked down at your hands. Fingers on your knee, quiet. White plastic flakes from picking the basin rim still on your nails.

You thought of Mom. Mom off night shift, sleeping. Home kitchen, the tray on the counter. Yellow mug, handle right. White pillbox, three compartments, tablets already in each. Whole-wheat toast, individually wrapped, transparent bag printed with the wheat stalk in a chef's hat.

You ran the routine at noon: medicine. But not toast. Toast was still in the tray.

You didn't "get hungry and find food." You didn't have that routine. Your routine: Mom sets the tray, you take medicine and eat toast. If Mom doesn't set it, you don't eat.

But now Xiaoman was hungry. Xiaoman's stomach was growling. Xiaoman was crying.

You didn't know what to do.

You stood there, fingers quietly at your pants seam. Your stomach wasn't growling. You didn't know if it was hungry. You only knew: Xiaoman needed food. But you had nothing to give her.

Your money was in the watch. But the market closed. Granny was home, but Granny said "I'll wait at home for you," not "I'll come cook now."

You looked down at your backpack. Inside: scrub brush, soap, picture book. No food.

You looked up at Xiaoman. She still cried, shoulders twitching, stomach still growling.

"Xiaoman." You said.

"Yeah..."

"Wait for me."

You turned and ran toward the parking-lot exit.

Not running home. You didn't know what to take from home. You ran the other way—the building where Granny Xiulan lived.

You remembered Granny said: "I'm going back for the cart and the freezer things." Granny had a freezer. Things in the freezer. Granny was the host; the host cooked for the guest.

You reached Granny's building. The gray six-story apartment, sheets flapping on the roof terrace in the wind. You stood at the stairwell mouth and looked up, shouting:

"Granny Xiulan—!"

Your voice was thin and crisp, like a glass bead on a porcelain plate. But in the empty stairwell it amplified, echoes rising layer by layer.

"Granny Xiulan—!"

No response. Only your own echo, bouncing around the stairwell, growing smaller.

You called again. Louder.

"Granny Xiulan—! Xiaoman is hungry—! Needs to eat—!"

Footsteps thudded from above. Granny appeared at the third-floor stairwell, poking her head out, reading glasses squinted into two slits.

"Who is it—?"

"Me. Miaowu."

"Miaowu?" Granny held the railing, stepping down one step at a time. "Why are you back? Where's your mom?"

"Mom's sleeping. Night shift."

Granny reached the first floor, stood before you. She wore an apron, holding a spatula with something white on it—probably flour. Her hair was messy, hair clip askew.

"Then why aren't you home? What are you shouting for?"

"Xiaoman is hungry." You said, tone like reporting your student ID. "She ate two pots this morning. Now it's afternoon. Afternoon is lunch. She didn't eat lunch. Her stomach is growling. She's crying."

Granny paused. Her reading glasses blinked; she turned the spatula in her hand.

"...she's hungry again?"

"Yeah." You nodded. "She cried. She said 'I'm so hungry.' Her stomach is growling. Loud. Like a washing machine."

Granny was silent two seconds. Then she turned and shouted upstairs:

"Old man—! Bring down that half pig from the freezer—! And noodles—! Three bundles—! Eggs—! Two trays—!"

A muffled male voice from above: "Can't lift it—!"

"Can't lift it and you lift it anyway—! The young lady is hungry—!"

Granny turned back to look at you. Her eyes were slits, but something glinted inside.

"Wait here. I'll go up and get things. Then we go to the parking lot together."

You stood there, fingers quietly at your pants seam. You didn't know to say "thank you." Not impolite, but you didn't know if "thank you" was the thing to say now. You just stood there, waiting.

Granny thudded back upstairs. Sounds of spatula clinks, freezer door opening, plastic bags rustling. Plus the muffled male voice: "This half pig is really heavy..."

About five minutes later. Granny came down, carrying a large plastic bag with noodles, eggs, and some green vegetables. Behind her followed an old man, white-haired, bent over, carrying half a pig—smaller than Granny's half, but still heavy, his face flushed red.

"Let's go." Granny. "To the parking lot. Cook for the young lady."

She walked two steps, then looked back at you.

"What about you? Have you eaten?"

You tilted your head. You didn't know what "eaten?" meant. Your routine was "medicine, toast," not "meal."

"I took medicine." You said.

Granny's reading glasses squinted tighter. She looked at you—the messy-haired, crumpled-uniform, still-wet-pants second-grader. Your face was very white, not healthy white but the transparent white of someone who rarely sees sun. Your eyes were bright but strangely bright, like two clean lightbulbs with no current.

"You..." Granny paused. "What did you eat for lunch?"

You thought. Noon routine: medicine. Toast in the tray, you didn't eat it.

"Medicine." You said. "Two tablets. White. Round."

Granny's mouth opened, closed. She gripped the spatula tighter.

"...you didn't eat?"

"Ate medicine." You repeated, tone like reporting your ID. "Medicine is food. Mom said. Take medicine and you won't get sick."

Granny and the old man exchanged a look. The old man's mouth opened too, but he said nothing, just shifted the pig higher on his shoulder.

"Let's go." Granny said, voice softer now. "First to the parking lot. Cook for the young lady. Then..." she paused, looking at you, "then you eat too. I'll cook noodles. You eat a bowl."

You didn't answer. You just turned and walked toward the parking lot. Sneakers slapped the concrete, arms pumping, backpack smacking your back.

You didn't know what "eat a bowl" meant. Your routine didn't have "eat a bowl of noodles." But you knew: Granny was going to cook for Xiaoman. Xiaoman was hungry. Xiaoman was crying. Granny went, Xiaoman would stop crying.

That was enough.

When you reached the parking lot, the sun had fully set. The horizon held a last dark red glow, like a burning coal slowly cooling. Parking-lot streetlights came on, dim yellow light on Xiaoman—she still sat there, back against the wall, cross-legged, hands holding her stomach. Her head hung lower, long hair fully covering her face. Her shoulders shook, but slowly, like a dying-battery machine.

"Young lady—!" Granny called. "We're here—!"

Xiaoman raised her head. Her eyes were dark under the streetlight, like two moons fully covered by clouds. Her lips were chapped, the white dry skin on her lower lip curled, with a trace of blood at the edge.

She saw Granny. Saw the old man. Saw you.

Her eyes lit. Faint, but there.

"Granny Xiulan..." her breath was light as a feather. "You... came..."

"Came came!" Granny set the plastic bag on the ground and started taking things out. "Noodles! Eggs! Pork! And greens! I'll cook you a big pot!"

She directed the old man to set down the pig, then took out the iron wok—not the 1.2-meter one, another, smaller, about eighty centimeters. She set it on the open ground, took a lighter from her pocket, lit the high-pressure burner.

Blue flame licked the wok bottom.

Granny poured water into the wok. From the parking-lot hose, roaring in. Then she tore open the noodle packages, put all three bundles in—white noodles quickly softened and turned transparent in boiling water, like a school of white fish swimming. She cracked eggs—two trays, sixty total, thudded into the wok, yolks dispersing in boiling water into golden flowers. She cut pork into small pieces, tossed them in, meat rolling in boiling water, turning red to white. Finally she tossed in a handful of greens, green leaves floating on the soup like small lotus leaves.

"Done!" She announced. "Young lady—! Eat!"

Xiaoman looked at the soup. Her pupils dilated under the streetlight, like two lit amber stones. Her lips parted; the two-meter tooth gap flashed in the lamplight.

She reached, pinched the wok rim with two fingers. To her, like holding a small scalding teacup. She carefully lifted it and brought it to her mouth.

Then she drank a sip.

The moment the soup touched her mouth, her eyes closed. Lashes trembled in the lamplight, like wind-blown reeds. Her cheek bulged—chewing, she'd bitten a piece of pork. The meat dissolved in her mouth, soft, with noodle sweetness and egg aroma. She chewed three times, swallowed—glunk, like a stone in a deep well.

Then she opened her eyes.

"...hot." She said, with the grievance of a burnt tongue not wanting to spit it out. "But it's so good."

Tears welled again. But this time not from hunger, not grievance, but something else. She sniffled, wiped her eye corner on her skirt hem, then drank again.

This sip bigger. She tilted the wok; noodles, eggs, pork, greens slid into her mouth. She chewed, cheek rising and falling like a pink-white hill. She swallowed, drank again, again.

Granny stood on the ground looking up. Her hands stayed in the apron-wiping pose, forgotten. Her reading glasses reflected the streetlight's yellow and Xiaoman's big-eating face.

"Slow down," she said, suddenly less crisp, a little hoarse. "Don't choke. There's more."

"More?" Xiaoman showed half her face above the wok rim, a green leaf on her mouth corner.

"There's more in the pot. More in the freezer. More tomorrow." Granny said. "Eat slow. You won't go hungry."

Xiaoman set the wok down, bottom thudding on concrete. More than half the wok empty, only a layer of golden broth and a few broken noodles at the bottom. She licked her lips—that tongue normal to her, giant and pink to you, oily with broth.

"Granny Xiulan," she said, "this is the third good meal I've eaten today. First was the morning stew. Second was noon..." she paused, ear tips red, "noon I didn't eat. This is the third."

Granny turned away, back to her, blew her nose into the handkerchief. Her shoulders shook twice, then she stuffed the handkerchief back, turned, wearing her "no big deal" expression again.

"If it's good. Next pot—tomorrow morning. I'll make breakfast for you. Egg pancake. With noodles. With pork."

You stood beside, watching Xiaoman eat. Your face was still blank—eyes wide, mouth slightly open. Your fingers quietly at your pants seam.

Granny walked over and crouched before you. Her reading glasses squinted into two slits, looking at your face—the rarely-sun transparent white, the strangely bright eyes, the "medicine is food" tone.

"What about you?" She said softly, as if afraid to startle something. "Do you want to eat?"

You looked down at the soup. A layer of broth remained, golden, oil a thin film. A few broken noodles at the bottom, like white stones.

You didn't know what "hungry" felt like. Your stomach wasn't growling. You couldn't "want to eat."

But you thought of Mom. Mom off night shift, sleeping. The toast on the home tray. At noon you didn't complete the full routine—took medicine, didn't eat toast. Routine scrambled.

When routines scramble, you don't know what to do.

You looked at Granny. Her eyes were bright under the streetlight, like two worn glass beads polished for years. Her mouth corners drooped, but lightly, like a crumpled and smoothed paper.

"I..." you started, voice thin as a breaking thread. "I don't know."

"Don't know what?"

"Don't know... whether to eat." You said. "My stomach isn't growling. I'm not hungry. But..." you paused, finger at your pants seam moved. "But you said 'eat a bowl.' You said 'you eat too.' Is this... is this a rule?"

Granny froze. Her reading glasses blinked; she gripped the spatula tighter.

"Not a rule." She said, voice softer than before. "It's... it's that I want you to eat. You didn't eat at noon. You only took a pill. Medicine isn't food."

"But Mom said," you said, tone like reciting. "Take medicine and you won't get sick. Medicine is important. More important than food."

Granny's hand reached over. Her hand was rough, knuckles large, veins bulging on the back like an old tree's roots. She gently touched your hand—not gripping, just touching. Fingertip touching your hand back, cool, with water from washing noodles.

"Medicine is important," she said. "Food is too. You didn't eat at noon. It's evening now. You should be hungry."

You looked down at your hand. Granny's fingers still rested on your hand back, cool, wet. Your finger at your pants seam moved, but you didn't pull away.

"I'm not hungry." You said. "My stomach isn't growling."

"But your face is very white." Granny said. "Your lips are dry. Your eyes are bright but strangely bright. Like..." she paused, searching for a precise word, "like a dying-battery lamp."

You tilted your head. You didn't know what "dying-battery lamp" meant. You only knew: Granny wanted you to eat. Granny touched your hand. Granny's eyes were bright under the streetlight, like two worn glass beads polished for years.

You thought of Xiaoman. Xiaoman had touched your hand too. With her finger, gently, like a butterfly landing on a petal. Xiaoman said "you're really really small. But I like you very much."

You liked being touched. When touched, you knew you existed.

"Okay." You said.

You walked over to the iron wok. A layer of broth remained, golden, oil a thin film. You tiptoed to look in. A few broken noodles at the bottom, white, transparent, like white fish sinking.

You reached, pinched a broken noodle. The noodle was cool on your fingertip, slippery, oily with broth. You put it in your mouth.

No taste.

Not "bland," but "none." Your tongue was a cloth numbed by medication, unable to feel sweet, salty, sour, spicy. You chewed twice; the noodle broke into smaller fragments in your mouth, then you swallowed.

"Good?" Granny asked.

You thought. You didn't know what "good" meant. Your routine didn't have "good." Only "eat" and "don't eat."

"I ate." You said, tone like reporting your ID. "Noodle. One piece. Cool. Slippery."

Granny's reading glasses squinted tighter. Her mouth corners drooped more, like a paper crumpled many times.

"...eat more." She said. "There's more in the pot. Hot."

You pinched another. This time you deliberately scooped from the soup surface; the noodle was warm, carrying the soup's temperature. You put it in your mouth, chewed twice, swallowed.

Still no taste.

But you knew: Granny wanted you to eat. Xiaoman was watching. The old man stood beside, still carrying half a pig, face flushed red, but his eyes on you.

You ate a third. Fourth. Fifth.

Each no taste. But you counted. One, two, three, four, five. Like counting pills. Like counting picture-book pages. Like counting everything you could count.

On the seventh, you stopped.

"Enough." You said. "Seven pieces. More than pills. More than toast. Enough."

Granny looked at you. Her reading glasses were bright under the streetlight, like two worn glass beads polished for years. Her mouth corners drooped, but lightly.

"...okay." She said. "Seven pieces. Enough."

She stood, tapped the iron wok rim with the spatula—clang, clear and loud.

"Alright alright! Everyone stop standing! The young lady is full—! The young girl ate too—! Tomorrow morning—egg pancake—!"

She turned and directed the old man to pack up. The old man shouldered the half pig, huffing toward the stairwell. Granny carried the plastic bag, following. Two steps, she looked back at you.

"What about you? Going home?"

You thought. Home routine: evening, read the picture book, put it in the drawer, lights out, sleep. But it wasn't evening. Sun just set, sky still dark red, streetlights just on. The routine's timing was wrong.

"I..." you said. "I want to stay a little longer."

Granny's reading glasses blinked. She looked at you—the child standing by the iron wok, fingers oily with broth, face still blank. Then she nodded.

"Stay. Not too late. Your mom will wake up looking for you."

She walked off. The old man's huffing faded, finally disappearing into the stairwell.

The parking lot grew quiet.

Xiaoman drank the last of the broth. She set the wok down, bottom thudding on concrete. Then she lowered her head to look at you.

You still stood by the iron wok, fingers oily with broth, glinting under the streetlight. Your face had no expression—not happy, not sad, just the "task completed" blankness.

"Miaowu..." her breath fell from above, light as a feather. "Did you... did you eat?"

"Ate." You said. "Seven pieces. Noodles. Cool. Slippery."

"Good?"

You tilted your head. You didn't know what "good" meant.

"I ate." You said. "It's a rule. Granny said 'you eat too.' I ate. Seven pieces. Enough."

Xiaoman froze.

Her pupils trembled slightly under the streetlight. Like someone tossing a very small stone into those two amber moons. She looked at you—the child standing by the iron wok, fingers oily, face still blank.

Then she understood.

You couldn't "be hungry." Couldn't "taste good." Couldn't "want to eat." You ate because others said "eat." You counted because counting let you know "enough." Your world had no "taste," only "routine." Routine correct, you felt at peace. Routine scrambled, you didn't know what to do.

Xiaoman's eyes reddened. But this time no tears fell. She just raised both hands from her knee, held them before her, palms up, like two giant pink-white shells.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"Come here."

You walked over. Step by step, to Xiaoman's thumb. You leaned against the thumb base—that thumb taller and thicker than you, pink-white skin forming a slight warm curve behind you.

Xiaoman's palms slowly closed. Not clapping, but cupping—like two giant pink-white shells, forming a warm semicircular dome above your head. Not touching you, just hovering. Streetlight leaked through her finger gaps, dim yellow lines falling on your hair, uniform, knees.

"Like this?" Her breath fell from above the dome, muffled.

You looked up. Her palms showed a warm translucent pink under the streetlight, shadows in the palm lines like river tributaries.

"Too big."

"Then..." the dome shrank a little.

"Still too big."

Shrank again. Now only a meter above your head; you could feel warmth from her palm.

"Like this?"

"Yeah." You nodded. "Like this. Just right."

Xiaoman's palm hovered a meter above your head. Not touching, just hovering. Streetlight leaked through finger gaps, dim yellow lines.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"You ate seven noodles."

"Yeah."

"You have no taste."

"Yeah."

"But you want me to not be hungry."

"Yeah."

"Why?"

You didn't answer immediately.

You looked down at your hands. Fingers on your knee, quiet. White plastic flakes from the basin rim still on your nails, now with a little broth oil. You opened and closed your fingers.

Then you looked up at Xiaoman. Streetlight from above dyed her face dim yellow. Her eyes were bright, dark pupils holding fine light, like two watermelon seeds soaked in starlight.

"Because," you said, tilting your head, "because you're Xiaoman. Full as in fullness. You said that yourself."

Xiaoman's lashes trembled.

"And," you added, "you said 'you have me in your eyes.' I have you in mine. Two people in each other's eyes have to eat together. It's..." you paused, "it's a rule."

"Not a rule." Xiaoman said, voice light but clear. "It's friendship."

You tilted your head. Your bangs blew back in the evening wind.

"Friendship?"

"Yeah." Xiaoman. "Friendship is... the person who makes me eat even when you have no taste."

You thought. Your second-grade brain turned.

"I don't have friends." You said. "Kindergarten kids don't play with me. They say I talk weird. Say I always repeat the same words. Say I don't smile."

You paused. Finger at your pants seam moved.

"But you don't say I'm weird. You said 'you have me in your eyes.' You said 'this is friendship.'"

You looked up at Xiaoman. Streetlight from above dyed her face dim yellow.

"Then I'm your friend."

Xiaoman's palm slowly descended. Not clapping, but covering—like a giant pink-white cloud, slowly descending from the sky, stopping ten centimeters above your head.

Her thumb reached over, gently touched the top of your head.

Not pressing. Just touching.

Warm. Soft. With living skin's elasticity.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"You're my friend."

"Yeah."

"I won't forget you."

"Yeah."

"Even if I go home. Even if I go back to the giant country. I won't forget you."

You didn't speak.

You just sat there, back against Xiaoman's thumb base, her thumb on your head, fingers quietly on your knee. Your stomach wasn't growling. You didn't know what hunger felt like. You ate seven noodles, no taste. But you knew: Xiaoman's stomach wasn't growling. Xiaoman wasn't crying. Xiaoman said "friendship."

That was enough.

Streetlights fully came on. Ivy leaves rustled in the evening wind. Distant evening bus beeps, soy-milk steam becoming white mist in the dark.

Xiaoman leaned against the wall, cross-legged, stomach quietly rising and falling. You sat at her feet, back against the wall, fingers oily with broth, nose tip red, fingers quiet.

Neither of you spoke.

But your shadows pressed together under the streetlight—the shadow of a giant light-blue mountain, and the shadow of a small black hamster. Ten centimeters between the two shadows, but stretched very long by the light, extending to the wall root, to the white salt crystals, to a very far place.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"See you tomorrow."

"See you tomorrow."

You stood, legs numb, stamped your foot. You turned toward the parking-lot exit.

At the wall corner, you looked back.

Xiaoman still sat there, both hands held before her, palms up, like two giant pink-white shells. Her eyes looked at you, amber, reflecting your image—small, standing at the wall corner, red nose, mouth slightly curved up.

You raised one hand, palm up, like a small pink shell.

Then you ran off.

Sneakers slapped the concrete, arms pumping, backpack smacking your back.

But this time, your legs didn't ache.

Your stomach still wasn't growling.

But your mouth—curved up a little.

Yours.

See you tomorrow.

She lowered her hands. Palms on her knees, pink-white, like two quiet mountains under the streetlight.

She smelled it. Rising from beneath—ammonia smell, warm, fermented, mixed with sea salt and iron. She looked down. The dark mark on the concrete had dried on the surface, a ring of white salt crystals at the edges. The peony quilt piled at her feet, lower half still wet, color dark as wet old newspaper.

Her ear tips reddened.

She shifted aside. Boom. The ground shook gently. She moved to dry concrete, farther from the mark. But the smell remained. Wind blew from the sea, stirring the air under the awning; the smell wrapped around her like a wet coat.

She hugged her knees. Chin on her knees. Her stomach was still digesting—noodles, eggs, pork, greens, slowly decomposing in her stomach. She felt the stomach's weight, heavy.

She yawned. Tears squeezed from her eye corners, not crying, but from the yawn.

She was sleepy.

But she didn't dare sleep. Not not-sleepy. Daring not.

She looked down at the mark. White salt crystals under the streetlight like a small rough snowfield. She shifted again, farther into the corner by the wall. But the parking lot was only so big; where could she move?

Her bladder was bloating.

Not the immediate-release kind, but the "already accumulating" kind. She drank two pots of soup this afternoon, plus lots of water. Her kidneys had been working, drop by drop, gathering into a warm expanding lake. She placed a hand on her belly; through the skirt she felt the pressure.

She clamped her legs.

Miaowu was gone. Granny was gone. Everyone was gone. Only her in the parking lot.

She didn't dare shout. Her voice would shatter glass. She didn't dare walk far; her steps would break the road. She didn't dare go to the beach—too far, in the dark she couldn't see the rails beneath her.

She could only wait. Wait for tomorrow. Wait for Miaowu to come.

Her eyelids drooped. Like small heavy weights hung on them. She propped them with her fingers, pushing her eyebrows up; they fell again.

She shifted. Side-lying. Boom. The ground shook gently. She pulled the peony quilt over herself. The lower half was still wet, cool against her legs; she shivered but didn't push it away.

She turned her face to the wall. She stared at the cracks in the concrete wall, and as she stared, the cracks became the giant country's road—the road from her home to the swing, with Mom's forget-me-nots along the side, blue, like her dress.

Her eyes closed.

Not fully, but half-open. Her breathing slowed. Her bladder still bloated, but sleep like a thick quilt muffled the signal.

She fell asleep.


Then, middle of the night.

She woke.

Not from needing to pee. From cold.

Her dress was wet. Not urine—the quilt was wet, the quilt against her dress, her dress against her skin, heat pulled away by wet fabric, like a layer of ice on her. Her back was chilled, goosebumps rising layer by layer on her calves, each ball-sized, glinting like small pearls under the streetlight.

She sneezed.

"Achoo—"

Not loud, just a soft sound to her. But the parking-lot concrete hummed, ivy leaves on the wall rustled, a few early-yellow leaves falling onto her hair.

She sat up. Boom. The ground shook.

She looked down at herself. The dress clung to her legs, dark-blue wet marks from knee to hem. She thought she'd wet—but touching the dress, it was cool, not warm. It was the quilt pressing the afternoon's wet part against her, held for hours, cooling.

She hadn't peed. At least not this time.

But she needed to.

The lake in her bladder had reached the warning line. The water and soup she'd drunk this afternoon, filtered for hours, all gathered here. A round hard bulge below her belly, like hiding a watermelon—a watermelon to her, about a balloon to normal people.

She stood. Boom. The ground shook.

She looked around. The parking lot was dark, streetlights dim, not reaching corners. Broken glass on the wall glinted cold in the dark. Distant cat calls, "me-ow," like someone imitating your name.

She didn't dare shout. Didn't dare walk. Didn't know where to go.

She looked toward the pool. Beyond the wall, the abandoned pool in the dark like a giant black mouth. The hose still dripped pitter-patter, the sound loud at night, like someone constantly turning on a leaky tap.

She walked over. Step by step, very slowly. Her soles crunched on the concrete, each step raising fine dust. She reached the pool edge and looked down.

The pool bottom was dry. Afternoon's water had drained, only a dark-green moss line on the wall, and a few brown dried water stains on the bottom. This was no longer the pool where she could stand and wash her skirt that morning. She'd taken several steps on the bottom today—those old tiles had cracked under her weight; several rows near the wall had broken. Tiles embedded in concrete had made extremely faint cracks when crushed; she hadn't noticed, focused on swishing her skirt.

She could make a decision.

She lifted one foot over the pool edge. Boom. The wall shook; a loose broken tile clattered off, falling to the dry bottom, chipping white fragments. She pulled her foot back, afraid to move.

She couldn't go back in. Last time she'd stood on the bottom and tiles were already cracking. If she went in again, more tiles would break, concrete would collapse, she'd sink in, legs stuck, unable to move. Then she'd be more afraid, then she'd cry, then her cry would shake the whole parking lot down.

Where could she go?

She couldn't go to the beach; her walking would wake people.

She crouched at the pool edge.

Not sitting—sitting would spread her skirt and get it dusty. Crouching, both feet on the concrete outside the pool, knees together, skirt hanging into the bottom like a light-blue waterfall falling into a dry canyon. Her hands braced on the pool edge, fingers spread, palm pads pressing the rough concrete rim, gray cement crumbs still under her nails from that afternoon—no, noon—digging the ground.

The pool bottom was dry.

She adjusted her posture. Knees closer, skirt hanging deeper into the bottom. Her bladder contracted like a slowly wrung sponge. Not full—the afternoon's two pots and water had released once in her noon dream. Now midnight, kidneys working for hours, gathering a new smaller lake.

Then, it began.

The moment liquid left her body, she felt the temperature: slightly higher than her skin, with kidneys' filtered warmth, like a small river of her own rising from inside. It flowed between her parted legs, no fabric blocking, directly into air, then—

Plop.

Hit the pool bottom.

The sound was louder than she expected. The bottom was dry concrete, non-absorbent; liquid hitting the ground made a crisp echoing plop, like someone breaking a bowl in an empty room. Then second drop, third, sounds blending into a low continuous shush, like someone spreading sand far away, or first heavy raindrops hitting hot asphalt.

She held her breath.

Liquid flowed on the bottom. Not absorbed, but spreading—the bottom was flat, slope near zero, liquid like a thin transparent oil on the gray-white concrete spreading in all directions. It sought every tiny depression, crack, drain inlet, but most areas were flat, hard, refusing to receive.

She looked down.

Liquid drew irregular patterns on the bottom—radiating from directly beneath her, like a fallen tree's roots, branching, thinning, finally disappearing into concrete texture. Some spots pooled slightly deeper, glinting pale amber under the streetlight; some were just wet marks, like a giant wet rag wiping the ground, smear marks remaining but water seeping into invisible pores.

The smell rose.

Not strong. Thin, continuous, like an invisible film. Midsummer night, air stagnant, temperature still in the high twenties, like a thick warm quilt over the city. Urea decomposed quickly in heat, ammonia molecules evaporating from the pool bottom, accumulating in the pool's depression, forming a faint but undeniable cloud.

She smelled it. Not "stinky," but familiar—like home's toilet, like bathroom tile gaps, like her own private smell after waking up each morning before Mom complained "you forgot to flush again." But here, in this abandoned dry pool that wasn't hers, this familiarity became an invasion, a marking, an indelible proof of her existence in this world.

Her nose wrinkled. Not disgust, but instinct. She thought of Miaowu.

Miaowu on the lawn, on the branch, by the pool, smelled this smell—or a stronger, more complex version mixed with earth and grass sap. Miaowu didn't cover her nose. Miaowu didn't turn away. Miaowu just sat, holding the red basin, fingers picking at the rim, motionless.

But Miaowu wasn't here. Miaowu was home, sleeping, beside Mom, in the routine of picture books and pills and toast.

She kept releasing. Liquid pooled into a thin trembling lake—not a "lake," but a water film, a wetland, an existence forcibly spread out and refused absorption. It flowed toward the wall, but the wall was vertical; liquid hit it like a wave hitting a cliff, rebounding, pooling into a shallow curved river at the bottom-wall seam, then slowly seeping into the narrow dust-filled drain.

The drain was partially blocked. Years of leaves, broken glass, dead insects, wind-blown plastic bags, forming a thick black spongy sediment on the bottom. Liquid seeped into these gaps, absorbed by dust, blocked by leaves, forming small irregular wet patches. Some liquid seeped down through cracks into the underground pipe system—but pipes were old, rusted, partially collapsed, flow slow, like an old man swallowing.

More liquid stayed on the bottom. Under the streetlight, the wet area kept expanding, from beneath her to the wall, rebounding from wall to center, forming an uneven glistening film. The film surface had tiny ripples stirred by night wind, like a captive ocean shrunk a thousand times.

The smell was stronger. Not "strong," but persistent. Like an invisible paint brushed into every corner of the pool. Cracks in the bottom, tile gaps in the wall, drain sediment, even dust particles in the air had adsorbed the smell. Midsummer heat accelerated evaporation, but evaporation wasn't "disappearing" but spreading—smell molecules escaping from the liquid surface, mixing with night wind, forming a faint but omnipresent cloud above the pool.

She stood. Boom. Knees straightening from crouch, thigh muscles aching in protest. Her shadow on the bottom quickly shrank, from giant deformed profile to a thin swaying pillar. Liquid on the bottom rippled, circles expanding, hitting the wall, breaking into smaller ripples, slowly calming.

But liquid didn't calm. It still flowed, seeking lower exits, still evaporating, still releasing smell. The bottom's color changed—from gray-white to dark gray, from dark gray to near black, like a giant water-wet sponge. But sponges don't absorb; water only flowed on the surface, stalled in cracks, queuing before drains.

She turned and walked to the awning.

Each step raised a faint wind, stirring the dense warm cloud above the pool. The smell followed her, like a wet invisible coat on her shoulders. She walked back under the awning and sat. The concrete was dry—the spot she'd sat before, but not fully dry, noon's "map" had evaporated, leaving a faint white salt ring, like earth's scab.

She pulled the peony quilt over her legs. The lower half was still damp from noon, cool against her skin, making her shiver. But she didn't push it away. She needed the quilt—not warmth, but covering. Even in this empty parking lot, even in this government-shutdown, unmanaged midnight, she needed something on her legs, something separating her body from the world.

She rested her chin on her knees, long hair falling like a black waterfall, half-covering her face. Her eyes half-open, looking toward the pool—that thin trembling glistening wetland on the bottom, lying quiet, like an irregular mirror left at the city's edge.

Her bladder was empty. The bloating below her belly was gone, replaced by a light, almost dizzy emptiness. Her stomach still digested—noodles, eggs, pork, greens, slowly decomposing in her stomach, making a low satisfied gurgle. Her eyelids drooped, like small heavy weights hung on them.

But she didn't dare sleep. Not not-sleepy. Daring not.

She was afraid. Afraid that if she slept, she'd pee again. Afraid peeing would dirty things again. Afraid dirtying things, tomorrow when Miaowu came, she'd smell it. Afraid Miaowu smelling it would stop talking. Afraid Miaowu not talking meant not liking her.

She knew this "fear" was unreasonable. Miaowu had said "I like the natural you." Miaowu on the lawn, on the branch, by the pool, hadn't left. Miaowu's eyes held no fear, only the pure, second-grader calm of full attention on "right now."

But "knowing" and "believing" were two things.

Her body knew Miaowu wouldn't leave, but her heart was still practicing believing. Like her new tooth—the growing front tooth, gum itchy, she always wanted to push it with her tongue, but Mom said pushing it grows crooked. Believing was also a muscle, needing practice, time, nights of not running away.

She looked at the pool. The wetland on the bottom slowly darkened under the streetlight—liquid evaporating, the thin glistening film on the surface shrinking like sun-dried soap. But the smell didn't darken. It was still there, faint, continuous, like an invisible but undeniable background noise.

Her eyelids drooped further. She propped them with her hand, pushing her eyebrows up; they fell again. She shifted, side-lying, boom, the ground shaking gently. She pulled the peony quilt up to her shoulders. The lower half was wet, but the upper half was dry, smelling old and sun-baked, like Granny's closet.

She turned her face to the wall. Cracks in the wall, weeds in the cracks, leaves swaying in the night wind. She stared at the cracks, and as she stared, they became the giant country's road—the road from her home to the swing, with Mom's forget-me-nots along the side, blue, like her dress.

Her eyes closed.

Not fully, but half-open. Her breathing slowed, deepened. Her fingers unconsciously twisted the quilt edge, making small folds.

Her bladder kept working in sleep. Kidneys filtered blood, drop by drop, gathering a new warm expanding lake. But this time, the lake was very small—just drops, just drops. Her body in sleep tightened the sphincter, like a gently closed door, keeping liquid outside.

She fell asleep.

Not sinking, but drifting. Like a leaf on water, pushed gently by waves. No dreams, or dreams too fragmented to catch—she smelled a scent, Mom's strawberry toast, but only for a second, then dispersed; she heard a sound, Xiao Gu laughing on the swing, but only one call, then cut off; she felt a touch, Miaowu's fingertip on her head, warm, soft, but only for an instant, then disappeared.

These fragments didn't form a story. They just flashed, like someone rapidly switching a light in a dark room, each flash a different image, no logic, no order, no meaning.

Then, nothing. Dark. Quiet. Only breathing, only heartbeat, only the distant low hum of blood flowing in vessels.

Her body slowly relaxed in this "nothing."

Not all relaxing. Piece by piece, like a door moved by wind, the bolt slowly sliding out of its slot in the vibration. Her pelvic floor—that door she'd guarded tightly all day—in the darkness of sleep, in the blankness of "nothing," couldn't find a reason to guard.

No dream told her "this is home's toilet." No hallucination gave her permission. Simply, mechanically, without consciousness—she relaxed.

Liquid flowed out.

Not a jet, not a dam break, but seepage. Like a roof crack slowly leaking in rain, like old pipes quietly dripping inside walls. A stream, then another, warm, continuous, soundlessly sliding from her body, flowing between her parted legs, into the concrete under the awning.

She didn't feel the start.

She only felt warmth—that warmth rising from inside, slightly hotter than her skin, like someone laying a low-set electric blanket under her, just enough to keep her not-cold on a midsummer night. Her subconscious classified this temperature as "safe," as "can keep sleeping," as "no need to wake."

Liquid kept flowing. The bladder in sleep contracted slowly and continuously, like a sponge slowly squeezed by gravity, each drop leaving without pressure-release pleasure, just plain, mechanical, almost unfelt loss. Her breathing didn't change, heartbeat didn't change, lashes didn't flutter.

She just slept.

Liquid pooled on the concrete. Spreading from beneath her to all sides, no barrier—the concrete flat, slope near zero, liquid like a thin transparent oil spreading in all directions on gray-white ground. It overflowed the peony quilt corner; fabric soaked through, color from bright peony red to dark heavy brown-red. It kept spreading, over the white salt ring she'd left at noon, dissolving the salt, making a larger paler more irregular wet mark.

The smell rose.

Strong. Much stronger than the pool time. Because here it was open, exposed, directly contacting midsummer heat. Urea quickly decomposed on the concrete surface, ammonia molecules escaping from the liquid surface, mixing with stagnant night wind, forming a dense almost-visible warm cloud under the awning.

Her toes moved slightly in the liquid.

Not conscious. Reflex. Warm liquid flowing between toe gaps, bringing a strange infantile sensation. Her toes spread and closed, leaving shallow wet marks on the concrete. Her heels were wet too, skin slightly whitened.

She didn't wake.

Her body completed the last wave of release in sleep. The moment her bladder emptied, her breathing deepened slightly—not a sigh, not satisfaction, just the diaphragm instinctively expanding as abdominal pressure lightened. Her lips parted, exhaling warm air carrying faint mushroom glass-noodle soup flavor from her stomach. Her head tilted, cheek against the wall, mouth corner slightly moist—not saliva, but vapor from her breath condensing on her lips.

She kept sleeping.

The peony quilt piled by her hand, lower half in water, corner soaked, color dark as wet old newspaper. Her white tank top hem hung at her thigh roots, splashed wet in a small patch, clinging to skin, cool—but she slept, didn't feel the cool. The concrete beneath her had become a large dark wetland, area expanding, slowly evaporating in night wind, releasing a strong warm undeniable ammonia smell.

Far away, the thin water film in the pool remained. Two wetlands, one big one small—the awning one big, the pool one small—across the wall and awning, glinting under the streetlight.

But she didn't know. She slept. Dreamless sleep. No "home toilet" hallucination, no "Mom calling her to wake" comfort, no "Xiao Gu laughing outside the door" warmth. Pure dark blank sleep, and her body completing release alone while she knew nothing.

Night wind blew from the sea, carrying salt and distant soy-milk steam, stirring ivy leaves on the wall, lifting the awning's tarpaulin edge, dispersing—but couldn't disperse—the dense warm cloud belonging to her under the awning.

She slept. Didn't know. Didn't dream. Didn't wake.

Until dawn.
