<a id="Chapter20"></a>
## Chapter 20: First Night · Discovery

When you turned the doorknob, the living-room clock showed 20:47.

You dropped the key into the glass bowl on the shoe cabinet—ding. Routine complete. But your sneakers were wet, pant legs dripping, hair smelling of sea salt and... a strong warm fermented smell. Like banana peels left in a summer trash can all day, but you didn't think it stank. You thought it was the "parking lot" smell, "Xiaoman's" smell.

Mom walked out from the kitchen. She wore her nurse uniform, just off night shift, hair still pinned under her cap. She held a glass of water; when she saw you, the glass stopped mid-air.

"Miaowu."

You bent to take off shoes. Left shoe. Toe out. Right shoe. Toe out. But the shoes were wet, squelching inside. You stopped, because you didn't know if wet shoes belonged in the shoe cabinet.

Mom walked over. Her nurse shoes made soft sounds on the floor. She crouched, eyes level with you. Her finger touched your bangs—wet, with a green seaweed-like thing stuck.

"Where did you go?"

"Parking lot. Beach. Farmers' market." You said. You arranged the routine in order: wake, go to the beach, meet Xiaoman, lawn, pool, wall, railway, market, Granny Xiulan, stew, dinner, home. But the routine was too long, like a knotted rope.

"With whom?"

"Xiaoman."

"Who is Xiaoman?"

"Xiaoman. Full as in fullness." You said. "She's eighteen meters tall. She wears a light-blue dress. She's hungry."

Mom's eyes widened. The widening of a nurse finding new symptoms. Her pupils contracted slightly, like checking a pupillary light reflex.

"Eighteen meters." She repeated.

"Yeah. She's missing a front tooth. She cried. She doesn't want to break houses."

You raised your hand, index finger and thumb spread, gesturing a two-meter gap—the "bread size" Xiaoman had gestured. You wanted Mom to understand. But Mom grabbed your wrist. Her fingers were cool, callus on the pads.

"Miaowu," her voice was soft but with something hard underneath, "look at Mom. Is someone... making you buy things? Did someone touch you?"

You looked at her. You didn't understand. Many people touched you. Granny's hands rough, the high school student's fingers cool, Xiaoman's fingertips warm and soft. You wanted to say "Xiaoman touched my head," but Mom's eyes stopped you.

"No bad people." You said. That was a kindergarten sentence.

Mom's breathing quickened. She stood, walked into the living room, picked up your smartwatch. Screen still lit. Green numbers: $736.03.

"You spent money."

"Xiaoman was hungry." You said. "Bought rice. Bought eggs. Bought noodles. Granny Xiulan stewed meat."

Mom's finger swiped the screen. Transaction records flipped page by page—this morning, farmers' market. One, two, three, four. Each only amount and merchant: East Sea Rice, Old Zhou Vegetable Wholesale, Ah Fen Eggs—significant amounts. She quickly added them up—about nine hundred-plus. The watch didn't show what was bought, only where and how much. But what you said—rice, eggs, noodles, meat—matched the merchants.

"Two hundred-plus jin of rice?" Her voice trembled. "For whom?"

"Xiaoman."

Mom turned away from you. Her shoulders tensed. She picked up her phone, pressed a button. Ring—ring—

"Hello, Dr. Zhang? This is Miaowu's mom... yes, she's developed something new. Spending impulse, large expenditures, and..." her voice was low, "a detailed delusional narrative. An adult friend. Eighteen meters tall. Yes, I know how it sounds... but never before. No, medication hasn't stopped. Yes, I'll keep a record. See you Monday."

She hung up, walked over. Her face wore "Mom" expression again, but something hard underneath.

"Go shower." She said. "Take off your clothes. All of them."

You walked into the bathroom. Mom followed, turned on the shower. Water rushed down, very hot. You frowned but didn't dodge. You were used to discomfort in routines. She peeled your uniform off, balled it, stuffed it in a black trash bag. Your red basin was still in your backpack; she didn't find it.

She soaped you. Hard. Your arms reddened from scrubbing. She washed your hair, fingers in your scalp as if washing away a smell. You closed your eyes. You smelled soap, lemon, not Xiaoman's milk smell.

"Mom." You said.

"Yeah."

"Xiaoman isn't a delusion."

Mom's hand paused. Then kept scrubbing. "We'll talk after."

But you didn't speak again. Because you didn't know what "after" meant. You knew what "delusion" meant—the doctor said, things in your head that aren't real. But Xiaoman was real. Xiaoman's tears hit the ground making basin-sized craters. Xiaoman's fingers were wider than your whole body.

After showering, you put on pajamas. Mom took your wet uniform and sneakers, set them outside the door. She poured you a glass of water, set it on the nightstand. Then she took your smartwatch, put it in her bedroom's drawer—the bottom one, locked.

"You're not going out for three days." She said. "Mom took leave. Mom will stay home with you."

You lay in bed. Pillow propped against the headboard. You waited for Mom to pick up the picture book from the nightstand and put it in the drawer. But she didn't. She sat on the bed, hand on your forehead. Her hand was cool.

"Sleep." She said.

You closed your eyes. But you didn't sleep. Your routines circled in your body: picture book not in the drawer. Wet shoes not handled. Pillbox not in the far-left counter corner. These unfinished things like three thorns in your spine.

You heard Mom walking in the living room. Drawer opening, paper and pen rustling. She was making a record. You knew that paper—ABC behavior record. Antecedent: mother on night shift, unsupervised. Behavior: unsupervised departure, large spending, unknown environment. Consequence: sensory stimulation (wet clothes, strange smell), attention (mentions "adult friend").

At 2 AM, you got out of bed. Barefoot on the floor. You walked to the bedroom door, pushed a crack open. Mom slept on the couch, still holding the record sheet. Her brow was furrowed.

You turned back to the bed, pulled the red basin from your backpack. Plastic, warm, carrying your body heat. You placed it beside your pillow. Then lay down, hand reaching into the basin. Fingers touched the bottom. Empty. But you felt better.

You counted your breathing. One, two, three, four. On the seventh, you fell asleep.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter21"></a>
## Chapter 21: The Second Day · Structure

7 AM, Mom didn't wake you. You woke yourself. Your heartbeat knocked in your chest: thump-thump, thump-thump.

You sat up, looked at the nightstand. The picture book was still there, cover up, Little Bear Bubu holding the red bucket. Not in the drawer.

You got out of bed, picked up the picture book, stuffed it in the drawer. Pushed it shut. Click. Half the routine complete.

You walked into the kitchen. The tray was still on the counter. Yellow mug, handle right. White pillbox, three compartments. Whole-wheat toast, individually wrapped. But next to the pillbox was a new piece of paper. You picked it up. It had a row of boxes drawn on it, each with a time and activity.

7:00-7:30 Wake, wash, take medicine
7:30-8:00 Breakfast (toast + milk)
8:00-9:00 Quiet time (must not leave seat)
9:00-10:00 Social story reading
10:00-10:30 Sensory integration (playdough)
...

This was a visual schedule. You'd seen it at kindergarten. You flipped it over; the back was blank. You put it back, pillbox on top.

You poured out two tablets, dry swallow. Then you picked up the toast. You didn't eat it. You put it back in the tray, pushed it to the far end of the counter.

Mom walked into the kitchen. She wore yesterday's pajamas, the dark circles under her eyes deeper. She held a pen, writing on another piece of paper.

"Why aren't you eating?"

"Not hungry." You said.

"Miaowu." She walked over, crouched, eyes level with yours. Her finger touched your wrist. "You didn't eat at noon yesterday either. What about dinner? What did you eat at night?"

"Seven glass noodles. Cold. Slippery."

Mom's pen stopped. She wrote something on the paper. You tilted your head, wanting to see. She folded it.

"Dad's home today." She said.

You then noticed a pair of leather shoes in the entryway. Dad walked out from the bedroom. He wore a plaid shirt, sleeves rolled to the elbows. He looked at you with eyes different from Mom's—Mom's eyes had worry, Dad's had something you couldn't read, like looking at a report he couldn't understand.

"Miaowu." He said. "Come here."

You walked over. He brought you to the sofa, took a folder from his bag. The folder said "Social Story."

"Let's read a story." He said.

He opened the first page. It had a picture of Little Bear Bubu, but the story wasn't from your picture book. This was a new story.

"Little Bear Bubu Goes to the Store."

"Little Bear Bubu has many coins. He wants to buy something. He walks into the store. He sees candy. He wants to buy it. But Bubu should ask Mom first. If Mom says yes, he can buy. If Mom says no, he can't buy. Bubu puts the coins back in his pocket. Good Bubu."

Dad's voice was slow, loud, like reciting lines. He looked at you, waiting for a reaction.

You looked at the page. Bubu put the coins back. But Xiaoman was hungry. Xiaoman's stomach was growling. Xiaoman's tears were basketball-sized.

"Xiaoman is hungry." You said.

Dad and Mom exchanged a look. Dad turned the page.

"Bubu has many friends. Friends are small. Friends are Bubu's size. Big friends aren't real. Big friends are imaginary. Bubu knows big friends are only in stories. Bubu doesn't look at big friends. Good Bubu."

Your finger moved on your knee. You wanted to pick at something. But the red basin was in the bedroom. You picked at your pajama hem. The fabric was thin; your nails sank in, making very light hissing sounds.

"Miaowu." Dad's voice came from far away. "The 'Xiaoman' you saw yesterday was imaginary. Just like Bubu's big friend. Do you understand?"

You didn't know. You didn't know how to know. You'd seen Xiaoman's eyes. Amber, with two yous inside. One left, one right. Imaginary eyes don't have reflections.

"I saw." You said.

"Saw what?"

"Xiaoman's eyes have me. Two of me."

Dad's hand tightened on the folder. The paper edge crumpled. He stood, walked to the window, double-checked the window lock. Then he took something from his tool bag and installed it on the window frame. It was a metal plate, with a lock.

"Don't open the window these two days." He said. "It's windy."

You went back to your room. The visual schedule was taped to the door. You stood in the doorway, looking at the boxes. 8:00-9:00 Quiet time. You walked to the desk, sat down. Chair hard. Back straight, hands on knees. You didn't move.

You heard sounds from the living room. Dad was on the phone, voice low: "...yes, behavioral regression... fixed delusional object emerged... need to strengthen structured environment... yes, we're already doing it..."

Mom was in the kitchen. Washing things. Hard. Water rushing.

You sat in the chair, back to the door. Your fingers were quiet on your knees. But your back itched. Like ants crawling. You wanted to rub against the wall. But the schedule said "quiet time." You didn't move.

You counted your heartbeat. One, two, three, four... at the forty-seventh, you stood. You walked to the wall, leaned back against it, and rubbed. Up, down. Up, down. Your shoulder blades scraped the wallpaper, making shush-shush sounds. The pressure made you feel better. Like someone pressing your back.

The door opened. Mom stood in the doorway. She looked at you, eyes changed.

"Miaowu, what are you doing?"

"My back itches."

She walked over. Her hand landed on your back—not rubbing, but kneading. From neck to waist, circles. Her fingers were cool, thin callus on the pads. You closed your eyes. Your back stopped itching.

"Sit." She said.

You sat. She sat beside you, holding the social story. But she didn't open it. She just looked at you.

"Tell Mom," her voice was soft, like sharing a secret, "did you... were you scared yesterday?"

You thought. Were you scared yesterday? When you sat on Xiaoman's collarbone, the wind was strong. You weren't scared. When you sat on the branch, your legs went numb. You weren't scared. When you were at the farmers' market, rice sacks were tall. You weren't scared.

"No." You said.

Mom's eyes reddened. Not crying, but post-all-nighter red. She reached over, brushed the stray hairs from your forehead.

"Then tell Mom, why did you go?"

"Xiaoman was hungry." You said. This was the simplest sentence. You pulled it out like a Band-Aid.

Mom's tears fell. Not strings, but one, hitting the back of your hand. Warm.

"Miaowu," her voice trembled, "if... if there really were bad people, pretending to be 'Xiaoman,' tricking you out, tricking you into spending money... tell Mom. Mom isn't angry. Mom just wants to protect you."

You looked at her. You thought of Xiaoman's finger. That index finger, wider and thicker than you, pink, nails round. When she touched your head, you felt you existed. When Mom touched your head, you also felt you existed. But they weren't the same person.

"Xiaoman isn't bad." You said.

Mom turned her face away. Her shoulders shook. You sat there, fingers quiet on your knees. You didn't know which sentence to play. You remembered Xiaoman said: "You succeeded. You said your own words."

You opened your mouth. You wanted to say: "Xiaoman is an eighteen-meter-tall girl. She wears a light-blue dress. She was hungry. I bought rice. She was full. She touched my head. She said I'm her friend."

But these words were too long. They fought in your throat, couldn't find an exit order.

Finally you said: "I'm hungry."

It wasn't true. Your stomach wasn't growling. But Mom said "medicine isn't food." Granny Xiulan said "medicine is important, food is too." You wanted to try—if you said "hungry," would Mom's eyes be less red.

Mom turned her head. Her face had water streaks. She looked at you, eyes wide.

"You're hungry?"

"Yeah."

She stood, walked into the kitchen. You heard the fridge open, microwave humming. After five minutes, she carried a plate in. On the plate were seven glass noodles. Cold. Slippery. Drizzled with a little soy sauce.

"Granny Xiulan dropped by this afternoon." Mom said. "She said you ate seven yesterday. She was afraid you'd be hungry."

You looked at the seven glass noodles. They coiled together like seven white little snakes. You picked up chopsticks, picked up one. Put it in your mouth. No taste. But you chewed. Swallowed.

One, two, three, four, five, six, seven.

You counted. Seven. Enough.

Mom looked at you. Her expression was strange. Not happy, not sad. That complex expression you couldn't read.

"Is it good?" She asked.

You thought of Xiaoman. Xiaoman said: "Is it good?" You'd said: "I ate. Seven. Glass noodles. Cold. Slippery." Xiaoman's eyes reddened. But she didn't cry.

Now Mom asked too. You didn't know the answer. But you remembered Xiaoman then held both hands before her, palms up. She said: "Friendship is... the person who makes me eat even when you have no taste."

You put down the chopsticks. You looked at Mom.

"Mom."

"Hm?"

"You have me in your eyes."

Mom's chopsticks slipped from her hand, hitting the plate with a soft ding.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter22"></a>
## Chapter 22: The Third Day · Medicalization

6 AM, you woke. Not from an alarm. From a sound. Hum—hum—very low, continuous. You sat up in bed, listening. Not Xiaoman's stomach. The fridge compressor.

You got out of bed, walked to the door. It wasn't locked. You pushed it open, walked into the living room.

Dad sat on the sofa, several papers spread before him. Mom sat beside him, holding a pen. They spoke in low voices. You stopped at the hallway corner, didn't go over. You listened.

"...Dr. Zhang says add a behavioral therapy session Monday first. If the fixed delusion persists, consider adjusting the risperidone dose." Dad said.

"She never had delusions before." Mom said. "Never. She was just... just rigid. Just wouldn't look at me. Just repeated speech. She never... never fabricated a whole person."

"That's not fabrication." Dad's voice was hard. "That's a symptom. Fantasy play associated with ASD, or worse... needs to be ruled out."

"Rule out what?"

"You know." Dad paused. "Childhood schizophrenia. Or... dissociative hallucination after inducement."

Mom's hand covered her mouth. Her shoulders shook.

"That smell on her," Dad continued, "I looked it up. Ammonia. Urea decomposition. She might have fallen in sewage, or... or touched excrement. She was soaked. She was out alone for four hours. Four hours, what could she encounter?"

"Stop it." Mom's voice trembled.

"We have to face it. She spent over two hundred dollars, bought two hundred jin of rice, sixty eggs. She mentioned an 'eighteen-meter-tall girl.' She mentioned 'basketball-sized tears.' She mentioned 'sitting on a collarbone.' This isn't real, do you understand? This isn't real."

You stood at the corner. Your back against the wall. The wall was cool. You slowly slid down, sat on the floor. Your pajama pants leg was rolled up, knees exposed. You wrapped your arms around your knees.

You knew what "real" meant. Real is what you can touch. Real is what you can smell. Real is what you can sit on. Xiaoman was real. But Dad's words were like thick cotton, muffling your knowing.

You stood, walked back to the bedroom. You pulled the red basin from your backpack. You placed it on the floor. Then lay down, put the basin over your face.

Plastic smell. Faint, carrying the warmth of your breath. You couldn't smell ammonia anymore. You couldn't smell lemon soap anymore. You could only smell yourself. You breathed in the basin, one, two, three, four. Your breath bounced off the plastic walls, becoming a soft hum.

You thought of Xiaoman. You remembered when she placed you on her head, you said "hold my hair tight." You remembered when she placed you on her collarbone, her pulse beating at your thigh. You remembered she said: "Miaowu. You're really really small. But I like you very much."

You took off the basin. You sat up. You took colored pencils from the drawer. Red, blue, yellow. On a white paper you drew. You drew a very large girl, light-blue dress, long black hair, missing a front tooth. Her palm was open, in her palm a small girl. You wrote beside her: Xiaoman. Full as in fullness.

Then below you wrote: Miaowu. Not a little hamster.

You folded the paper, stuffed it in the basin.

The door opened. Dad stood in the doorway. He held a piece of paper, not a social story. A checklist.

"Miaowu," he said, "come here. Dad has some questions."

You walked over. He brought you to the sofa. He crouched, eyes level with yours. His gaze was serious, like running an experiment.

"Tell Dad, did 'Xiaoman' let you touch her?"

You thought. Did Xiaoman let you touch her? She let you sit on her collarbone. She let you sit on her head. She let you sit in her palm. Did these count as "touch"?

"I sat." You said.

"Sat?" Dad's brow furrowed. "Sat where?"

"Collarbone. Head. Palm."

Dad and Mom exchanged a look. Mom's hand covered her mouth.

"She... she let you sit on her?" Mom's voice trembled.

"Yeah. Her palm is pink. Warm. Has a pulse."

Dad stood, paced two steps in the living room. His fingers tapped the phone quickly. You watched from beside. His lips moved but no sound came out. Then he put down the phone.

"Monday," he said, "we're going for a full physical exam. Including... including an intimate area check."

You tilted your head. You didn't know what "intimate area" meant. But you knew exams. The doctor has you open your mouth, say "ah—." The doctor taps your knee. The doctor has you walk a straight line. These are all exams.

"Okay." You said.

Dad looked at you. His expression softened. He walked over, placed his hand on the top of your head. His palm was bigger than Mom's, rougher, warmer.

"Dad is doing this for your own good." He said. "You know?"

"Know." You said. This was playback. You pulled the sentence from memory like playing a recording.

He withdrew his hand. He walked to the window, drew the curtains. Sunlight blocked, living room dimmed. You heard him lock the window. Click. Click. Click.

"No sun today." He said. "Stay in your room. Don't come out."

You went back to your room. You sat on the bed. You picked up the red basin. You placed it on your knee. Your fingers picked at the rim. Plastic made soft creaking sounds.

You counted your heartbeat. One, two, three, four. At the seventh, you heard Mom outside the door say: "She keeps picking at that basin. Should we take it away?"

Dad said: "Don't. It's a transitional object. Taking it away causes breakdown. Let her pick at it."

You picked harder. Creak. Creak. Creak.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter23"></a>
## Chapter 23: The Fourth Day · Loosening

When you woke, the room was gray. Not day, not night. The color between them.

You sat up. Your back hurt. You'd rubbed against the wall too long yesterday. Your calves still twitched, but slowly.

You looked at the nightstand. The picture book was in the drawer. You'd put it there yesterday. Routine complete.

You got out of bed, walked to the door. Pushed it open. No one in the living room. But a new paper was on the coffee table. You walked over, picked it up.

It said:
"Little Bear Bubu's Friends"
"Bubu has many friends. Friends are small. Friends are Bubu's size. Big friends aren't real. Big friends are imaginary. Bubu knows big friends are only in stories. Bubu doesn't look at big friends. Good Bubu."

You looked at the paper. Flipped it over. Blank back. Flipped back. Big friends aren't real.

But Xiaoman was real. Xiaoman's tears were basketball-sized. Xiaoman's fingers were wider than your whole body. Xiaoman said: "Miaowu. You're my friend."

You placed the paper on the coffee table. You walked to the window. Curtains drawn. You wanted to open them. But the window had locks. You touched the lock. Metal, cool.

You turned back, walked to your room. You pulled the red basin from under the bed. You placed it on the floor. You sat in the basin. The basin was small, your butt wedged in the bottom, legs sticking out. But you felt better. Like being surrounded by something.

You heard keys. The door opened. Mom walked in. She carried a plastic bag.

"Miaowu." She called you.

You stood from the basin. Walked to the living room. Mom took something from the plastic bag. It was a grilled sausage. The kind by the school gate, sizzling on an iron griddle, brushed with sweet sauce, sprinkled with cumin.

"Got this for you." She said. "You said you were hungry yesterday."

You looked at the grilled sausage. It was brown, glossy with oil, sprinkled with white sesame seeds. You used to like it. You used to count the sesame seeds on it, one, two, three, four...

You picked up the sausage. Bit a piece. Sweet sauce dissolved on your tongue. But you couldn't taste sweet. Your tongue was a cloth numbed by medication. You chewed twice, swallowed.

"Is it good?" Mom asked.

You thought. You didn't know. But you knew Mom wanted you to say "good."

"Good." You said. This was playback.

Mom's mouth corners lifted slightly. Weak. She walked over, crouched, eyes level with yours. She reached over, brushed stray hairs from your forehead.

"Miaowu," she said, "tomorrow morning, you can go out."

You looked at her. Your eyes widened.

"Really?"

"Really." She said. "But you have to promise Mom—no more spending money recklessly. No more running out alone. No more saying..." she paused, "no more saying strange things."

"Strange things?"

"Like..." Mom's eyes looked away, "like things about 'Xiaoman.' That big friend. She's fake. You know? She's fake."

You looked at her. You thought of Xiaoman's eyes. Amber, with two yous inside. One left, one right. You thought of Xiaoman's finger. That index finger, wider and thicker than you, pink, nails round. When she touched your head, you felt you existed.

"Xiaoman isn't fake." You said.

Mom's shoulders drooped. Her eyes reddened.

"Miaowu," her voice trembled, "Mom doesn't know what to do. Mom read many books. Books say your condition... needs structure, needs behavioral intervention, needs to rule out organic lesions. Mom did everything she should. But Mom doesn't know... doesn't know how to make you understand."

You looked at her. Her face had tear tracks. Dark circles under her eyes. Her fingers trembled.

You thought of Xiaoman. Xiaoman had cried too. Xiaoman's tears were basketball-sized, making basin-sized craters on the ground. When Xiaoman cried, you said: "This is the natural you, I like the natural you!" Then Xiaoman stopped crying.

You wanted to say this to Mom. But you knew this sentence was playback. It was the sentence Xiaoman told you not to play back.

You opened your mouth. You wanted to say your own words.

"Mom."

"Hm?"

"You have me in your eyes."

Mom's lashes fluttered.

"Only one me." You said. "Xiaoman's eyes have two me. One left, one right. So Xiaoman is real. Your eyes are real. Xiaoman's eyes are real too."

Mom didn't speak. She just looked at you. Her tears fell. One. Hitting the floor. Very soft sound. Not like Xiaoman's tears.

You reached out. Your fingers were short. You touched the back of Mom's hand. Her skin was cool, dry thin callus.

"Mom," you said, "I can go out tomorrow. I want to see Xiaoman. She might still be waiting for me. She might be hungry."

Mom's hand gripped back. She wrapped your hand in her palm. Her hand trembled.

"Miaowu," she said, "if... if you go out and can't find her? If she... if she isn't there?"

You thought. You didn't know. But you knew one thing.

"She's there." You said. "Because she promised. Ten custard buns. Held together."

Mom gripped your hand tighter. Her tears fell on the back of your hand. Warm.

"Okay." She said, voice soft as a feather. "Tomorrow morning. You go out."

You looked at her. Your mouth corners—lifted a little. Not playback. Yours.

"Okay." You said.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter24"></a>
## Chapter 24: The Fifth Morning · Release

6 AM, you woke.

You sat up. Your heartbeat knocked in your chest: thump-thump, thump-thump. But not scared heartbeat. The kind of... about-to-complete-the-routine heartbeat.

You got out of bed, opened the drawer. Took out the picture book. Placed it on the nightstand. Cover up, Little Bear Bubu holding the red bucket. You didn't need to put it in the drawer anymore. Because today there was a new routine.

You put on your school uniform. Mom washed it yesterday, folded on the chair. It was dry. Smelled of soap. You buttoned to the top. Then sneakers. Dry. Toes out.


============================================================
<a id="Part2"></a>
## Part 2: Days of Living Together

## Chapter 25: The Second Day · Waiting

Xiaoman woke to the sticky feeling on her body.

Not morning wind, not sea smell, not distant bus air brakes. The cold-wet of the dress clinging to her legs—like someone splashed a basin of cold water on her while she slept, then the cold water turned to cold glue, dried into stiff fabric taut against her skin, pulling tight with every movement.

She opened her eyes. Above was the awning's waterproof tarpaulin, dark green, with a hole at the edge leaking a thin, bright white line of light. Her head tilted to one side, cheek against the wall, a dried drool mark at her mouth corner, taut. It took her several seconds to remember where she was.

She slowly sat up. Boom. The ground shook gently. The peony summer quilt slid from her chest, piling on her knees. She looked down—the lower half of the quilt was the wrong color. Not peony red, but dark brown, like water-soaked old newspaper, edges fringed with a white, thin salt frost.

Then she smelled it.

Not the warm scent mixed with grass and earth from yesterday morning's lawn. Thicker, stuffier, the smell of something fermented after being covered all night. Like banana peels left in a summer trash can for a day, but sharper, more private, more like some irreversible proclamation evaporated from deep within her own body.

She lifted the quilt. The dress was wet. From the waist down, light-blue fabric turned dark blue, clinging to her legs, ice cold. She reached and touched it—fingers cold, no warmth. She touched the concrete beneath her—a dark, irregular wetland, edges half-dry, leaving a ring of white salt frost. Area bigger than yesterday noon's, spreading to the awning edge, absorbed at the concrete-grass boundary by dry soil, leaving a winding dark boundary line.

She froze, fingers still pressed on the concrete, fingertips slowly sinking into the cool wet mark.

Then she remembered.

She'd woken once in the middle of the night. Her bladder was bloated—too much soup yesterday, two pots, plus tap water. She remembered walking to the pool edge, crouching on the rim, skirt hanging into the dry bottom, then—plip, plip, plip—the sound of liquid hitting dry concrete echoing in the empty pool. She thought that was enough. She thought emptying it meant sleeping till dawn.

But the body wasn't something she controlled. After she fell asleep, her body slowly relaxed in that "nothing" blank. Not all at once, but bit by bit, like a door moved by wind, the bolt slowly sliding out of its slot in the vibration. No dream told her "this is home's toilet," no hallucination gave her permission. Simply, mechanically, outside consciousness, she relaxed. Liquid leaked out—not a jet, not a dam break, but seepage, like a roof crack slowly leaking in rain. One stream, then another, warm, continuous, soundlessly sliding from her body, flowing between her parted legs, into the concrete under the awning. She felt no start in her sleep. She only felt warmth—that warmth rising from inside, slightly hotter than her skin; her subconscious classified that temperature as "safe," as "can keep sleeping," as "no need to wake."

Now she was awake. The warmth had long cooled. Only cold-wet remained, and the smell, and that map slowly evaporating in the morning light.

Her ear tips reddened. Not sunburn—the sun had just risen, hadn't reached under the awning. It was the boiling, inescapable shame welling up from deep in her skin. She looked at her hands—the hand that had held Miaowu yesterday, now palm empty on her knee, fingertips sticky with a bit of white salt frost. She wiped her fingers on her skirt, made it wetter, leaving pale white marks on the skirt.

She stood. Boom. The ground shook. The dress clung to her legs, ice cold, skirt edges still dripping—not water, but something she didn't want to acknowledge. She grabbed the skirt hem, twisted it. A few drops of turbid liquid squeezed from the fabric, hitting the concrete with very soft plips.

She picked up the quilt. The peony summer quilt was heavy, lower half soaked through, color deep like water-soaked old newspaper. She shook it open, draped it over the wall to sun. The quilt corner hung down, still dripping, hitting the wall with pat-pat sounds.

She walked to the faucet. The old wall-mounted faucet, orange hose still attached—yesterday she'd drunk from it and washed her skirt with it. She turned it on, cupped water, splashed it on her face. Water ran down her chin into her collar, making her shiver. She cupped another handful, splashed it on the skirt, rubbed the dark blue wet mark with her hand. Wouldn't come off. Fabric already saturated, urine mixed with water; rubbing only lightened the color a little. She rubbed a few more times, then stopped.

Useless.

She turned off the tap, walked back under the awning and sat, back against the wall. Water dripped from her bangs onto her knees. She didn't wipe. She just looked toward the parking-lot entrance.

Morning wind blew from the sea, carrying salt and distant soy-milk steam smell. Ivy leaves on the wall rustled. Through the hole in the awning tarpaulin, the sky slowly turned from light gray to pale blue, a few tiny clouds like torn cotton candy, drifting very slowly.

She watched those clouds. One drifted left to right, taking about two minutes. Another, smaller, followed behind, like the first cloud's tail. Her eyelids drooped a little, then forced open.

Growl—

Stomach. Not yesterday's satisfied, lazy low call, but lighter, weaker, like a leaky bellows. Stomach wall rubbing stomach wall, empty feeling spreading from the belly center outward, like someone put a small, expanding balloon in her body, balloon with nothing inside, only hunger.

She swallowed. Her mouth was dry, tongue licked around her lips, catching a bit of salt frost saltiness. She pressed her back harder against the wall, hands on knees, ten toes slightly curled on the concrete—the concrete had warmed from the sun, no longer midnight-cold, but the wetland edge was still cool, evaporation taking heat.

She turned toward where food had been stacked. The rice sacks were still there—three hundred-jin rice sacks stacked in the awning corner yesterday. She reached, lifted the top one. Empty. Light as a snake's shed skin. She flipped it, opening down, shook. A few white rice crumbs floated from the opening, landing on the concrete, smaller than her thumbnail, scattering in the wind.

She lifted the second. Empty. Third. Empty.

She folded the three empty rice sacks, set them aside, movements slow, like completing a ritual. Egg carton—she didn't need to look to know it was empty. Yesterday when Granny cracked the last two trays of eggs into the wok, shells piled in the carton, yellow-white fragments stuck to cardboard, dried after a day in the sun, releasing a faint fishy smell. Pork bones piled outside the awning, baked in the sun for a day and night, ants crawling all over them—black, tiny, in a winding line, carrying the last bits of meat scraps to the nest. Those ants carried white crumbs bigger than their bodies, extending along the wall root to the drain crack.

Nothing left.

Xiaoman looked at the pile of bones, looked long. Her stomach growled again, louder this time—"grrr—"—like a broken washing machine spinning empty in the distance. The sound echoed under the awning, then was scattered by morning wind. She swallowed again. Her mouth still dry; the salt frost her tongue caught had dissolved, now only the coolness from tap water on her lips.

She hugged her knees, arms wrapping her calves, chin on her knees. This pose made her shrink into a smaller mountain. Light-blue dress wrapped her, skirt spreading on the ground, edges still a bit damp. Her long hair hung down, covering half her face. From between strands, her eyes watched the parking-lot entrance.

Nothing there. No delivery rider's e-bike, no Granny's vegetable trailer, no high schooler's shared bike, no that small second-grader in a crumpled uniform, messy hair, sneakers that squeak on concrete.

She'll come. She said "see you tomorrow."

She folded the three empty rice sacks, set them aside, slow movements like a ritual. Egg carton—empty. Pork bones—ants had them.

Nothing left.

Then a different sensation came from her lower abdomen.

Not hunger. A deeper, dull heaviness. Like something slowly moving downward in her body, not urgent, but heavy. She moved her hand from her knee, pressed it on her lower belly. Through the skirt fabric, the muscles there contracted slightly—not under her control, body doing what it needed to do. Gastrocolic reflex—she didn't know the word, but she knew what it meant: yesterday's three meals of stew, glass noodles, eggs, cabbage, after a night of digestion, residue had reached the end of the colon.

Her bladder was also bloating. Not the unconscious warm-wrap release from midnight, but conscious, a fullness she needed to actively deal with. Kidneys worked all night, filtering salt and water from yesterday's salty soup; new urine had accumulated in the bladder for hours. Midnight's emptying only cleared part, the rest accumulating drop by drop at a steady rate.

She released her knees, slowly stood. Boom. Ground shook slightly. The moment she stood, the lower belly heaviness sharpened—gravity helping, pushing colon contents more determinedly toward the exit. She clamped her legs—not urgent can't-hold-it urgency, but body gently but firmly reminding her: time to go.

She walked toward the pool.

Each step slow. Not from hunger—though hunger already lay like a hummed background noise across her whole body, from stomach to throat to brain, everywhere. But because each step made abdominal contents sway slightly, the downward heaviness more pronounced. Her soles made dull thuds on concrete; skirt edges still damp, occasionally brushing the ground, kicking up dust.

The pool was on the other side of the awning, around a corner of the wall. As she rounded it, she smelled it first. Not the stuffy ammonia under the awning from being covered all night, but thinner, driftier, scattered by morning wind but half remaining—what she'd left by peeing poolside last night. She approached, looked down.

The pool bottom was dry but not completely. Last night's thin water film had partially evaporated, leaving irregular pale yellow traces on the bottom, edges fringed with white salt frost. A few deeper depressions still held a little liquid, reflecting fine light in morning sun. Smell rose from the bottom, faint but perceptible—ammonia sharpness, mixed with that sweetish fishy tang of urine sun-baked.

Her ear tips reddened again. But this time she didn't stop. She walked to the edge, turned her back to the bottom, hands braced on the rim, then slowly crouched.

She'd done this pose several times—yesterday on the lawn, yesterday poolside, midnight in the same spot. Each time back to the exit, facing bushes or wall, skirt hanging into the bottom like a light-blue waterfall falling into a dry canyon. But this time her movements were slower, more cautious. Because this time it wasn't just pee.

She gathered her skirt in front, all hanging into the bottom, then adjusted her feet, knees apart, weight shifted slightly back.

Pee came first.

Not a jet, but a gentle, continuous stream. She felt the bladder fullness lightening bit by bit, from a hard ball in her lower belly slowly shrinking, finally becoming a flat, soft emptiness. Liquid hit the dry concrete bottom, making plip-plip sounds—as crisp as midnight, echoing in the empty pool. But this didn't last long. Bladder contents weren't much; after ten-plus seconds it became drip, drip, then stopped.

She waited. Not for pee—pee was done. For another signal. That dull heaviness was still there, just below where the bladder had been full. She could feel colon peristalsis—not under her control, smooth muscle contracting on its own, pushing feces from descending colon to rectum. That sensation wasn't pain, but an unavoidable pressure from deep within.

She braced both hands on her knees, leaned forward slightly, then held her breath.

Not deliberate breath-holding, body doing it—diaphragm descending, abdominal pressure increasing, the sphincter at the end of the rectum slowly loosening under pressure. This took longer than peeing. Feces isn't liquid, it doesn't flow on its own when a valve opens. It needs to be pushed out section by section, each section requiring colon peristalsis and abdominal pressure working together. She felt the first section slowly moving—not expelled, but on its way, stuck at the final gate, needing her to push a little.

She pushed. Not red-faced straining, but gentle, sustained abdominal pressure. Abdominal muscles tightening mixed with the stomach-wall rubbing of hunger—upper belly empty and growling, lower belly full and pushing. Two sensations simultaneously, forming a strange contrast: upper body craving food, lower body expelling food.

When the first section left her body, she felt sudden relief. Not all relief, just part of the pressure released. Then the second, softer, expelled faster. Then the third—softer, carrying residual water from yesterday's glass noodles and eggs after digestion. She glanced down. Feces piled directly beneath her, occupying the small area under her—about a basin-sized patch to normal people, but for her, just that small square dark patch on the pool bottom. Color dark brown, surface slightly dried from dehydration, edges cracked. Smell rose, mixing with residual urine smell on the pool bottom, forming a more complex, stronger, excrement-unique aroma.

She crouched there, didn't stand immediately. Not unwilling to stand, but legs numb. Crouching made thigh-front muscles work the whole time, knees starting to shake. She braced one hand on the rim, reached back with the other—not wiping, she had no paper, nothing to wipe with. She just used her fingertips to rub off the outermost residue, then wiped her fingers on the rim concrete. Rough concrete took most of it, but fingertips still left a sticky sensation. She wiped her fingers on her skirt, adding a light brown mark.

She stood. Boom. Knee clicked, thigh muscles protesting sorely. She turned, looked at the bottom.

The pool bottom now had three things: last night's residual urine trace, half-dry, edges a ring of white salt frost; newly added pee, small area, pooling in a low depression; and the feces just expelled, piled directly below, dark brown, surface already drying in morning wind. Smells mixed together, accumulating in the pool's sunken space, blocked by the wall unable to disperse.

She looked at that pile of feces. Not big—for her, only a little over half her palm—but for this abandoned pool, it was an unavoidable, dark, slowly releasing presence. How long would it stay? A day? Two? No one would come clean it. No one knew she was here. Only she, and her body, and these cycles her body couldn't stop—hunger, eat, digest, excrete, hunger again.

Xiaoman stood at the edge, looking at the dark brown pile. Morning wind poured through a gap in the wall, scattering the smell, but the ammonia accumulated in the pool's depression was still strong enough to make her wrinkle her nose. She turned, walked back to the awning.

Each step slow. Not from hunger—though hunger lay like a hummed background across her whole body. But because her legs were weak. Yesterday's three meals' calories had long been digested, stomach empty, body starting to consume reserves. She felt that weakness seeping out from the bones, like someone drawing her strength away bit by bit.

Under the awning, the dark wet mark from midnight had dried. Edges rolled with white salt frost, like a miniature snowfield. The peony quilt draped over the wall, lower half still wet, color deep like water-soaked old newspaper. She pulled it down, folded twice, spread it on dry concrete, then sat on it. Boom. Ground shook.

She hugged her knees, chin on her knees. This pose made her a smaller mountain.

Outside the wall, city sounds slowly grew. Bus air brakes, convenience store auto-door chimes, some early riser warming up their voice by the river. But no one came to the parking lot. No one called her name.

She stared at the parking-lot entrance, stared long. Eyes ached, blinked, kept staring.

No one.

Xiaoman sat under the awning, hugging her knees, staring at the entrance.

Morning light slowly shifted from the east, pulling her shadow from the wall root to the center of the concrete. Shadow long, eighteen or nineteen meters, like a flattened mountain.

Her stomach growled again. Not "grrr" loud, but lighter, duller—like a ball of wet cotton stuck at the stomach bottom, can't be wrung. She was past the point of hunger. Yesterday's three meals' calories were consumed after last night's release; now her body consumed reserves. She felt that weakness seeping from bones—not pain, but emptiness. From stomach to throat to brain, whole body like a gutted tree trunk, would fall with one gust.

She licked her lips. Lips dry, tongue touched the white dry skin on her lower lip's middle—still there, thicker than yesterday. The saltiness of Granny's glass noodle soup from yesterday was still in memory, but saliva had thinned to plain water.

The faucet was on the other side of the wall. She stood, boom, ground shook. Knees wobbly, like two water-soaked pieces of wood. She walked over, turned the tap, bent down, mouth under the spout. Water hit her face, made her shiver, then she drank. Drank fast, glunk-glunk sounds from her throat, water leaking from mouth corners, down her chin into her collar, skirt front wet again. She didn't care. By the fourth sip, water stretched her stomach a little, the hollow feeling temporarily filled—but only temporarily, she knew. Water wasn't food, couldn't last long.

She straightened, wiped her mouth with her skirt hem. Then stood there, looking at the parking-lot entrance.

Miaowu said "see you tomorrow."

Now it was tomorrow.

She walked back to the awning, sat. Water sloshed in her belly, each step making her stomach clunk. She pressed her abdomen—upper belly bulging from water; lower belly also gaining pressure. Yesterday's soup and this morning's water being filtered by kidneys, drop by drop into the bladder. She clamped her legs, didn't go to the pool. Still early, could wait.

She waited an hour. Or longer.

Sun fully risen, ivy leaves glistening on the wall. Concrete started reflecting light, heat rising. She sat in awning shade, but the tarpaulin was small; sun moved and shade shifted, her left foot already in sun. She pulled her foot back, knees to chest, huddling tighter.

Stomach empty again. Water long absorbed and eliminated, upper belly collapsing back, almost no distance from skin. Her stomach started growling—"grrr—boom"—sound changed from water to hollow, like a drum struck. She pressed her hand on it, fingers sinking into sunken belly. Through the skirt she could feel her stomach's outline—a flat, empty pouch.

She closed her eyes. Remembered home in the giant country. Mom's strawberry toast, cut palm-thick slices, butter spread on top, butter edges melting from bread warmth, seeping into bread pores. She could eat a whole slice in one bite. Xiao Gu sat across, licking her wiggly front tooth, mumbled "sister you're eating too fast."

She opened her eyes.

Parking-lot entrance still empty.

She stood. Not going anywhere—there was nowhere to go. She walked to the wall, flipped the peony quilt over, wet side up to dry. The dark brown wet mark on it was half-dry, edges stiff like starched cloth. She walked to the rice sacks, refolded the three empty ones. Fold in half, fold in half, stack them, edges aligned. Egg carton also folded, set beside rice sacks. Pork bone pile almost entirely carried off by ants, only a few dried tendon threads left on bones, like gray threads.

She swept the ground under the awning. No broom, she used her sole to push scattered bone fragments and leaves to the wall root. Concrete left gray-white scrape marks. Done, she sat back under the awning, hugged knees, stared at the entrance.

Noon.

Sun almost overhead, shadow shrinking to a small ball at her feet. Temperature rose above 30, concrete hot enough to fry an egg. She moved deeper into the awning, back of her head against the sun-warmed wall. Sweat beaded on her forehead, ran down her temple, leaving a glistening trail on her cheek.

Stomach started hurting. Not sharp pain, but dull, sustained burning, like someone lit a candle in her stomach, flame licking the stomach wall. Her body was digesting itself—first food, then reserves, then muscle. She felt that consumption from inside out, like a coal stove, coal burned out, started burning wood, wood burned out, flame still alive.

She swallowed. Her mouth had a strange sweetness—ketosis. She didn't know the word. She only knew her mouth tasted sweet, belly empty, head a bit dizzy.

Bladder bloated again. This morning's tap water plus last night's accumulation was full. She stood, clamped legs, walked toward the pool.

This time slower than morning. Not from holding it—she was used to holding, from the first day on the lawn, she'd been holding. From hunger. Hunger made her legs heavy, each step like dragging iron on concrete. Her soles made sha-sha friction sounds on concrete, no longer boom thuds.

She rounded to the pool, looked down.

The sight stopped her.

Not scared of herself—she no longer got scared by her own waste. A deeper, heavier feeling, like someone stuffed a cold stone in her chest. Urine on the pool bottom had partially evaporated, leaving several layers of irregular salt frost, new over old, dark yellow over pale yellow, oldest already whitening, edges curling, like shed snake skin. This morning's feces still piled directly below, surface dried by wind into a shell, color from dark brown to dull brown, edges cracked like a dry riverbed. Smell stronger—not a single smell, but accumulated over days, layer by layer, ammonia sharpness, feces rot, plus that sweetish fishy tang of sun-baked.

She didn't cover her nose. She was used to this smell. Just as she was used to the quilt's cold-wet, used to the rice sacks' emptiness, used to no one ever appearing at the parking-lot entrance.

She just stood at the edge, looking at the bottom. That was her trace. Proof she'd lived here. No one would clean it. No one knew she was here. Only she, and her body, and these cycles her body couldn't stop.

She gathered her skirt in front, all hanging into the bottom. Then turned, crouched, knees apart, hands braced on the rim. Rough concrete texture pressed into her palm, tile edge had a crack, a tiny weed growing in it, she saw it every time she crouched here.

Pee first. Liquid from bladder left her body, not a jet but gentle, continuous stream, carving a new trace through salt frost on the bottom. Old frost dissolved, new liquid pooled in a low depression into a small transparent lake. She felt bladder pressure lightening bit by bit, from "tight" to "empty," the hard ball in lower belly shrinking, finally becoming soft emptiness.

Then seconds after pee ended. Not fully done—dripping tail continued, but pressure basically released. She waited, leaned forward, legs clamped a little. Not holding back, but waiting. Waiting for another signal.

Signal came. Colon-end heaviness still there, clearer than morning. Gastrocolic reflex—she didn't know the word, but she knew eating brought this feeling. Problem was, she hadn't eaten. But her body still worked—digestive juices still secreted, intestines still peristalsis, just peristalsis of air and previous residue. She felt something slowly moving in her descending colon, not urgent, but definitely going down.

She held her breath. Diaphragm descended, abdominal pressure rose, then relaxed. Not pushing—didn't need much this time, residue not much, texture softer. They slid out, shorter and thinner than this morning's three sections, hitting the bottom with a plop, then a string of soft plip-plips, like rain.

She looked down. This feces softer, paler—yellow-brown, surface still glossy with a bit of mucus. Because soft, spread more on hitting concrete, no clear shape like morning's sections. Shape an irregular pool, blurred edges, mixing with previous urine, forming a small dark mud patch on the bottom.

She waited more. More. Another small section, thinner, almost just pinky-width. This came out without her pushing—it slid out on its own, she only felt it. Then last few drops of liquid, not urine, but residual digestive juice in the intestine, colored by feces, dripping on the bottom with plip-plip sounds.

She confirmed—lower belly heaviness basically gone. That area of intestine temporarily empty. But new gas was accumulating deeper—she felt a slight gurgle from ascending colon direction, that's air and digestive juice mixing during peristalsis. But that was next round's business.

She crouched there, used fingertips to rub off outermost residue, wiped on rim concrete, wiped on skirt. Skirt hem already had several light brown marks, overlapping, like an abstract painting she couldn't understand.

She stood. Boom. Knee clicked. She turned, looked at the bottom.

Pool bottom traces added another layer. New over old, varying shades, from oldest dark brown to newest pale yellow, like geological strata. Smell rose stronger in harsh sun—ammonia and skatole volatilized faster at high heat, a thin, slightly distorted heat haze above the pool, carrying smell farther.

She looked at the bottom, looked long. Then turned, walked back to the awning.

Faster than coming. Not from strength—strength hadn't recovered, was weaker. Because she knew what came next: back to awning, sit, hug knees, stare at parking-lot entrance. She'd done this three days. This was the fourth.

She sat. Boom. Ground shook. Back of head against wall, eyes closed. Stomach still hurt, but from burning to deeper, duller ache, like a fist pressing her stomach wall from inside. Dizziness more pronounced—not vertigo, but the kind where standing up makes vision black out for a second, blood sugar bottoming out and brainstem sending a distress signal.

She swallowed again. Sweetness in her mouth stronger. Her body had started breaking down fat. Fat breakdown produced ketones, ketones exhaled, carrying that faint rotten-apple sweetness. She smelled her own breath, frowned, thought it was bad-brush-breath.

Sun slowly tilted west. Shadows lengthened again.

3 PM. She stood, went to the faucet for water. This time longer—she crouched under the tap, mouth on it, drank almost a minute. Water poured into her empty stomach, making a temporary fullness, but satisfaction lasted only ten minutes, water absorbed and eliminated. She went back to awning, sat.

Then.

Around 4 PM.

Her stomach emitted a completely different roar.

Not hunger. Not the previous "grrr—boom" empty growl. Deeper, more urgent, a twisting from lower abdomen—like an invisible hand twisted a wring in her intestines. Her body arched sharply, hands pressing her lower belly, nails scraping shhh on her skirt.

Not pain. The unavoidable warning before diarrhea. Intestines cramping—smooth muscle irregularly contracting, pushing intestinal contents hard in one direction. She felt that force starting at transverse colon, moving to descending colon, much faster than usual.

Her body made a judgment. Not conscious judgment—body's own. Gastrocolic reflex: intestine actively empties end contents when stomach wall is stretched, to make room for new food. But her stomach wasn't stretched by food—by water. She'd just drunk too much, too fast, stomach wall suddenly expanded, nerve signal to colon: "something came in, time to push."

Colon obeyed. Colon expelled its only contents. But problem—what came in was water, not food. Water needs no digestion, passes right through. So colon emptied, but stomach was also stretched by water, then water eliminated, stomach emptied again. Now her whole digestive tract was empty—upper belly empty, lower belly empty. But intestines still cramped. Because the peristalsis signal hadn't fully faded, smooth muscle still excited, kept pushing down. Pushing what? Air, digestive juice, mucosal epithelial cells.

Her fingers gripped her skirt tighter. Not holding—waiting. Waiting for her body to tell her "done" or "not yet." Intestines twisted again. This time more urgent. Her toes dug into concrete, nails leaving white marks.

Then, it came.

Not feces. Liquid. Watery, thin, pale brown. It seeped from intestinal wall gaps—unabsorbed digestive juice mixed with fecal residue in colon folds, squeezed out by cramping intestines, like the last drops squeezed from a wrung sponge. Sound soft—"psss—"—like someone squeezing a half-empty water bag far away. Then a thinner tail, "hiss—"—like a balloon letting out its last air.

She looked down. Skirt hung into the bottom, she couldn't see the bottom. But she could feel—that warm, wet, intestinal-micro-sweet scent rising from the bottom, mixing into the pool's existing smell.

Her ear tips reddened. That boiling, inescapable shame from deep in skin again. She'd been doing this for days—morning, noon, evening, needed to go every time. But this was different. This wasn't normal bowel movement, it was watery diarrhea. Her body was telling her: you have nothing left to digest. What you're expelling now is only you.

She crouched there, didn't stand immediately. Not unwilling. Too weak. Crouching made legs numb again, knees shaking, but more importantly—she didn't know what pose to face what might happen next. Her intestines still lightly cramped. Not violent twisting, but wave after wave, slow, like someone pressing deep in her intestines with fingers. She knew there might be more.

She waited a few minutes. Another small gush of watery liquid slid out, less, thinner, almost just drops. Then silence again. Then another wave of light cramp—this position lower, at rectum end. She felt something sliding out, but not liquid, something lighter, airier, almost weightless. She didn't push, it came out on its own.

Pfft—hiss—pfft.

A string of fine, wet bubbles passed through the mud of liquid and residual feces, splashing almost invisible droplets. Sound not loud, but echoed clearly in the empty pool. Smell stronger—not ammonia sharpness, but sulfides from bacterial fermentation in the gut, mixed with digestive juice acidity, spreading fast in heat.

Her ear tips reddened more. But this time not from shame. She discovered something: she didn't feel it. Not that she didn't feel gas exit—the touch was clear, she felt the slight vibration of the sphincter and warm airflow at the exit. But "this is embarrassing" didn't come. Or rather, she found her shame was blunting bit by bit. Like a knife used too many times, blade worn flat, no longer clean.

First day on the lawn, she nearly broke. First day poolside, her face hot enough to fry eggs. Midnight wetting the awning, she woke clutching her skirt, ears red as blood. But now—now she crouched poolside, expelling watery liquid, digestive juice, and strings of fine wet bubbles, her feeling wasn't "shame," it was "oh, here we go again." "Done yet? Can I stand?" "So hungry, hunger louder than shame."

Her body made the judgment for her. A body starved three days had no strength left for shame, that high-tier emotion. Her hypothalamus routed the last energy to brainstem—breathing, heartbeat, temperature, these couldn't stop. As for shame, that's prefrontal cortex business. Prefrontal cortex running on low battery now, survival first.

She used fingertips to wipe residual wetness at the exit, wiped on rim concrete, wiped on skirt. Then stood. Boom. Dizziness, vision black for less than a second. She braced on the rim, waited for vision to return, then slowly turned.

Pool bottom traces layered again. New watery liquid pooled in a low depression into a turbid lake, surface with a faint oily sheen. Ripples from those bubbles slowly spread, forming small concentric waves on the lake. Smell—sulfide sharpness, ammonia bite, digestive-juice acidity after fermentation, mixing, steaming in 4 PM sun. She looked at the bottom, no expression. Someone who's hungry too long also saves battery on expression.

She walked back to the awning, sat. Boom. Belly still lightly cramping, but slowly settling. She put her hand on her belly, through the skirt feeling intestines moving—that slow, uneven peristalsis, like a very long snake turning over in her belly. Her stomach still growled, but sound changed from "grrr" to weaker, balloon-leaking "hiss—hiss—." Back of her head against the wall, eyes closed.

Tap water still wasn't enough. She needed salt. Sugar. Anything to get through this day. But there was nothing here. Parking lot had only concrete, wall, awning, pool. And herself. And those cycles in her body that wouldn't stop.

She opened her eyes. Sunset began to tilt west, ivy shadows on the wall lengthening again. She stared at the parking-lot entrance long. Until sky fully darkened, streetlights came on. Dim yellow light spilled over the wall, making her and the awning into a vague dark shape.

She stood. To the pool. Bottom traces reflected faint oily sheen under streetlight. Skillfully gathered her skirt in front, skillfully crouched, skillfully completed the other end. Then stood, skillfully wiped fingers, skillfully wiped on skirt. Skirt hem light brown marks already layered dozens deep, oldest whitening, newest still wet.

She walked back to awning, sat. Night wind blew from the sea, through wall cracks, making a low whine in her ears. She tightened the peony quilt; the smell mixed with her own body scent had thinned in night wind, but residual salt frost on the quilt rubbed her cheek, still slightly gritty.

She closed her eyes, but didn't sleep. Stomach no longer growled, hunger changing from sharp cramp to diffuse weakness across her whole body, like bones removed. She could feel her heartbeat—slow, heavy, like a drum wrapped in cotton struck far away. Each heartbeat reminded her: body still working, still consuming, still squeezing the last energy from every muscle, every inch of fat.

Tomorrow. Miaowu will definitely come.

She rolled over, face buried in the quilt. The quilt smelled of Granny Xiulan's closet, old, like mothballs mixed with sun. That smell made her think of home in the giant country, of Mom hanging washed sheets in the yard—sheets ballooning in the wind, like a white sail. She breathed in deep, tucked that smell into her lungs, then slowly exhaled.

Night still long.

The pool lay quietly in the dark.

That ring of wall was like a giant lidless coffin, sealing all traces on the bottom in shadow where moonlight didn't reach. The pool bottom was no longer dry gray-white concrete. After a whole day of repeated excretion—morning pee and poop, noon pee and soft stool, afternoon watery diarrhea and bubbles, evening's last pee—a thin layer of turbid liquid had pooled on the bottom.

This liquid wasn't uniform. The bottom had a slight slope; low spots pooled deeper, deepest about an adult ankle deep—not deep for Xiaoman, but for a pool, already an undeniable puddle. Liquid surface had a faint rainbow oil sheen, a film from digestive juices and intestinal secretions mixing. Those piles of feces directly below—morning's dark brown hard stool, noon's yellow-brown soft stool—partially dispersed by later watery diarrhea and urine, becoming half-dissolved clumps, sinking to the deepest depression, edges blurred, like cookies soaked too long.

Urine was the main component of this mixed liquid. Multiple pees during the day pooled in low spots, mixing with later intestinal fluid from diarrhea and a little unabsorbed digestive juice, forming turbid, pale yellow-brown standing water. Waterline left a new trace on the pool wall—lower than yesterday's moss line, darker, gleaming faintly in moonlight. On the surface, strings of fine bubbles slowly popped—gas from intestinal bacterial fermentation, rising from gaps in bottom sediment, plip, plip, plip, each pop releasing a bit of sulfide sharpness.

Smell accumulated in this sunken space all day. Daytime sun baked, ammonia and volatiles rose from the surface, blocked by the wall unable to disperse, forming a dense, almost visible fog. At night, temperature dropped, smell molecules less actively rising, but concentration unchanged. It pressed quietly above the bottom, like an invisible heavy carpet—ammonia sharpness, skatole rot, digestive-juice acidity after fermentation, plus a faint rotten-apple ketone smell from long-term carb shortage and fat metabolism. These smells weren't independent; they mixed, layered, becoming a unique, strong, complex aroma belonging only to this abandoned pool.

If someone stood at the edge and looked down—no one would. No one knew here. Only Xiaoman. She rolled over under the awning, tightened her quilt, breathing even, no dreams. Her body continued working in sleep—kidneys filtering blood, drop by drop into bladder, new liquid slowly accumulating.

The pool waited for her to come again tomorrow morning.

Liquid on the bottom swayed gently in moonlight, not wind, but distant bus vibrations transmitted here. Ripples spread from center to wall, broke into smaller waves on tiles, then slowly settled. Surface returned to calm, covering the oil sheen and sediment again, like a turbid, expressionless face, reflecting the crooked-neck tree branch silhouette on the wall.

Deeper night. Smell still there, puddle still there, everything there, waiting for dawn.
