<a id="Chapter26"></a>
## Chapter 26: The Third Day · Hunger

Not yesterday's "grrr" empty growl, not that dull ache. Sharp, sustained, like an invisible hand wringing a towel in her stomach. She opened her eyes and found she'd curled her knees to her chest, whole body shrimp-like, hands pressing her belly, fingers sinking into the light-blue dress, knuckles white.

She held this pose long. Cold sweat beaded on her forehead, ran down her temple into her ear. Lips dry and stuck together; she used her tongue to pry them apart, tasting saltiness—the salt frost from yesterday's tears dried on her lip, plus a strange sweetness. She didn't know that was ketosis—her body had started breaking down fat, acetone exhaled, carrying faint rotten-apple sweetness. She only knew her mouth had a weird taste, like holding a rusty coin.

Yesterday all day, she'd eaten nothing.

First day's three pots of stew, glass noodles, eggs, long digested. Yesterday morning she found rice sacks empty, egg cartons empty, pork only a pile of ant-carryed bones. She drank lots of water, went to the toilet many times, but nothing entered her stomach. Yesterday all day, her stomach was empty. Now her body started consuming itself—first stored glycogen, then fat, then muscle. Stomach empty, but stomach acid still secreted. No food to digest, so acid started burning the stomach wall itself. That pain wasn't "hunger" but hunger pangs—mucosa irritated by acid, mixed with smooth-muscle spasms from idling.

She slowly sat up. This took longer than yesterday because she had to brace on the wall, and stopped once—dizziness, vision black for seconds, ears humming. Her blood sugar had bottomed out, body sending alarm. She waited for vision to return, then stood.

Boom. Ground shook. Her legs shook. Not yesterday's muscle soreness, but low-blood-sugar tremor—hands and feet cold, fingertips slightly numb, knees soft as water-filled. She braced on the wall a moment, let her body adapt to standing, then walked toward the faucet.

Those few steps to the tap, she stopped once. Not from weak legs—though legs were weak. Because her stomach twisted again. She bent over, hands on knees, waited for the cramp to pass. Cold sweat dripped from her forehead, hitting concrete, making a dark dot, then quickly evaporating.

She turned the tap, bent over, mouth under spout. Water hit her face, made her shiver, then she drank. Drank fast—glunk glunk glunk—water leaked from mouth corners, down chin into collar, skirt front wet again. She didn't care. Water poured into empty stomach, making a temporary fullness. The moment water stretched her stomach, pain lightened a little—but only a little. She knew the fullness was fake, water wasn't food, wouldn't last. But right now, fake was better than nothing.

She drank long. Longer than yesterday. Because her body was screaming thirst—too much water lost yesterday, waking this morning with lips stuck together, throat like sanded wood. She crouched under the tap, mouth on it, drank almost two minutes. Water sloshed in her belly, each step making her stomach clunk.

She straightened, wiped mouth with skirt hem. Then stood there, looking at the parking-lot entrance.

Miaowu didn't come yesterday. All day yesterday, no one came. No delivery e-bike, no Granny's trailer wheels, no high schooler's shared bike chain, no Miaowu's sneakers pattering on concrete. She waited all day, from sunrise to sunset, waited for streetlights to come on and go off, waited for night wind to make ivy leaves rustle. Nothing.

But she still watched. Because Miaowu said "see you tomorrow." Miaowu kept her word. Miaowu had said "you are Xiaoman," said "I like the natural you," said "let's go buy custard buns." Miaowu never lied to her. So if Miaowu didn't come yesterday, there must be a reason. Not unwilling. Trapped by something. Just as she was trapped in this parking lot.

She walked back to the awning, sat. Back of head against wall, eyes closed. Stomach hurt again. The water-stretched fullness had dissipated, stomach shrinking back into an empty, cramping pouch. She pressed both hands on it, fingers sinking into sunken belly. Through the skirt she could feel her stomach's outline—a flat, empty pouch, acid inside burning the mucosa.

She started waiting.

Sun slowly rose. Today's morning fog thicker than yesterday, white, drifting from the sea, wrapping the city outside the wall into hazy silhouettes. Air had a wet, sea-salty scent, mixed with her own body smell—the ammonia under the awning had thinned, but morning wind occasionally carried up the sharp tang from the pool.

Her stomach growled again. Not "grrr" loud, but sharper, more painful twisting. She arched her back, forehead on knees, hands pressing her belly. Pain. Really painful. She'd never been hungry this long. At home in the giant country, Mom always had meals ready on the table—strawberry toast, big mug of hot milk, cut apple chunks. Xiao Gu would secretly take an apple chunk from her plate, then hide behind the curtain to eat, thinking she couldn't see. She pretended not to see, because Xiao Gu licking her teeth was funny.

She wanted to go home. This thought wasn't new, but this time especially clear. Not from fear, not shame, not dirty skirt and puddles. Just hunger. Hunger pushed all emotion to the background, leaving only the most primal, most bodily need: I need food. Mom. Xiao Gu. Strawberry toast. Hot milk. I want to go home.

Her eyes started to heat. But tears didn't fall—not unwilling to cry, but she had no extra water to allocate to tears. Her body was using its last energy for heartbeat and breathing; tears were a luxury her body couldn't afford.

She buried her face in her knees, shoulders shaking twice lightly, but no sound.

Then she lifted her head.

She didn't wipe her eyes—no tears to wipe. She just rubbed her dry eye corners with the back of her hand, then turned her gaze back to the parking-lot entrance. Fog slowly cleared, morning light spilled over the wall, staining concrete pale gold. Entrance still empty.

She kept waiting.

About an hour, or longer. Sun high, fog fully cleared, ivy leaves glistening. Stomach still hurt, but from sharp cramp to deeper, diffuse weakness, like someone drawing her strength away, leaving an empty shell.

Lower belly started feeling. Not hunger. Another pressure—yesterday's water, this morning's two minutes of tap water, being filtered by kidneys, drop by drop into bladder. She clamped her legs, stood, walked toward the pool.

Each step slow. Sloshing water in her stomach made clunk-clunk sounds like an empty bottle. Her legs weak, soles dragging faint sha-sha sounds. At the pool, she stopped, braced on the rim, looked down.

Pool bottom traces added another layer. Yesterday she'd come many times—pee, and those bowel movements of nothing but digestive juice and bubbles. Old frost over new, dark yellow over pale yellow, oldest whitening, edges curling like shed snake skin. Lowest point held a small turbid pool, surface with faint oily sheen. Smell same—ammonia sharpness, digestive-juice acidity after fermentation, rising in morning light. She was used to this smell. Or rather, she had no extra energy to care. Caring was strenuous.

She gathered her skirt in front, all hanging into the bottom, then turned, crouched. Knees apart, hands braced on rim. Rough concrete pressed into palms. She looked at the bottom, waited.

Pee first. Not a jet, but gentle continuous stream—bladder liquid leaving her body, splashing concentric ripples in the pool's standing water. She felt bladder pressure lightening, lower belly hard ball shrinking, finally soft emptiness. She waited. Colon end had a dull heaviness—not feces, no feces. All day yesterday no food, this morning no food, intestine empty. That heaviness was just thin liquid of digestive juice and shed epithelial cells, plus gas from bacterial fermentation.

She held her breath. Not pushing—didn't need much, residue little. Pfft—hiss—pfft. Strings of fine wet bubbles slid out, splashing almost invisible droplets. Then a few drops of thin, pale yellow liquid, plip-plip hitting the standing water, mixing with previous turbidity. Smell stronger—a thin layer of sulfide micro-rot and digestive-juice acidity on top of already-accustomed background.

She crouched waiting. Intestines lightly twisted again, then another small bubble slid out—pfft, this time single, lighter, almost just a micro-vibration at the exit. Then empty. She waited a few seconds, confirmed nothing more, then used fingertips to wipe residual wetness at the exit, wiped on rim concrete, wiped on skirt. Skirt hem light brown marks added another.

She stood. Boom. Knee clicked. Dizziness, vision black for less than a second. She braced on the rim, waited. Then slowly walked back to the awning.

Sat. Pulled the peony quilt over her legs. Lower half still wet, cool, but she didn't care anymore. She leaned against the wall, hugged her knees, chin on knees. This pose made her a smaller mountain.

Noon. Sun almost overhead. Shadow shrank to a small ball at her feet. Concrete reflected light, heat rising. She huddled deeper into the awning. Stomach still hurt, but from sharp to sustained dull ache, like a cold stone pressing there.

She started thinking about Miaowu.

Not "why isn't Miaowu here yet" thinking, but another—she remembered Miaowu sitting on her collarbone, legs dangling six stories up, swinging. She remembered Miaowu saying "you have me in your eyes," then raising her finger, pointing to her left eye and right—"this me has messier hair, this me has pollen on my face." She remembered Miaowu eating glass noodles—pinching one, putting it in mouth, chewing twice, swallowing, then saying "I ate. Seven. Enough." No taste. Miaowu couldn't taste. But she ate anyway, because Granny told her to.

Would Miaowu be eating glass noodles now? Did Miaowu's mom make her breakfast? Did Miaowu take medicine? Those two white round tablets with the score line—Miaowu said "medicine is food," but she can't not eat. She was too thin. Xiaoman remembered when Miaowu sat in her palm, fuzz balls on the uniform cuff, wrist thin as a twig. She'd thought then: Miaowu needs to eat more. Now she was also starving, she was also thinking: Miaowu needs to eat more.

Her stomach growled again. Not sharp cramp, but weaker, duller—like a ball of wet cotton stuck at the bottom, can't wring. She hugged her knees tighter.

Afternoon, sun tilted west. Shadow stretched from her feet. She went to the pool again—same as morning, only urine, a few drops of thin liquid, strings of fine bubbles. Her bladder had learned in these three days to fill fast and empty fast, because she kept drinking—not from thirst, but from hunger. Drinking temporarily fooled the stomach into thinking something came in. But the deception lasted shorter and shorter; the stomach got smarter. Water poured in, absorbed and eliminated in ten minutes, stomach shrinking back into an empty cramping pouch.

Evening, sky darkening. She tightened the peony quilt. Streetlights came on, dim yellow light spilling over the wall. She looked at the parking-lot entrance, looked long. Entrance empty.

Miaowu didn't come today either.

She buried her face in her knees. This time, she finally squeezed out a few tears—very few, just circled in her eye sockets, wetting lashes a little, dried by night wind before rolling down. She sniffed, pulled the quilt up a little, covering her shoulders.

"Miaowu," she said to her knees, voice so soft only she could hear, "did bad people trap you too?"

No one answered. Only ivy leaves rustling in night wind on the wall.

She closed her eyes. Didn't wait today. Tomorrow might not wait either. But she'd still wait. Because Miaowu said "see you tomorrow." Miaowu kept her word. Miaowu never lied to her.

She waited. In stomach cramps and bodily weakness, in awning shadow and streetlight dimness, under the peony quilt wet and dried by urine, she kept waiting.

Late night, a soft "pfft" from the pool—that's bottom bubbles popping in the mud. Smell still there, puddle still there, everything there. Her breathing slowly deepened, slowed. She fell asleep. Before sleeping, her lips moved silently, maybe saying something. Maybe "Miaowu," maybe "see you tomorrow," maybe "I'm hungry." Maybe nothing—just habitual lip movement.

But her body didn't sleep. Her body continued working in the dark—stomach acid kept burning empty stomach wall, kidneys kept filtering water into bladder, intestines kept slowly peristalsising, pushing only digestive juice and shed cells toward the exit. She'd wake before dawn when her bladder called again, walk to the pool again, crouch again, expel those non-food residues.

Then she'd come back. Keep waiting.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter27"></a>
## Chapter 27: The Fourth Day · Coma

Fourth morning, Xiaoman didn't wake.

Not dead—her breathing continued, shallow and slow, each exhale carrying a strange faint rotten-apple sweetness. Her heartbeat too, just weak, slow, like a dying-battery clock striking far away. She'd fainted. The prelude to hypoglycemic coma—medically called "neuroglycopenia," brain sugar-deprived, consciousness shutting down non-essential functions. She hadn't eaten anything for two full days.

Her body shrank into the smallest ball in sleep. Knees to chest, arms around belly, forehead almost touching knees. Peony quilt slid from shoulders, piling by her feet. Light-blue dress clung to her, a patch damp with cold sweat—her body secretes epinephrine and cortisol during hypoglycemia, these hormones cause cold sweat, sticky, icy, running down the back of her neck, pooling into a damp mark in her collarbone hollow.

Sun rose. Morning light spilled over the wall, first touching her toes, then calves, then knees. Her toes were pale—peripheral circulation already degrading, body prioritizing scarce blood to brain and heart, "non-essential regions" like hands and feet temporarily abandoned. Toenails still had poolside salt frost, glinting in morning light, but she couldn't feel sun warmth.

She didn't move.

An hour passed. Sun higher. Flies came—not one, several. They flew from the pool, drawn by the smell on the bottom, now discovering the huge quiet target under the awning. They circled her twice, one landed on her knee, rubbing its front legs. Her lashes fluttered—not conscious, neural reflex. The fly flew off, came back. She still didn't wake.

After a while. Her lips moved silently, maybe talking in a dream. Maybe "Mom," maybe "Miaowu," maybe "hungry." Maybe just lip muscles twitching involuntarily—hypoglycemia causes slight local muscle spasms. Then her finger moved. Right hand, index finger, lightly tapped on her knee. That motion was exactly like her picking at the basin rim—nail scraping a very light, shallow white mark on the skirt, then stopped.

She woke. Not all at once. Slowly floating up from subconscious depths—first felt pain, stomach burning, like someone lit a candle in her belly. Then cold, back cold-sweat chilled by morning wind. Then hard, concrete pressing her hip bone, that bone more prominent than before—two days no food, she'd lost weight.

She opened her eyes.

Vision blurry, eyes dry and aching. She blinked, blinked again, world slowly focused. Above was awning tarpaulin, dark green, hole at edge leaking a thin bright white line. She stared at that light long; brain took a while to process "what is this." Hypoglycemia slowed her cognition—not stupid, but lagging, like a low-RAM computer struggling to open a file.

Then she heard her stomach. Not yesterday's sharp cramp, not the day before's "grrr" empty growl. Weaker, more like a balloon-leaking "hiss—hiss—." Stomach wall no longer had strength for violent spasms, just occasional twitch, like an exhausted person barely lifting an arm.

She lifted her head from her knees. This simple action made vision black for several seconds—orthostatic hypotension plus hypoglycemia, body unable to quickly adjust blood pressure. She waited for vision to return, then slowly sat up. Peony quilt slid from her feet to the ground, she bent to pick it up—stopped halfway, stomach position hurt more when pressed. She straightened, scooped up the quilt with her hand, folded it, set it beside her knee.

Then she looked at the parking-lot entrance. Same as yesterday, same as the day before, same as every time. Empty, no one.

She braced on the wall and stood. Boom. Ground shook. But this time's shake was lighter than yesterday—not intentional control, but no strength. Standing, her knees shook badly, thigh muscles trembling. She leaned against the wall, eyes closed, deep breathing. But deep breathing didn't help much; her blood sugar was already low enough that brain started prioritizing shutdown of non-essential functions. She only felt sleepy—not sleep-deprived sleepy, but brain-fuel-starved fog, like being wrapped in thick cotton.

Go drink water. From awning to tap, about twenty steps. Yesterday she took about two minutes, stopped once. Today took longer, stopped twice. First from weak legs, knees almost buckling, she braced on the wall to steady. Second from stomach suddenly cramping, she bent over, hands on belly, waited for the cramp to pass.

Faucet. She turned it, bent over, mouth under spout. Water hit her face, made her shiver. She started drinking—glunk glunk—only two sips then stopped. Not not thirsty, she was parched, lips like sandpaper. Stomach wouldn't accept. An empty two-day stomach suddenly stretched by water, stomach wall spasming harder. She held water in her mouth, swallowed little by little, like tricking her body: slow, no rush, something coming in. But body wasn't that easy to fool anymore. Water poured in, stomach pain intensified—not burning, but bloating pain, like someone shoving something into a balled-up pouch.

She drank half her usual amount, dared not more. Straightened, wiped mouth with skirt. Then stood there, looking at the parking-lot entrance, long.

Miaowu still didn't come. Yesterday no, day before no, today no. She stood there, suddenly remembered something—not sudden, this thought had always been in her head, just now finally took clear shape. Miaowu said "see you tomorrow." Miaowu kept her word. If Miaowu didn't come, there must be a reason. Not unwilling. Not forgot. Something blocked her. Just as her home was blocked by a mountain, just as she was trapped in this parking lot.

She should go find Miaowu. When this thought surfaced, it startled her first. She'd never actively sought anyone. In this world, she'd always been passive—discovered by Miaowu, fed by Granny, given supply lists by the high schooler, given tarpaulin by the delivery rider. All she'd done was crouch in the parking lot, waiting for others to come. But waiting three days resulted in: no one came. Rice gone, meat gone, eggs gone, glass noodles gone. She had no food, no one coming to deliver. She needed to go find.

But she looked down at her legs. They shook. Not post-run soreness, but the kind of weakness that makes standing hard. From here to parking-lot exit, about two hundred meters. She wasn't sure she could make it. She wasn't sure if she fainted halfway, someone would find her. She wasn't sure—then her stomach growled again. "Hiss—" weak, like a leaking balloon's last cry. She suddenly felt that compared to everything that might happen walking out, the scariest thing was continuing to sit here and wait. She'd waited three days, waited out everything there was to wait.

She decided to go find Miaowu. This decision wasn't the fiery "go for it" kind, but quieter, more certain—like her first day on the lawn deciding to crouch so Miaowu could see her face. Like her deciding in the pool to hold that handkerchief-sized blue tarpaulin and say "then it's a hat." It was the calm of someone who'd already decided and didn't want to go back on it.

She walked out of the awning. Step. Boom. Concrete shook. She folded the peony quilt, set it beside rice sacks. She had nothing to bring—no bag, no food, no water bottle. Only herself, and this dirty light-blue skirt, and the warmth on her collarbone where Miaowu had sat, faded by wind.

When Xiaoman walked out of the awning, her steps made dull echoes on the parking-lot concrete. She hadn't eaten for three days, legs shaking, vision blacking out in waves. But she still walked toward the parking-lot exit—the direction Miaowu always appeared and disappeared from, the only direction she knew.

Then she heard a voice.

Not Miaowu's. An adult's, from the alley mouth, distorted by wall reflection. When the sentence entered her ear, her brain took a moment to parse it into understandable words.

In her hearing, the sentence was like this:

"Hello" was received by her eardrum as a low resonance rising from ten-plus meters below the ground, like a cello bow slowly drawing the lowest string. The speaker used very loud volume—probably shouting—but by the time it reached her height it had attenuated by more than half, sounding like speaking through a thick quilt. The tail of "need" was scattered by alley wind; she only caught the front half. "Help me?"—the "me" rose, but that rising intonation sounded less like a question and more like eagerness she wasn't familiar with, like talking about something the speaker himself was excited about.

She heard it, but didn't immediately respond. She just stopped, turned toward the sound.

She saw two people.

Two adults, man and woman, standing at the alley mouth by the parking-lot entrance. To her, they were only about her pinky size. But she could make out their outlines: man in a dark T-shirt, woman in a bright top, both figures to her like two small differently-colored Lego blocks. Their movements were fast—not natural fast, but purposeful fast. The woman held something up, black, rectangular, pointed toward her. The man also held something, smaller, adjusting its angle.

That black rectangular thing had a tiny red dot, flashing in morning light.

She recognized it. Not from seeing it, but because she'd seen similar things on the giant country's TV—it was a camera. People use it to film, then show the footage on bigger screens to others.

She stood there, motionless.

Her body still weak. Stomach burning, legs shaking, a faint black ring at vision edges, hypoglycemia slowing her thinking. It took her several seconds to piece together the image: two strange small people, two cameras, a red dot, flashing. Did they speak to her? Yes. What did they say? "Need help?" She heard, but didn't answer. Not unwilling, but not knowing how. She needed help—desperately. She'd been hungry three days, her stomach hurt, her body was consuming itself, she'd almost couldn't get up from the awning just now. But she didn't know whether to say it to these two. When she'd said "I'm hungry" before, it was facing Miaowu crouching at her feet, facing Granny with a spatula, facing the high schooler calculating in his notebook. Those people had no cameras, no red dots. They just looked at her, like looking at a person.

These two were looking at her too. But their eyes were different—not bad, but excited, the excitement of having found something. That excitement reminded her of children in the giant country's garden discovering a rare beetle for the first time. That beetle was later put in a glass bottle, lid screwed tight.

She didn't answer. Her fingers unconsciously twisted the skirt hem, toes curling slightly on concrete. Then she heard her stomach growl again—not "hiss," but a louder "grrr," echoing in the empty parking lot. The two obviously heard it too, because the man poked his head from behind the camera, said something to the woman, voice too low for Xiaoman to catch. But she saw the woman nod, then raise the camera a little higher, aimed at her face.

Xiaoman turned her head. Not angry—she had no strength for anger. She just moved her gaze from that red dot, back toward the parking-lot exit. That was where Miaowu always appeared, where she'd been going. She didn't plan to stop here.

Xiaoman had turned, about to continue toward the parking-lot exit. Her full attention was on that direction—where Miaowu always appeared, the only direction she was certain of.

"We have good food for you!"

This exploded from the alley mouth. The man's voice, shouting at full volume, sound waves hitting the wall, pushed back by morning wind, echoing across the whole parking lot. Xiaoman stopped. Her sole was already half-lifted from concrete, suspended, then slowly came back down—boom, light shake. She turned. Not a "look back" turn. A slow, hesitant, whole-body re-facing of the alley mouth. Her stomach at this moment cringed again—not hunger pain, hunger stress. That word—"good food"—like a stone hitting her empty two-day stomach, sending out ripples.

She looked at the two.

The woman held the bread higher, phone in her other hand steadily aimed at Xiaoman's face. Her voice had an excited vibrato, like coaxing a stray cat, like displaying a rare treasure: "Come on—come here and there's good food—watch everyone, she moved! She really moved!"

Bullet comments rolled wildly on the screen. The woman didn't have time to look, but she knew what they were—gifts floating, popularity rising, viewers jumping from five to six digits. Her mouth corners lifted, camera chasing Xiaoman's face, chasing those enlarged amber eyes fixed on the bread.

Xiaoman moved.

Not the slow wall-braced shuffle from before. She walked straight toward the alley mouth. Her legs shook—hypoglycemia made her knees soft as cooked glass noodles, each step like pulling a leg from mud—but she didn't stop. Her sole hit concrete, boom boom boom, one shock after another, heavier than any time in the past three days, because she no longer had strength to control light steps. She was now a body driven by hunger, reason like a wind-blown leaf already trampled by primal survival instinct.

She stopped about ten meters from the alley mouth. Not voluntary—alley too narrow, her toes hit the wall edge, concrete crumbs falling from the wall top. She looked down at the row of food on the ground—crackers, ham sausage, bread, mineral water—to her only sesame-sized, scattered on the alley-mouth concrete like bait scattered on the ground. Her stomach cramped wildly, stomach acid burning the empty wall, saliva surging and swallowing, surging and swallowing. She hadn't chewed in three days, her teeth itched, her tongue moved restlessly in her mouth. She couldn't crouch—that motion too hard for her weak body now. She knelt.

Boom—! Ground shook violently. Her knees crushed two shallow pits in the alley-mouth concrete, dust rising a meter high. The woman screamed, stepping back several paces, phone almost slipping. But she steadied quickly, camera re-aimed—that shock had made the image shake like an earthquake, bullet comments exploded. The man jumped back a step too, but his camera still raised, muttering rapidly—"holy shit holy shit she knelt"—his voice trembling, but there was something else in it, not fear, but traffic-fueled adrenaline.

Xiaoman bent over. Her body arched into a slanted mountain, long hair like a black waterfall pouring from her shoulders, tips sweeping the alley-mouth concrete, kicking up a dust wind. She used two fingers to pinch the crackers on the ground—to her, that cracker bag was only a sesame seed. Her fingers too thick, the cracker bag crushed under her fingertip, crumbs embedding in fingerprint grooves. She tried again, still couldn't pick them up. Her fingers shook—hunger shakes, weakness shakes. She pressed her whole palm to the ground, like a giant pink-white rag wiping across the concrete. Crackers, ham sausage, bread, plus ground dust and gravel, all stuck to her palm.

She brought palm-sized food scraps to her mouth, stuck her tongue out and licked—gone. Those few breadcrumbs, cracker crumbs, ham bits, on her huge tongue didn't even have time to release a trace of taste before vanishing into her mouth. Her stomach didn't even feel something came in. Instead it grew hungrier—those few scraps stimulated more stomach acid, burning intensified, like throwing a spoonful of water on a burning furnace; the fire didn't go out, it exploded fiercer.

She lifted her eyes.

Those amber eyes, from the alley-mouth height, looked straight at the woman—looked at the bag of bread in the woman's hand. That was the bag she'd held above her head. The woman wasn't holding it now; she pressed the bread to her chest, phone still raised, but the lens shook. Not ground-shake shake, her own hand shaking. Xiaoman's gaze moved from bread to woman's face, from woman's face to her hand—the hand holding the bread. Her expression wasn't anger, wasn't threat, but pure, unhidden craving of a child starved three days. Like a stray cat staring at a ham sausage in a passerby's hand. Just thousands of times bigger.

The woman saw herself in those amber pupils, she also saw her own phone screen—on screen, bullet comments weren't gifting anymore. Comments were spamming the same sentence. She didn't have time to read, but she heard the man—he finally put down the camera, elbowed her, whispered: "Give it to her. Don't you see her eyes aren't right? Give it to her."

The woman threw the bread. Not handed, thrown. Her arm swung back, like throwing a grenade, body leaning back, tossing the bread into the air. The bread bag traced a glistening arc in morning light. Xiaoman's eyes tracked it. Her body reacted before her brain—she reached to catch, but her reaction speed in hypoglycemia was more than a beat slow. Her fingers grabbed empty air. The bread bag fell on concrete before her, plip, the plastic bag breaking, a few slices of white bread sliding out of the tear, landing in dust.

Xiaoman didn't hesitate. She picked up the bread—plus dust, gravel, grass bits stuck to the bag—and stuffed it all in her mouth. She chewed twice. Bread was sweet, cheapest supermarket white bread, lots of sugar and additives. To her, this was the first real food in three days. Her stomach finally waited. Although only one bag of bread, about one bite to her, but when that bite entered her stomach, her stomach wall finally stopped cramping. Like a storm lasting three days, suddenly quiet for a moment.

She swallowed. Then her eyes lifted again. Looked at the man. Looked at the backpack by his feet. Backpack zipper open, something inside—she saw the corner of another bread bag, and a mineral water bottle cap, reflecting fine light in morning sun. She just looked, didn't speak.


============================================================
<a id="Chapter28"></a>
## Chapter 28: The Fourth Day (Cont.) · Alley Mouth

Those two streamers were still livestreaming.

The phone screen in the woman's hand was already flooded with bullet comments. "Give it to her" "holy shit she's so pitiful" "where is this" "host just give it" layer upon layer, like colored tide rising. The woman didn't watch comments. She stared at Xiaoman's eyes—those amber, huge eyes reflecting her own image, motionlessly watching the backpack by the man's feet.

"She wants more." The woman said. That excited vibrato still in her voice, but underneath pressed something else—not softhearted, but calculation. Her eyes darted, glanced at the jumping viewer count on the phone. Six digits. Still rising.

"Obviously." The man unzipped the backpack. "I can see that."

He pulled out a bag of bread. This bag had been pressed at the backpack bottom who knows how long, packaging crumpled, the production-date spray code on the seal half-blurred. He didn't look at the date. This was what he'd grabbed at last week's supermarket discount—buy-one-get-one, cheap, he didn't eat it himself, it was all for "feeding." Throw a bit when filming strays, throw a bit when filming stray dogs, now filming a giant, throw a bit too.

He pulled out two more bags of crackers, a ham sausage, a mineral water. Crackers were near-expiry, he'd bought them by the jin at the wholesale market, normally stashed in the backpack as props. The ham sausage wrapper had a thin layer of dust—unknown how long it'd rolled around the bag. The water was new, because water didn't expire, but he twisted the cap, didn't open. Not sealed too well, but something glued between cap and bottle mouth. He twisted hard, click, opened. A faint, wrong smell for mineral water drifted out—the seal ring on the bottle mouth had aged. He didn't smell. He was busy laying things out one by one on the alley-mouth concrete in a row, then stepped back two paces.

"Left them here. Get them yourself." He said.

Xiaoman looked at those things. Sesame-sized food arranged in a row, packaging glinting in morning light. She didn't immediately take them. She looked up at the man. She remembered this person—not recognition, but remembering his actions just now. He set them down himself, didn't throw. He stepped back two paces, gave her space. She didn't know where the food came from, didn't know the production date was half-blurred, didn't know the ham sausage wrapper had backpack-bottom dust. She only knew this was food. This was food someone placed before her after she'd been hungry three days.

"...thank you." She said.

This was her first word in four days. Voice soft—a whisper to her, but the alley air still hummed. The woman's phone screen flashed, comments suddenly went quiet for half a second, then surged more wildly. "She said thank you" "I'm crying" "so polite" "host are you even human filming this" layer upon layer. The woman didn't look. She watched Xiaoman's fingers—those two huge pink-white fingers, carefully pinching toward the food on the ground.

The man paused. Then he laughed. Not the "filmed something good" laugh, not the "don't know how to respond after being earnestly thanked" laugh. A more complex laugh—mouth corners up, but eyes not laughing. Eyes calculating. Viewers online. Gift value. How many platforms this video could post-edit to. Title already thought up: "Giant Girl Trapped in Parking Lot, We Gave Her First Meal." No, better: "She'd Been Hungry Three Days, Said 'Thank You' to Us." Viral. Definitely viral.

"You're welcome. You... eat." He said. Voice gentle, intonation just right, every word stepping on the "kind passerby" persona.

Xiaoman used her fingers to pick up the food one by one. The bread bag made a soft plip under her fingertips—the bulging side of the packaging crushed—not her strength too great, but the bag was already bulging. That bulging—gas from bacteria reproducing weeks in the sealed bag inflating the plastic. She didn't notice. She put bread, crackers, ham sausage all in her mouth, chewed slowly. Bread had a faint sourness when bitten—not fermentation acid, but acid from decomposed spoiled protein by bacteria. Crackers had a faint rancid smell, oil oxidation. Ham sausage had a faint sticky texture, starch and meat paste separating and re-sticking after expiration.

But she couldn't taste it. Not dull taste buds—hunger overwhelmed everything. Someone starved three days has taste buds sensitive to only one thing: calories. Anything with calories automatically classified as "good" on her tongue. Sour? That's fermentation. Rancid? That's oil. Sticky? That's starch. Her tongue translated all these signals to the same word: food. Food is good. Food is survival.

She swallowed. Bread sweetness dissolved on her tongue—that sweetness wasn't from freshness, but from the bread having tons of sugar and preservatives, sugar stubbornly retaining sweetness after expiration. Ham sausage saltiness followed—that saltiness wasn't from good ham, but salt as preservative, more salt = longer shelf life. Cracker crumbs went crunch between her teeth—that crunch wasn't freshness, but crackers protected by preservatives and desiccants too long, moisture fully evaporated, only a fragile about-to-shatter skeleton left.

Her stomach finally got its second bite. Stomach wall stopped cramping. Like a storm quieting. But this wasn't real quiet—this was just the storm gathering for the next wave. Spoiled-food bacteria were traveling down her esophagus, soon reaching her stomach, then small intestine, then large intestine. Her immune system was already weakened by hunger, but hadn't detected these new invaders. Now she only felt warm—the thermic effect of food finally gave her body some temperature, from stomach to chest, chest to fingertips. Her fingertips weren't so cold.

She swallowed. Then twisted off the mineral water cap—to her only a grain of rice, she carefully pinched the cap with her nail tip, twisted gently, opened. A strand of sticky stuff pulled between cap and bottle mouth—not a plastic seal, but aged seal-ring residue dissolved in the water. She didn't see. She poured water into her mouth. Water flowed through cracked lips, down throat, into stomach. Water tasted a little off—not rotten, not sour, but the taste after sitting in a plastic bottle for half a year, sun-baked then shaded then sun-baked again. Like a summer trunk. Like forgetting. She tasted it. But didn't care—she'd drunk three days of rusty tap water in the parking lot, that rust was much stronger. This odd taste was practically nothing to her.

She closed her eyes, let her body absorb this sip. Then opened, looked toward the parking-lot exit. That was where Miaowu always appeared. She'd been going there. Now she had two bites of bread and a sip of water—not enough, far from enough, but a little more strength than before. Her stomach worked quietly, stirring those foods—fresh and not, harmful and harmless—together, digesting, pushing to small intestine.

"Can you understand us?" The woman suddenly asked. Phone still raised, lens on Xiaoman's face. Comments rolled wildly, but she didn't watch. She watched Xiaoman's expression—that huge pale face with cracked lips, those amber eyes focusing on her.

"Yes." Xiaoman said.

"What's your name?"

"Xiaoman. Full as in fullness."

The woman's mouth corners lifted. Not moved. But the satisfaction of "interview Q&A going smoothly." She'd done homework before coming—this kind of "encounter with non-human" livestream's core selling point wasn't visuals, it was interaction. Viewers wanted to see "they're just like us"—can talk, have a name, can be moved, can say thank you. Only proving "they're just like us" turns viewers' curiosity into sympathy, sympathy into gifts. Her stream was now six-digit online, gift value rising at two-digit-per-second speed. She needed this giant girl to say more. The more the better.

"How long have you been here?" She asked, voice with deliberate gentleness, like coaxing a kindergarten kid.

Xiaoman thought. Day one Miaowu found her, Granny cooked. Day two she waited long, no one came. Day three no one either. Today is day four. Her finger lightly traced her knee, like counting days.

"Four days." She said.

"You've been hungry four days?!" The woman's voice suddenly rose. Not acting this time—she was genuinely shocked. But shock lasted less than a second. Next second, her peripheral vision caught the exploding comment count on her screen; her expression instantly switched from shock to anger—the kind for the camera, performed, speaking-for-the-weak anger. "This is terrible! How could she be left here hungry four days!" She said to the phone, voice with righteous trembling, then turned to the man: "You—do you have anything in your car? Bring it all!"

The man caught on. He knew what this "anger" was—not real anger, but plot requirement. The stream needed conflict, needed "good guy" and "bad guy," needed a "savior" persona. He turned toward the car parked outside the alley. A white SUV, trunk-opening sound carrying through the wall. He rummaged a while, pulled out a plastic bag. Same as before—supermarket discount grabs, wholesale market by-the-jin, production dates blurred. But he didn't care. Viewers couldn't see dates. Viewers only saw him carrying a bag of food toward the poor giant girl.

"It's all yours! Eat slow!" The woman called to Xiaoman, laying the bag's contents on the ground one by one. Sliced bread, ham sausage, crackers, chocolate, mineral water, a bag of apples. She arranged them neatly, saying into the phone after each: "this is bread" "this is ham" "this is apple," like an unboxing review. Comments spammed "may good people have peace" "host is so kind" "followed followed." She saw, mouth corners lifted again.

Xiaoman looked at the pile of food on the ground. To her, about the size of a small handful of sunflower seeds. But she saw the apples—red, round, glinting in morning light. She used her fingers to pick up an apple. Apple like a tiny red candy ball on her fingertip. She put it in her mouth, bit gently. Juice exploded in her mouth—sour. Very sour. Not the apple's natural sweet-sour, but the sourness of fermenting flesh from sitting too long, with a faint alcohol tang. This apple had been pressed in a bottom wholesale-market box for at least ten days, skin already had a few brown soft spots, but soft spots too tiny for Xiaoman's fingertip to feel. She only tasted juice. Sour, sweet, alcohol, mixing into a strange taste on her tongue. But she didn't care. Sour—that's fruit taste. Lemons she ate in the giant country were sour too. She swallowed. Flesh mixed in her stomach with spoiled bread, oxidized crackers, expired ham, becoming a warm, fermenting mixture.

She finished. Stomach had a little weight now. Far from enough, but her legs didn't shake as badly as before. She wiped her mouth with the back of her hand, then looked down at the two.

"Thank you." She said again.

The woman stood there, phone in hand, lens on Xiaoman. Comments were crazy—gifts floating, likes rising, viewers jumping from six to seven digits. Her palms sweated, but she didn't wipe. She was thinking a question: what next? Food given, Q&A about over, viewer novelty sliding from peak. She needed a new spark. A spark that could take this stream from "seven digits" to "eight." She looked at Xiaoman—this eighteen-meter, four-day-starved giant girl who said "thank you." Then she raised the phone, aimed at Xiaoman's eyes, in that most gentle, most caring, kitten-coaxing voice:

"Xiaoman, do you need anything else? Tell me. Big sister will help you."

Xiaoman looked at the woman. She didn't understand "big sister"—she only had one sister, Xiao Gu. But her attention was caught by the question. "Do you need anything else"—that was a question she could understand. Her needs were many. She needed food, water, a clean skirt, to find Miaowu, to find her way home, to hear Mom say "Xiaoman wake up breakfast is ready." These needs lined up in her heart like kindergarteners lining up for the slide. She didn't know which to say first. Then the one at the front jumped out on its own.

"I want to find my friend." She said.

The woman's phone steadied. Her pupils slightly contracted behind the lens—not moved, but excited. A new keyword: "friend." This giant girl had a friend. Where was this friend? Who? What species? How big? Could they appear in the next stream? The woman's brain was already running three scripts simultaneously—one emotional, helping the giant girl find her lost friend, sad piano soundtrack; one suspense, what is the giant girl's friend, layer upon layer, teasing; one variety, putting her and her friend together, doing interactions, doing tasks, making a "giant sisters" IP.

"Your friend?" The woman's voice grew gentler, sweet enough to drip honey. "What's her name? What does she look like? Tell big sister, big sister will help you find her."

Xiaoman looked down at the woman. Those amber eyes glinted in morning light—not the weak glint from before, but the glint of grabbing a driftwood. Her lips moved, voice soft, but echoed in the alley like wind from a valley.

"Her name is Miaowu." She said. "Very small. Much smaller than me. Wears a blue school uniform. Hair messy. Her voice is thin, like a hamster. She said... she said see you tomorrow. But she didn't come."

The woman's finger swiped fast on the phone screen—opened notes, thumb typed fast: Miaowu, child, uniform, thin voice, see you tomorrow. She finished the last character, mouth corners lifted, then quickly pressed them down, switched to a "I'll definitely help you" determined expression.

"Miaowu," she repeated the name, tone with just-right heartache, "such a pretty name. Don't worry, big sister will definitely help you find her."

They left.

The woman put the phone in her bag, screen still hot. The man zipped the backpack, walked out of the alley. Their footsteps faded down the alley, occasional muted conversation—not about Xiaoman, but about stream data, platform cuts, whether to come back tomorrow to a new spot. Their car started, engine sound by the alley a while, then scattered by wind.

The parking lot was quiet again.

Xiaoman watched them go, didn't shout, didn't chase. She just slowly stood—boom, ground shook—then walked back to the awning. Walk steadier than before, because stomach had something. Those bread, crackers, ham, apple being digested in her stomach, thermic effect giving her body a little warmth. But legs still weak, knees shaking, dizziness not fully gone. She walked under the awning, sat, back of head against wall, pulled the peony quilt over her legs.

Morning wind blew from the sea, carrying salt and distant soy-milk steam. Ivy leaves rustled. Through the awning hole, sky fully bright, a few tiny clouds like torn cotton candy, drifting very slowly.

Then, about half an hour later, the first wave of nausea came.

Not a "bit nauseous" feeling. Deeper, more urgent, a force surging from the stomach bottom upward, like an invisible hand twisting her stomach then pushing up. Xiaoman was staring blankly at the parking-lot entrance when her throat suddenly felt blocked. She frowned, swallowed, tried to push the feeling down. But after swallowing, her saliva was sour—not normal acid reflux, but sharper, more irritating, with a chemical burning sensation. Her tongue root recoiled from the sourness, salivary glands flooded, mouth sour and astringent, like holding a rusty coin.

That swallow wasn't ordinary acid reflux. It was bacteria in spoiled bread—Bacillus subtilis, Bacillus licheniformis, possibly a little Staphylococcus aureus—dying struggling in stomach acid, releasing enterotoxins. These toxins aren't destroyed by acid, instead stimulate stomach wall cells to secrete massive acid, while toxin molecules cross gastric mucosa into bloodstream, stimulating the vomiting center in the medulla.

Her stomach started violent contractions. Not slow empty hunger, but spasms—pylorus closed tight, cardiac sphincter open, whole stomach like a squeezed fist pushing in reverse. She felt the contents—bread, crackers, ham, apple—being stirred by acid into a sour-smelling chyme, then shoved by cramping stomach wall toward the esophagus.

She bent over. This action was body's own—abdominal muscles suddenly contracted, diaphragm dropped violently, chest pressure spiked. Her hands braced on concrete, fingers scraping shush-shush on rough concrete. Peony quilt slid from her knee, piling by her feet.

Then she threw up.

Not slowly. Contents shoved by spasm, bursting through cardiac sphincter, into esophagus, into throat, then "whoosh—" erupted from her mouth. Sound huge—not liquid spilling, but the deep, water-and-air roar of large-volume semi-fluid matter ejecting at high speed. Vomit sprayed radially on the concrete before the awning, range wider than her whole body.

Her throat burned from acid. This was her esophagus's first chemical burn of this intensity—expired food stimulated excess acid, pH low enough to burn mucosa. Esophageal epithelial cells were being corroded by acid, nerve endings sending sharp pain signals to brain. Mouth full of sour rot, plus a strange bitterness—maybe fat oxidation byproducts from expired ham, maybe alcohol and other spoiled byproducts from fermented apple. She wiped her mouth with her skirt, then looked down at what she'd vomited. Vomit was pale brown, mixed with white bread crumbs, pink ham paste, yellow cracker bits, red apple peel fragments, and yellow-green gastric juice. Liquid part turbid, a thin oil layer floating—that was fat from expired ham. Smell sharp—acid rot mixed with food putrefaction, steaming under the awning.

She panted, hands still bracing on the ground. Heart fast—vomit reflex stimulates vagus nerve, vagal excitement causes heart rate rise then fall, now her rate much faster than before, temple vessels throbbing. Cold sweat beaded on her forehead, ran down her nose bridge, dripping into the vomit. Her hand shook. Not hunger shake, but body's instinctive reaction to violent abdominal pressure changes after vomiting, and weakened muscle control from water and electrolyte imbalance.

After about two minutes, she thought it was over. She wiped her mouth with her skirt—the light brown marks on her skirt hem added several new ones, wetter, sourer. Her throat still burned, esophageal burn not fading, sour stench in her own nose making her wrinkle it.

Then the second wave came. Not "again," but something deeper.

Her colon was peristalsising. Not normal peristalsis, but spasmodic, violent, uncontrolled. Bacteria in spoiled food—Salmonella, E. coli—hadn't been fully killed in the small intestine; they reached the large intestine alive, found a warm, moist, nutrient-rich environment, started multiplying wildly. Toxins from bacterial metabolism stimulated intestinal mucosa, secreting massive water and electrolytes into the gut, while stimulating intestinal nerve plexus, triggering a strong excretion reflex. She felt that familiar signal—not the gentle normal urge that can wait. More urgent, more violent, unavoidable twisting. Like an invisible hand wringing her lower belly hard.

She stood. This made her dizzy—orthostatic hypotension plus post-vomit dehydration, brain blood supply briefly lacking. She braced on the wall, waited for the blackness to clear, then clamped her legs and walked toward the pool. Not walking, but shuffling—each step as small as possible, because every step pushed abdominal pressure outward. She felt that stuff already pressed to the exit, not solid, but liquid, watery, warm, intestinal secretions and digestive juices flushed out by bacterial toxins.

She reached the edge, turned, crouched. Skirt hanging into the bottom—she didn't even have time to gather it properly; just as she crouched, her body gave up control.

Not normal bowel movement. Watery diarrhea.

Thin, turbid, yellow-brown liquid erupted from her body like a broken tap. Water wasn't absorbed by the colon—bacterial toxins shifted colon mucosa from absorption to secretion mode, water forcibly pulled from blood and tissue fluid into the gut, mixed with unabsorbed chyme residue, bacterial corpses, intestinal mucus, and shed epithelial cells, all expelled. Sound loud—"psss—"—then continuous, jetting "whoosh," like someone opened a water bag inside her. Smell much stronger than usual—sulfides, ammonia, indole, skatole from bacterial fermentation, mixed with expired-food chemicals, forming a sharp, pungent stench that almost stung the nasal mucosa. Old traces on the bottom washed by new liquid, old salt frost dissolved, old feces dispersed, whole bottom becoming a turbid, bubbling mud lake.

She crouched at the edge, hands gripping the rim. Nails leaving white marks on concrete. Cold sweat dripped from her forehead, onto her knees, onto the bottom, mixing with the turbid liquid. Her legs shook violently—not low blood sugar, but muscle tremors from repeated abdominal pressure changes during diarrhea. Anus and rectum end burned—frequent bowel movements made perianal skin red and swollen from friction, while bile acids and digestive enzymes in watery stool continuously irritated skin, causing stinging. She felt like she was being hollowed out. Vomit from the top, diarrhea from the bottom. Both ends flowing out. Her body was using its most violent way to expel things it shouldn't have eaten.

Stopped.

She panted, crouched there not daring to move. Legs completely soft, knees lightly knocking on concrete, making faint tremors. Her fingers still dug the rim, knuckles white. Then she felt her lower belly twitch again. Not another diarrhea signal—the intestinal muscles slowly relaxing after spasm, that slow, uneven peristalsis, like a very long snake slowly straightening in her belly.

Twitched again. This time lighter. Then a gust of air slid from her anus—not on purpose—the previously backed-up gas pushed out the exit the moment the intestine released after spasm. Sound small, almost just a slight vibration at the exit. But smell stank—sulfides from bacterial fermentation, mixed with the stench of previous watery stool, adding another layer of invisible but smellable thick fog above the turbid lake on the bottom.

She lifted her skirt from the bottom. Skirt edge dipped into the bottom's liquid—the turbid, feces-and-digestive-juice-mixed liquid. Light-blue fabric stained pale brown, edge still dripping, hitting rim concrete with small plips. She grabbed the skirt in her hand, twisted—water squeezed from fabric, carrying that pungent stench. Her ear tips reddened, but not as red as before. Not shame, but weakness. Weakness overwhelmed everything. Her body had no strength to allocate to blushing, that high-energy facial vasodilation.

She stood. Boom, light shake. Knees still shook. Those few steps from pool to awning, she stopped once—not from weak legs, but stomach churned again. She bent over, dry-heaved once. Nothing came out. Stomach already empty. Bread, crackers, ham, apple, all vomited clean; now her stomach only had a little acid and bile mixture, yellow-green, bitter, corrosive. Dry heave made her throat hurt more—acid flushed her esophagus again during vomiting, burn area enlarged. She wiped her mouth with her skirt. Then kept walking.

Back to the awning. She didn't immediately sit—she picked up the peony quilt from the ground, folded it, set it beside the rice sacks. Lower half still wet, but the vomit smell had covered half of it. She looked down at the vomit patch—that large pale-brown patch on concrete, mixed with bread crumbs and ham bits, slowly steaming in morning light, smell spreading under the whole awning. She moved to the corner farthest from the vomit, sat. Back of head against wall. Eyes closed.

Now her digestive tract was empty. Stomach empty—vomit emptied it. Large intestine empty—diarrhea emptied it. Those expired foods walked through her body, leaving not nutrition, but trauma. Stomach mucosa inflamed, esophagus inflamed, colon mucosa inflamed. Gut flora disrupted by bacterial contamination, probiotics and pathogens at war in her colon, results not revealed tomorrow—but in the next few hours, layer by layer, through new waves of cramping and excretion. Her immune system had started—white blood cells gathering at gastric and intestinal mucosa, inflammatory factors releasing, temperature might rise slightly in the next few hours. But she wouldn't notice. She only knew she shook—not from cold, but low blood sugar plus electrolyte imbalance after vomiting and diarrhea causing muscle weakness. She only knew her heartbeat was fast—but weak, like a dying-battery clock striking frantically.

She hugged her knees, chin on knees. Long hair hanging, covering half her face. Lips cracked worse—vomiting and diarrhea lost massive water, although she'd drunk half a bottle of water just now, that wasn't nearly enough to replenish. She needed water, but didn't dare go to the tap. Not afraid of not being able to walk, but afraid that walking would churn her stomach again, twist her intestines again. She needed to go, but needed to wait. Needed her body to tell her "okay, temporarily safe now." She waited long. Wind from the sea made the awning tarpaulin flutter. Vomit smell scattered bit by bit, pool smell also carried over—the two smells mixing under the awning, sour and stinking, but she had no strength left to frown. She buried her face in her knees, eyes closed. Stomach empty, but body kept working—acid still secreted, intestines still peristalsising, spoiled-food toxins still traveling down layer by layer.

Unknown how long. Sun moved overhead. Then west. Awning shadow moved left to right. Her body expelled twice more in the next few hours—not that violent jetting diarrhea, but slower, thinner, small amounts of watery stool. Each time first felt a light intestinal cramp, then stood, walked to the pool, crouched, expelled a bit of turbid yellow-brown liquid. Amount less and less—because intestines empty, only mucosal secretions and bacterial metabolic water. But she still didn't dare not go. Because each came urgent, she was afraid if she didn't go she'd mess in the awning. She'd left too many traces on this concrete already, didn't want to add more. Although the vomit patch was still there, although the pool bottom had become an indescribable swamp, she still walked to the pool each time, crouched, completed that round of excretion. This was the only "cleanliness" she could maintain.

Sun started tilting west. Ivy shadows on the wall stretched very long. Through the awning hole, sky turned from pale blue to orange-red, then orange-red to dark purple.

She went to the pool once more. This time less—only a few drops of thin mucus-mixed liquid, color so pale it was almost just yellowish. Crouching poolside, she heard her stomach growl. Not the pre-vomit dull sound, but hunger. She'd vomited empty. Diarrhea empty. Those expired foods left not nutrition but trauma. Her stomach shrank back into an empty, cramping pouch. A body starved four days, further drained by vomiting and diarrhea of its last energy.

She went back to the awning, sat. Pulled the peony quilt over her legs—lower half still wet, but her legs colder than the quilt. She hugged her knees, chin on knees. Her lips trembled—not from cold, but hypoglycemia and electrolyte imbalance altering neuromuscular excitability.

Sky fully darkened. Streetlights came on, dim yellow light over the wall. She looked at the parking-lot entrance. Entrance empty. Miaowu still didn't come.

She closed her eyes. Her heartbeat still light, weak, like a dying-battery clock striking far away. Her body kept working—acid still secreted, gut flora still fighting, damaged mucosal cells replaced by new cells. These processes went on where she didn't know. She just breathed. One, two, three, four. At the seventh, she remembered Miaowu sitting in her palm, fuzz balls on the uniform cuff, wrist thin as a twig. She'd thought then: Miaowu needs to eat more. Now she was also hungry, she was also thinking: Miaowu needs to eat more. Her lips moved silently. Maybe "Miaowu," maybe "see you tomorrow." Maybe just trembling lips.

Night wind blew from the sea, through wall cracks, scattering the last vomit smell under the awning. A soft "pfft" came from the pool—bottom bubbles popping in the mud. She fell asleep. Not deep, but drifting. Her body worked on in the dark, repairing what could be repaired, enduring what couldn't. Tomorrow would be the fifth day. She didn't know. She just slept.

<a id="Chapter29"></a>
## Chapter 29: The Fifth Day · Reunion

Fifth morning, Miaowu stood at the door tying shoelaces.

Sneakers dry. Uniform dry. Hair in a ponytail, new blue hair tie. She tied laces—left over right, right over left, pulled tight, bow—then stood, picked up the smartwatch from the shoe cabinet, wore it on her wrist. Screen lit. Green numbers still jumped: $736.03. She didn't bring the pillbox today, didn't bring toast, didn't bring the red basin. She brought only one thing—the strawberry lollipop in her pocket, wrapper already wrinkled, the one she'd gotten from the butcher and never had the heart to eat.

Mom wasn't home. She hadn't come back from night shift. A note was pressed on the coffee table, written in marker, big characters: Breakfast in microwave. Medicine in tray. Mom home at 9.

Miaowu took her medicine. Two white tablets, dry swallow. Didn't touch toast. She flipped the note over, wrote four characters on the back—going to find Xiaoman—pressed it under the pillbox. Then she pushed open the door, walked into the alley.

Alley sunlight was already bright. She walked alone, sneakers pattering on concrete. No Mom following, no Dad asking "where are you going." Today was the fifth day, she'd waited four "tomorrows," today had to be the one she'd promised.

Parking-lot entrance. Ivy leaves rustled in morning wind on the wall. Delivery rider's e-bike gone. Granny's trailer gone. Iron gate half open, the faded blue sign still there, characters washed more blurred by rain. Miaowu rounded the wall corner.

Awning still there. The hole in the tarpaulin still there. Peony quilt folded and set beside the rice sacks—neatly folded, though the corners had a ring of white salt frost. Three empty rice sacks stacked, edges aligned. Egg carton also folded, set beside rice sacks. Bone pile cleaned by ants, only a few dried tendon threads left. Concrete had gray-white broom marks—no broom, pushed by sole.

Then she saw the vomit. On the concrete before the awning, already half-dry. Edges caked in a dark brown shell, center still had a little unevaporated liquid, glinting dull oily in morning light. Smell had mostly dispersed, but close up still detectable—sour, rotten, mixed with gastric juice and spoiled-food residue. A few flies crawled on it. Farther, toward the pool, a paler water stain—edges dry, center with a little residue, glinting white salt frost.

Miaowu stopped. She looked at the vomit, looked long. Her nose wrinkled, but she didn't cover it, didn't turn away. She just stood there, finger moved at her pants seam—not picking, but tracing. Drew a small circle in the air. She remembered Xiaoman crouching with her back to everyone on the lawn four days ago, remembered her voice trembling when she said "Miaowu... can you... can you... not look." Then she walked around the vomit toward the awning. Her sneakers hit concrete, patter, patter, patter. Each step light but firm, as if afraid to wake something.

Under the awning, Xiaoman leaned against the wall, curled on the peony quilt.

Her pose was exactly like four days ago—knees to chest, arms around belly, forehead almost touching knees. Long hair hanging like a black waterfall, fully covering her face. Light-blue dress covered in mark after mark—brown, yellow, white, layered, like a map drawn over many days. Skirt edges still damp, clinging to her ankles. Her toes slightly curled in sleep, nails sticky with salt frost and dust. Her breathing shallow and slow, each exhale carrying a very faint, ketosis-specific rotten-apple sweetness. Her face paler than four days ago, lips cracked worse, the dry skin on her lower lip's middle curling up, edges with a little brown scab. She'd lost weight. Collarbone more prominent than four days ago, wrist bones sticking out from cuffs like two thin twigs. But her breathing was even. She was alive.

Miaowu stopped before her. She looked up at this curled, dirty, shallow-breathing pink-white mountain. Her face had no expression—that blankness of a zoning-out second-grader, eyes round, mouth slightly open. Then she looked down, took the strawberry lollipop from her pocket. Wrapper wrinkled, she carefully peeled it, held the lollipop up, above her head, like holding a small pink flag.

"Xiaoman." She said, voice thin as a glass bead on a porcelain plate. "I'm here."

Xiaoman's lashes moved. Didn't wake.

"Xiaoman," Miaowu said again, "I brought you a lollipop. Strawberry. The butcher uncle gave it. I never ate it. Kept it for you."

Lashes moved again. Then that broom-thick lash row slowly lifted. Xiaoman's eyes showed a slit beneath—amber, turbid, like two moons half-covered by clouds. She blinked. Blinked again. She saw Miaowu—that small second-grader in a crumpled uniform, ponytail, holding up a pink lollipop, standing by her knee, looking up at her.

"Mia... owu?" Her breath was hoarse as a broken horn, soft as a feather on water. "Is... is it you?"

"It's me." Miaowu said.

Xiaoman's pupils slowly focused. Those amber eyes finally reflected Miaowu's image—small, holding a lollipop, fingers steady. Her lips started trembling. Not hunger trembling, but something else. Her eyes reddened, but tears didn't fall—not unwilling to cry, but body had no extra water to allocate to tears. She just moved one hand from her knee, slowly reached toward Miaowu. That hand—pink-white, wider than a car, palm lines embedded with concrete dust and poolside salt frost—stopped before Miaowu. Not grabbing, but open. Palm up, like a huge, dirty, but still warm pink landmass.

"You... came back..." Xiaoman said, voice breaking into pieces. "You said... see you tomorrow... I counted... four tomorrows..."

"I know." Miaowu said. "Mom locked me at home. She thought you were imaginary. She thought I was spending money recklessly. She locked my watch in a drawer. I couldn't get out."

Xiaoman's lashes fluttered. It took her several seconds to process this—her brain still in low-blood-sugar fog, but one word pierced the fog. "Mom"—Miaowu has a mom. Miaowu's mom locked Miaowu at home. Not that Miaowu didn't want to come, but Miaowu couldn't. She pushed that pink landmass a little farther, to the tips of Miaowu's toes.

"Your... your mom... doesn't like me?" Her breath held a new, carefully timid fear.

Miaowu thought. "She hasn't met you. She doesn't know you. She doesn't understand. Adults get scared of what they don't understand. When scared, they lock doors. But she went to work today. Door unlocked. I came out."

She stepped onto that pink landmass. Sneakers on Xiaoman's palm lines, sole tread overlapping Xiaoman's lifeline, like two rivers of different proportions meeting. She walked to the palm center, turned, sat cross-legged. She raised the lollipop, brought the candy ball to Xiaoman's nose—to her only a sesame seed, but strawberry scent already drifted, sweet, in the awning's sour-rot vomit smell and pool stench, like a small pink star.

"For you. Dessert after a meal. No—before a meal. You haven't eaten yet."

Xiaoman used two fingers to pick up that "sesame seed." Her fingers shook—hypoglycemia had degraded her fine motor control. It took two tries to pinch it, then she carefully put it in her mouth. Strawberry sweetness exploded on her tongue. Not expired bread's sour fake sweetness, not spoiled apple's fermented alcohol sweetness. Real, clean, the sweetness of a strawberry lollipop a second-grader had treasured for four days. Her tongue activated by this sweetness, saliva surging from sublingual glands, wrapping the candy ball, sweetness spreading from tip to root, to throat, to her whole mouth.

She closed her eyes. A tear finally condensed on her lashes—very small, only ping-pong sized, hanging at her eye corner, refracting a small rainbow in morning light.

"Sweet." She said, voice muffled, candy still in her mouth. "It's strawberry."

"Yeah." Miaowu said. "You said last time lemon was sweet, strawberry is sweeter. I remembered."

"You... remembered four days?"

"Yeah. Kept it in my pocket. Slept with it. Put it back after shower."

Xiaoman took the lollipop from her mouth. Candy ball had shrunk a size, like a transparent pale pink gem in morning light. She looked at the cross-legged second-grader in her palm—ponytail, watch on wrist, two quiet hands on her knees. She remembered Miaowu four days ago saying "I don't know what to say," remembered Miaowu saying "my stomach isn't growling, I'm not hungry," remembered Miaowu eating glass noodles counting one two three four five six seven then saying "enough." This kid had no taste, didn't know hunger, but she remembered strawberry is sweeter than lemon. She kept the candy in her pocket four days, waiting to give it to her.

"Miaowu." She said.

"Hm?"

"You just said—your mom locked you at home. She thought you spent money recklessly."

"Yeah."

"Then you... will you still buy custard buns?"

Miaowu looked down at her watch. Screen lit. Green numbers still jumped: $736.03. She poked the screen with her finger, then turned it toward Xiaoman.

"Still seven hundred thirty-six dollars and three cents. Enough for lots of custard buns. The flowing-out kind."

Xiaoman's eyes lit. Weak, but genuinely lit. Her stomach growled at that moment—"grrr—"—not previous sharp cramp, not yesterday's balloon-leaking hiss, but a more normal, more "I'm ready" hunger signal. Her stomach after four days of idling, two days of burning, yesterday's vomiting and diarrhea, finally—this moment—in strawberry sweetness and Miaowu's voice—recovered some gentle, anticipatory peristalsis. Not spasm, but peristalsis. Normal, food-accepting peristalsis.

"I'm hungry." Xiaoman said. This was her first time saying it in a normal tone—not pleading, not aggrieved, not breathy, just ordinary "I'm hungry." Like at home in the giant country, waking morning, smelling Mom's strawberry toast, then rolling over and calling through the door "Mom I'm hungry."

Xiaoman's stomach growled at that moment—"grrr—"—not previous sharp cramp, not yesterday's balloon-leaking hiss, but a more normal, more "I'm ready" hunger signal. She'd just said "I'm hungry," voice hadn't dissipated under the awning, when her stomach suddenly churned.

Not a hungry gurgle. A deeper, more urgent force surging from the stomach bottom. She'd just said "I'm hungry," her stomach had answered "grrr," but that growl's tail hadn't landed before being cut off by a more violent sound. Her stomach was contracting—not normal hunger peristalsis, but spasm. Yesterday's expired-food bacteria—those Bacillus subtilis, Staphylococcus aureus—were mostly killed in stomach acid, but their enterotoxins still remained on her gastric mucosa. Just now the strawberry candy stimulated saliva, saliva flowed down the esophagus into the stomach, stomach wall activated by sugar, started secreting acid, then acid hit those un-repaired mucosal injuries—her stomach hadn't recovered from yesterday's vomiting and burning.

She didn't even have time to turn her head. Vomit rushed from her mouth—not yesterday's full sequence of nausea then spasm then bending. This time no transition from "normal" to "out of control." She was still looking at Miaowu, lips still in the shape of saying "I'm hungry," then her stomach pushed the contents up.

Not a small mouthful this time. Large-area, violent, yellow-green liquid mixed with acid and bile. Yesterday's spoiled-food toxins still remained on her gastric mucosa; this morning's few sips of water—no food to dilute—mixed with acid, forming a highly irritating acidic environment. Her stomach wall was cramping, cardiac sphincter forced open in spasm, then stomach contents shoved from below like an invisible hand pushing upward.

She had no time to turn, no time to move Miaowu away, no time to do anything. Vomit erupted from her mouth, fanning downward—to her only a bowing height, but to Miaowu, it was a warm, sour-smelling torrent pouring from six stories up. Sound huge—not liquid spilling, but the deep water-and-air roar of large-volume semi-fluid ejecting at high speed. "Whoosh—" like a kicked-over bucket of water.

Vomit hit the concrete before Miaowu, splashing a radial spray. But more poured on Miaowu. From head to feet, hair to uniform to sneakers, whole body shrouded in that yellow-green, warm, acid-and-undigested-strawberry-candy-residue waterfall. Her bangs plastered to her forehead, ends dripping turbid liquid. Uniform's deep blue turned darker, yellow-green wet mark from shoulders to knees. The frayed cuff threads soaked, sticking to her wrist. A small stream of liquid poured into her sneakers, shoe upper from gray-white to yellow-brown.

Smell exploded. Not yesterday's stuffy slow-steaming sour rot under the awning. Stronger, more direct, acid-and-bile pungency, plus a little strawberry candy sweetness—that candy hadn't fully digested, sugar sweetness sticking out in acid rot, like finding an un-melted rock candy in a pile of rotting leaves.

Miaowu stood in place.

Her hair dripped. Her uniform dripped. Her sneakers made squelch sounds. Her face was splashed too—a small patch of yellow-green droplets on her left cheek, running down her chin. She didn't move. Not stunned. Because her routines had no "what to do when vomited on" option. Morning routine was wake, toilet, brush teeth, change, medicine, toast, leave. Evening routine was picture book, drawer, lights out, sleep. Being drenched in Xiaoman's vomit wasn't in any routine. Routine crashed. So she stood there, motionless, eyes round, mouth slightly open. Her face was still blank—but blanker than usual, like a machine suddenly fed unprocessable information.

Xiaoman froze.

Her pupils contracted violently at that moment, like two moons suddenly covered by storm clouds. Her gaze nailed to Miaowu—that small second-grader in crumpled uniform, ponytail, standing on the concrete before her, dripping yellow-green liquid head to toe. Miaowu's bangs on her forehead, ends dripping; uniform front soaked, clinging; liquid in sneakers seeping out, pooling into a small turbid puddle under her soles. Xiaoman watched that yellow-green liquid run down Miaowu's left cheek, to chin, drip on her collar.

Her hand started shaking violently. Not low blood sugar—though her sugar was still low. Another kind of shake. The inescapable panic welling up from deep within, like someone stuffed ice in her chest, ice melting, meltwater gushing from her eye sockets. Her breathing grew rapid and shallow, each inhale raising wind, making the awning tarpaulin flutter, making ripples on the vomit surface.

"I'm sorry sorry sorry—!"

Her voice doubled volume—on purpose, out of control. Breath gushed from her mouth, blowing liquid from Miaowu's bangs backward. She frantically raised her hand, wanting to wipe the vomit from Miaowu's face, but her fingers stopped abruptly before Miaowu—she was too big. Her fingertip was wider than Miaowu's whole body; if she wiped, she might knock Miaowu over. Her hand hung in midair, fingers open then clenched, open then clenched, like a huge pink-white bird not knowing where to land. Her tears finally burst—not one by one, but strings, rolling from eye corners, down cheeks, pooling into bigger drops at her chin, then plip-plip-plopping on concrete, on the vomit, splashing concentric turbid ripples.

"I'm sorry—I—I didn't mean to—it was my stomach—my stomach itself—it—it didn't listen to me—I'm sorry—I—you—you waited four days to come—and I—I got you dirty—I got you dirty—I got you—" Her voice shattered into pieces, every word trembling. Her fingers still hung in the air, not daring to touch Miaowu, only digging into her own knees. Nails scraped against the dress fabric with a crunching sound, exactly like the sound four days ago when she'd scratched white marks into the concrete. "You—you waited four days to come—and I—I got you—" She dry-heaved again. Not because there was anything left in her stomach—the stomach was empty, that last mouthful had emptied it of acid and bile. It was her throat spasming. Panic made her diaphragm contract violently, esophagus twitching from reflux irritation, and she emitted dry heave after dry heave, each time arching her back, lowering her head, pressing both hands hard against her belly—but nothing came up. Only a few drops of yellow-green bile leaked from the corners of her mouth, falling on her dress, on her already unspeakably soiled skirt.

"I'm sorry—I really—I didn't want to—I didn't want to throw up on you—I—" She dry-heaved apologies, apologized while dry-heaving, her whole body curled into a trembling, soggy, tear-and-bile-stained little mountain. Her long hair slid from her shoulders, ends dipping into the vomit on the ground, stained yellow-green. Her nails dug deeper into her knees, knuckles white as if about to pierce the skin.

Miaowu stood in the middle of that yellow-green puddle. Her hair still dripped, her uniform still dripped, her sneakers still squelched. Her face was still blank—but her lashes twitched. Not a scared twitch, a program-restart twitch. Her brain ran a background search: comfort friend—person crying—say "it's okay"—play comfort phrase. She opened her mouth, about to say "it's okay because this is the most n—" then she stopped. Not because she'd choked on vomit. Because her own program had frozen. She remembered what Xiaoman had said in her palm four days ago: "You don't need to play back what other people say. Your words are enough." She stood there, dripping yellow-green from head to toe, her brain in a blank crash, struggling to boot a different program—not playback, but thinking. Thinking herself. From zero.

Xiaoman threw up. Xiaoman cried. Xiaoman said "sorry." Xiaoman dry-heaved. Xiaoman felt terrible. What should I say? The player has no lines for this scene. The teacher never taught "how to respond when a friend vomits all over you." Mom never taught it. The doctor never taught it. Picture books didn't have it. Social stories didn't have it. Social stories said "friends are small, as small as Bobo." But Xiaoman isn't small. Xiaoman is enormous, Xiaoman is someone who accidentally vomits on you. But Xiaoman is also the one who put you on her collarbone, who lifted you to eye level, who counted to eight when her finger touched the top of your head. So what should you say?

She looked down at her uniform front. Soaked through. Yellow-green liquid trickling downward, the fuzz around her cuffs matted together. She looked back up at Xiaoman. Xiaoman was still dry-heaving, shoulders jerking, tears plopping one by one, mumbling "sorry" through her teeth, dry-heaving with each apology. She looked a mess—worse than the first day on the lawn, worse than wetting the shelter in the middle of the night, worse than yesterday's expired-food torture. Her face crumpled from retching and crying, tears, snot, and bile mixing together, smearing the vomit residue at the corner of her mouth even worse. But those amber eyes looked at Miaowu through the gaps between tears—not looking away, not pretending nothing happened, just looking, waiting. Waiting to be scolded, waiting to be pushed away, waiting for this only friend to say "you're disgusting, I'm leaving."

Miaowu stepped forward. Not back—forward. Sneakers squelched in the vomit, splashing a muddy spray. Another step. Two. She reached Xiaoman's outstretched hand on the ground—that index finger, pink-white, nails round, nail seams embedded with salt frost and dust, unconsciously scratching at the concrete, making that crunching sound. She reached out her own hand. Tiny, five fingers spread, not even as big as Xiaoman's fingernail. She placed it on Xiaoman's index finger. Not patting—placing. Light as a leaf landing on water.

"It's okay," she said, her voice thin as a glass bead dropped on a porcelain plate, bouncing once. "Letting it out is better. You ate something bad. Throw up, and your stomach won't hurt anymore."

Xiaoman's dry heaving stopped. Not because the stomach stopped spasming—her body froze in that instant. She looked down at the tiny, soggy, yellow-green second-grader on her index finger. Miaowu's hair still dripped vomit, bangs stuck to her forehead, uniform cuffs still trickling yellow-green, but she'd put her hand on her finger and said "letting it out is better." Her lips began trembling again, tears rolling from her eye sockets, one by one plopping on her own knees.

"You... you don't think it's dirty?" Her breath was hoarse as a broken trumpet, light as a feather on water. "I—I threw up on you—there's some on your hair—your clothes—your shoes too—"
