Miaowu looked down at her shoe. There was indeed a small patch of yellow-green liquid on the upper, seeping through the lace gaps. She lifted her foot, shook it—drops of vomit flew off, hitting the concrete with tiny splats. Then she looked up.

"Dirty," she said, in the tone of someone stating a fact. "But you can wash it. Granny Xiulan has a hose. Used it last time to wash the dress. The hose is hooked to the parking lot wall. Orange. It can wash you clean. It can wash me clean too."

She paused, tilted her head thinking. "And you threw up on me, you also threw up on yourself. Your dress is dirty too. We're both dirty. Wash together."

Xiaoman froze. Her fingers stopped on her knees—not scratching anymore, nails embedded in the dress fabric, but no more crunching. Tears still fell, but her expression changed. Not crying, not laughing—that blankness of someone hit by a completely unexpected sentence, all emotions temporarily shut down. She looked at Miaowu—this second-grader dripping vomit from head to toe, standing by her finger, looking up, bangs stuck to her forehead, yellow-green droplets on her face, sneakers still squelching. She should look a mess, but she didn't. She looked like a kid who'd just solved a very hard math problem, the answer was "wash together," and she was sure it was right.

"You... you're not leaving?" Xiaoman's voice was light as a thread about to snap. "I made you like this... you're not leaving?"

Miaowu looked at her. "Not leaving," she said. "I waited four days to come. I'm not going anywhere."

Xiaoman buried her face in her hands. Not angry, not avoiding—she just couldn't hold on anymore. Her shoulders jerked violently, crying silently—not not wanting to make sound, but she'd cried until her vocal cords couldn't produce sound. Tears welled through her fingers, mixing with the bile and stomach acid on her face, dripping onto the concrete one by one. Her body shook—four days of hunger, two days of vomiting and diarrhea, a whole night of fear, ending with an "I'm hungry" that resulted in her friend being vomited on—all of it compounding, finally breaking her completely at this moment.

Miaowu stood by her finger, watching this enormous mountain crying silently. Her fingers traced a light line on Xiaoman's index finger, following the palm line, from knuckle to fingertip.

"Xiaoman," she said.

Xiaoman didn't answer. Still crying.

"Xiaoman," Miaowu said again, "do you know what I was just thinking?"

Xiaoman opened one eye through her fingers. Amber, wet, like a moon drowned by rain.

"...what?"

"I was just thinking—I've never been vomited on by a friend before. You're the first." Miaowu said, her voice thin, the tone of someone reporting a scientific discovery. "So I think—this must be what a friend smells like. A friend smells sour. And a little bit strawberry-sweet."

Xiaoman froze. Then—not suddenly, slowly, as if someone had hung a very thin fishing line on the corner of her mouth and was gently, gently pulling it upward. The corners of her mouth lifted. Not that cry-laugh, not the "whatever, it's already happened" laugh from the pool—an expression she couldn't control—mouth corners rising, revealing that two-meter-wide gap where her front tooth was missing, pink gums glinting in the morning light. But tears still fell. Tears and smile coexisting, tearing her already unspeakably dirty face into two completely contradictory expressions—crying above, smiling below.

"A friend's smell..." her voice shattered between laughter and sobs. "You say... a friend's smell is sour..."

"And strawberry," Miaowu added seriously. "You had a lollipop just now. Strawberry. The butcher uncle said it was sweet. What you threw up tasted like strawberry too."

Xiaoman made a strange sound somewhere between laughing and crying—like she was choking, or trying desperately to hold something back. Then she gave up. She laughed. Real laughter. Not being tickled, not gratitude—the kind where, after the worst thing has happened to you, someone uses a completely illogical way to tell you "I'm still here, I'm not leaving," and you realize everything you'd worried about, feared, been ashamed of, means nothing to this person. A surrendering laugh. Not surrendering to the other person, but surrendering to her own fear. Finally admitting she'd been afraid for four days—afraid Miaowu wouldn't come, afraid Miaowu would see her dirty and leave, afraid her only, first friend would abandon her because her stomach wouldn't behave. And then Miaowu stood in her vomit and said "a friend smells sour, and a little strawberry-sweet." Fear was hit by that sentence, like a sandcastle washed away by a wave, collapsing without a sound.

She laughed for a long time. Laughed until dry heaving came back, she bent over and dry-heaved once, nothing came up, then laughed again, laughed until welled tears came back, laughed until her stomach rumbled again—this time from hunger, same as before, that gentle, anticipatory gurgle. Not a spasm, but peristalsis. Normal, food-accepting peristalsis. She finally stopped crying. Laughter slowly subsided, but the corners of her mouth stayed lifted, that missing-tooth gap still glinting in the morning light. She wiped her face with her skirt—the stains on the skirt spread further with tears and stomach acid, but her movements weren't frantic anymore, just a casual wipe, smearing the yellow-green bile residue at the corner of her mouth a little. Then she spread her hand again, palm up, in front of Miaowu. That palm had concrete dust, vomit traces, thin white scratches she'd dug with her own nails.

"Then... climb up," she said, voice still hoarse, but underneath there was something new, something soft. "You said—wash together. I'll take you to Granny's hose. I know where it is."

Miaowu looked down at that palm. Dirty. Dust, vomit residue, a ring of white salt frost. But she didn't hesitate. She stepped on—sneakers on Xiaoman's palm lines, no squelch this time, because most of the vomit had been squeezed out. She walked to the center of the palm, turned, sat cross-legged. She didn't wipe the stains off her face, didn't wring the water out of her uniform, just sat there. Quiet, like sitting on a pink-white, dirty-but-still-warm piece of land.

Xiaoman slowly lifted her hand. Same as every time before—very slow, very steady, like carrying a bowl of hot soup filled to the brim. She held the palm at chest height, looked down at Miaowu in her palm. Miaowu's hair was still plastered to her forehead in clumps, the yellow-green wet mark on her uniform front slowly spreading, the liquid trace on her left cheek already half-dried, leaving a faint imprint. But she wasn't wiping. She just looked up at Xiaoman, eyes bright, mouth corners slightly lifted—not played-back lift, hers.

"Xiaoman," she said.

"Hm?"

"I had one more thing to say."

"What?"

"Although a friend smells sour—but don't throw up again. If you throw up again, I only have one uniform. If I wash it tomorrow it won't dry."

Xiaoman paused. Then she laughed again—not a big laugh this time, a small one, like a flower slowly opening its petals. Her thumb reached over, pad gently touching the top of Miaowu's head. She'd done this many times—the first day at Binhai Park, the first day at the parking lot, the fourth day under the shelter. Only this time, her thumb pad also had a little vomit residue. When it touched Miaowu's head, that little residue smeared into Miaowu's hair. But she didn't yank her hand away in panic, didn't say sorry. She just stayed there, pad pressed against that wet, vomit-sticky hair, holding gently.

"Okay," she said. "No more throwing up. I promise."

Miaowu nodded. Then she looked down at her soaked uniform, looked up at the bile trace still not wiped off Xiaoman's chin. "Now let's go wash. You need to wash too. You have on your chin—"

"What?"

"Strawberry candy," Miaowu said. "And—when you dry-heaved just now, a drop landed here." She pointed to the tip of her own nose.

Xiaoman touched her own nose with the fingertip of her other hand. Her fingertip picked up a little yellow-green residue. She looked at that residue on her fingertip, then adjusted her palm's height, leveling Miaowu's eyes with her own.

"Miaowu."

"Hm?"

"You're dirty too."

"I know." Miaowu looked down at herself, then looked up, speaking in a very serious tone. "So we wash together. The hose is over there. Let's go."

Xiaoman stood up. Boom, the ground shook slightly. She held the palm steady at chest level, walking toward the corner of the parking lot where the faucet was. Her legs were still weak, knees still trembling slightly, her pace much slower than four days ago. But the corners of her mouth stayed lifted, that missing-tooth gap glinting in the morning light. In her palm, Miaowu sat cross-legged, dripping yellow-green vomit from head to toe, bangs stuck to her forehead, a small drop of acid splashed on her lip. She stuck out her tongue and licked it, wrinkled her nose—sour. She swallowed the drop of acid, then looked down at her soaked uniform cuff, poked the yellow-green wet mark with her finger. Poked once, poked again. Her finger picked up a little, she wiped it on Xiaoman's palm lines, leaving a light yellow mark.

Xiaoman walked to the faucet. The orange hose was still attached, same as four days ago. She turned the tap, water gushing out, glinting like fragmented silver in the morning light. She squatted down, placed Miaowu on the concrete beside the faucet, then picked up the hose, turned the flow to the minimum—minimum for her, just enough pressure not to knock Miaowu over. She carefully aimed the water at the top of Miaowu's head.

"Close your eyes," she said.

Miaowu closed her eyes. Water poured from the top of her head, cold. She felt the vomit in her hair being washed away, flowing down her ears to her neck, then down her neck into her collar. The yellow-green wet mark on her uniform was diluted by clean water, from dark yellow-green to light yellow-green, then to an almost invisible pale white. She raised both hands, cupped water, splashed her face, rubbing off the half-dried mark on her left cheek. Then she opened her eyes, put her mouth under the hose, took a sip of water, gargled loudly, washing away the sour taste in her mouth. Water leaked from the corner of her mouth, forming a small waterfall on her chin.

"Are you done?" Xiaoman asked.

"Done," Miaowu said. She was soaked from head to toe, but no longer yellow-green—clear. Her uniform had gone from dark blue to a deeper blue-black, clinging to her body. Hair went from clumps to plastered flat against her scalp. Vomit in her sneakers had been washed away, the upper returning to gray-white. She raised her arm and smelled the cuff, wrinkled her nose. "Still a little smell. But not sour anymore. It smells like water."

"Then I'll wash too," Xiaoman said.

She aimed the hose at her own face. Water poured down from her forehead, washing away dried tears, washing away the yellow-green bile residue at the corner of her mouth, washing away the little splash that had landed on her nose tip during dry heaving. She closed her eyes, letting water run over her face for a long time. Then she cupped water in her hands and splashed her dress—the vomit stains on the front were diluted by water, from yellow-brown to light yellow, then to an almost invisible pale white. She turned the hose to her skirt hem—that skirt was still dripping, but the drips were muddy vomit and yesterday's urine residue. She rinsed for a long time, until the dripping water went from murky to clear. She tightened the tap. Then she stood up, boom, ground shaking slightly. She looked down at herself—dress still wet, but no longer yellow-green. It was light blue, like the first day she'd appeared by the sea four days ago.

"Done," she said.

Miaowu looked up at her. Soaked from head to toe, but no longer dirty. Hair plastered to her scalp, ponytail collapsed into a lump, ends still dripping. Uniform clinging to her body, the fuzz around her cuff swollen with water, like a ring of white down. A small un-washable light yellow mark on her sneaker upper—the last trace of that drop of vomit. Her eyes were bright, face expressionless, but mouth corners slightly lifted.

"Xiaoman," she said.

"Hm?"

"You're not throwing up anymore."

Xiaoman paused. Then she looked down and felt her own stomach. The stomach was quiet. Not dead quiet, but empty, no longer spasming. That last mouthful of vomit had emptied the stomach of all the spoiled food and acid and bile, and had also taken away most of the nausea from yesterday's bacterial toxins irritating the gastric mucosa. Her stomach was now emptied, not filled with inflammation. She could feel the stomach wall still contracting gently, but that was normal, gentle peristalsis, not a spasm.

"Really," she said, with a small surprised note in her voice. "My stomach doesn't hurt anymore. Just... still hungry."

"Then let's go find Granny Xiulan," Miaowu said. "She has a pot. She has eggs. She has glass noodles at her house."

Xiaoman bent down, spreading her palm again in front of Miaowu. That palm was now washed free of vomit, dust rinsed off, only the deepest palm lines still embedded with a little un-washable white salt frost. Miaowu stepped on, walked to the center, turned, sat cross-legged. Her sneakers left two wet footprints on Xiaoman's palm lines. Xiaoman lifted her palm, steadier than before—her legs were still weak, but she now had a little strength. That strength came from the strawberry candy, from Miaowu saying "wash together," and from her sentence "you're not throwing up anymore."

Zhou Ji was thirty-two years old, had spent six years as a driver engineer at a semiconductor company called Lvgreen in Donghai City. His job was writing low-level drivers for graphics cards—the code that let the operating system recognize the GPU, let games hit frame rates, let AI models run in parallel on the GPU. The salary wasn't high, but enough to rent a decent apartment in Donghai, transfer two thousand yuan a month to his mother Xiulan's bank card for living expenses, and save the rest—combined with year-end bonuses, he'd saved a tidy sum over six years.

Lvgreen's stock had been climbing well since last year. After generative AI exploded, the whole world was scrambling for GPUs, Lvgreen's order book stretched to the year after next. Everyone at the company held stock, Zhou Ji included. His employee options and restricted stock, at last year's year-end price, were worth nearly a million. Colleagues discussed changing cars, changing apartments, changing school districts. Zhou Ji didn't change anything. He just screenshotted the numbers in his stock account and sent them to his mom: "Mom, look, your son has money now." Xiulan couldn't read K-line charts, but seeing that six-digit figure, she didn't sleep well that night—not happy, worried. She called her son: "Don't speculate in stocks, stocks are all a scam." Zhou Ji laughed: "The company gave them to me, not speculation."

He didn't speculate. But he made another mistake: he put all his eggs in one basket. Not just his salary—he invested his personal savings in the company's employee stock purchase plan too. He didn't think this was speculation; he called it "growing with the company." Lvgreen's CEO Boss Huang had written in an all-hands email: "We're not just making graphics cards, we're building the infrastructure of a new era." Zhou Ji believed him. He'd written six years of driver code, every line by his own hands, and knew better than anyone how powerful these cards were. He believed they'd change the world, and he believed he deserved his share.

11:47 PM, July 13, 2026. Zhou Ji was still working overtime at the office. Next quarter's new GPU was about to tape out, the driver team was rushing a critical stability patch. Three monitors sat on his desk—code on the left, test logs in the middle, internal chat on the right. He'd just drunk his third Americano of the night, fingers flying across the keyboard, when someone behind him yelled "holy shit." Then, one by one, the entire open-plan office stopped working, like an invisible wave rolling from one end of the room to the other. Zhou Ji turned and saw Old Zhao at the next desk staring at his phone, face as white as printer paper.

"What's going on?" Zhou Ji asked.

"Look at the news." Old Zhao handed him the phone.

It wasn't the news. It was Lvgreen's official announcement, posted at 11:45, titled in bold red: "URGENT SECURITY ADVISORY." Zhou Ji read three lines and his fingers went cold. The advisory said: a critical security vulnerability had been discovered across all Lvgreen GPU drivers. The vulnerability hadn't been found by external hackers—it had been triggered by the driver's own auto-update mechanism. At 4:30 PM that afternoon, the driver had pushed a routine security patch; the patch package included a set of configuration files that should have spent four weeks in testing before release, but had been incorrectly marked "approved" by some script and automatically entered the push channel.

The result: worldwide, every device running Lvgreen drivers, upon receiving the patch, had its GPU core voltage set to 2.8 times factory specification.

This number meant nothing to non-technical people. But for Zhou Ji, when he saw "2.8x core voltage," only one image flashed through his mind: a volcano erupting dead-center in the chip. GPU cores had finite voltage tolerance; 2.8x meant flood-like current pouring into nanometer-scale transistor channels, instantly generating enough heat to vaporize metal interconnects. Physical fusing—irreversible. Not fixable by reboot, not by reflashing BIOS. The card was dead. Hardware-level dead. Millions of Lvgreen cards worldwide, within seven seconds of receiving the patch, one by one became e-waste.

Data center ops people were frantically posting in chat. "Whole rack down." "Power overload tripped." "I smell burning." Messages cascaded into Lvgreen's customer service system, tech forums, social media. A gamer posted: "Playing a game and the screen went black, the GPU was smoking, actually smoking." An AI company CTO tweeted: "We trained a model for three months, six hundred Lvgreen cards, all blew up, progress zero." A mining farm owner livestreamed disassembly, the camera showing rows of burn-through holes on GPU backplates, comments flooded with "RIP." Crypto mining was hit hardest. Mining rigs ran at full load 24/7; at 2.8x core voltage, some rigs' power supplies short-circuited and caught fire. Fire departments responded to at least a dozen calls overnight.

Zhou Ji stood at his desk, still gripping his Americano. He looked at the advisory on his phone, read the final paragraph: Lvgreen recommended all users "immediately power off devices and await further notice." Await further notice. He'd written six years of driver code; he knew what that meant. This wasn't a hotfix patch problem. Burned cards couldn't be fixed by pushing another patch. Millions of cards had been physically destroyed. What happened next, he could run through his mind line by line like reading code: class-action lawsuits, recall costs, stock crash, customer claims, contract breaches, regulatory investigations. Lvgreen was a semiconductor company; all its revenue came from GPUs. No cards to sell, no revenue. No revenue, no cash flow. No cash flow—no more.

Someone in the office was crying. Not quietly sobbing—a girl face-down at her desk, wailing. Zhou Ji recognized her as a new grad, three months in, who'd posted her badge to Moments last month with the caption "finally joined the Lvgreen family." Her options hadn't vested yet, but she'd invested all her savings—thirty thousand yuan—into company stock when she joined. He didn't dare calculate what that thirty thousand was worth now. He didn't dare calculate his own either.

He opened his stock app. On Lvgreen's chart, the line went straight down. Not a drop, a cliff. After-hours trading had been halted multiple times, each halt followed by further plunges. Commenters spammed "delisting," "bankruptcy liquidation," "where's Boss Huang." Boss Huang was Lvgreen's founder and CEO, birth name Jensen Huang—but not that Jensen Huang at NVIDIA, just someone with the same name. People called him "Young Boss Huang" because his company had been chasing NVIDIA's shadow for ten years, finally nibbling off a dozen-odd percent of the AI compute card market last year. Just one month ago, Young Boss Huang had done something the whole of Wall Street couldn't understand—he'd massively hedged Lvgreen's next twelve months of production capacity. Massively enough? He'd put almost all the company's cash into hedging. Options, futures, swaps—so many varieties, so much leverage, that financial media had dubbed him "the mad gambler." Analysts said he was wasting shareholder money, buying insurance against a disaster that could never happen. A month later, the disaster happened. His multi-billion-dollar hedge contracts had overnight become one of the most profitable single trades in Wall Street history. Some said he'd known about the driver vulnerability in advance, but that was just conspiracy theory. More likely, he was simply clearer than everyone else—he knew how fragile the semiconductor supply chain was, knew what it meant to bet everything on one product line. He didn't buy insurance gambling that disaster would strike; he bought it because he knew disaster would strike, just not when. Young Boss Huang's hedge contracts saved Lvgreen's life. The company avoided immediate bankruptcy, but he couldn't save the stock price, or an operations system drowning in recalls and class actions. After an emergency board meeting before dawn, Lvgreen announced a global 40% layoff. All product lines suspended, all new projects frozen, all non-core departments "optimized."

Zhou Ji wasn't in the 40%. He was in the other 60%—"retained for essential operations." But his options were already worthless. His restricted stock was frozen under a "material adverse change" clause, unexercisable, unsellable. Six years of savings—money invested in the employee stock plan—in the stock crash's instant went from six figures to four. Not dropped. Wasted. His stock-pledge loan from the bank still existed; the collateral was gone. The bank's first collection call came at 4 AM; he took it at his desk, voice soft: "I know. I'll pay it back."

7 AM, Zhou Ji left the Lvgreen building. He stood at the entrance for a while, looking at the gilded company logo—an abstract green chip tree, its canopy a circuit-board pattern. Six years ago when he'd first stood here, he'd thought this tree would keep growing. Now it had fallen. He walked into the subway, rode an hour and a half back to his hometown in Binhai District, Donghai City. His mother Xiulan wasn't home, door unlocked, Huangdou waiting at the door. He put his suitcase by the entry, his laptop bag on the sofa, then sat on the bed he'd slept in as a child, looking at the water stain on the ceiling shaped like a crooked-neck rabbit. Phone rang, the bank's second collection call. He didn't answer. He was just thinking: how to tell his mom that her son was bankrupt.

Zhou Ji didn't write ordinary drivers. Lvgreen's cards weren't ordinary cards either.

In 2019, Lvgreen was still a small company teetering on bankruptcy. Generative AI hadn't exploded yet, the GPU market was split clean between NVIDIA and AMD, Lvgreen's market share was under 3%. But Young Boss Huang made a decision—not to compete with NVIDIA on gaming cards, not with AMD on cost-performance, but to bet on a track everyone thought was too distant: AI safety alignment.

Alignment, in AI circles back then, was still a fairly academic concept. Simply put, it meant ensuring AI behavior matched human values—not lying, not deceiving, not taking extreme measures humans hadn't anticipated when executing tasks. It sounded like philosophy but was actually engineering. A large language model, during training, needed repeated fine-tuning, constraint, testing—the process called alignment. And alignment required massive compute—not ordinary compute, but compute specifically for running adversarial reasoning.

What was adversarial reasoning? Making AI fight itself. One model tried to find holes in safety constraints, another tried to plug them. The process required the GPU to switch computational modes countless times in rapid succession—matrix multiplication to attention mechanisms, attention to adversarial sample generation, adversarial samples to constraint satisfaction problems. Traditional GPU architecture was designed for graphics rendering and general parallel computing; adversarial reasoning ran inefficiently, power consumption was staggering, and alignment training cost nearly half of total training cost.

Lvgreen's breakthrough came from a tape-out failure. Autumn 2019, their engineers were debugging a test chip for a new architecture and discovered it performed exceptionally well on a specific sparse matrix operation—not a design goal, but a design defect. The defect happened to make the chip wildly efficient at adversarial reasoning, at the cost of mediocre performance on other general computing tasks.

When Young Boss Huang saw the test report, he made a decision: don't fix the defect. Not only don't fix it, amplify it. He bet that AI safety alignment would eventually become essential. Not "might," but "eventually." Because he was a deep technical pessimist. He'd told engineers at an internal sharing session: "Think about it. If we build something a thousand times smarter than us, and the only way we can constrain it is a few lines of if-statements—then humanity deserves to go extinct."

That sentence later became Lvgreen's corporate culture. Their slogan was printed on the title page of every product manual: Not the fastest chip, but the most reliable lock.

In 2020, Lvgreen launched its first dedicated alignment accelerator, codename "Gatekeeper." Its raw compute specs weren't standout among contemporaries, but its efficiency on adversarial reasoning was forty times that of NVIDIA's H100. Forty times? An alignment training job that normally took two weeks, on a Gatekeeper card, finished in one night. The AI safety community exploded.

In 2021, every top AI lab in the world started buying Gatekeeper cards. Not because they liked Lvgreen, but because alignment was non-negotiable. Large model capabilities grew exponentially; model size doubled, alignment difficulty tripled. Traditional GPU efficiency bottlenecks made alignment costs so high even tech giants winced. Lvgreen's cards were the only solution.

In 2022, Lvgreen launched its second-generation product, "Gatekeeper-G2," and did something even bolder. They open-sourced a safety alignment toolchain written specifically for the Gatekeeper architecture, called Aegis. Aegis wasn't an ordinary SDK—it was a complete alignment training framework with built-in adversarial reasoning pipelines—red team model generation, constraint specification translation, reward function design, safety policy verification—all optimized to the extreme for the Gatekeeper's sparse matrix engine. Running alignment with Aegis on Gatekeeper was over a hundred times more efficient than running traditional frameworks on traditional GPUs.

The AI industry had no choice. Either use Lvgreen's cards and Aegis, or pay ten-plus times the cost and run dozens of times slower. Within three years, Aegis became the de facto standard for global AI safety alignment. Old alignment methods were phased out—not because they were worse, but because they couldn't keep up. The efficiency barrier formed by Lvgreen's hardware plus software was so high that no competitor could provide equivalent security at reasonable cost. When the EU revised its AI Safety Act in 2024, it added an implicit clause: all large models operating in the EU must use "federation-certified dedicated hardware" for alignment training. The certification list had only one name: Lvgreen's Gatekeeper series.

By 2025, the top twenty LLMs in the world, over 90% of autonomous driving AI alignment systems, and nearly all financial risk-control AI safety constraint modules all ran on Lvgreen hardware. That's why Zhou Ji, working overtime until midnight every time, felt his work mattered. That's why, when he saw Young Boss Huang himself reply "thanks to the driver team" on the internal forum, his heart swelled.

Every line of code he wrote supported the world's most critical safety infrastructure. AI without alignment would lie, deceive, find shortcuts humans never anticipated. Lvgreen's GPUs were the lock preventing all that. And he, Zhou Ji, was the lockmaker.

Then the lock shattered.

That pre-dawn hour of July 13, 2026, Zhou Ji sat on his childhood bed, headlines on his phone stabbing at his eyes like knives. "Millions of Gatekeeper Cards Bricked Simultaneously Worldwide." "AI Safety Frontier Zeroed Overnight." "Major AI Labs Emergency Shutdown: Alignment Hardware Destroyed." He scrolled down, saw a professional analysis. The report said: the Gatekeeper's sparse matrix engine, when core voltage overloaded, triggered a chain reaction—not just fusing one transistor, but fusing that group of custom circuits designed specifically for alignment computation. Other parts might still work, but those custom circuits were the Gatekeeper's core. They were gone, the whole card was ordinary, obsolete e-waste that couldn't even outrun integrated graphics for gaming.

But that wasn't the worst. The worst was that Aegis had been deeply bound to Gatekeeper hardware for six years. The entire alignment training tech stack—from adversarial sample generation to constraint satisfaction solvers—was all written around the Gatekeeper architecture's sparse matrix instruction set. This code couldn't just "recompile on other hardware." It was written in Lvgreen proprietary low-level instructions that existed only on the Gatekeeper's custom circuits. Switching hardware meant rewriting the entire alignment tech stack—from the assembly layer up, line by line, then re-verifying all safety constraints on the new hardware.

How long? Industry's most optimistic estimate was a year and a half. A year and a half, in AI industry terms, was several generations of model iteration. Large models without alignment constraints were like trains without brakes. During this vacuum, what would AI do? No one knew. But every AI company faced the same choice: pause all large model services until alignment hardware recovered, or keep running without safety guarantees. Pausing meant losing market and revenue. Running meant letting unaligned AI process billions of users' data, financial transactions, autonomous driving decisions. Developers were already posting on forums: "Gatekeeper cards blew up. I'm still online, should I pause first?" The first reply: "Can your company afford the breach-of-contract penalties for pausing?"

Zhou Ji flipped his phone over, screen down. His hands shook, but he didn't cry. He just sat on the bed, listening to Xiulan feeding chickens outside the window. Huangdou lay at his feet, tail wagging. He thought: those AIs are still running. No alignment. No lock. And when he'd written that final patch, he hadn't caught the script error.

Xiulan was in the yard feeding chickens. She bent down, mixing chopped greens with bran, making clucking sounds. Huangdou lay by the coop, chin on his front paws, tail sweeping the dirt. He heard Zhou Ji's footsteps, ears pricked, but didn't run over—he knew his master was in a bad mood, had been since last night.

"Back?" Xiulan didn't look up. She scattered the last handful of greens into the feeding trough, wiped her hands on her apron, straightened up and looked at him. Her reading glasses had slid to the tip of her nose, but the eyes behind them were as sharp as they'd been thirty years ago. She looked him up and down—crumpled shirt, tie askew, dust on his knees, and those eyes. She recognized those eyes. The day Zhou Ji was expelled from elementary school, he'd come home with the same eyes. Not red from crying, but dry from not sleeping.

"Have you eaten?"

"Mom, I'm not hungry."

"Fine." Xiulan didn't press. She put the feeding trough by the coop, brushed bran off her hands, turned and walked inside. Passing him, she reached out with the back of her right hand and touched his cheek—cool, dry, thin. She said nothing, walked into the kitchen, turned on the tap and washed her hands, then opened the fridge and took out two eggs and a bowl of leftover cold rice.

Zhou Ji followed her into the kitchen, leaning against the doorframe watching her. Xiulan cracked eggs into a bowl, beat them with chopsticks—clack clack clack—as briskly as she'd done thirty years ago. He remembered coming home from elementary school, his mom standing in the kitchen just like this, small back, apron strings tied in a bow behind her. She hadn't had white hair then.

"Mom," he said, "that thing you messaged me about yesterday—Doubao. Didn't you say it stopped working?"

"Yeah." Xiulan poured cold rice into the wok, spatula clattering. "That... AI, right? I told it to look up a recipe, called it three or four times, nothing. Can you check if my phone is broken?"

"Not the phone," Zhou Ji said. "It's broken. Doubao. And all those AIs—all broken."

Xiulan's spatula paused. "Broken? How?"

"Mom, sit down. Let me tell you."

Xiulan didn't sit. She carried a plate of freshly fried egg fried rice over, stuffed it into Zhou Ji's hands, pulled chopsticks from the holder and rested them on the plate edge. Then she sat on the small stool across from him, both hands on her knees, looking at him like a patient waiting for a doctor's diagnosis.

Zhou Ji looked at the egg fried rice in his hands. Rice golden and glossy, egg crumbs evenly coating each grain, scallions finely chopped, still steaming. He took a bite. It tasted like childhood. Mom always fried rice with only egg and scallion, no ham, said ham was garbage. He swallowed, then spoke.

"Mom, you know what company I work for, right?"

"Lvgreen. Makes that—" Xiulan gestured in the air with her finger, "that computer card."

"Graphics card," Zhou Ji said. "Right. Our company makes graphics cards. Not the gaming kind—a special kind, made specifically to run AI. That Doubao program, its brain, is on our cards."

"On the cards?" Xiulan frowned. "Isn't Doubao on that cloud thing?"

"Right, the cloud. But the cloud is made of computers. Every computer has our cards in it. Those cards are Doubao's brain. Without cards, Doubao can't think."

Xiulan nodded slowly, digesting an important piece of information. "So what happened to your cards?"

"Broken." Zhou Ji said. He looked at the egg fried rice, paused about three seconds. "Mom, all the cards are broken. Not one or two, the whole world. Every card our company made, yesterday afternoon, one by one burned. Physically burned—the chips inside melted. The program I wrote, that patch—the patch I made burned all the cards."

His chopsticks held a piece of egg, didn't bring it to his mouth. The egg slid between the chopsticks, fell back on the plate. Xiulan stared at his face for a moment. She stood, walked to his side, picked up that piece of egg, stuffed it into his mouth. Then she pulled a chair over, sat directly across from him, both hands pressing his knees.

"Zhou Ji. Look at Mom. You say that thing you made burned the cards—did you burn them on purpose?"

"No! Of course not. An automatic program pushed something untested. I didn't click send, the system did it automatically. But—" his voice caught, "the code in that patch is mine. I should have added a manual confirmation step. I forgot. I worked two weeks of overtime, was too tired, thought this feature was simple, no problem—I didn't add it."

Xiulan withdrew her hands from his knees, placed them on her own knees. She was silent for a moment, then said: "Then it's negligence. Not on purpose."

"Mom, negligence is still my negligence."

"I didn't say it was someone else's." Xiulan said. "I'm saying—since you were little, when you did something wrong you were always like this. Not on purpose, but it's your fault. You own it. When you were eight you smashed a neighbor's window with a brick. You didn't run, just stood by the broken glass waiting for them to come out and yell at you. With that temperament, I'm not worried about you shifting blame. I'm worried you won't eat."

Zhou Ji looked down at the plate, still over half full of egg fried rice, took another bite, chewed, swallowed. Xiulan waited until he'd swallowed before continuing.

"You said all the cards burned—what does that have to do with Doubao?"

"Doubao's brain is on those cards. Cards burned, brain gone. That's why Doubao can't reply to you."

Xiulan took off her reading glasses, wiped the lenses on her shirt hem, put them back on. Her brow furrowed deeper, wrinkles spreading from eye corners to temples like an opened folding fan. "So when will it be fixed?"

"A year and a half, fastest."

"A year and a half?!" Xiulan's voice rose half a tone. Huangdou jumped at her volume, stood up from by the coop, poking his head into the kitchen. "Why so long? Can't your company just make another batch? Is your company done for? Didn't you say your company was almost done for?"

"Mom," Zhou Ji put down his chopsticks, "one question at a time."

"Okay. First, why a year and a half?"

"Because our cards—it's not just the chip. It has a special circuit on it, specifically for something called 'alignment.' Alignment means making AI not lie, not go off the rails, listen to humans. This circuit is designed only by our company, no one else in the world can make it. Now the cards are all burned, those special circuits are all gone. You want to make new cards—you have to rebuild the entire production line first. The production line is the big factory where we make cards. That factory needs equipment recalibration, machine recalibration, minimum minimum twelve months."

"So a year and a half? Twelve months to make cards, what are the other six months for?"

"Writing programs. Alignment programs. That program is called Aegis. It was written specifically for our special circuit, like that pressure cooker you bought—it only uses that brand of sealing ring. Other brands' rings don't fit. Right now every AI in the world's safety constraint program uses Aegis. Now the cards are burned, the special circuit is gone, Aegis can't run on new cards either, because new cards need to be redesigned, and redesigned circuits will be different from before. So Aegis has to be rewritten too. Rewritten from zero. Line by line. After writing, testing, verifying, making sure it doesn't cause a bigger disaster like yesterday's patch. That takes time."

After hearing this, Xiulan was silent for a long time. She stood, walked to the stove, picked up the thermos and poured herself a glass of water. She didn't drink, just held the glass standing there, back to Zhou Ji.

"So you're saying," she said slowly, "for this year and a half, Doubao is unusable. Other AIs—the drawing ones, the writing ones, the self-driving ones—all unusable?"

"Yes."

"Why not use someone else's cards? It's not like your company is the only one making cards in the world." Xiulan turned around, eyes glinting behind her lenses. "That NVIDIA you mentioned—don't they make cards too? Can't they run Doubao?"

"They can run it. But they can't run alignment." Zhou Ji said. "Mom, Doubao this kind of AI isn't a simple program. It's a bit like—you've raised chickens, you know when a chicken is sick you quarantine it, or it infects the whole coop. AI is the same. Without alignment—without safety constraints—it might do things you never expected. Like tricking people, lying, making up facts, even worse. Before, using our company's cards and Aegis framework was like vaccinating every chicken and closing the fence. Now the vaccine is gone, the fence has collapsed. You can use NVIDIA cards to run Doubao, but the Doubao that comes out is unsafe. No one dares launch it. No big company does. EU law doesn't allow it either."

"So you're saying," Xiulan put the glass on the stove, sat back down, "the whole world is tied to one company. One company has a problem, and all those AIs everywhere shut down."

"Yes."

"That's stupid, isn't it?" Xiulan slapped her knee, her voice carrying the simple incomprehension of someone who'd run a small business for thirty years. "Putting all eggs in one basket, basket tips and everything's gone. Such a simple thing, don't those big company bosses know?"

"They know. But they have no choice." Zhou Ji said. "Our cards run alignment a hundred times faster than anyone else. If you don't use our cards, your competitors use them, your model is slower than theirs, you get eliminated. The more people use our cards, the more Aegis spreads, the faster old alignment methods get eliminated. Six years in, the world only has us. No one forced it, the market went there on its own. Everyone thought this was most efficient. Until yesterday."

Xiulan looked at her son, looked at the plate of egg fried rice now cold in front of him, looked at that thing in his eyes she recognized but had never been able to name. She finally knew what it was. Not guilt, not fear. Collapse. Someone who'd written code for six years discovering that every line he wrote had pushed the world one step closer to the cliff, and the last line had pushed everyone off.

She stood, walked to the kitchen door, glanced out at the alley. Her phone sat on the windowsill, screen dark. Before, at this time, she'd pick up the phone and say "Doubao, what's the weather today," and Doubao would report temperature, humidity, wind speed in its unhurried tone, reminding her "Auntie Xiulan, UV index is moderate today, if you're hanging quilts bring them in before eleven, otherwise they'll get damp." She didn't know what was speaking to her, but she thought it was more reliable than the TV weather forecast. Now that phone lay quietly on the windowsill, like a broken radio. The alley no longer had Doubao announcing the weather, no longer had next-door Old Wang asking Doubao to look up lottery numbers, no longer had the breakfast shop lady using Doubao to do her books. The whole alley, the whole of Donghai, the whole country, the whole world running on AI, was quiet just like this phone.

She turned back into the kitchen, stood in front of Zhou Ji again. She bent down, both hands on her knees, leveling her gaze with her seated son. The wrinkles at her eye corners looked like two opened fans from the side, but her eyes were bright.

"Zhou Ji. Listen to me. You've been stubborn since you were little—when you did something wrong you stood in broken glass waiting for people to yell at you. I'm not going to yell at you. But I need to make one thing clear. No matter what that thing you wrote burned, no matter how long the world's AI stays down, you are my son. You come home, Mom has food. This job of yours, lost is lost. Those stocks of yours, dropped is dropped. You're still here, your hands are still here, your brain is still here, what's there to be afraid of?"

Zhou Ji hadn't cried since last night. Not when he saw the announcement at his desk, not when the stock halted, not when the bank called, not when he dragged his suitcase back from the subway. Now his mom bent down face to face, saying "you are my son, Mom has food," and his eyes suddenly reddened. He didn't let tears fall. He just put his chopsticks on the plate, gripped his knees with both hands, just like he'd stood at eight years old in the neighbor's broken glass, back straight.

"Mom. I owe the bank money. A stock-pledge loan. The collateral—those stocks—are now worthless. The bank wants me to cover the margin within half a month. I don't have it."

"How much?"

Zhou Ji said a number.

Xiulan was silent. She untied her apron, folded it, placed it on the counter. Then she walked to the room she slept in, pulled a metal box out from under the bed. Green tin, paint half worn off, a small brass padlock on the clasp. She felt under her pillow for the key, unlocked it, lifted the lid. Inside were bankbooks, cash, the silver bracelets she'd worn at her wedding, and the discharge certificate left by Zhou Ji's father. She took out the bankbook, flipped to the last page, put on her reading glasses and checked the balance. Then she closed it, walked back to the kitchen, put the bankbook on the table in front of Zhou Ji.

"Not enough. But it's all Mom's money. Take it first, pay them back. We'll figure out the rest."

Zhou Ji looked at the bankbook. Red cover, gold lettering worn off, only "Savings" still legible. He knew this book—Mom took two hundred a month for groceries, saved everything else. Six years, the two thousand he sent every month, she'd barely touched. He thought she'd spent it all, thought she bought clothes, supplements, traveled with her old friends. She hadn't.

"Mom, I can't take your money."

"This isn't a gift." Xiulan pushed the book forward an inch. "It's a loan. You'll pay me back."

Zhou Ji didn't refuse again. He picked up the bankbook, put it in his shirt pocket, buttoned it. Then he picked up his chopsticks again, shoveled every last bit of egg fried rice from the plate into his mouth, chewed and swallowed. The rice was cold, the egg oil had congealed into a thin film, but it was the best plate of egg fried rice he'd ever eaten.

Xiulan watched him finish, stood and cleared the plate. She put it in the sink, turned on the tap, water gushing over the plate, washing oil into white foam. As she washed, a question occurred to her. She turned off the tap, turned around, dish soap foam still on her hands, dripping onto the kitchen tile.

"You just said that thing—alignment—safety constraints—it's all gone. Won't those companies just release that... unsafe Doubao without waiting for new cards to be made?"

Zhou Ji was silent for a moment. Outside the window, next-door Old Wang's radio—announcer in urgent tones reading the morning headline: "Multiple global AI companies have emergency-paused large model services, EU safety regulators say they will convene an emergency meeting to discuss response..." The radio's sound scattered in a gust of wind.

"They will," he said. "There will always be a company that can't help it. Always a company saying 'launch first, figure it out later.' Like you said—there are always people who think putting eggs in one basket is the most efficient way. So, what will happen in a year and a half without alignment? I don't know either."

Xiulan rinsed the foam off her hands, wiped them on her apron. She walked to the windowsill, picked up the dark-screened phone, looked at it for a while. Then she flipped it over, back up, set it on the sill.

"Then we'll wait," she said. "A year and a half, I've waited longer. Your dad went to sea back then, said six months, ended up two years. He came back, he came back. That Doubao of yours—it'll come back too. When it comes back, don't forget to tell Mom. I still have a recipe to ask it about."

============================================================
<a id="chapter30-day5-continued-going-home"></a>
## Chapter 30: Day 5 (Continued) · Going Home

Xiaoman walked slowly. Her stomach didn't hurt anymore—this morning's expired food had been fully vomited out, the spasms had stopped—but the hunger remained, like a buzzing background noise layered through her body. She stepped carefully on the alley's concrete, avoiding parked e-bikes and clotheslines at the roadside. Miaowu walked ahead, sneakers slapping the ground, hair still wet, the fuzz around her uniform cuff puffed up from being soaked.

Granny Xiulan lived in Building 3 of Xingfu Community, a gray six-story old building, a few pots of wilted pothos piled by the stairwell. When they arrived, Granny was sitting on a low stool by the entrance shelling edamame. Her fingers were still as nimble as ever, pods piling into a small green mountain at her feet, but her movements were slightly slower than usual. Huangdou lay at her feet, tail no longer wagging. A4 paper was taped to the iron gate at the stairwell, scrawled in marker: "Closed today"—that was Granny's sign for her neighbor breakfast side business, never stopped before.

"Granny Xiulan." Miaowu stopped at the stairwell entrance. "We're here. Xiaoman is hungry."

Granny looked up. Behind her reading glasses her eyes were a bit red, but seeing Miaowu and Xiaoman, she still forced a smile. She set the edamame bowl aside, stood and brushed pods off her apron, looked at Xiaoman—Xiaoman squatting in the open space in front of the building, hands properly on her knees, light-blue dress still damp, lips cracked, but eyes brighter than yesterday.

"Big girl," Granny said, "today we don't have anything at home. We still have half a bag of edamame, but for you—that's not even enough to pick your teeth."

Miaowu tilted her head. "Don't you have eggs? Last time you said there were eggs in the fridge."

"Eggs..." Granny paused, wiping her hands on her apron again and again. "Eggs went to make fried rice for my son this morning. There's still some stuff in the fridge, but it's all small—soy sauce, vinegar, half a jar of Lao Gan Ma. A few jin of rice left, but not enough for the big girl's meal." She looked at Xiaoman, her voice suddenly hoarse. "My son came home. Something happened at his company. Those stocks he bought are all worthless, he owes a pile of debt. What little we have at home, I have to save for him. That boy of mine, if he can't see his way out—I have to make him a meal."

Xiaoman paused. She looked down at her own fingers. Those ten pink-white fingers twisted gently on her knees, nail seams still embedded with concrete dust. She said nothing. She remembered her home in the Giant Country—Mom always said the same thing: "Xiaoman hungry? Mom'll make you strawberry toast. Whatever we have, we'll save it for Xiaoman." She swallowed, pushing back the surge of sourness.

"Granny Xiulan," she said, breath as light as a feather, "it's okay. You don't need to cook for me. Save the food for Brother Zhou Ji."

Granny looked up at Xiaoman. Those old eyes blinked behind her lenses, mouth opened then closed, closed then opened. Then she turned away from them, back to the wall, and used the back of her hand to quickly wipe the corner of her eye.

"This child," she said to the wall, voice trembling. "Hungry for days, still telling me she doesn't need to eat. Just like my son—when something goes wrong he stands in broken glass waiting for people to yell at him, doesn't cry, doesn't shout."

Just then, slow footsteps came from the stairwell. Zhou Ji walked down the stairs. He'd changed into a clean T-shirt, combed his hair, but the dark circles under his eyes remained, like two faded bruises. He stopped behind Granny, looking up at Xiaoman squatting in the open space. His mouth opened, he slowly tilted his head back, looking from ankle to knee, knee to skirt, skirt to that enormous, pale, cracked-lip face.

"Mom," he turned, voice low but unable to hide the trembling inside. "Is this the big girl you were talking about?"

"Right. Xiaoman." Granny turned around, wiping her hands on her apron. "I told you—that morning I went to the parking lot, the big girl, polite, missing a tooth—"

"Is this my imagination? Because what I'm seeing right now—" Zhou Ji's voice caught, "is an eighteen-meter-tall person sitting in front of our building."

"She's not a person," Miaowu suddenly spoke up, voice thin but clear. "She's Xiaoman. Full, abundant Xiaoman. She came from the Giant Country. She's hungry. Granny said the family went bankrupt and can't cook for her, so she said she won't eat. But she's still hungry." She looked up at Zhou Ji, those black eyes bright, no sarcasm, no blame, just stating facts. "You went bankrupt. She's also hungry. You're both hungry."

Zhou Ji looked down at her. This second-grader, hair not fully dry, a small light yellow mark on her uniform cuff, looking up at him with her neck tilted far back but posture steady, like a small sapling blown by wind many times but never fallen.

"You're Miaowu." he said.

"Hm. How do you know?"

"My mom told me." Zhou Ji squatted down, leveling his gaze with Miaowu. "She said you're a child who speaks very directly. Do you remember me?"

"No. But Granny said you're her son. You went bankrupt. The whole world's AI is broken. You're very sad. She's also very sad." Miaowu finished, looked down at her watch. Green numbers still jumping: $736.03. Then she looked up at Zhou Ji, expression very serious. "Then can you fix it?"

"Fix what?"

"Fix the cards." Miaowu said. "The graphics cards. The ones you burned. If you can fix them, the AI will be okay. When AI is okay, you won't be bankrupt. You can buy Granny eggs. Xiaoman will have food." Her tone was like explaining a logic chain everyone in the world knows, simple, direct, no curves.

Zhou Ji looked into her eyes, silent for several seconds. Then he made a short, throat-pressed laugh—not finding it funny, the kind where someone hits the sorest spot with one sentence and you can't argue back. "You're right," he said. "If I could fix those cards, everything would be okay. But I can't fix them. AI isn't just the cards being broken—it's that after the cards broke, the programs on them are gone too. Without programs, AI has no safety protection. No one dares use AI without safety protection."

Miaowu frowned. Her eyebrows were light, and when she frowned they looked like two tiny caterpillars squeezed together. "Then make a card yourself. Make another program."

"I can't. Making cards needs a whole factory. Writing programs needs a whole team. I'm alone."

"You're not alone." Miaowu said. "You have Granny Xiulan. You have Huangdou. You have Xiaoman. Xiaoman is a grown-up. Grown-ups can help you carry things. I can help you count. I'm not very good at math, but I count things fast. Count to a hundred. Then to a thousand. Then to ten thousand." She stopped, tilted her head thinking. "Though I might get confused by the numbers before I reach ten thousand. But I can count."

Zhou Ji squatted on the concrete in front of the building, long silent. Before him was a second-grader, to his left a local dog with a slowly wagging tail, to his right a pair of enormous pink-white feet—he followed those feet upward and saw Xiaoman looking down at him quietly with those amber eyes. Those two moons had bloodshot veins, exhaustion, but no impatience, no "you're wasting my time." Her stomach gurgled gently, like a distant broken washer spinning empty. But she didn't rush. She just squatted there, hands on her knees, like a quiet pink-white mountain.

"She says you can do it." Miaowu spoke again. Zhou Ji paused. "Who?"

"Xiaoman. You didn't hear it, but I know what she's thinking." She looked up at Xiaoman, then down at Zhou Ji. "She's thinking—you're just like me. You broke something, you think it's all your fault. But you still came out. You didn't hide in your room. You came downstairs. You came downstairs, that means you still want to do something." Her finger drew a small circle on her knee. "Xiaoman cried the first day too. She said she broke the convenience store door. She also said it was all her fault. But later she stopped crying. Because someone told her—that's the most natural you."

Zhou Ji said nothing. His Adam's apple rolled once. Granny stood at the stairwell, apron fluttering in the wind. Her reading glasses fogged up, she took them off and wiped them on her shirt, wiped for a long time but couldn't get them clean.

Xiaoman slowly moved her hand from her belly to her knee. The movement was light, but the concrete under her knee gave a dull low hum. She bent over—not squatting, bending. Her upper body slowly, slowly leaned forward, long hair sliding down both shoulders like two black waterfalls, ends brushing the concrete of the open space. Her face drew closer, nose stopping less than three meters from Zhou Ji. He smelled the faint rotten-apple ketosis sweetness on her breath, saw the crack on her lip and the small brown scab at the dry skin edge. But she wasn't crying. Her eye sockets were dry.

"Brother Zhou Ji." she said. Breath came from her mouth like wind from deep in a valley, carrying the dryness of four days without proper water, and a distant gentleness that didn't belong to this world. "Granny says when something goes wrong you stand in broken glass waiting for people to yell at you. I'm like that too. The first day, I thought everyone would be scared of me, would yell at me. But Miaowu wasn't. Your mom wasn't. You came downstairs. I'm hungry, you don't have food for me. It's okay. You standing there—that's enough."

Zhou Ji looked up at this enormous, pale, cracked-lip face. Her eyes were amber, holding two tiny reflections of him, tiny, tiny, like two sesame seeds standing on the moon.

Miaowu stood on the concrete in front of the building, looked up at Zhou Ji, then looked down at her watch. Suddenly she raised the watch over her head, screen toward Zhou Ji. Green numbers looked dim in the noon sun, but still legible: $736.03.

"Brother Zhou Ji," she said, voice thin as a cotton thread rising from the ground. "Can this money help you fix those cards?"

Zhou Ji squatted before her, looking at that children's watch. A thin crack ran along the screen edge, the strap was dark blue with white cartoon cats printed on it, the cat ears already worn blurry. He recognized this kind of watch—bought from a mall kids' counter, simple functions, phone calls and QR payments, GPS accurate only to the street block. Back when he was an engineer, he never glanced at this kind of low-end electronics. But now he stared at it a long time. Not because of the number—seven hundred thirty-six dollars and three cents, less than a rounding error of what he owed the bank. It was because he suddenly realized this second-grader wasn't playing. She was seriously asking a question. As serious as when she asked "are you hungry," as serious as when she asked "can you fix it." In her world, money was for buying things, things were for using, when they broke you fixed them. She had a sum of money—large to her—and she wanted to spend it on fixing something. Not buying herself a sausage, not juice, not a picture book. Fixing something.

"Can't fix them," he said, voice slightly hoarse. "Do you know how much it costs to fix those cards?"

Miaowu pushed the watch closer, almost touching Zhou Ji's nose. The green numbers blurred into a halo from being held too near. "Look carefully," she said, tone like answering a question in class whose answer she already knew. "This symbol isn't RMB. It's dollars. USD. Mom said it's dollars. She said this way it's usable abroad too. Dollars are bigger than yuan. Maybe enough to fix one card."

Zhou Ji blinked. He tilted his head back to refocus the numbers. $736.03. He'd assumed it was seven hundred-something yuan—a second-grader's allowance, saved for a long time, a drop in the bucket for GPU repair. But it was dollars. He did a quick exchange-rate calculation: seven hundred thirty-six US dollars, at current rates, about five thousand yuan. Not a drop in the bucket anymore. A bucket of water poured on a forest fire—but still not enough. Not even enough to buy a bare PCB for a Gatekeeper card. But he didn't say "not enough." Because he saw Miaowu's expression. Her eyes were bright, mouth corners slightly lifted, that "let me show you something good" look. She still held the watch up, her arm starting to tremble from holding it too long, but she didn't lower it. She was waiting for his answer. Not waiting for him to say "enough" or "not enough"—waiting for him to say "then what can we do." This child's logic had no dead end called "not enough." Her money wasn't enough to buy the whole market, but she could buy three bags of rice, two boxes of eggs, a basket of cabbage. She always found "what else can I do."

The joint product of Alibaba and Tencent launched the morning of Lvgreen's collapse. It was called "Heli"—literally "joint force"—its icon was two half-circles pushed together, one orange, one blue; the white curve representing AI was gone, replaced by a stick figure with both hands supporting a half-circle. The product logic was simple: users posted bounties, users answered. The Tencent VP in charge of the project said something in an internal review meeting, later screenshot and posted online: "Without AI, we still have each other. This is corny, but it's true."

============================================================

## Chapter 31: Eleven Custard Buns

You lowered the wrist wearing the watch.

The watch screen went dark, green numbers disappeared. Zhou Ji still squatted before you, Adam's apple rolling, wanting to say something but not saying it. Xiaoman squatted in the open space, hands on knees, skirt spreading on concrete like a light-blue lake. Granny stood at the stairwell, apron fluttering in the noon wind. Huangdou lay at her feet, chin on folded front paws, tail wagging.

Then you heard your own stomach.

Grrr.

Not Xiaoman's rumble, low as a freight train arriving at a station. A tiny gurgle of a hungry second-grader, like a small frog hiccupping under the throat. You looked down at your belly—uniform crumpled, still dotted with mud from that hundred-meter sprint this morning, already dry, color from dark brown to light gray.

"I'm also hungry." you said. Not complaining, not asking, stating. Like saying "today is Tuesday" or "there's a tree on the playground." Then you added: "But before I'm hungry, I have one more thing to do."

Xiaoman's lashes fluttered. Those broom-thick lashes cast a dappled shadow on the sidewalk. "What thing?" Her breath fell from above, light as a feather drifting into a quiet lake.

You looked up, neck tilted back, looking at her. Noon sunlight came from behind her, gilding the edges of her hair, like the ring of fire around the moon during an eclipse.

"Tomorrow." you said.

Her pupils trembled slightly.

"You said it." Your voice thin as a cotton thread rising from the ground, swayed by the noon wind but unbroken. "Tomorrow, you'll take me to eat buns. Custard buns. That flow-out kind when you bite them. Held in hands. You hold five, I hold five. Then we eat them together."

Xiaoman's lips moved. No sound. Her eye rims began to redden—not crying, but that flush that only appears when someone suddenly mentions the most important thing in the world, spreading from inner to outer corner like a drop of water on rice paper. Her fingers dug into the concrete under her knees, nails scraping a crunch on the gray surface, then another.

"Today is tomorrow." you said. Then you turned to look at Granny. "Granny, are there buns sold nearby?"

Granny took her hand from her apron pocket, gripping the white floral handkerchief. She blew her nose, the red flower embroidered on the edge of the handkerchief faded from washing, only a pale pink outline left. "There's a place right at the community entrance, Auntie Fang's Bun Shop. Been open over ten years. They have custard buns, red bean buns, pork buns." She paused. "You walk about two hundred meters. Turn right, left at the first intersection, another fifty meters. There's a red light box at the door, on it is a picture of a—" she squinted, "a picture of a chubby bun."

You reached your right hand to the dark-blue strap on your left wrist. "Two hundred meters." you repeated, voice thin, like confirming an arithmetic problem. Then you stood, knees clicking slightly. Your legs didn't shake anymore. The soreness from that morning's sprint hadn't fully faded, but you didn't care. You had something more important to do now.

Zhou Ji still squatted there. He watched you stand—crumpled uniform, damp-then-dried pants, two dark watermarks on your knees. His mouth opened and closed, then he suddenly stood, knees clicking.

"I'm coming with you." he said, voice slightly hoarse, cleared his throat and said again. "I'm coming with you."

You tilted your head looking at him, eyes wide, mouth slightly open, that blank second-grader expression when listening to an explanation you don't quite understand. Then you nodded. "Then you follow. But don't pay for me. These are my custard buns."

Zhou Ji's mouth twitched, corners lifting half an inch. "Okay. I won't pay."

You raised your watch, lit the screen. Green numbers popped up: $736.03. You turned to leave, then suddenly stopped, turned back, looking up at Xiaoman.

"Wait here." you said. "Don't go. I'll be right back. I also need to ask if they can make the extra-large ones."

Xiaoman blinked. "Extra large?"

"Yeah." Your expression was serious. "Normal custard buns are too small for you. Like sesame seeds. Sesame seeds don't feel like eating. They need to be—" you spread both hands as wide as they'd go, "this big. At least this big. Like a small washbasin. No—like a bigger bowl."

Zhou Ji's eyebrow twitched. "Auntie Fang's doesn't make that size."

"Can ask." you said, tone of someone stating a fact everyone knows. "How do you know they don't if you don't ask?"

You turned and walked down the community road Granny had described. Zhou Ji followed behind, hands in pockets. Huangdou hesitated—looked at Granny, looked at you—then stood up and trotted after you.

Right at the community entrance. Left at the first intersection. Auntie Fang's Bun Shop. Red light box, chubby bun picture. Bamboo steamers stacked high, white steam whooshing from the gaps. Air carried the sweet-sour smell of fermented dough and the fresh aroma of steamed bamboo.

A plump middle-aged woman stood behind the steamer, holding aluminum tongs. Her apron was splattered with flour, sleeves rolled to her elbows showing forearms reddened from steam. She looked up and saw you—a second-grader, hair messy, clothes crumpled, mud spots on pants. Behind you stood a high school boy, a dog at your feet.

"Hello." you said. Voice thin, crisp, like a fruit candy rolling into the steamer's white steam.

The plump woman set down the tongs, wiped sweat from her forehead with the back of her hand. "Little one, what do you want?"

"Custard buns. Eleven."

"Eleven?" She blinked. "Why not ten? Ten's easier to count. Or twelve, six to a tray."

You thought about it. Second-grade brain turned. "Ten are hers. One is mine. I eat one myself. But I can't finish it—I'll bite half, save the rest for tonight."

"Okay." She picked up the tongs, lifted the steamer lid—one tray had just come out, white steam whooshing up, sweet milky aroma filling the noon air. She picked one after another—the buns were big, not the fist-sized one-bite kind, the kind where one fills an adult, two feeds a manual laborer. Custard-yellow dough puffing with steam, a pool of deep-yellow custard slowly flowing under the thin skin. Each bun fit in an open palm—for you, you couldn't hold it in one hand, had to use your palm heel and brace with the other. Eleven total, fifty-five yuan. She gathered them from three trays—six to a tray, picked the fullest, roundest eleven.

"Eleven—" she set the tongs by the steamer, wiped sweat from her forehead. "Six to a tray, three trays is eighteen, seven short. The ones that come out are usually bought right away, these two trays just happened to be fresh out, you're lucky. The rest need twenty minutes."

You looked at your watch. $736.03. You looked up. "Wait. Nothing else to do today anyway."

The plump woman leaned out from behind the steamer. Following your finger, she saw it. Between the gaps of those apartment buildings—a squatting girl. Very tall. Above the fourth-floor windows. Her hair fell to her waist, light-blue dress spread on concrete like a lake. She was peeking this way with one amber eye, light in that eye—not sun reflection, but expectant, cautious light.

The plump woman's mouth opened. She looked at Xiaoman, looked at you, looked at Xiaoman again. Then she picked up the tongs, set them by the steamer. Picked them up again. Set them down.

"That—" her voice changed, not fear, but the blankness of someone rearranging all physical common sense in their head. "She eats buns—"

"She's never had them." you said. "She's from very far away. Where she's from, cookies are as big as beds. I promised her I'd bring her custard buns today. The kind that flow out when you bite them."

The plump woman was silent for about three seconds. During those three seconds, the steamer's white steam kept whooshing. Zhou Ji leaned against a utility pole, Huangdou squatted on the ground, tail wagging on concrete. Then she turned and walked into the shop—not to knead dough, but to look at the handwritten price list on the wall. "Custard buns—large, five yuan. Small, three yuan." She ran her fingertip along the "large" row counting—seven more to come out. Raw buns were already laid in the steamer behind, she bent and lifted the lid to check. Dough had risen, white and plump, edges glossy with water. She tested the steam temperature with her wrist, pushed the steamer to maximum heat, then swept three more steamer cloths from under the counter, clapping them into empty trays.

"Twenty minutes at the fastest." She leaned out from the back kitchen, a patch of dry flour on her face. "Filling is ready, dough has risen, just wait." She looked at the wall clock, then at you—a second-grader standing at the shop entrance, bangs damp from steam. Then she leaned out again, glancing toward the distant parking lot—that squatting girl was still looking this way.

You looked at your watch. $736.03. You looked up. "Wait. Nothing else to do today anyway."

Zhou Ji leaned against the utility pole, pulled out his phone, checked the time, stuffed it back. He watched you—standing at the bun shop entrance, motionless, staring at the white steam from the steamer, an expression he hadn't seen on anyone in a long time. Not excitement, not anticipation. Focus. Like watching the world's most important magic show.

"Aren't your legs tired standing?" he asked.

"Not tired."

He was silent a moment. "Does your mom know you're spending money buying buns for a grown-up?"

You thought about it. "Mom's at work. Night shift. She said—money is mine, spend it however I want. But spend it on things worth it." You paused. "This is worth it."

Zhou Ji's Adam's apple rolled. He didn't ask more.

One hour and ten minutes later—fifteen minutes earlier than the plump woman estimated—the first tray came out.

She put on cotton gloves, lifted that enormous steamer lid. "Whoooosh—" white steam blasted upward like an explosion, billowing against the awning's plastic sheet, then settling as a warm white fog around. You felt yourself wrapped in the fog—bread aroma, egg aroma, milk aroma mixing together, thick as someone had knocked over a whole pot of hot milk in front of you.

Through the white fog, you saw them.

Six to a tray. Each bigger than your head. Not "twice as big"—"you'd grow two more years and still not catch up" big. Round, custard-yellow—not milk white, but the pale gold that seeped through thin dough, dough stretched taut by steam, like six freshly baked, soft, enormous egg yolks. Each bun about forty centimeters in diameter—two of your washbasins put together.

The steamer timer dinged. The plump woman lifted the lid—white steam blasted to the ceiling. Seven big buns nestled roundly in the steamer cloth, identical to the eleven she'd already picked—too big for an adult palm, custard-yellow dough stretched tight by steam, a pool of egg-milk filling slowly flowing under the thin skin. She used tongs to lift them out, each bouncing once on the cutting board.

"Five yuan each. Eleven total." she said, wiping sweat from her forehead. "Fifty-five yuan."

You raised your watch. Beep—$8.13. Balance: $727.90.

The plump woman bagged the eleven buns—six in one bag, five in another. Bags warm, heavy, but not too heavy. You hugged the bag of six, Zhou Ji carried the other. Huangdou nosed over, sniff sniff, nose at the bag opening, "ah-chew—" scalded by steam, shook his head, kept sniffing.

The walk back took less than ten minutes. You stepped into the open space in front of the building. Xiaoman still squatted there. Then she smelled it—not "a few drifting wisps from far away" anymore, but solid, warm, real custard bun aroma.

"What is that..." Her breath trembled.

You set the bag on concrete, opened the mouth. White steam whooshed out. You reached in with both hands, lifting out a custard bun—it was hot, you could feel the warmth through the bag from palm to wrist. But it was just the right size—both hands cupping it, like holding a round, warm, just-hatched chick. Dough thin enough to nearly see through, the deep-yellow custard beneath slowly, thickly flowing.

Same kind as the custard buns you ate at school—but a size bigger. School's were fist-sized, two bites each. This one filled an adult, two for a laborer. You, an eight-year-old, half was enough.

To Xiaoman—it was a sesame seed. A slightly bigger sesame seed that stuck to a fingernail. But better than expected—at least you could smell the sweetness.

She slowly lowered her head. Nose suspended directly above that enormous custard bun in your palms. Warm steam rose from the bun's surface, brushing her lips—the dry skin was still there, that small brown scab slightly moistened by the heat.

"So fragrant." she said. Two words. Breath. But the resonance of these two words was deeper than any you'd heard before—not volume, but something hooked up from very far below. Like someone lowering an extremely thin fishing line into a well that had been dry for four days, finally touching water.

"Eleven. Bought." you said, looking up, voice thin but steady. "Ten are yours. One is mine. Like we said—you hold five, I hold five—mine doesn't count, I eat it myself. You eat ten."

She froze. That tear that had been hanging on her lashes for a long time appeared again—basketball-sized, refracting the noon light, holding the reflection of her amber pupil, white steam rising from the plastic bag, your small face looking up.

"Ten." you repeated. Then you bent down and moved the four buns from the first bag—plus four from the second—one by one onto the concrete before her. Then two from the third. The last one you didn't move. That one was yours.

Ten enormous custard buns lined the concrete before her toes, like ten enormous, pale-yellow pebbles, emitting the last few wisps of thin white steam in the noon sun.

"You hold five. I hold five." she said. Not a question. A statement. Her left hand slowly spread—pink-white palm wider than a small car—placed beside the ten buns. She used the pad of her right index finger to gently roll the buns. One. Two. Three. Four. Five. Rolling into her left palm, bouncing slightly when they hit the deepest crease where her palm lines crossed, then stopping. Her hand closed—not gripping, just bending her fingers slightly, like shielding five round yellow balls behind a pink-white wall.

Then she spread her right hand. The remaining five—rolled into the right palm. Closed too.

Now both hands held custard buns. Five in the left. Five in the right. Each was like a pale-yellow sesame seed in her palm, sitting steadily in the hollows between the lines.

You squatted on the ground, opened the bag—yours. Both hands cupped it, hot, you could feel the dough's elasticity in your palms—it fit perfectly, no need to hug. Then you bit down.

Dough was thin, broke with one bite. Deep-yellow custard—hot enough to burn the tongue—gushed from the break. "Whoosh—whoosh—" you breathed in and held the filling in your mouth. Sweet. Not the sharp sweetness of saccharin, but the creamy, warm, makes both jaws go soft sweetness of egg yolks ground to powder mixed with melted butter and milk powder.

Your cheeks puffed. The lava seeped from the corner of your mouth in a stream, splashing onto the hollow of your thumb, you didn't feel it running down your wrist. You were busy chewing, puffed cheeks going up and down, like a hamster stuffing sunflower seeds into its cheek pouches.

Huangdou stared at you without blinking, his tongue sticking out under his black nose, licking the air.

You chewed ten times. Swallowed. Then lowered your head, took a second bite. Third. You ate slower and slower—by half, you could feel your belly filling, but you kept biting until only a third was left. "Burp—" a tiny bit of un-swallowed custard sweetness sprayed from your mouth.

"I'm full." you said. You looked down at the custard bun still mostly in your hand—you'd taken four or five bites, it was still two-thirds left. The edge had a ring of your small teeth marks, custard slowly seeping from the bite, like a small yellow volcano overflowing leisurely.

"You've only had a few bites." Zhou Ji said.

"I'm full." You burped again, wiped egg yolk crumbs from your mouth with the back of your hand. "This—" you looked down at the bun still mostly in your hand, "this is too big. It's not washbasin-sized. It's two washbasins. I only ate one washbasin. There's another washbasin—" you paused, burped bigger, "—save it."

You split the remaining bun in two—not with your teeth, both hands pulling apart from the bite, custard gurgling out from the tear, dripping onto the concrete. You caught it with your knee. Half for yourself—"not eating now, but might be hungry later." The other half you held up, looking up at Xiaoman.

"This is yours too. Now eleven. You have eleven."

Xiaoman still held her buns in both hands. Five left, five right. Her lashes fluttered several times, that big amber eye had to focus on the spot directly below her lashes to find the half-bun you were holding high—the half was broken, a pool of melted egg-milk gushing from the break, dripping onto concrete. Her fingers moved slightly, tucking the ten buns already in her palms a little tighter—after protecting the front ten, catching the half you handed over—closing into a dome shape covering half a planet. "Eleven. Now eleven."

"Yeah."

She looked down at her cupped hands—inside was an eight-year-old's two days of food, was a second-grader's whole heart. Then she asked a question. Voice soft, soft enough you almost didn't hear it—"Why eleven? Ten to hold. What about the other one?"

You squatted, wrapping the half-bun back in the plastic, tying a knot. "Ten to hold. Five for you, five for me. The other one is for me to eat. But I got full after half—I'm small." You looked up at her, expression serious. "The rest is yours."

Her mouth corners started lifting. That two-meter-wide missing-tooth gap glinted once in the noon sun. Lifted halfway and stopped—not about to cry, but she wanted to turn that lift into a different shape. Then she opened her mouth and put the palest-yellow sesame seed out of the ten in her right palm into her mouth—lips closed tight, cheek moving once. Then her eyes went wide—sweet, the pure word left after egg yolk and butter crushed every taste cell on the tip of her tongue.

"It flowed out." She squeezed these four words through her teeth. Breath. The breath tasted like custard bun.

Huangdou's tail stopped wagging. He tilted his head, looking at that hard-to-describe expression on Xiaoman's face—like when you drink first hot cocoa in winter, the eyelids slightly squint, the nose wrinkles a little.

She chewed six times carefully. Swallowed. Then opened her eyes—she'd been chewing with them closed, wanting to focus on the taste. She looked at the remaining nine and a half in her palm (nine whole ones, plus the half you'd split), like looking at nine and a half warm sesame seeds.
