You squatted, took the half back out of the plastic, took a small bite. You really couldn't eat any more—but you were using your front teeth to slowly cut the dough, just cutting air. You just wanted to sit with her a little longer.

Granny stood at the stairwell. Zhou Ji leaned against the wall. Huangdou lay at your feet. Noon wind blew. In the distance, Auntie Fang's steamer still whooshed white steam, the plump woman standing at the door holding tongs, watching from far away—in the open space in front of the building squatted an eighteen-meter-tall girl, both hands holding a pile of pale-yellow "sesame seeds," chewing them seriously one by one like candy beans. Her mouth corners lifted.

You looked at your watch. .90. You looked up at Xiaoman—she was chewing the sixth custard bun, a big chunk of egg yolk on her lip. She noticed you watching, stopped, pushed that missing tooth gap with her tongue, then lowered her right hand—to five meters above your head—fingers flicked a whole unchewed bun. The bun flew toward you, caught mid-air by Huangdou's jumping dog mouth, crunches echoing deep in his throat.

"For me?" you asked.

"No." Xiaoman said, mouth corners lifted. "For the dog."

Huangdou's tail wagged faster.

You looked down at the half-bun still in your hand—you'd bitten another circle around it, now only a quarter left. You wrapped it back in the plastic, tied a knot. Then you stood up, neck extended—noon sun harsh, you squinted, looking at the sky.

"Xiaoman."

"Hm."

"Like we agreed. You held five, I held five. Mine I ate. Your ten—you ate them yourself."

Her lips moved. The small chunk of egg yolk on her upper lip trembled, then fell, landing on the tiger's mouth of her left hand. She looked down at that egg yolk—egg crumbs, butter, milk powder, sugar—in her world these would take dozens of eggs plus several pounds of butter plus a whole can of milk powder to assemble. But here—she'd bought eleven for fifty-five yuan.

"...Like we agreed." she repeated. Then she smiled—revealing that two-meter-wide missing-tooth gap, pink gums. Not that ugly held-back laugh, not that "finally got through it" relieved laugh. It was the "see you tomorrow" kind of smile. That smile held no sadness, no "how many days of food left," no "will Mom scold me." Only pale-yellow, warm, just-swallowed custard bun.

You squatted back down. Placed the "quarter-bun" tied in plastic on the edge of Xiaoman's new skirt, dusted with yellow earth and grass clippings. Then you stood, stretched—arms over head, fingers reaching for the sky. Noon wind blew your round, flushed face, blew your crumpled uniform, blew your sneakers now completely dry.

You spent fifty-five yuan. Bought eleven absurdly large custard buns. You got full after half. Xiaoman ate ten, plus the half you'd split. Balance—$727.90.

You licked your mouth corner. Licked an unswallowed grain of egg yolk.

============================================================

## Chapter 32: Smells

You licked your mouth corner. Licked an unswallowed grain of egg yolk.

Noon wind blew from the parking lot direction—the kind of July wind, as if someone had turned on an enormous hair dryer far away, pushing the whole city's heat at your face. You squinted, feeling a smell mixed into the wind. Not the sweetness of that quarter custard bun still in your hand—something thicker, deeper, had been coiled on this concrete for days.

You looked up at Xiaoman.

She sat cross-legged before you, like a pink-white mountain. Both hands still held the bun-cradling posture—palms cupped, loosely shielding the half custard bun inside. Her hair fell to her waist, ends swaying in the noon breeze. Sun slanted from behind her, rimming her hair in gold, also illuminating things on her body you hadn't looked at closely before.

You shifted one step toward her. Not consciously—your nose decided before your brain did. It smelled something.

First layer: the salt-stink of skin that sweated and dried. Not seawater salt—sweat. Armpits, back of neck, knee pits, toe crevices—five days without a shower, squatting and standing on July concrete, body heat baking the sweat dry, then new sweat flowing, layer over layer, forming a warm, slightly sticky scent, like the smell that steams out of a sneaker after you run on the track in summer afternoon—but magnified hundreds of times.

Second layer: the faint brine of dried saliva. That gap where her front tooth was had been leaking drool—leaking when she smiled, when she cried, leaking worse when she chewed custard buns. The drool dripped down her chin to her collar, to her collarbone, dried into rings of pale white marks. You breathed in a gust of wind pouring from her cuff—that smell bloomed in your nasal cavity, like someone opening a row of enamel basins that had just held raw meat on a humid afternoon. You blinked, but didn't back away.

Third layer: vomit residue. You knew this smell. You threw up often. Before and after medicine, on an empty stomach in the morning, when programs glitched—your stomach was more sensitive than other kids your age, throwing up wasn't a big deal for you. But Xiaoman's vomit wasn't medicine smell, it was sour rot. When she threw up—those spoiled apples, expired ham, oxidized cookies—had already started fermenting in her stomach; even after she'd rinsed with water, those sour rot residues seeped into her nail seams and the fibers of her skirt. This smell was faint now, faint enough that you could only catch it when she bent down to talk to you, nose tip dropping to within three meters. But you were exactly within three meters.

Fourth layer: custard bun. Fresh, sweet, milky, eggy. Just swallowed, still on her tongue, between her teeth. Every breath she exhaled carried a warm egg-milk sweetness. This was the only smell that wasn't old. It covered the sour and fishy beneath, but only for a moment—after each exhale, those old smells crept back, like sediment stirred up after sitting for a long time, then slowly settling back into place.

You remembered when you'd tiptoed and held the bun to her mouth—that action put you within two meters of her face. You hadn't noticed then. Because you were busy, busy dividing buns, busy saying "this is for tomorrow." But now you squatted there chewing the last bit of dough, and suddenly realized—that whole layer of scent you'd just smelled, was your friend's.

All of Xiaoman. Her hunger. Her waiting. Every drop of sweat and tear and accidental drool that fell on that same patch of concrete over four days. She hadn't showered. She hadn't changed clothes. She only had this light-blue dress—washed once, but not thoroughly, the ring of brown mud along the hem had faded into faint, ink-like streaks. Those streaks were the complete record of her: first day going to the bathroom, second day squatting by the pool, third day shaking with hunger, fourth day vomiting on you.

Her finger moved slightly on her knee. Not scratching—tracing, drawing a tiny circle on the skin. Like asking: what are you looking at? You didn't answer. Your nose was still working.

It caught a fifth layer: the faint sour milk-secretion from deep in her armpit after being trapped for a morning—not milk, but the warm, sweet-and-sour heat that skin emits when activated by sweat and body temperature. When she raised her hand to adjust the awning, that smell poured from her cuff—you stood directly below her shoulder, catching the whole warm air mass. Like standing under a warm cow that had just rolled in a mud puddle.

Sixth layer: legs. She'd sat cross-legged too long—knee bends, inner thighs, lower back, those skin-on-skin folds steamed by sweat for five days. When you moved to her side, that smell rolled up from under her skirt—not vomit, not drool, but pure, concentrated, oxygen-starved body scent. Like a whole pot of unsalted bone broth simmered down to only the layer of oil at the bottom—thick, stuffy, heavy, clinging to your sinus lining and refusing to leave.

"Miaowu?"

Her breath fell from above. You quickly moved your gaze from her skirt hem. "I..." You licked your mouth corner, tasted a little sweetness—but your nose was still working, telling you: your friend's upper back had a stuffy, oily scent from skin sweating after being in the sun all day, the side of her thigh had an earthy tang from dried concrete dust, behind her ears had a stagnant, salty-nectar smell mixing old tears with leftover shampoo.

You didn't say it out loud. But your nose wasn't lying. Your nose was a second-grader's nose—it didn't classify smells as "microbial degradation," "sebum oxidation," or "urea crystallization." It used one word: Xiaoman. All of it was Xiaoman. The good too, the not-good-smelling too. All of it was Xiaoman.

You looked down at the quarter custard bun still in your hand, now completely cold. Then you looked up, into her eyes. Those two amber moons had bloodshot veins, exhaustion, but brighter than before—given by the custard buns, though not much.

"Xiaoman. You're going to shower tonight."

She blinked.

"Not now. Tonight. Brother Zhou Ji said it'll rain tonight. A big, big rain. You stand in the rain, rainwater is cleaner than pool water. More than hose water. Then you'll be clean."

Xiaoman turned to look at Zhou Ji. He still leaned against the wall, pulling out his phone—a weather alert had just popped up: "Donghai City: Thunderstorms, 6-7 PM, 15mm/h rainfall, brief gusty winds." He held the phone up, screen toward them. Green text hard to read in noon light.

"Thunder too." he said, voice soft. "Loud thunder. Are you scared?"

Xiaoman looked at the alert. She didn't quite read this world's writing—but she recognized rain. That small icon with raindrops, she'd seen it in the Giant Country too. She looked at it a long time. Then her heel pressed into the ground—her toes, clenched tight for five days, slowly spread on the concrete for the first time. Five pink-white toes like five posts just pulled from soil, crevices embedded with black mud and dried sweat and a small dead leaf that had gotten stuck at some point.

"Not scared. When it thunders, Mom holds me. Now Mom isn't here—holding me in the rain works too."

Huangdou's tail stopped. He tilted his head, ears rotating one after the other, looking at Xiaoman. Zhou Ji stuffed his phone back in his pocket, looked down, poked a small loose stone on the ground with his toe.

You stood up. Wrapped the quarter-bun back in plastic, tied a tight knot. Then you walked to Xiaoman's right big toe—that toe was taller than you, nail pink, a thin horizontal crack along the edge, from when she'd been digging mud in the lawn on the first day. You reached out, fingertip lightly touched that crack.

"All smells good. All of you."

Her toe curled slightly—not dodging, but instinct from being touched, like a giant pink-white cat whose paw pad had been scratched.

You didn't speak again. Your nose was still working. It told you—your friend is dirty. But she didn't mean to be. She just hadn't met a rainstorm yet.

In the distance, a small puff of gray cloud piled on the horizon. Small, thin. But Zhou Ji said it was a cumulonimbus. It was growing.

Xiaoman gently placed the half custard bun she'd been holding into the plastic bag by your feet. Then she curled her fingers—five left, four right plus a half—putting these remaining "rations for tomorrow" on her skirt. That light-blue edge she'd been using as a tablecloth. Done with that, she placed both freed hands back on her knees, palms down. Noon wind blew again. That multi-layered scent dispersed for a moment in the wind—then gathered back. But you didn't cover your nose. You just stood there, looking up at her.

"Later we'll go to the railway. There's a crooked-neck locust tree. Locust flowers are edible. Sweet. Free."

Her stomach gurgled gently. Not "rumble," but a muffle, before anticipation, the kind that was afraid to disturb you—glrk. Sounded once. Then she smiled, revealing that two-meter-wide missing-tooth gap.

"Free." she repeated. "I like free things."

============================================================

## Chapter 33: Locust Flowers

"Free." she repeated. "I like free things."

You turned and glanced at Zhou Ji. He still leaned against the wall, phone screen already dark. Granny stood at the stairwell, apron fluttering in the noon wind. They didn't move. They just stood there—two adults, an open door, a dog lying on the steps with tail wagging. Then Huangdou stood. He looked at Granny, looked at you, ears rotating back and forth several times—then trotted after you. Four paws pattering on the sidewalk.

Two people. One dog. A railway.

Right at the parking lot exit, walk 150 meters along the wall. Under the blue-gray wall, asphalt had baked all morning, steaming a dry, sharp heat. Xiaoman walked beside you—not squatting, not shuffling, walking. Her pace was slow, each step taking several seconds, but no longer that "the ground is lava" fear—she was sure her feet could be on this road.

Crossed the rusty wire fence. Xiaoman's foot landed on the wasteland, flattening a patch of foxtail grass.

Rail tracks stretched south in the afternoon sun. Two parallel steel rails rust-streaked, weeds between the ties slightly wilted from the sun—foxtail bent over, morning glory edges curled, dandelions already blown away leaving bare stalks. Xiaoman walked along the railway. She walked half a step ahead—not leading, but her legs were so much longer than yours, one step was ten of yours. Her skirt swept the gravel, rustling.

Your nose was still working. But the smell was different now. Not the six sediment layers on the parking lot concrete—railway. The metallic tang of sun-baked steel rails. The green sap crushed from dry grass underfoot in the gravel cracks. Dozens of locust flowers tucked in Xiaoman's hair—you hadn't noticed in the parking lot, now wind blew from behind, and you smelled it: thin, thin, thin sweetness.

Then you saw the crooked-neck locust tree.

It grew beside the railway—trunk leaning to one side, bark all cracked. Branches weren't dense, but every bough was heavy with flowers. Pale-yellow, tiny, clustered on branches, swaying in the noon breeze. Some already wilted, some still blooming. Sunlight through the petals showed the honey hidden inside.

You walked to the tree. Trunk about chest high on you. Xiaoman squatted before it, knees bending over gravel, click click click. She placed her skirt holding custard buns beside her feet, then bent down—her face drew closer, nose dropping to about two meters from the canopy. She reached one hand, spread index and thumb, gently pinched the highest bough.

Crack.

The branch snapped in her fingertips. Half a branch fell into her palm, crumbling into three small bark flakes and a few wilted flowers. She looked down at those crushed flowers—bright yellow, translucent, an extremely thin tear crushed through them.

"Too big." she said. Not crying. That soft, can't-look-at-the-teacher expression of tripping over a jump rope and looking at the broken plastic handle in PE.

You looked up at her. Second-grade brain turned. You remembered how you picked flowers—not Mom taught you. One time you passed the community flower bed and saw a bee bump into a branch, the branch swayed, whoosh, petals fell. The bee didn't pick flowers. The bee just touched them. The flowers fell on their own.

"Don't pick." you said. "Touch them. Just touch."

She blinked. The wind from her lashes blew away that crushed flower stuck to her nail. Then she moved her hand to the trunk—index and thumb gently pinched the thickest part of the trunk. That trunk was about chopstick-thin to her. She took a deep breath. Then lightly touched.

Not shaking. Touching. Like the first time she touched the top of your head—index finger pad descending from the air, stopping the moment it touched bark.

The whole tree shivered—not she shook it, it shivered on its own. The kind of rebound a tree has when poked by an eighteen-meter-tall human crayon. Pale-yellow locust flowers fell from the branches—not one by one, but in sheets. Like this drowsy, sun-baked afternoon woke up for one second. Petals fell on gravel, on rails, on the dried mud marks on her skirt hem, on your tilted forehead—you brushed them from your bangs, spread them in your palm: three intact, two with half petals, a pinch of pale-yellow pollen.

Xiaoman watched the falling petals. Eyes widened, then her mouth corners started lifting—revealing that two-meter-wide missing-tooth gap. Not surprise. The laugh of someone discovering for the first time that touching is better than picking, that touching won't break them, that touching makes more flowers fall.

She touched again. And again. Each touch gathered locust flowers into her palm—the pale petals and pollen slowly piling along her palm lines. She didn't pluck another branch. She was using the smallest force she'd learned—lighter than touching the top of Miaowu's head—to touch a tree. The tree was responding.

She raised her palm to her mouth, stuck out her tongue, gave the base of her palm a quick lick. Sweet. Not sugar sweet. Not the rich sweet of custard bun crushing egg yolk on the tongue. Water-thin, transparent—a drop of honey hidden in the calyx at each flower's base, tiny, but a handful of a dozen flowers was a whole spoonful. She popped the second handful into her mouth. Sweet. Third. Every flower's base hid that drop of honey, each drop so small it almost didn't exist, but—a handful. Another handful. Another handful. Stopped counting.

Huangdou circled under the tree. His tail brushed a fallen locust flower, pollen sticking to the tail tip, he lowered his nose and sniffed, sneezed—"ah-chew." Then he stuck out his tongue and curled that locust flower blown off course by his sneeze into his mouth. Chewed once. Spit it out. Didn't taste good. Lowered his head and sniffed the ground again—he'd just discovered the ground he'd circled over a dozen times around the tree was covered in flowers, he just hadn't noticed before.

You picked a locust flower, plucked the calyx, placed the tiny drop of clear honey at the base on your tongue. Sweet. Free. You thought about the fifty-five yuan spent this morning. That was sweet too. But this didn't cost anything. Free sweetness has a different taste—not egg yolk, not butter, not long-kneaded dough. The tree made it itself. It's July. It's the railway.

Xiaoman kept touching the locust tree. She'd touched off most of the outermost layer of flowers, only the inner branches hidden by leaves remained. She used one finger to gently push aside a clump of leaves—inside, more. Small. Hidden in gaps between leaves, pale-yellow little clusters. She looked at that small cluster. Then she withdrew her finger. Gently, gently pushing the pushed-aside leaves back.

"Leave that little bit."

"Why?"

"Because—" her eyes moved to that cumulonimbus thickening on the horizon, "because it'll rain soon. Rain will water them. Flowers that grow back after rain will taste sweeter than these."

You followed her gaze to the horizon. That cloud had grown again. Color from white to gray-white, edges darkening. Wind changed too—not the dry hot south wind from before, but cooler, carrying a trace of dampness, east wind.

You looked at your watch—$727.90. Then you looked up at Xiaoman. Countless locust flowers were tucked in her hair—not pinned on purpose, bounced up when she shook the tree. Pale-yellow specks embedded between black strands, swayed by the wind about to change weather. She hadn't noticed. She was using her tongue to count the drops of honey stuck to her palate—this one is one, that one is two, the one just swallowed is three. Four at the tongue root. Five. Six touched a tooth. Seven. Eight slid into that missing front tooth gap. Nine. Ten. Ten drops of honey total. Exactly the amount gathered after two palms held five times.

She didn't say "ten." You didn't count for her either. You just stood by her feet, holding a locust flower wilted warm by your palm, looking into her eyes—those two amber moons looking at that cumulonimbus already dark on the horizon. Not fear. Waiting. The waiting of wrapping your hair in a towel after a shower. The waiting of sitting in the living room watching the alarm clock when Mom works night shift at the hospital. The waiting of knowing it's coming so no rush.

"Let's go." you said.

She stood up. This time a little faster than when she'd arrived—her legs weren't numb. She turned and walked back along the railway. Her back to the sunset direction—no, sunset was still early, but the sun was slowly moving west. Her shadow dragged a very long outline on the rails, hair in the shadow still swaying, every sway shaking a locust flower or two from between her strands, falling on gravel, on rails. You bent to pick one up, placed it on your tongue. Still sweet. Thin. Free. This time the sweetness left your tongue a little slower—you used your upper front teeth to bite open the calyx, close, bite open, close. This movement had turned into a rhythm you didn't know when. Not from a program. Yours.

You glanced back at the railway. The crooked locust tree still stood there, a thin new crack in the trunk—not from touching just now, from sun, the bark was old anyway. A bead of resin oozing from the broken branch—amber, a hard little scar. Less than two meters from the nearest gravel Xiaoman had stepped over on her way by—she'd seen it herself. She hadn't stepped on it.

Huangdou followed at your feet. That grain of pollen still stuck to his tail tip, he didn't know. Pollen tangled in his tail fur, wouldn't blow off no matter the wind. His black nose sniffed the air—sniffed locust flowers, sniffed steel rails, sniffed that scent drifting from the parking lot, already diluted by the south wind, six smells blended into one sentence.

You looked up. Xiaoman walked ahead, skirt holding the bag of custard buns—her walking rhythm steadier than before. Not the caution of confirming the ground wasn't collapsing every step, but a simpler, less effortful steadiness. She still watched the road, still avoided stones. But her head no longer needed to stay down watching her toes.

Zhou Ji and Granny were still at the stairwell. Zhou Ji wasn't leaning against the wall anymore—he squatted there, holding a small screwdriver, dismantling Granny's broken radio. Granny sat on the low shelling edamame, Huangdou ran over and spat the locust petal in his mouth onto her shoe.

Xiaoman squatted back in her corner of the parking lot—that spot already had a faint white ring, worn into the concrete over five days. Exactly the width of her foot. She placed the custard buns in her skirt by her feet, then sat cross-legged. The cumulonimbus on the horizon thickened another layer. Color from gray-white to dark gray, cloud bottoms turning black. Zhou Ji glanced up at it. Granny glanced up too. Then they went back to what they were doing—turning screws, shelling edamame. Waiting for rain.

============================================================

## Chapter 34: Heli

When you came back from the railway, Zhou Ji still squatted at the stairwell.

Before him lay an old newspaper, on it Granny's broken radio—casing already opened, revealing dense circuit boards and two broken wires. His left hand held a small screwdriver, right index finger poking at his phone screen. Poke, pause, poke. Not chatting—chatting used two thumbs alternating, he used only one index finger. That index finger's nail edge had an old scar from a soldering iron, small as a pale brown mole.

Huangdou darted past your feet, spat the locust petal in his mouth onto Granny's shoe. Granny looked down at the petal on her shoe, tsked, didn't wipe it, kept shelling edamame. Pods piled into a green mountain in the enamel bowl at her feet. Beside her sat a plastic bag of shelled beans—pale green, plump, glistening wet in the afternoon sun.

You walked up to Zhou Ji. Not walked over—squatted. Your knees clicked softly when you squatted, the same sound his knees made this morning when he squatted to look at your watch.

"What are you doing?"

Zhou Ji's finger stopped. He looked up at you—a second-grader squatting before him, locust flowers still in her hair, pale-yellow pollen on her collar, dry lines on her lip from licking honey.

"Fixing the radio." he said.

"No. Your fingers are poking a phone. The radio doesn't have a phone."

Zhou Ji looked down at his right hand—index finger still hovering over the screen, aimed at a line of dark blue text in the Heli app: [Bounty ¥20: How to calculate metastability window in multi-clock-domain SoCs, consequences of insufficient synchronizer stages?]

He didn't answer. He set the screwdriver on the old newspaper, turned the phone screen toward you. The interface was simple—white background, chat bubbles like a messaging app, but each bubble had a small red-background-white-text "Bounty" tag hanging off it.

"Heli." he said, voice soft, like explaining something he was a little embarrassed to explain. "Alibaba and Tencent made it together. Launched this morning. No AI anymore, so people answer people's questions."

You looked at the screen. You didn't know what "synchronizer stages," "MTBF," "CDC path" meant, but you recognized that "¥20."

"You're making money."

"Yeah."

"How?"

"Someone asks a question. If I know it, I answer."

He scrolled down. A list of completed bounties—green checkmarks followed by question summaries and amounts. Multi-bit CDC synchronizer data flip rate—¥15. DDR5 PHY DCC calibration accuracy at 28nm—¥18. UPF power model regression after VCG to AVG switch—¥30. Each with a green checkmark.

You looked down at those checks, looked up at the soldering-iron scar on his finger. You didn't know what "a chip engineer's knowledge going from being worth tens of millions to twenty yuan" meant. You only knew he was poking his phone. Like how you poked your watch this morning to buy custard buns. Every time you poked the watch, beep, money went down a little. Every time he poked, ding, money went up a little. Not a lot. But the direction was reversed. Your arrow pointed down, his pointed up.

"How much did you make today?"

"Three hundred-something."

You calculated—three hundred, divided by five yuan per bun—"sixty." you said, voice thin. "Enough for sixty custard buns."

Zhou Ji's finger paused on the screen. He looked at you, the small vein at his temple jumped. Then his mouth corners lifted less than a millimeter—not a smile, the expression of seeing a little light in a very dark place. Not sunlight. Phone screen light. But light anyway. He didn't say the thought out loud—this second-grader did division in less than two seconds, the answer exactly what he'd calculated three times in his head.

"Yeah." he said. "Twenty-four. You calculated faster than me."

You looked at your watch. Green numbers: $727.90. You looked up at Zhou Ji. His phone screen lit up—a new bounty on Heli: [¥25: How to choose cross-clock-domain synchronizer stages in multi-clock-domain SoCs, MTBF requirement >10 years?] He didn't know which small company's chip engineer had posted this—they used to hand RTL to Fable5 for automatic synchronizer insertion, now Fable5's SDK was shut down, the whole CDC path was stuck. He wasn't alone. The whole project was stuck.

Zhou Ji glanced at the question. Then his left hand—the hand that had held the screwdriver—started typing on the phone screen. Not thumb. Left index finger, one tap at a time, steady. He knew the answer to this. At the company he'd been responsible for the whole SoC clock architecture—from selection to CDC verification to timing signoff, not one stage he hadn't touched. Back then the VP said he "understood clocks deeper than Synopsys's tools." Now worth twenty-five yuan.

Granny pushed the bowl of shelled beans to Zhou Ji's feet. "Eat."

"Wait. One more line."

You watched his index finger type the last few characters on the screen—"recommend 4-stage synchronizer, add handshake protocol." Then he tapped the green "Submit" button. Screen jumped—ding—+¥25. He flipped the phone face-down on his knee, grabbed a handful of raw shelled beans from the enamel bowl, stuffed them in his mouth and chewed.

"Sweet?" you asked.

"Raw. Not sweet."

"Then why eat them?"

"Because my mouth wants to chew something."

He picked up the phone again—screen still lit, Heli interface refreshed, another bounty in the list: [¥20: What cell is most area-efficient for output ISO strategy after UPF power domain shutdown?]

Xiaoman listened to this conversation from afar. She didn't understand "synchronizer stages," "CDC path," "UPF," but she understood "twenty," "three hundred-something," "direction is reversed." Her fingers tightened slightly on her knee—not scratching, gripping. Nails digging into the flesh of her knee, leaving four white indentations. She knew what it felt like to worry about money. In the Giant Country she washed dishes for gold coins—one coin, five plates. Each plate was as big as her bed. She'd saved thirty-seven. But those coins hadn't come with her. All she'd brought was herself, and a light-blue dress swayed by pre-rain wind.

You stood up. Walked to Xiaoman's right big toe—that horizontal crack on the toe was still there, but you noticed an extremely thin, pale-pink new line growing below it. Healing. Five days. The nail had grown without anyone knowing.

"Xiaoman. Do you know what Brother Zhou Ji is doing?"

"I know." Her breath fell from above, light as locust petals. "He's washing dishes."

Huangdou's tail stopped. He tilted his head, ears rotating, first looking at Xiaoman, then Zhou Ji, then you. Then he yawned—tired from the railway run. After the yawn he rested his chin on Granny's shoe, closed his eyes.

Zhou Ji didn't look up. He was typing the second answer. The small screwdriver still lay on the old newspaper, next to the half-dismantled radio—the two broken wires still unconnected. But now he was connecting something else. Not wires. Lines and lines of text typed on a phone screen. Each line worth fifteen or twenty yuan.

Granny put the last shelled edamame in the plastic bag, tied a knot—exactly like the knot you'd tied at the bun shop. Then she set the beans by Zhou Ji's feet. "These are enough for two stir-fries." she said. Zhou Ji's head stayed down. His index finger poked at the screen, poke, wait for refresh, poke. "Mm." He just mm'd. But when he poked the second time, his index finger pressed lighter than the first—not afraid of disturbing anyone, but the kind of thing where: if the screen doesn't break, you can poke it many more times; you can poke until it rains tonight, poke until the radio is fixed, poke until this month's mortgage in the bank account stops being a negative number.

"When I get my first full paycheck this month, I'll pay Mom back." he said, voice so soft you almost didn't hear it.

Granny didn't answer. She took off her reading glasses, slowly wiping them on her shirt hem, wiped for a long time but couldn't get them clean. She didn't mention the AI massage chair Zhou Ji had bought her last year—the chair wasn't broken, the AI was broken, now it was just an ordinary chair that didn't talk, just hummed and vibrated. She didn't mention it. She just put her glasses back on, said: "Soak the beans, stir-fry tonight, add some garlic." Then she stood, picked up the low stool, slowly walked back into the stairwell. Huangdou's tail swept twice behind her heels, then once more.

You squatted back beside Zhou Ji. His phone screen lit up—at the very bottom of Heli's home page a tiny line of text: "Without AI, we still have each other." Not an ad. That product VP's line from this morning's review meeting, screenshot and posted online. Zhou Ji wasn't looking at that tiny line. He'd scrolled to the next page of bounties. But you weren't watching him scroll. You were looking at the tiger's mouth of his left hand—the spot where he'd held the screwdriver had a deep red indentation, a small patch of calloused skin at the edge. In his third year at Lvgreen, calluses had started forming at this spot—now the company was gone, the calluses remained. You could write a bounty on that callus, offer twenty yuan. But you didn't.

You brushed the leftover pollen from the top of your head. "Brother Zhou Ji, can I come back tomorrow?"

Zhou Ji's finger paused on the screen for a full second. He didn't say "Aren't you afraid your mom will yell at you for coming here," didn't say "of course you can." He just picked up the screwdriver from the old newspaper, aimed it at the radio's two broken insulated wires again. "The enamel wire has insulating paint on it. Need to scrape it first." he said. His voice was steady. Not forced steadiness, but the kind of steadiness only someone counting gold coins one by one has.

You stood up. Walked to Xiaoman. Noon wind blew again—this time no six smells. Only three: locust flower sweetness, edamame greenness, and that tiny, inaudible but you-know-it's-always-ringing—ding—leaking from the edge of Zhou Ji's phone screen.

============================================================

## Part 5: Getting Closer

## Chapter 35: One Cent

You took Zhou Ji's phone. Not snatching—reaching. Small hand, fingers spread, like catching a leaf falling from a tree. Zhou Ji glanced at you, placed the phone in your palm.

"Don't delete anything."

"I won't delete."

You turned the screen toward yourself. Heli's interface was simple—a search bar at the top, a row of category tags below: #chip #communications #materials #algorithms #medicine #law. You didn't know these tags. Your finger swiped the screen, flipping through one by one, past #agriculture, past #bridge engineering, past #minor-language translation—when you reached #elementary-math you stopped. But when you tapped in, the questions were like this: chickens and rabbits in a cage, 35 heads, 94 legs, how many chickens and rabbits—¥0.30. You scrolled down four screens more, every question only a few cents or even pennies. None over one yuan. A hundred thousand people were racing for these questions—faster than you could flip pages, you could watch them disappear from their spots the instant a green checkmark and someone's avatar popped up. Because these questions were for everyone willing to grind ranks cheaply—not for a second-grader like you.

You switched tags. #After-school-tutoring was fifth from the end in the third column, a cold tag merged in later by the system. You tapped the first question: a list of a dozen two-digit addition and subtraction problems—perfect for you. But ten seconds before you could open it, a fifth-grader had already snapped it up. Second question was chickens and rabbits again. You flipped through every tag you could tap, then exited #after-school-tutoring. A new tag popped up—actually not a tag, a line of small text the system auto-pushed. You almost swiped it away. But when your thumb hovered over it, a super simple, super short question appeared: [Test question: 1+1=? — ¥0.01]

You tapped in. Green button. "2." Ding. Zhou Ji's phone vibrated: +¥0.01.

You looked up at Zhou Ji. "I earned one cent."

Zhou Ji looked down at the screen, looked at you. "If you'd answered faster, you could've earned two cents."

"Faster and there's nothing left." you said. "Two plus two isn't there. Three plus three isn't there. I flipped every tag. Only this one question."

Zhou Ji's finger paused on the screwdriver handle. He looked at you—you sat on the low stool, holding his phone, expression a quiet, unwavering calm. No "I'm useless," no "why can I only do this kind of problem." You were just stating. Like saying "the wind today is from the east" or "Xiaoman is missing a front tooth."

You handed the phone back to Zhou Ji. He took it, didn't immediately go back to grinding questions. He put Heli in the background. Then he looked back down at the radio's two broken insulated wires.

You stood up from the low stool. Your legs weren't sore anymore—the lactic acid from that morning's hundred meters had fully metabolized by afternoon. Your knees didn't click. You walked to Xiaoman's feet, looked up at her once—she was scraping a patch of dried mud off the tiger's mouth of her other hand with her thumbnail. That mud had come from under the locust tree, different from the dust accumulated in skin crevices over the past few days—it was blacker, stickier, because the soil under the locust tree roots was wetter than concrete dust. She looked down and saw you watching. Her nail stopped, fingers bending slightly—like asking: were you just looking at something? You didn't answer. You just extended your pinky, touched the pad of her index finger. "Wait a sec." you said. "I'm going back for a bit." Then you turned toward home.

Mom was in the bedroom. Curtains drawn, light dim, her phone on the nightstand, charging cable still plugged in. You didn't go in. You crossed the living room—the iPad on the coffee table, screen dark, blue cover, with cartoon cat stickers you'd put on it. You picked it up, tiptoed to the balcony, pushed the glass door open just wide enough to squeeze through, went in and closed it behind you. The balcony was hot—afternoon sun baking the tile scalding. You squatted, iPad on your knees. Opened the Heli web version, checked again: under that #after-school-tutoring tag a new set of 30 problems, three-digit divided by two-digit, ¥1.50. You couldn't do it. One problem took ten minutes, thirty you'd never finish by tomorrow—and you hadn't learned division yet.

You opened email. To: moxuanyue2016@hnu.edu.cn. You hadn't memorized this address—you found it in contacts. Contacts were short: Mom, Dad, Grandma, Dr. Zhang, Mo Xuanyue. You poked the screen slowly with your index finger, one character at a time. After each character you paused to check the strokes for typos.

[Subject: There's an app called Heli, you can earn money]

[Mo Xuanyue: Heli is a new app after Alibaba and Tencent merged. No AI anymore, so people answer people's questions. I earned one cent on it today. The question was what's 1+1. I spent ten minutes flipping before I found this one. I can't do any other questions. But you know everything. Brother Zhou Ji says I'm too slow, but he doesn't know I only found one question. You type much faster than me. And you've already finished fourth grade, you're previewing sixth-grade fractional equations over summer—though you say you always forget common factors when reducing fractions, take forever finding least common denominators, but you have X. With lots of horizontal lines. You'll definitely understand the elementary-school questions on Heli. Help me see if Heli can earn more money. I remember Miia also uses this email, if you're in class Miia might see it first. Whoever sees it first, please reply. My friend's stomach is growling. She needs food. But my watch can't convert to RMB. Heli pays in RMB. If you can answer questions on Heli, the money is RMB, can directly buy rice. Buy eggs. Buy custard buns. Custard buns are five yuan each, Auntie Fang's, the big kind, one fills an adult. Half fills me.]

Miaowu.]

You hit send. The little spinning flower spun three times. The email jumped from outbox to sent—sent. Xuanyue might still be in that two-hour afternoon writing class, iPad locked in the classroom cabinet. Miia might be doing summer homework. Neither of them saw the unread alert. But sent is sent—you put the iPad back on the coffee table, plugged in the charger, screen down in its original spot, beside a well-thumbed copy of Elementary School Math Journal June Finals Special. The right corner of the paper had two small tears—that missing corner you'd torn off, taped to another sheet of paper and framed into that pencil drawing you made for Xuanyue, the one titled "The Moment Her Pencil Tip Broke While Solving a Fractional Equation."

Then you stood up, quietly closed the balcony door, slipped out. The concrete in the stairwell was cool, your sneakers from carpet to concrete, socks still holding the heat from the balcony. You walked back to the parking lot.

The parking lot was quiet. Noon heat had weakened—not temperature dropped, but the cumulonimbus had covered the concrete that had been baking all day, sunlight dimmed, shadows faded. Granny walked out of the stairwell, holding a clothes pole with two empty hangers, hangers swaying in the wind, clinking together. She looked up at the sky, muttered "bring them in, bring them in," started taking down clothes drying by the wall—a white undershirt, a dark-blue apron, a yellowed pillowcase. Huangdou lay by the low stool she'd been sitting on, chin on folded paws, tail sweeping slowly on the ground. Zhou Ji still squatted at the stairwell, screwdriver by the radio, two small screws now lying on the old newspaper. He was using a cotton swab dipped in alcohol to wipe the oxidized green contact points on the circuit board.

Xiaoman still sat in her original spot. Cross-legged, skirt spread on concrete. But her posture had changed—not the posture itself, but the bearing. She raised her right hand to her chest, thumb hooked around the tiger's mouth of her other hand, pushing mud out bit by bit. That mud was black earth from under the locust tree roots, mixed with fine sand and a small broken grass root, embedded deep in the skin lines of her tiger's mouth. She pushed a dozen times, mud crumbs falling from the tiger's mouth onto her skirt—she didn't brush them off. She was just doing the thing. Just like you'd just finished "send email." She looked up. Those amber eyes found you.

You walked over. Not to her feet—but around to her side, walking up the outside of her crossed legs, like circling a mountain. The mountaintop was her shoulder, eighteen meters high, through the light-blue sleeve you could see the thin layer of softened skin on the surface, soaked by sweat—not dirt, but her body saying "I haven't scrubbed myself in five days." On the outside of her forearm was a very faint, almost invisible scratch—not someone else's, hers. Probably some night, hungry and unable to sleep, she'd run a nail lightly across her own skin to feel what being awake was like. The mark wasn't red anymore, just a thin line slightly lighter than surrounding skin.

Those six layers of smell on her body were still there. Sweat, drool, vomit-sour, custard bun, armpit sebum, knee-pit bone broth. But a new seventh layer was added—locust flower sweetness. Not the strong sweetness from shaking the tree just now, but that thin, faint, just-barely-there tail note released when petals warmed to body temperature, like the afterimage of a flower melting on your tongue and lingering on your sinus lining. Because she was waiting for rain. She spread her left hand flat beside her, right thumb having just pinched the last crumb of mud from her left tiger's mouth. She looked down at that crumb of mud roll once on the concrete, stopping at the edge seam of her skirt. Then she used her fingertip to lift her dress hem and wipe her chin—a drop of sweat had been running down just now. You weren't looking at how pretty she was. You were looking at all of her—dirty, hungry, sweet, sour, five days without changing clothes, mud in her nail seams, dry skin and scabs on her lips, her stomach gently gurgling. She was calling, but she didn't speak anymore. Her eyes no longer had that careful "afraid of disturbing you" floating from a few days ago. She sat there—hungry but no longer apologizing; dirty but no longer curling her fingers; still carrying six old smells plus one new sweet layer, but no longer whispering "don't look." She was just waiting. Waiting for rain. Waiting for food. Waiting for Xuanyue to reply to the email. Waiting for the day she could finally eat a whole bowl of hot rice.

The bottom of the cumulonimbus on the horizon was already dark. The wind carried dampness. Granny took down the last piece of clothing, empty hangers on the pole still clinking softly. Huangdou's tail stopped for a second—he smelled the cool ionization in the pre-rain air. Zhou Ji closed the radio's back cover, didn't screw it shut—he'd continue after the rain. Then he stood, hands in pockets, looking at the horizon. Big drops hadn't fallen yet. The wind came first. A cool, solid wind straight off the sea, rolling up the dry dust from the road surface.

You stood at Xiaoman's leg side—a spot where you could see her profile, and just see the lightning slashing down from the horizon. Lightning was thin, silver-white, stretching from cloud bottom to horizon. Four seconds apart—boom. Thunder was low, muffled, like someone beating a drum wrapped in cotton with a drumstick.

The instant the thunder's echo hadn't died away—grrrr. Not thunder. Coming from deep in her body, that long-empty stomach quietly releasing under cover of thunder—a very deep, long low rumble. She quickly pressed both hands to her belly. Not covering her stomach—covering that sound, like afraid it was competing with thunder. But thunder had already won. Thunder buried her stomach's growl, leaving only a thin, ground-hugging residual vibration. You felt it—you sat on the concrete at her feet, and the concrete under your butt transmitted a very faint tremor, not thunder, not wind, stomach.

You looked up at her. She was looking at the sky. No embarrassment on her face—not the girl who'd whispered "I'm hungry" on the lawn the first day. She just kept looking up, fingers pressing through her dress on her stomach, nails slightly sinking into light-blue fabric. She didn't say "I'm hungry." She knew you knew. You knew she'd been hungry for days. No need to say it. Thunder said it for her.

Then she stuck out her tongue, swept it across her lower lip—dry. Swept again, tasting a thread of locust honey leaking from her back molars. Sweet.

You sat down. You sat on the concrete at her feet, back against her calf, looking up at the sky. Through the hair on the back of your head you could feel the warmth radiating from her leg, steamed by sweat all afternoon, flowing toward you. Wind picked up another notch. Another lightning bolt. This thunder was closer. Closer. Closer still. Huangdou lifted his chin from his paws, let out a very soft, tiny whimper he didn't even know he was making. Granny stood at the stairwell holding that pile of freshly taken-down clothes, didn't go inside. She didn't go inside because she saw Xiaoman standing up. When Xiaoman stood, the ground didn't shake. Not because she got lighter, but because the ground had gotten used to her weight.

She looked down at you—you lay on your back by her right foot, knees bent, sweat from your calves rubbed off onto the concrete. Then she looked at the sky. That black wall of clouds pressing down had pushed from the sea to directly above the parking lot. The first raindrop fell on your forehead—small. Cool. Like a locust flower falling from the sky, not sweet. But clean. Second drop. Third drop. Then the whole world flipped over.

============================================================

## Chapter 36: Rain

The first raindrop fell on your forehead—small. Cool. Like a locust flower falling from the sky, not sweet. But clean.

Second drop. Third drop. Then the whole world flipped over.

Not you flipped. The rain did. The cumulonimbus above had been torn open from below, all the water pouring down at once. Not "pitter-patter"—but "whoosh." The sound of raindrops so dense they shattered each other mid-air, hitting the ground already not as drops but as a sheet of water. The parking lot's old awning thwacked under the rain, water running through the tarp's folds into small waterfalls, gushing down. Concrete in an instant went from gray to dark gray—the color of earth soaked through, rain pooling into a thin film on the ground, each new drop punching concentric circles in the film, layer over layer, like countless transparent eyes opening and closing on the ground.

You sat at Xiaoman's feet. Just now the back of your head was feeling the warmth radiating from her calf; now that warmth was cut off by rain. Rain ran down her legs—not drop by drop, but stream by stream. Water flowed down the front of her calves, splitting at the ankle bone into two streams: one around the instep between toes, the other straight down from under the ankle bone, punching small pits in the water film. Her calf skin changed color under the rain—from the dull, pink-gray of days of dust to a brighter, more translucent, pale milky pink, like a bamboo shoot peeled of its dry outer shell. Water streamed down her knee pit, rinsing that red indentation pressed by bent knees for five days into a flowing sheet. You could see her leg—you were close enough—water peeling the sweat, dust, dried tear trails layer by layer from her skin.

You stood up, stepped back a few paces. You wanted to see clearly.

Xiaoman stood in the center of the parking lot. She didn't hide—not like the first day when she'd whispered "I'm afraid I'll crush houses." She just stood. Both hands naturally hanging at her sides, palms outward, head tilted back, eyes closed, lips slightly parted. Rain washed her hair from crown to shoulder—that layer of black long hair stuck to her neck by locust flower fragments, sweat, concrete dust, came apart in the flowing water. Not slowly, but like someone soaking a whole bundle of knotted thread in warm water—water first saturated the pollen on the surface, stripping pale-yellow fragments from between strands, flowing to her collarbone and stopping; then the next, bigger rush of rain washed it pollen plus dust from the hollow down into her collar. Then water pushed her hair from root to tip, separating strand by strand, thick to thin, clumped to individually drifting. The patch of skin behind her ears—where tears had slid down the first day she cried, five days ago—was now washed clean, revealing the earlobe. The small mole on her earlobe you hadn't seen before, covered by dust. Now rain washed it out—black, smaller than a sesame, glistening.

Her face changed in the rain. Not prettier—cleaner. Fine sweat on her forehead washed away, the patch of dried-and-re-dried sebum on her nose gone. The tear tracks on her cheeks—that whitish salt trail running from eye corner to jaw line—dissolved in the rain, not washed away, but salt re-dissolved in water, flowing along the curve of her cheek to her chin tip, hanging there forming a water bead, then shattered by the next raindrop. The dry skin on her lips—that hard, brown, curled crust that had been lifting for days—softened in the rain; she pushed her tongue out between her lips and pushed that softened piece out, it stuck to her tongue tip in the downpour like a faint, almost invisible scrap of paper, hovering several seconds before a raindrop flicked it away. The newly revealed skin was pink. Tender. But not the puffy red from crying the first day—pure, clean, the tender freshness of old skin shed, breathing again. The mud marks on her face faded too—the brown lines mixed with dried saliva and sweat, traveling downstream in the water, to the chin tip, dripping into the rainwater pooled into a small river on the concrete, dispersing. Dispersing into an extremely fine, faint brown mist, circling once on the puddle surface, gone.

She raised her hand. Not shielding from rain, but scrubbing. Right hand scrubbing left forearm—from wrist to elbow bend, back to wrist. Slow movements, careful, like doing something she'd wanted to do for five days but no one had taught her was allowed. Water flowed through her fingers—not ordinary tap water, but a downpour, the kind that punches water mist into concrete—water ran down the slope of her scrubbed forearm to her palm heel, back to the elbow bend, carrying a small gray turbid stream outward from a mud particle at her wrist that had softened. Scrubbed left forearm, then right. Scrubbed back of neck—she lifted her hair—that layer of back-neck skin hidden under hair was also pink-white, in the rain sprouting fine goosebumps, but not cold—tactile. Skin's tactile response to being clean.

Her dress. The light-blue sundress was soaked through by the downpour, fabric from light blue to dark blue—not dirt, but water squeezing the air out of the fibers. Rain ran out of every fiber of the dress, washing out the old urine stains, vomit spots, mud, oil stains along the hem—the ring of dark brown, pale yellow from day one, plus the golden yellow layered on over these five days—all swelled, diluted, washed away. The embroidered hem thread—that extremely faint blue stitching, almost invisible before, clogged by mud and oil—now showed a complete, clean petal-shaped border, never appeared before. She hadn't changed into a new dress. This dress, after four full minutes of being rinsed, recovered its true appearance from the first day she'd arrived in this world.

She tilted her face up, toward the black cumulonimbus in the sky. Mouth open. Rain fell directly into her mouth, she took the first sip. Second sip. Swallow—gulp—sound deep, like a stone dropping into a well. She took several sips, throat rolling, water leaking from the corners of her mouth, running down her chin into her collar. She lowered her hands, letting them hang at her sides again—palms still outward, catching rain. Rain pooled in her palms into two small, trembling lakes. She looked down at the lakes in her palms. Then she flipped her hands, palms down, fingers spread, letting rain run over the backs of her hands and wash away the last locust dirt in her nail seams. That black dirt she'd dug and dug at under the locust tree without dislodging dissolved in the downpour. It, and all the other dirt together, seeped out from her nail seams, dripping from her fingertips onto the concrete, no longer hers.

You stood about twenty meters from her feet. Rain dripped from your bangs into your eyes, you didn't wipe it. You were watching her—not watching sickness, hunger, dirt. You were watching an eight-year-old girl, in a downpour, becoming eight years old again.

Granny still stood at the stairwell. A pile of freshly taken-down clothes lay on the stairs behind her, one sleeve dripping from the top. Zhou Ji stood beside her, hands in pockets, pants legs already spattered by eaves splash, he didn't move. Huangdou squatted at Granny's feet—when he'd gone to catch that custard bun Xiaoman flicked down, his tail had been soaked by rain, now his tail was thin as a soaked paintbrush, stuck flat on the ground. But he didn't go inside. No one went inside. Everyone was watching.

Xiaoman suddenly bent over. Not stomach ache—she noticed a black mark on her instep, shielded from rain by her leg bones just now, hadn't washed off. That was the oldest mud, from the first day she'd squatted on the lawn digging in the dirt, black, dry, like a thin layer of black paint stuck to the skin over her fifth metatarsal, mixed with a few dry grass bits that had stuck at some point. That week of grime she hadn't washed off in four days, washed away by this rain in less than a minute, looked more and more like watercolor fading—edges already spreading, revealing the almost-glowing-white skin under the instep. She squatted—boom—the ground shook—she used her right index finger to press lightly on her instep, guiding water onto that black mark. Then she used her thumb to scrape outward along the curve of the fifth metatarsal. Scraped several times. The last faint, gray-crayon-wash layer at the edge of the black mark was pushed by her thumb from instep to toe root, then washed away by rain. Her instep was clean now.

She didn't stand up. She squatted, both hands braced on the concrete of her knees. Rain ran down her back—dress stuck to her back, you could see her spine. Not a spine with prominent bones from long hunger—that shallow line every child has when their back is wet, from back of neck to tailbone, glinting with alternating water in the dim noon covered by storm. Her shoulders shook—not cold, crying. But not the crying before. The kind of crying after you peel off five days of dirtiest skin and find yourself still the same face in the mirror—crying without knowing you're crying. Rain dripped from her nose, couldn't tell rain from tears.

She looked up at you. Hair stuck to both sides of her face—clean, wet, no pollen, washed a hundred times. Tear tracks were still on her face, but now they were wrapped in new rain—old salt long gone, new ones freshly cried. She looked at you, mouth corners lifted—not ugly, not crumpled. The kind of "look, I showered—look I'm clean now—smell, I don't smell like anything but rain—smell" smile. Revealing that two-meter-wide missing-tooth gap, pink gums, slightly deeper in color after rain—not jarring red, but healthy pink, blood vessels working normally on the gums. That gap held a drop of rain, glistening, refracting the gray-white noon light filtered through dark clouds, like a transparent little pearl held in the gum, gently trembling.

You didn't speak. You just walked over—stepping through the thin sheets of rain pooling on the concrete, flowing toward drains—to where she'd just squatted.

You glanced at the pool as you passed.

The pool was unrecognizable. Rain filled it faster than drains could empty—water level visibly rising, dry feces, urine salt rings, old stains at the bottom swollen by rain, churned up, dispersed. Pool water turned gray-brown and turbid, surface floating with soggy leaf residue and a fragment of old plastic bag blown in at some point. The drain hissed, sucking water—but too slowly, dead leaves and broken glass and years of silt at the drain bottom had formed a waterlogged sponge, water only seeping through the sponge's pores bit by bit. The pool filled fast. A stream of gray-brown water overflowed the pool edge, flowed down the concrete slope toward you—warm, slightly warmer than rainwater, carrying faint ammonia and the fishiness of diluted feces. But it didn't flow long on concrete. New rain caught up, scattered it, mixed it into larger sheets of clear water, carried it toward the drain. The pool kept overflowing, but each overflow was lighter, thinner, closer to rainwater color than the last. First wave was darkest, second half-transparent, third you couldn't tell it from ordinary puddle water. You stood there watching a few seconds. Rain hit your face, cool. That layer of five days of marks on the pool bottom was being scraped away millimeter by millimeter by the downpour. Not washed, scraped. Like a stranger holding the world's biggest brush, sweeping all that salt frost, feces, dry heave residue, broken tile fragments—all into the drain. The drain still hissed. Slow. But it kept sucking. It would suck all night.

You kept walking, leaned against her left ankle. She didn't look down, but she gathered you into the smell range of her left ankle skin. That smell now was wet skin, dress freshly soaked, a faint soap scent—not newly applied soap, but soap residue left in the fibers from when she'd washed her dress last time, woken up by this heavy rain. Rain pushed it out from deep in the fibers then diluted it, from a footprint's worth of soap concentration to the extremely faint soap-flower scent drifting off the whole dress. You closed your eyes. In the instant your eyes closed, rain was still loud. But her stomach growled again—grrrr. No thunder this time. Only rain. But her stomach didn't care about thunder anymore. Rain had already taken away the dirtiest things on her—the rest was just hunger, and hunger could be filled with rice.

You pressed your face to her ankle. Warm pulse under cool skin.

Granny stepped out of the stairwell holding an umbrella—held it low, the edge flipped up in the wind once, she pressed it back with her hand. She walked over to Zhou Ji, stuffed the umbrella in his hand, then walked back to the stairwell, sat on the low stool and resumed shelling edamame. Only a small handful of edamame left, she shelled slowly, rolling each one between thumb and forefinger before dropping it in the bag. Rain thwacked against the awning's tarp—hitting the back of Zhou Ji's hand holding the umbrella handle, hitting the still-clinking empty hangers on the pole. Hitting that puddle of circuit diagram ink spread by the old newspaper on the ground—it was slowly, slowly spreading. The "2.8x core voltage" written on the last line of the circuit diagram was being punched by raindrops into the newspaper fibers, spreading outward, forming a new ring of water shadow at the edge of that already-blurry blue halo. No one went to close it. They were all watching.

Xiaoman placed her hand on her knee. Palm up. Rain pooled in her palm, thwacking in, overflowing, pooling again, like two lakes that never dried. By the time rain dripped into her hair you had your eyes closed. But you knew she looked down at you once. She moved her hand from her knee, above your head twenty meters—fingers spread, like a huge roof woven from fingers and rain, leaking pattering down. Rain leaked through her finger gaps onto your head, onto your nose tip. You didn't dodge. You tilted your head up—that stream ran along your forehead into your eye socket, to your mouth corner, and the rain you tasted was warm fingers, quiet ground reflected figure—not cold, warm. Her fingers had held above your head for several minutes, struck by hundreds of raindrops, skin temperature seeping into the rain, rain dropping on your forehead with that touch of warmth. Then you raised your eyebrow tips—two raindrops by the brow seeped into your eye. You curved the outer corners of your eyes in the rain, squeezing that warm rain out.

In the distance, the cumulonimbus bottom slowly pushed forward. Rain would continue a while. But thunder was gone. Thunder had finished voicing her stomach's growl; now it was rain's turn to speak for her—rain kept talking, gush gush gush, so many words, words a child couldn't understand. But you could feel under her skin that layer still slowly beating in its own pulse—your friend, had finished her shower.

============================================================

## Chapter 37: Day After Day

Day 6

You woke before the alarm. Not because of Mom—Mom had slept in yesterday afternoon then gone to night shift again, bedroom empty now. You woke because a program was running in your body: Xiaoman. Custard buns. Yesterday's eleventh went to Huangdou. Need to buy new today. You stepped toes into sneakers—the sole had touched a thin film of rain yesterday, half dry half wet, insole still carrying the cool damp smell of rain-soaked parking lot concrete. You didn't change shoes. "Change shoes" wasn't in the program.

Auntie Fang's Bun Shop. The plump woman had just rolled up the shutter, steam just starting to rise from the steamer. She saw you at the door—clean uniform this time, but same sneakers. "Back again?"

"Yeah. Ten." You held up the watch-wrist to the scanner. She looked at you, didn't ask "your friend still hasn't left." She just picked up tongs and started bagging buns, put ten in, then added one more—an eleventh—into the bag opening. "This one's free. Regular customer discount."

"I haven't bought that many times—"

"You will."

You didn't argue. You held the bag carefully—lighter than yesterday, because you already knew how to hold it without burning yourself. Walked out of the shop, plastic bag swaying in the morning breeze. Huangdou waited at the community entrance—Granny had let him out at some point, tail wagging slowly, nose sniffing the air, recognizing the custard bun smell in the bag.

Xiaoman was awake. Not woken by you—she might not have slept at all. Those amber eyes were wide open, but different from yesterday. Not the "I've waited for you so long" brightness—flatter, dimmer, like two lamps turned down. When you reached her leg side she looked down at you. Pause. Not didn't recognize you—hunger was making her prefrontal cortex shut down less-urgent functions. Looking down took 0.8 seconds. Yesterday was 0.2. You didn't know what that difference meant, but your nose did—that seven-layer smell on her body, now only one layer left: rain-washed clean skin. When there's no smell at all, a person is closest to nothingness.

You placed the buns in her palm. She ate. One sesame seed at a time. Didn't say "it flowed out." Didn't count how many. Just swallowed the sesame seeds. Then she leaned back against the wall, chin on her knee, watching the sky lighten.

You went to school. But actually you weren't in school—it was summer break. You just sat by her feet, leaning against her left ankle. Zhou Ji came out of the stairwell holding the half-repaired radio. He didn't speak, just sat down beside you, screwdriver on the old newspaper, phone screen lit—Heli. Chip questions were the same few, no one racing today. He tapped a ¥15 one, typed a few lines, then picked up the screwdriver again.

Granny boiled yesterday's leftover edamame with a handful of salt. Pods piled into a green mountain much smaller than yesterday's.

Sun climbed east to west. Xiaoman said two words: "Miaowu. Water." Granny turned on the tap for her, she cupped water in her palm and poured it into her mouth several times—each gulp a heavy "glk." Her throat, having accepted rainwater yesterday, accepted the world's liquid again today.

Evening, your watch went ding. Not Mom. Not alarm. A green notification—WeChat. WeChat you were logged into on the iPad. You fished the iPad out of your backpack, opened it. The sender's avatar was Mo Xuanyue—a glasses-wearing panda, a mole on the panda's nose tip.

[Mo Xuanyue: Class ended. Tried Heli, elementary math is easier to grab—I did eight chicken-and-rabbit problems, 30 cents each. Grabbed three fourth-grade profit-loss problems, 50 cents each. Found a set of fifth-grade logic reasoning questions, 80 cents each, did five. Total earned ¥6. Transferred to your WeChat. Is your grown-up friend still there? Doubao told me AI broke. But didn't tell me people can earn money. I'll continue tomorrow. But I have writing class during the day, can only grind at night. Miia isn't out of class yet. She went to the children's palace for informatics training this afternoon—she passed the retest last week, teacher put her in summer B class, six hours a day. Her watch isn't allowed in the computer lab, she hasn't seen the email. But she'll see it tomorrow. Everything you wrote I saved. Your friend won't starve. Mo Xuanyue.]

¥6.00—WeChat wallet deposit. You looked down at the number. Not much. Only enough for one bun. But you weren't alone anymore. After a whole world shut down, you had two people's combined force.

Xiaoman looked down at your iPad screen—she couldn't read those characters, but she recognized that "¥6." Her lashes twitched, then the outer corner of her eye lifted less than a millimeter. Not a smile. Knowing.

---

Day 7

You went to buy ten custard buns again. The plump woman didn't ask. She just bagged them, added one more, then said: "Did you eat that free one from yesterday?" You said yes. She nodded. Huangdou waited at the community entrance again, tail wagging slightly faster than yesterday—he recognized the rhythm now.

While Xiaoman ate, her hand shook. Holding the fourth "sesame seed" between thumb and index finger, lifted to mid-air—stopped about three meters from her lips—the tiger's mouth of her left hand suddenly cramped. Not pain, but a fine tremor from low blood sugar. The sesame fell on her skirt, rolled to the hem seam and stopped. She didn't pick it up right away. She looked down at the sesame for a long time—not sad, but her visual cortex was losing resolution as the prefrontal cortex shut it down. You stood up at her feet, under her skirt hem, tiptoed, picked that crumb of custard bun out of the skirt fibers and placed it in her palm. Your fingers were thinner than her palm lines.

"Fell off." you said.

She looked down at you. Nearly a second's delay. Then nodded. Put the crumb in her mouth. Didn't chew—swallowed directly. Because swallowing takes less energy, chewing requires the jaw muscles to work.

Evening, the iPad dinged again. Mo Xuanyue: [Grabbed seven profit-loss problems today, ¥7. Transferred. How's your friend today?] You replied with two words: "Alive." She sent a panda-nodding sticker—the panda's nose had a mole, the one you'd carved into an eraser for her last year.

Afternoon. Zhou Ji's radio finally made sound. Not Doubao—a scratchy, broken static. He twisted the knob, still static. "Solder this capacitor and it'll get a station. I'll bring the iron tomorrow." He closed the radio's back.

Xiaoman didn't react. Her eyes looked at the horizon—not clouds, but light. Sunset light came from the west, opposite the morning light when she'd arrived six days ago. Her hair swayed in the breeze, ends brushing the fabric on her shoulder. She no longer feared crushing houses like the first day. She was shrinking another way now—not physically shrinking, but consciousness retreating deep into her body, only the essential prefrontal functions left controlling pupils, breathing, and basic language.

She watched the sunset. Lips moved. "Mia."—missing a vowel. You looked up at her. Her mouth corners lifted slightly—barely visible, less than a millimeter, the curve her amygdala still remembered. Then she said: "Tomorrow's. Bun—" also missing a vowel.

You looked down at the iPad—WeChat wallet ¥6.00. Watch $713.10. You looked up. "There'll be some tomorrow. Tomorrow one more than today. The plump auntie gives one free."

She didn't nod. She just placed her hand above your head—not lowering it, hovering about twenty centimeters above your hair. Her fingers trembled slightly. Low blood sugar. But you recognized that gesture. It's "Do Not Open Before Christmas." It's those last few seconds before she pulled her fingers back from your head after the rain stopped. It's saving. Not forgetting.

In the distance over the sea, cumulonimbus clouds were piling this way—not separate puffs like yesterday, but a continuous gray bank, bottoms already darkening. Zhou Ji looked up at the sky. "Heavy rain tomorrow." Then he looked down at Heli—new chip questions had come up, he tapped a ¥15 one, typed for half an hour. "Ding." +¥15. Today's earnings stopped at ¥500.

Granny poured edamame pods into the trash. Plastic bag piled at the stairwell wall. No more rice. No more eggs. Only yesterday's half-pot of thin porridge water, on the gas stove, lid half open, a thin film of rice milk congealed on the surface. But she didn't sigh. She just finished pouring pods, washed her hands, walked to your side, placed that white floral handkerchief on your knee. Then she bent down and picked up the old newspaper blown sideways by the wind—the newspaper that had lain spread three days, covered in circuit diagram ink stains—and folded it into a tiny square. Exactly the same knot as the one you'd tied at the bun shop.

Darkness fell.

Not the darkness of sunset—but the oppressive black of cumulonimbus overhead, the whole sky sinking. The parking lot's mercury lamp wasn't on—government shutdown, no one working at the streetlight department. Only the thin white line of light on Zhou Ji's phone screen on his tiger's mouth, the warmth of a few sesame seeds in Xiaoman's palm slowly spreading in the dark, and the green numbers on your watch: $713.10.

You sat at Xiaoman's feet, back against her left ankle. That crack on her left toe was more visible than five days ago—on the fifth night of calcium and protein deficiency, the nail had started shedding its keratin layer. You picked it up, put it in your uniform pocket. Then you felt her left toe curl slightly—not dodging, but touch. "I know you're here." Her amygdala was taking over the prefrontal cortex's low-battery warning line.

Tomorrow it'll rain. Tomorrow there'll be wind. Tomorrow Xiaoman will say fewer words.

You leaned against her left ankle, closed your eyes. iPad on your knees, WeChat interface still lit: the bottom line of the chat with Mo Xuanyue—"Your friend won't starve. Mo Xuanyue." Green checkmark. Sent. You hadn't replied yet. Not didn't want to—you were tired of talking today, your mouth wanted a rest. You typed in your head, pushing one character at a time toward her direction. But the wind was too strong. The characters scattered—into ten custard buns, 30-cent chicken-and-rabbit problems, that film of rice milk on the porridge, and an egg that didn't exist yet. The egg hadn't been laid. But the chicken was already on its way.

============================================================

## Chapter 38: Amygdala

Day 8

When you opened your eyes, it was still dark.

Not the alarm. Your own brain—about ten minutes before the alarm, it woke you by itself. Because a program ran in your body: Xiaoman. Custard buns. Yesterday's eleventh went to Huangdou. Need to buy new today. The program doesn't need an alarm. The program is more accurate than the alarm.

You stepped into sneakers—the sole had touched a mix of rain and concrete dust yesterday, now dried into a thin, pale gray shell. You bent down and picked at the shell, dislodging a nail-sized fragment. You placed it on the nightstand. "Pick at sole" wasn't in the program. You were adding new programs.

Auntie Fang's Bun Shop. Shutter half-rolled, the plump woman pouring water under the steamer. She heard your sneakers pattering on the concrete, didn't look up. "Ten."

"Yeah."

"Here." The bag was already packed—ten custard buns, plus one more. She'd started adding them on day two, not today. "Regular customer discount" has no expiration date.

You held up your watch. Beep. $7.39. Balance: $705.71. You looked down at the number. Enough to buy many more tomorrows. You held the bag carefully—the buns were hot, but you'd learned to hold the bag bottom flat instead of gripping the opening. The heat from your palm went from epidermis to dermis to blood, the only steady temperature in eight days.

Huangdou waited at the community entrance. He'd gone from "chance encounter" on day one to "on time." His biological clock was slightly off from yours—you needed one extra bun to press his tail down. You didn't add one. You just walked.

Parking lot. Granny sat at the stairwell, the enamel bowl on her low stool soaking the last handful of rice—she'd said "last handful" yesterday, it was "last handful" today too, because after sharing that half-pot of porridge with Zhou Ji yesterday she'd dug a small bag of old rice from storage, yellowed but not wormy. She soaked the rice in water, picking out the blackened grains one by one, setting them on the old newspaper beside her. Huangdou ran over and sniffed the blackened grains, sneezed—not scalded, but mold dust from old rice. Granny didn't look up. She just kept picking.

Xiaoman sat in the parking lot corner—same posture as yesterday. Cross-legged, skirt spread on concrete. But her spine wasn't straight anymore. Not leaning against wall—her whole body had shrunk inward, like a plant that hadn't had water too long, curling its leaves to reduce evaporation. Her head hung low, chin nearly touching collarbone, black long hair falling forward, covering her whole face.

You walked to her left foot. "Xiaoman." About two seconds—no, three—before she lifted her head. Hair slid from both sides of her face with an extremely faint sound—that sound of hair too dry, split ends, strands hooking and pulling apart. Her eyes—those two amber moons—were now two phone screens with one bar of battery, screen brightness forcibly dimmed to minimum by the system. Pupils fully dilated—not because she saw you, but because hunger had locked her autonomic nervous system into "searching for food" mode. Whatever she looked at, pupils were dilated.

"Bun—" she said. Missing a vowel and a consonant. Only the shell of one word left. You didn't correct. You set the bag on concrete, opened it, steam whooshed out. You lifted out a custard bun—hot, just holdable. You held it over your head.

She looked down. The motion was slower than yesterday—not slow motion, stuttering. Like a web page text that wouldn't load, characters popping one by one. Ch-in. Down. Ch-in. Down. Her nose tip stopped about five meters above your head. She could smell the custard bun. She knew it was a custard bun. But her brain took a very long path to translate "olfactory signal" into "food"—all the prefrontal blockades on that path had collapsed, the amygdala had set every detour to "express."

Her left hand reached over. Not descending from the air—but translating from the side, fingers spread, like a pink-white net. Not the "afraid of hurting you" little girl's motion. Amygdala-controlled. Direct. No hesitation. The angle between index finger and thumb was twice normal—not more careful, but greedier. Her fingertip touched the back of your right hand holding the bun. Then she stopped. Her amygdala was discerning—on the palm there was a warm, round, milk-scented thing. But right next to that thing, there was another very thin, also warm, also sweating a little, also moving thing. Two signals entered the amygdala simultaneously. Both marked as "can be put in mouth."

She made a choice.

Her thumb and index finger closed—not pinching the custard bun. Pinching you.

The bun slipped from your palm. You flipped half a circle in the air—not the flip of being thrown up, but being lifted by the waist. Your view switched from parking lot concrete to gray-white sky in a swoosh, then back to her face—that face looking up at you from below, the missing-front-tooth gap drooling. Saliva pooled in the gap's gum into a transparent, slightly reflective lake, hanging down as she leaned closer. Your left shoulder touched her lower lip—wet, warm, soft. You were between her lips and gums. Her tongue pushed against that missing-tooth hole, a drop of saliva taller than you slid from the edge of the gap—you could see your own reflection in it—then from deep in her throat came an extremely low, soft, viscous, prolonged "glk"—not a hungry stomach growl. Swallowing reflex. Her tongue tip was pushing open the upper and lower gums about to close, saliva pulled into a thin, unbroken strand above your head.

You saw her pupils. Dilated so wide the amber iris was almost invisible, only a thin gold ring around a black hole. In that black hole there was no you. Not that she didn't want to see you—her amygdala had taken over her visual cortex. Anything marked "can be put in mouth," her amygdala wouldn't allocate extra resources to confirm "who is this." Because of hunger. Because of survival. Because of eight days. Because of only ten sesame seeds a day.

"Xia—" Your voice was swallowed by her lips. After upper and lower lips closed, your world became pink, wet, saliva-wrapped darkness. Her tongue lifted you between her palate and back gum—you felt the rough taste mucosa at the top of her mouth sloping toward the throat. Then her tongue root arched—not licking. Pushing. Tongue tip contracted from front to back, pushing your back toward her throat.

Her throat opened.

Not mouth opening—throat. Epiglottis flipped down covering the trachea, upper esophageal sphincter relaxed. Your feet went in first—the rubber sole of your sneaker scraping past the ring of half-contracted muscles at her throat opening, then calves, then thighs, then torso. Not falling. Squeezing—her esophagus walls like a warm, peristaltic tube of flesh pressing from all sides onto your body. You could feel her pulse on the outer esophageal wall, twice the normal rate—not heart racing, but amygdala driving sympathetic nerves, routing all mobilizable energy to the digestive system. It was preparing stomach acid. It was waiting.
