Darkness. Not the light-blocked-by-eyelids kind of dark, but absolute darkness inside the esophagus. No light. No morning light refracted by saliva. Only pressure, only temperature, only layer after layer of muscle deep in her body rhythmically pushing you down.

Air was thin. You breathed as deep as you could—but the air here wasn't mouth air, it was the oxygen molecules left on the esophageal mucosa from the last swallow, covering the mucosal surface—extremely little, each breath carrying the mucus scent secreted by the esophageal wall: slightly salty, slightly briny, like the taste when you press your tongue to the inside of your sweaty wrist, but magnified countless times. Your uniform front pressed against the anterior esophageal wall—that mucosa was smoother, tighter, thinner than the inside of the mouth, through it you could feel her aorta pumping just below her manubrium. Every heartbeat traveled from your left shoulder to your right shoulder—boom—boom—boom—the same heart you'd heard on her collarbone on day one. Only now you were six centimeters directly behind that heart, separated by an esophageal wall, a mediastinal connective tissue layer, a pericardium, listening to it push blood into the aortic arch and descend. It didn't sound like distant thunder—thunder went boom and was gone. The heartbeat was always there. Since you entered her esophagus, it hadn't stopped. 120 per minute. Much faster than yours.

Your sneaker sole scraping against the esophageal wall made a very faint "scrape" sound—friction between rubber and wet mucosa. Each esophageal peristalsis pushed your body a few centimeters down, the sound rang again. "Scrape"—then stopped. Then you felt the esophageal circular muscle tighten on your ribs, squeeze past—"scrape"—tighten again, squeeze again. Like a very long, warm, wet python measuring your body with its own. No one had measured you from head to toe before—not with eyes. With an esophageal wall. It was remembering how big you were.

When your nose inhaled, it tasted a deeper smell drifting up from somewhere below the esophagus. Not sour—sour was stomach acid. This was fainter, like milk left at room temperature all day starting to slightly ferment, sweet rot. You knew this smell. The first day she leaned toward you—nose down to three meters—you'd smelled exactly this: "faint, warm milk smell, plus a little saltiness from after crying." This wasn't the stomach's smell. It was her own body's smell. Glands on her esophageal wall secreted something she couldn't smell, and you were now at the source of that smell.

The watch on your wrist glowed green—$705.71—the light passed through the esophageal wall and her skin to the parking lot, but what you saw now was only your fist-sized green halo wrapped by esophageal mucosa. You pressed the screen off with your thumb. Not afraid it'd wake her. Because in that instant you suddenly thought—this was maybe the last time she'd fill her entire esophagus without recognizing you. You didn't want a watch's light to cut all this into cross-sections and summaries and judgments—you wanted to stay inside her body a few seconds longer. Not because you liked it here. Because you wanted to hear her heartbeat one more time. From the inside.

Then you stopped.

Not esophageal peristalsis stopped—but your body stuck at some point in her mid-esophagus. There was a natural physiological bend there—the aortic arch crossing the esophagus, the esophageal wall narrower here than above. Your shoulders caught in that bend, esophageal wall gripping you from both sides. Her throat made an extremely deep "glk"—not the light sound of swallowing reflex, but the dull thud of muscle spasm when the esophagus was stretched to its limit. Then her body finally realized.

Not hearing—touch. Stretch receptors on the esophageal inner wall sent a signal. The signal shot up the vagus nerve, through the brainstem, through the already-retreating amygdala, all the way to the last small patch of prefrontal cortex still powered off. That patch had only one sentence—"My name is Miaowu. I'm not a cat. Mia—owu."—waiting to match this tactile signal. A second-grader's uniform cuff had a ring of worn fuzz, that ring of fuzz squeezed in the esophagus producing a fine sensation—the first two words her esophageal wall recognized. Miaowu.

Her whole upper body froze. Then she threw up.

Not voluntary vomiting—a vagus nerve reflex. Diaphragm spasm—her abdomen jerked inward, stomach contents—a few undigested sesame seeds, rainwater she'd swallowed this morning, a little bile residue from yesterday—all squeezed out of the stomach, up the esophagus—not down. You felt a warm, slightly acidic liquid surge up from below the esophagus, wrapping you entirely, then—her mouth opened. And you were pushed back out by her esophagus—not sliding out slowly like drool, but the fundus spasm in that second pushing you from mid-throat directly to the tongue root, then sliding back to the back of her lower lip. You landed in her palm—a thin stream of yellow bile dripping from her mouth corner. Drool, bile, undigested sesame crumbs, and you. All of it.

You sat up. Your hair was soaked—not drool, but an extremely thin film formed by diluted gastric acid mixed with drool in the air. Your uniform was soaked in the same liquid. Your sneakers squelched. You looked up. Her eyes—those amber moons—no longer had pupils so dilated they were invisible. They were, at extremely fast speed, panicking, at the speed an eight-year-old has after doing something completely irreversible—contracting. Focusing. Saw.

You were the first thing in this world she recognized with an esophageal wall. Not seeing. Sticking.

"Mia—" she only got out this one word. Then no more sound. Not didn't want to speak. Couldn't. Her amygdala—the amygdala that had swallowed its friend then spit it back out—was feeding her prefrontal cortex every hunger signal stored over eight days, every swallowing reflex, every object it had marked as "food." And now those signals were all covered by an older, deeper, completely unrelated region—that region said: you swallowed Miaowu. You swallowed the child who said on the lawn on day one "I like the natural you." She tasted her uniform fuzz with her esophageal wall, then spit her out. But before that, she swallowed her. She really swallowed her.

You stood up—in her palm, you stood only as high as her palm lines. You walked to the knuckle of her thumb bend, stomach acid slowly evaporating in the morning breeze. Then a faint sour smell, and the pale-red mark on the back of your wrist from being soaked in gastric acid—it congealed into an extremely thin line on your wrist. You didn't wipe it off. You looked up at her—her pupils were slowly, very slowly adjusting from dilated to normal size. She couldn't speak yet. She could only say with her eyes—not "sorry," but "am I still here?" She needed someone to tell her—the person who just swallowed Miaowu into her stomach, was that still her?

You said one sentence. Eight characters. Voice thin but clear. "It's okay. Because this is the most natural you."

She froze. Day eight. Hunger had slowed her memory search by nearly four seconds. Then the memory was found—eighth morning, lawn edge of chapter one, you sat in the tree branch holding the red basin, she'd been made to cry by her own accident, you drifted down from the branch and said it. "I won't be afraid of you. Because this is the most natural you, I like the natural you!" After that she washed her dress, washed the stains on her side, cried many times, laughed and said "whatever, it's already happened," picked locust flowers, waited through a heavy rain, waited four tomorrows, waited for a second-grader who never lied to her. Kept waiting.

Her eye rims reddened. Not the kind of red that drops tears. The kind of red when there are too many tears and you don't know whether to drop them. A basketball-sized tear hung on her lower lashes, refracting morning light—that tear held a child's wet face, no fear, only waiting. You'd waited a long time. You were just waiting—waiting for her to finally become herself again on day eight.

From her right palm holding you, she gently, gently placed you back—not on the ground. On the custard bun that had fallen on the ground. She picked it up from the concrete, wiped the dust off the bun skin with her fingertip—the same motion as when you'd picked up the sesame seed for her—then put the bun in her mouth. Chewed. Swallowed. "Sweet." Two words. Full vowels. Full consonants.

The distant cumulonimbus finally rolled over the parking lot. First raindrop hit the concrete, punching a small dark-gray wet mark. Granny looked up, carried the enamel bowl into the stairwell. Zhou Ji closed the radio—that capacitor was soldered, he twisted the knob, static—then a soft, broken voice came out: "...Donghai City... today daytime to night... thunderstorms... southeast wind force 3 to 4..." Not Doubao. A person's voice. An announcer, working with that capacitor in Zhou Ji's radio.

Huangdou rested his chin on Granny's shoe, tail wagging. Raindrops went from one to a sheet, hitting the parking lot awning, the drain, the bottom tiles of the pool. After Day 6 and Day 7, above the layer of old marks washed clean by Day 5's downpour, a new pale-yellow ring had formed—new salt frost from dried urine, thinner than the old layer, because it was so thin it immediately re-dissolved into water when hit by raindrops, squeezing through cracks in the pool bottom into the drain along the ripples. Hitting that tiny, faint line on Zhou Ji's phone screen on the Hili home page that had already been swiped away, that not-many-people-remembered-this-morning: "Without AI, we still have each other." Then his phone screen went dark. You pressed it. Your finger tapped Zhou Ji's screen, then pulled his sleeve away from the table, set it aside, walked back. When you walked back, Xiaoman was looking down at the indentation in her palm—the dent you'd made when you fell. Not big. About half a washbasin deep. She closed her palm, covering the dent—like hiding something that shouldn't exist, or like shielding a sesame seed she hadn't finished eating.

You walked to her calf. Sat. Leaned against her left ankle. Her left toe curled slightly—same as yesterday. Same as the day before. Same as the first day when you poked her toenail crack. Your hair was still dripping—her gastric acid mixed with drool had already formed an extremely thin salt frost on your scalp. But her left ankle skin was clean, rain-washed for two days, no smell. You closed your eyes. Rain covered all other sounds. Including the one that finally came from deep in her throat, muffled, like an old radio that just got a new capacitor—"Mia—owu"—vowels. Consonants. All there. Two words. Your name.

============================================================

## Chapter 39: Miia

Day 8, evening

When Miia came out of the children's palace, it was already dark.

The informatics training computer lab was in the corner classroom on the third floor of the children's palace. The AC was always broken, the windows never closed, the old switch fan in the corner went click every five minutes, like someone clearing their throat. Miia sat in the first row by the aisle—not teacher-assigned, chosen. Because the aisle was closest to the door, so when the bell rang Miia could bolt out first. Not didn't want to write code—after six hours of afternoon algorithm training, Miia just wanted to go home, crack the ice watermelon from the fridge, and take a bite.

Today's content was dynamic programming state compression optimization. The teacher put up a traveling salesman problem variant—sixteen cities, state count exceeding training limit—and asked who could do it. The classroom was quiet for about twenty seconds. Miia raised a hand. Not because wanting to show off, but because Miia wanted class to end early. When Miia wrote out the rolling array, the fifth-grade boy beside was still drawing a decision tree.

On the bus home, Miia pulled out that old iPad with a cracked case in three corners—a nail-sized black spot in the bottom right corner of the screen, from stepping on it last year. Miia opened email. Xuanyue said there was a new email today to remember to read, but Miia hadn't brought the iPad during training. Apple ID login forced a dozen update popups, Miia dismissed them one by one.

Then Miia saw that email. Sender: Miaowu. Time: five days ago. Subject: There's an app called Heli, you can earn money. The content read from "what's 1+1" to "my friend's stomach is growling" to "cookies are as big as beds"—

When Miia read here, the bus happened to pass a speed bump. The iPad bounced on Miia's knee, the dead pixel in the screen's black spot flashed a green line. Miia held the iPad down with a hand, reread that line: "Cookies are as big as beds."

Cookies as big as beds. How big a bed? Miia slept in a 1.2-meter children's bed. If a cookie was as big as a bed, how tall was the person eating it? That kind of person Miia had seen—farther from our building's roof than the roof is from the ground—no, Miia hadn't seen grown-ups. Miia had only seen the old willow canopies by the lake from the seventh floor window of the student dormitory, but those canopies were only ten-plus meters away. Miia didn't know if people could grow that tall. But Miaowu said it, Miaowu didn't lie. Miaowu was the first kid Miia knew who could use colored pencils to tell the three "de" particles apart. Miia opened Heli—Miia couldn't use it without practicing—not because Xuanyue recommended it, but because Miaowu had filled in all the holes Miia didn't know where to start in the letter. It took Miia a full minute plus a refresh to load the page.

Registered. Username: miiaalgo. No avatar. No bio.

Miia tapped the #algorithm tag. First question after refresh: #dynamic programming—minimum path sum in a number triangle from top to bottom—¥20. Miia looked for about three seconds. Not three seconds to understand—three seconds to remember that number triangle was popular-group problem 2 difficulty. Miia had written a thirteen-line recurrence before. Miia poked out those thirteen lines on the iPad screen with a finger—no indentation (iPad's built-in English keyboard couldn't handle large blocks of code), no comments, only variable names i, j, dp, read it back once to check for out-of-bounds. Submit. Ding. +¥20. Screen flashed—a system notice: [Congratulations on completing your first bounty! Current balance: ¥20.00]. Miia stared at the notice for three seconds, then pushed the black spot at the screen edge aside with a hand.

Second question: #graph theory—determine if a directed graph contains a cycle, O(V+E) required—¥25. Miia poked. DFS coloring, twenty lines.

Third: #greedy—interval scheduling, maximum non-overlapping intervals—¥15. Miia poked. Sort by end time, ten lines.

Fourth: #dynamic programming—0/1 knapsack variant—¥25. Miia poked. Rolling array optimized to one dimension, twenty lines.

Fifth: #number theory—sum of divisor counts of first N numbers—¥30. O(NsqrtN) passes N=10^5, Miia checked the data range again, wrote an optimized version first. N=10^6, harmonic numbers needed. Miia's hand slipped on the first line, wrote the wrong initial value, deleted and rewrote. Passed. +¥30. Miia thought 30 was today's biggest. But Miia didn't know that under the #chip tag, Lvgreen new grads were picking up bounties Zhou Ji had already answered for ¥5 each, while Zhou Ji himself started at ¥30 per question.

Five questions. ¥115. Miia looked at the total—115 yuan, enough for a month and a half of cold noodles with egg at the children's palace gate. Miia just thought this should be enough to buy many, many custard buns. Miia opened WeChat—Miaowu's WeChat avatar was a pale-blue stamp Miia had carved with an eraser last autumn, stamped on homework paper, one cat ear pressed crooked. Miia transferred ¥100 to Miaowu's WeChat wallet. One hundred. Miia stopped and thought. One hundred yuan bought twenty custard buns—two and a half a day lasted eight days, plus the previous six yuan, about eleven days. Then Miia remembered cookies as big as beds. But Miia didn't want to send something useless like "is it enough?" Miia just typed two words: "Done." Then—"Is your friend better now?"

After sending, Miia leaned against the bus window. The window was cool, glass covered in AC condensation. Miia drew a circle on it with a finger—two legs on the right of the circle, a tiny sesame seed on the left. The bus turned past the fourth intersection—Donghai City Binhai Park entrance light was still on, but the bench by the streetlamp was empty.

---

When Miaowu received the WeChat, she was sitting at Xiaoman's feet. The iPad vibrated first—Xuanyue, as usual, after daily writing class and elementary olympiad section, transferred a sum: [Today ¥6. Your algorithm-genius friend shares my email—Miia went home today, should have seen your email.] You replied with a cat-paw-pressing-screen sticker—the only sticker you knew. Then about half an hour later, the iPad vibrated again.

[Miia: ¥100.00 transferred] + "Done. Is your friend better now?"

You looked down at Xiaoman—her left toe had just been touched by your back, instinctively curling then relaxing. Her pupils were back to normal—after the storm of swallowing you then spitting you out, her prefrontal cortex had retaken the wheel. She was using her thumb pad to scrape the nail seam on her other hand's index finger—a slow, focused motion, completely unlike the motion when she was starving on day five, digging in rubble. Because this motion wasn't about digging out mud, it was touching. She was feeling for residue in her nail seams. Confirming—"I swallowed Miaowu and spit her out just now, is she hurt"—using the only way she could touch you in this world. You looked up into her eyes—those re-focused ambers. "Much better." you said. "Xuanyue and Miia together earned a lot. Plus the watch can buy ten more—tomorrow you can eat thirty buns."

Xiaoman looked down. Her lashes shadowed you. She didn't know how to write the two characters "Miia." She only knew—this is Heli. This is what you've been doing. Someone, after solving a traveling salesman state-compression problem in half an hour that afternoon, didn't go to the fridge for ice watermelon, but transferred that money to another second-grader. She didn't even ask who this child's friend was. She only knew cookies were as big as beds. Enough.

She raised her right hand—index finger slowly descending from the air, stopping half a meter before your face, fingertip lightly touching the top of your head. Ninth second. One, two, three—longer than when she touched you the first day. Not taking temperature, not confirming "is this kid still here." It's "you're here. It's good you're here."

In the distance, half the cumulonimbus had receded, revealing a patch of clean night sky through a gap. The first star over Donghai City happened to stop on the tip of Zhou Ji's radio antenna. That old radio, after finishing the thunderstorm forecast, switched to evening music—a very old singer singing a song whose name no one remembered. Granny wrapped the soldered capacitor in more insulating tape at the stairwell. Huangdou's tail stopped, then started wagging again.

You looked down at the iPad—WeChat wallet: ¥119.00. Watch: $705.71. Then you exited the wallet, opened Miia's chat window.

[Miaowu: Thirty isn't enough. She's huge.] You stopped and thought, a second-grader's brain didn't have the phrase "1728x metabolic rate." You just added: [She's taller than our whole building put together. You calculate.]

On the bus, Miia's phone screen lit up. Miia looked down—"taller than our whole building put together." Miia's finger paused on the screen for a second. Not scared. Converting—the student dormitory building Miia lived in, seven floors, about three meters per floor. Twenty-one meters. Multiply by that kind of thing—Miia thought it took two multiplications, but didn't know which two numbers. Just thought it, then quietly switched back to Heli, and saw a green question at the far right of the tag bar: #graph theory—minimum spanning tree, Kruskal + union-find path compression optimization—¥30. Miia tapped in. This wasn't traveling salesman. Not number triangle. Union-find was what Miia had learned in training class just last week—only done one template problem then moved on. Path compression was what Miia had glimpsed on the teacher's slides today, but hadn't written yet. "Add a line s[x]=find(s[x]) in the find function," Miia remembered that screenshot. Miia poked out the first line on the iPad screen—struct Edge. Needed sorting, wrote a cmp. Then wrote find—first time forgot path compression, deleted and rewrote. Second time put path compression after recursion, missed a return, deleted and rewrote. Third time. Passed sample. The screen's black spot covered half the compile error, Miia tilted the iPad.

"Got it"—Miia's WeChat avatar lit up less than a second. Then dimmed. Not angry. Switched back to Heli and kept scrolling. Outside the window, the willows had disappeared around the next block.

============================================================

## Chapter 40: Day Forty? No, Day Nine

Day 9

You didn't go to Auntie Fang's. Not the program changed—you changed the program. Yesterday you'd typed a question on the iPad, one character at a time: "What fills you up the cheapest?" First search result: flour. Second: rice. Third: don't buy buns.

You walked to the small supermarket at the community entrance. The owner was stocking goods at the door, bags of flour stacked on the steps, white woven sacks printed with green wheat ears—exactly like the rice sacks at the farmers' market that day. You looked at the weight printed on the bag: 5kg. ¥15.

You poked at your WeChat balance: ¥119.00. "Five bags of flour. Two trays of eggs. And yeast." Flour ¥75. Eggs two trays ¥36. Yeast ¥2. ¥113. You showed him the WeChat payment code. Beep—balance dropped to ¥6.00.

"And." You held out the watch again. "Three more bags of flour." ¥45. Watch beep—$6.65→$699.06. The owner looked down at the wrist holding the watch—stained with stomach acid from yesterday, not fully washed off. "Little girl, your family running a cafeteria?" "No. Running a flatbread shop." You held the eggs—two trays, sixty eggs total, stacked to your chin. Eight bags of flour—five via WeChat, three via watch. Forty kilos. You couldn't lift it.

Eight bags of flour, forty kilos. You couldn't lift it. The owner helped carry them to the shop entrance and stack them. You sent Zhou Ji a voice message on WeChat: "Come help me carry." He replied with a period. Three minutes later he walked out of the alley, closing Heli from background as he walked. He didn't ask why you bought flour, just stacked three bags on his shoulder, bent and added two more, then said: "You carry the rest."—he ran a second trip, carried the remaining three back.

You picked up the last 5kg bag of flour—heavy, but lighter than twenty jin. When you got back to the parking lot, Granny was at the stairwell picking worm-eaten grains out of the old rice.

When Granny saw the flour and eggs, she was picking worm-eaten grains out of the old rice. She looked up at what you were holding—flour, eggs, a small packet of yeast. "Bought at the supermarket. Flour is fifteen yuan a bag, can eat—" you stopped, couldn't calculate flour calories. But you remembered that search result line: "Flour has fifteen times the calories of a bun." So you looked up at Granny: "Fifteen times."

Granny didn't ask you "fifteen times what." She just pushed the old-rice bowl aside, stood, wiped her hands on her apron. Then took the flour bag from your arms. Not one hand—two hands, placed it by the cleaned 1.2-meter iron wok at the stairwell. The old oil stains at the wok bottom had been washed off, that faint gray ring from yesterday when she'd restarted the gas after you were swallowed and spit out—she'd scrubbed at it with a loofah for a long time.

"Flatbread." she said. Two words. Then she started pouring water. Started kneading dough.

When she added water, she did it by scooping the yeast-water into the flour well—not pouring, scooping, circling outward, letting water seep in on its own. Then palms pressed down. Press, fold. Press, fold. The dough slowly shrank under her hands—not less dough, but stronger. That strength traveled from those hands with edamame crumbs still in the nail seams, to the thud-and-bounce rhythm of the cutting board you could hear sitting at Xiaoman's feet. Huangdou lay beside, tail wagging, nose catching the flour dust drifting up each time the board bounced.

Xiaoman smelled it, just opened her eyes—that dry flour aroma heated by the iron wok wasn't meaty—but it was thick. Solid. The kind of aroma released when wheat flour was baked to the exact temperature it most wanted to be eaten at. Different from the sesame sweetness of the past eight days. Different from the big-pot stew on day one. Simpler, more primal, no seasoning to hide behind. She pulled her index finger back from the nail seam of her other hand. Today she'd already started scratching slower—not weaker, but because today she knew someone didn't just bring buns.

You stood up. Walked to the iron wok. When Granny scooped the first flatbread out of the wok bottom, oil spots sizzled on the surface, puffed up, collapsed, puffed again, flipped with the spatula. She used the spatula edge to lift it, placed it in your hand—not a bowl, not a plate, your hand. That bread was wider than your spread palm. Hot. But you didn't let go. "First one. For Xiaoman." you said. Granny scooped out the second. "This one for you." "Her first." You shook your head. "I'm not hungry. You eat first. I'll eat the next one." Granny didn't push, just withdrew her hand.

When Xiaoman put the first bread in her mouth, she didn't close her eyes. Not didn't taste it—but she was confirming every difference between this bread and yesterday's sesame seed. She chewed ten times. Swallowed. Then looked down at you—mouth corners still lifted, but a very full, very thin tear had condensed in her eye rim. Not a sad tear. "So this is what bread tastes like—the kind Mom used to make, but she hasn't been beside me for eight days."

You tore half the bread in your hand and gave it to Huangdou—he jumped and caught it, tail wagging three times fast. Then sniffed in the air, stopped and tilted his head, then spit the remaining half-bite on the ground. Not bad-tasting. Salty—Granny had added salt to the dough, Huangdou usually ate unsalted food. "Tsk." Granny glanced at him, then scooped the next bread into Xiaoman's palm.

Second. Third. Fourth—her tiger's mouth didn't cramp anymore.

Granny didn't stop. She finished the first bag of flour—wok oil splatter from gold to dark brown, spatula flipped who-knew-how-many times. Then the second bag. Then the third. Dough slapped on the cutting board—slap, slap—not sore hands, faster rhythm. She baked all morning, turning eight bags of flour into stack after stack of flatbread. Each bread she scooped out, she tossed it up—Xiaoman caught it in her palm. Not one by one, stack by stack. Bread piled in her palm into a pale-brown, steaming little mountain, then she poured the whole mountain into her mouth—chew. Swallow. Catch the next mountain. Didn't count how many she ate. Just kept reaching, kept chewing, kept making that gap-toothed mouth swallow everything in the flour that could become her body. Huangdou's tail wagged faster and faster, then slower and slower—not not excited, but stunned.

Fourth bag. Fifth. Sixth—Granny's arm shook. Not no strength, but the hand that had held iron woks for over thirty years was telling her: you baked six bags of flour today. Half a lifetime without baking this much at once.

Seventh bag—Zhou Ji set down his phone and stood. "Mom. Let me." "You know how?" "Teach me." "Don't scrape the wok bottom with your nail when flipping." Zhou Ji didn't speak. He took the spatula. First bread burned. Second tore. Third barely held its shape, lopsided, half the size of Granny's. But he scooped that ugly bread up, holding it in his hand, waiting for Xiaoman's palm to catch it. Then he looked down and started the fourth. Granny poured warm water into the eighth bag of flour beside her—her hand still shook, but she didn't spill. Yeast dissolved in the warm water, a layer of fine small bubbles forming.

Eighth bag of flour—last bread. When Zhou Ji scooped it up, no oil gloss remained on the wok bottom. The bread edges were a little charred, he folded the charred edge with the spatula. Xiaoman picked up that last bread with her fingertips. Didn't chew ten times like this morning—chewed four and swallowed. Her stomach—the stomach filled with eight bags of flour—was using every smooth muscle it could mobilize to contract, crush, push forward. But she looked at you, and you knew she was still hungry. Not hungry. The word "hungry" and "eight bags of flour only makes a small mountain" were indistinguishable. She sat on the ground made of flour sacks, skirt spread on the emptied woven bags. Those bags printed with green wheat ears—yesterday every sesame seed was a treasure, now eight bags of woven sacks lay around her like a white, flat, empty field. But she placed her hand on her belly—not pressing. Confirming—it was hot inside now. Not like the day before yesterday. Not like yesterday either. A whole pot of flatbread was in her stomach, slowly filling in her nine-days-empty body bit by bit.

She didn't say "still hungry." She just reached out, index finger lightly placed on the top of your head. Tenth second. Her fingers were steadier than yesterday. Not because blood sugar came up—but because she knew there'd be more tomorrow. More the day after.

Afternoon. You sat at her feet, iPad on your knees. Xuanyue transferred ¥6 as usual: [Today math class teacher did a chicken-rabbit variant, I found similar problems on Heli, did five. How are you over there?] You replied: [Xiaoman ate flatbread. Eight bags of flour all baked. She ate it all. Still hungry. But she said it was delicious.] Xuanyue sent a cat-paw-pressing-screen sticker—the only sticker you had. Then she said: [By the way, Miia's training ended today.]

You switched to Miia's chat. The messages were already there—sent half an hour ago, you hadn't noticed. Just now your full attention was on the browned crust of that flipping flatbread. Miia didn't waste words—

[Miia: Training ended. Did eight algorithm problems today, ¥240. Transferring you ¥200. Starting tomorrow I can do more in a day. ¥200.00 transferred.]

[Miia: Your friend is a real person right? You can't be lying to me. I don't distrust you—it's that I was solving problems and suddenly thought—if she's not real, who are we transferring all this money to? But Miaowu doesn't lie. Miaowu spent ten minutes just to grab a 1+1 question.]

You looked down at the iPad. Then looked up at Xiaoman—bread crumbs still on her lips, using her tongue to push gently against the missing tooth gap. Then you looked down and started typing—slowly, second-grade pinyin poked for almost a minute.

[She's real. Her name is Xiaoman. Full, abundant Xiaoman. She's eighteen meters tall. Missing a front tooth. Today she ate flatbread. Her stomach growls, but yesterday she swallowed me. Spit me out. Then we ate buns together. Then today I bought flour. Granny baked bread. Very delicious. Her tongue can taste salt now. Eight days ago she could only taste sweet.]

Miia didn't reply instantly—you guessed Miia was grabbing those eight algorithm problems not finished today on Hili. The last line in the window was #"Miia just initialized dy[0] to inf—typo, fixed. Passed sample." Then after ten-plus seconds: "Sent to you."

Your WeChat wallet popped: ¥200.00 received. Balance: ¥212.00. Watch: $699.06. Flour all gone. Need to buy more tomorrow.

On Zhou Ji's radio, the announcer was reading today's flour wholesale price—"up two cents from the day before yesterday"—then cut off by a pop song on the next channel. Huangdou's tail wagged. Xiaoman reached out, index finger lightly on top of your head. Tenth second. You didn't count. But your scalp remembered the steadier pad lines on her finger than yesterday—today she hadn't picked at her fingers, nail seams were empty, fingers warmed by eight bags of flour between dough and wheat ears. She was still hungry. She knew eight bags of flour wasn't enough. But she placed her hand on her belly—not pressing. Confirming—it was hot inside now. Tomorrow would be hot too.

============================================================

## Chapter 41: Full

Day 10

When you opened your eyes, the iPad was vibrating.

Not alarm—WeChat. Miia. Sent at 3:17 AM. The message was only two lines: [Miaowu. I remembered. Last year I installed a local model on the workstation in my mom's office. 32B. Full power. 128GB RAM. No censorship. Used it to run training data during training, then forgot. It helped me fix a segment tree. Then a tree chain. Then union-find. Tonight ¥900. All transferred. More tomorrow.]

¥900.00 transferred. Plus Xuanyue's ¥6 from last night—¥906. You rubbed your eyes and slid out of bed, sneakers stepping into that color with yeast powder still stuck on the sole—then you opened the iPad, used the calculator to poke a number: 906 ÷ 3 ÷ 5 = 60 bags of flour. Each bag 5kg. 60×5=300kg. You thought Xiaoman might need that much. But you didn't know if the supermarket owner had that many. You put on your shoes.

When the supermarket owner saw the number on your iPad screen, he was restacking instant noodles on the shelf. He looked down at the number, looked at you, then looked toward the parking lot—that light-blue mountain, which two days ago had carried flour itself, yesterday had wiped that pale-red mark on her palm with the back of her hand, was waiting for flatbread. He set down the instant noodle box in his hands. "In stock we have twenty-eight bags. Flour. Oil—five barrels. Eggs—six trays. How much do you want?"

"All of it."

Twenty-eight bags of flour: ¥420. Five barrels of oil: ¥140. Six trays of eggs: ¥108. Total: ¥668. WeChat balance ¥906—enough. Left ¥238. You held out the watch again—"three more bags." Three bags—¥45, $6.65→$692.37. The owner didn't speak as he stacked them at the shop entrance. Bag after bag of white sacks carried out from storage—he saw the mountain in the parking lot's finger twitch slightly on its knee. Not scratching. Gripping. Gripping yesterday's flatbread sesame seed. He carried faster.

Thirty-one bags of flour. Five barrels of oil. Six trays of eggs. Total 155kg flour + 25L oil + 180 eggs. You called to Xiaoman. She stood—three steps. Then bent down and swept all thirty-one bags of flour into her left palm. Five oil barrels she held by their necks between thumb and forefinger. You carried two trays of eggs, the remaining four trays she gently nested between the fingers of her right hand. The way she stood with both hands holding a day's food was exactly how she'd held thirty-seven gold coins in the Giant Country. Not heavy. Finally enough.

Granny turned the gas stove to maximum. That 1.2-meter iron wok had gone from flatbread-only to an all-day assembly line—she added a small pot beside it, called Zhou Ji from the stairwell: you mix dough in the small pot, bake by the big wok. She scolded you for dough too watery, you obediently pulled out your phone and read the dough-mixing ratio Miia had just sent—then you lifted the whole mixing bowl from the cutting board and poured it into Xiaoman's outstretched palm. She kneaded the huge dough ball in her fingers—for you it was a whole bag of flour kneaded into a giant ball, for her it was a soft white cloud she could tear directly from her palm into the wok. Oil poured from the barrel—she held the rim with her own nail and added it to the wok. Not a drop spilled. The first time she cooked for herself in this world.

Then she started eating.

Didn't count how many breads. Didn't count chews. Just kept opening, chewing, swallowing, left hand catching the next bread from the wok, right hand tearing fresh raw dough—she chewed and swallowed raw dough too, flour starch already breaking down into maltose in her mouth with salivary amylase, sweet. Oil ran down her mouth corner and she didn't wipe it—she used to be ashamed, now she only paused between her fortieth and forty-first bite to wipe the wok edge's char with the back of her index finger, licking the oil residue off the spatula. Then kept chewing.

Granny finished the twenty bags of flour in front of her. Zhou Ji mixed dough in the small pot, took off his apron and wiped sweat, put it back on—because Xiaoman had spread a fresh batch of dough in front of him—this was a new bag, the twenty-first waiting for him. Granny elbowed him aside, then scooped the wok. "Wait, when you flip—" "Flat in, diagonal out—I remember."

When the twenty-fifth bag of flour became bread it was already 3 PM. The gas canister was replaced with a new one—pulled from that iron cabinet for canister exchange under the bike shed. Granny had ordered it a month ago from the exchange station, originally planned for National Day spring roll frying. She stuck the spatula in the stove edge, wiped her eye corner with the damp corner of her apron—not tears, sweat. Sweat ran from her forehead to her reading glasses' nose pad, lenses fogged with heat, she pulled her glasses off her nose and set them on the wok lid, then tossed a thick bread right into the center of the sizzling oil ring. When this bread browned on both sides, she scooped it up and placed it in Xiaoman's hand.

When Xiaoman stuffed the twenty-fifth bag's bread into her mouth, the machine in her stomach—that she'd only realized on day nine could keep turning—suddenly sent a signal it had never sent before. Not "hungry." Not "still not enough." "Stop." The neurotransmitter smooth muscle sent back after contracting frantically all morning—telling the brainstem, telling the hypothalamus, telling the prefrontal cortex sitting behind the wheel—today is enough.

She burped. Loud. Blown Huangdou half a circle off the ground. Huangdou sneezed, spitting out a small piece of raw dough he'd stolen just now—he thought it was flour, didn't expect to choke on baking powder after one chew.

Xiaoman looked down at the wok bottom. Empty. Thirty-one bags of flour all became bread. She ate all of it. Oil left a barrel and a half. Eggs left. But her stomach was full. She placed her right hand on her belly—a shallow, round arc had risen there. Not the shrink of hunger-induced stomach spasm. But the arc of a stomach genuinely filled with flatbread and eggs and sugar and oil and water, stretched to the kind of arc a normal eight-year-old has in the Giant Country after eating Mom's strawberry toast. She looked at that arc—looked a long time. Then she looked up at you.

Tears glistened in her eyes but she wasn't about to cry. She said one sentence—not "thank you," not "is there more," not "what about tomorrow." She said "I can taste it now." You asked "taste what?" She looked down at her right hand still holding half a bread, then used her left index finger to pluck a sugar crystal from the tiger's mouth of her right hand—that crystal had spilled onto her fingertip when she was kneading dough. She put it in her mouth. Then she looked at you—"Full."

One word, complete. One more consonant than that day's "Mia." One less word than yesterday's "delicious." But when that word left her missing-tooth gap, hit by the 4 PM sun, scattered in the breeze that set Huangdou's tail wagging on the parking lot concrete—that word weighed as much as the thirty-one bags of flour she'd eaten today.

Evening, your iPad vibrated again. Not a transfer—because Miia had transferred everything that could be transferred at dawn. Miia had already written sixteen problems: two segment trees with pushdown, three tree chains, one LCA + tree difference, three digit DP, two bipartite matching, three interval DP optimization, Miia didn't count the rest. The model debugged Miia four times faster on average than Miia hand-wrote—time saved on each problem just enough for the workstation fan to blow another problem's solution space from the hot exhaust on the back of the GPU—then Miia pushed all merge logic back to the model to re-derive in 128GB RAM. Wrong. Fixed herself. Passed. Then transferred the earnings in again: ¥16.50. The note said "buy more oil tomorrow."

You calculated—WeChat balance ¥254.50, but no need to buy buns today. Tomorrow has its own starting line. You pocketed the iPad, leaned against Xiaoman's left ankle. Her stomach was still digesting—that smooth muscle pushing today's last bread's gluten into the small intestine. You closed your eyes. Listening to that slow, muffled peristalsis rhythm, no longer "grr" but "glk—glk—" very slow. Not the rhythm of hunger. The rhythm of digestion. The prefrontal cortex confirming—this signal is real. Not from the amygdala. From a stomach that, for the first time after ten days of hunger, had been genuinely filled by flatbread.

Huangdou lay beside, tail wagging.

============================================================

## Chapter 42: Local Model

Day 10, pre-dawn to evening

The workstation had been running over thirty hours straight. GPU fan sound changed from "whir" to "click"—the left bearing probably about to break. Miia logged in remotely and turned the fan speed down a notch, case temperature from 83°C to 79°C. Miia walked to the balcony for air—the wind from the lake beyond the willows was cool.

After dawn Miia ground eleven more problems. Segment tree. Tree chain. LCA + tree difference. Digit DP. Bipartite matching. Interval DP. The local model stayed on. It wasn't always right—quantization loss occasionally made it write the wrong variable name for a for-loop upper bound, or miss a line of initialization. But it debugged four times faster than Miia manually. Miia just pasted the error in, and it fished the bug location out of the code in a few lines.

After dawn Mom woke up. Leaned out from the kitchen: "You didn't sleep last night?" "Slept." "Your eyes are red." "Allergies." Mom didn't press—she knew Miia's informatics training had just ended, staying up late was normal at boot camp. She just placed a glass of milk and two slices of bread on the table. Miia drank the milk. Bread set aside—when Miia was doing the second bipartite matching problem, the model suggested Miia use the Hungarian algorithm instead of max flow, "for bipartite graphs the former has half the loops—you're not considering weights." Miia rewrote in Hungarian. Passed. ¥30. "Ding." Miia put the milk glass to her lips—empty. When Miia had drunk it, forgot.

Evening. Outside the window, the old willow canopies by the lake swayed gently in the evening breeze. Miia looked down at Heli—balance ¥0. Everything earned today transferred to Miaowu. Miia sent Miaowu two words: "Continue." Then closed remote desktop. The fan's "click" disappeared from the headphones. But the workstation was still running in Mom's office—Miia pulled fingers from the iPad screen, feeling that residual heat wave seemed to travel through the whole building's network cable to her fingertips.

Miia walked to the balcony. Behind the willows was the lake. Water calm when no wind, green; wrinkled when wind came. Miia had no time for wind today—today Miia only looked at code. But now, Miia looked down at a faint red mark on the tiger's mouth of her left hand—pressed by the iPad edge all day. Different from that pale-red mark on Xiaoman's palm the day before yesterday. Xiaoman's was from a flour sack. Miia's was from the keyboard edge. Both red. Both things that disappear on the skin after a while.

Miia went back inside. Mom was grading homework in the living room. Miia took another glass of milk from the fridge—this time Miia remembered to drink it. Then Miia opened remote desktop and reconnected to the workstation. Screen lit. The cursor was still there. Miia wouldn't do more problems today. Miia just wanted to tell that model: today Xiaoman is full.

But the model wouldn't understand. It was just a weighted matrix recompiled many times. Miia placed fingers lightly on the spacebar. Didn't press. Outside the window, the lake wind kept blowing.

============================================================

## Chapter 43: The Next Day

Day 11

When you opened your eyes, the iPad had already vibrated. Not just now—pre-dawn. Not Xuanyue. Not Miia. The supermarket owner. He'd added you on WeChat last night—when you'd held up the payment code for him to scan, he'd searched your phone number. The message was short: [Flour arrives tomorrow. How much? Warehouse can transfer. Oil too. Eggs too. Just say a number.]

You rubbed your eyes. Sat on the bed, poked a number into the iPad calculator: yesterday Miia transferred a total of ¥916. Already spent ¥512, left ¥404. Pre-dawn Miia transferred another ¥580—"Tonight's. More tomorrow." Plus Xuanyue's daily ¥6, WeChat balance: ¥990. You poked the watch—$692.37. In RMB about ¥4700. But you didn't want to use the watch today—Mom had seen supermarket spending for several days straight, under ¥50 was fine, more would be noticed. "How much can be transferred?" "Flour by the ton. Oil by the case. Eggs by the tray." "Flour—" you poked the calculator again. Xiaoman ate 31 bags yesterday, not full—no, she said "full," but that full was "stomach finally has something," not "I never want this again." Today you wanted to give her the second kind of full. "Eighty bags. Oil—fifteen barrels. Eggs—ten trays."

The owner took almost a minute to reply: [You sure?] You replied one word: [Yeah.]

"Deliver to the parking lot. I'll carry it myself." The owner didn't ask again if there was a grown-up. He'd seen that missing-tooth eighteen-meter girl yesterday walk from the parking lot to the shop in three steps and sweep fourteen bags of flour with one hand. When he called the warehouse, his voice was steady: "Old Zhang, flour—yes, that brand I ordered last time—eighty bags. Right. Right. Now. Oil too, fifteen cases. Eggs ten trays. Same address—community parking lot. Don't deliver to the shop, take it straight there."

The truck arrived at 2 PM. A white box truck pulled into the alley when Granny was changing her third gas canister—she'd already dragged a full backup case from under the bike shed, each canister screwed on, the wok bottom flame from blue to clear blue. Zhou Ji landed a big Heli problem today—a ¥45 chip question asking about CXL protocol latency jitter compensation in multi-chip interconnect, his answer was about 1200 characters, took nearly two hours. After submitting he picked up the spatula—dough bowl already moved by Granny to the stairwell, bowl with today's first batch of dough. He didn't burn bread anymore. His bread was a size smaller than Granny's, but edges charred just right. He called those charred edges "Miaowu Crisp"—because one day you'd broken up his ugly bread and soaked it in soup, ate three bites then went back for more. He didn't tell you the name. He just waited a moment longer each time he scooped the bread—waiting until the char edge curve was exactly the piece you'd break off first.

Truck rear doors opened. You and the owner counted bags against a crooked printed invoice: "One, two, three, four—" Xiaoman didn't wait. She'd already walked over from the parking lot—not three steps today. She'd heard the truck's engine long ago, that engine-and-tin-cab rumble had woken her early today. She squatted by the truck, index finger lightly pressing the invoice so you wouldn't turn the page wrong—her fingertip too big, covering half the characters, but the motion reminded you of the first day when she touched the top of your head on the lawn. Same finger. Different purpose. Today helping you count invoices.

Eighty bags of flour. Fifteen barrels of oil. Ten trays of eggs. Total 400kg flour, 75L oil, 300 eggs. Calorie count you hadn't calculated—but you saw Granny press her reading glasses down looking at the height of the flour stack, mouth corners lift. "Bake all day." "Let me." Zhou Ji said. She ignored him. She just pushed the mixing bowl used for three days under the bike shed, swapped for a bigger one—the last unused item from a whole set of stainless-steel canteen supplies she'd bought fifteen years ago when she ran a small restaurant. She set the bowl on the truck, level with the flour sacks. Grabbed a big chunk of fermented sourdough and threw it in. "This dough was started fifteen years ago. Today it finally gets this much flour." She said this. No one heard. Only the oil in the wok was sizzling.

Xiaoman carried herself—five trips. Not because she couldn't carry it—because she stacked every bag neatly by the iron wok, forming a white square twice as high as yesterday. Oil barrels lined in three rows, egg trays stacked beside. Then she looked down at the red marks pressed by woven bags in her palm—twice as deep as yesterday. She wiped them with the back of her hand, then squatted, starting to knead the dough Granny handed her between her fingers. Today she didn't need to roll into balls—today she just put one hand in the bowl, grabbed each risen dough and tore it off, torn to just-bakeable size. Granny left that charred-edge thick bread spot for her in the wok—when she tore each bread, she left a shallow nail scratch on the edge. That scratch later became the marker she could identify the taste of each torn bread by—the bread with a nail mark was baked in the second the wind from the lake willow side happened to blow across the parking lot.

When she ate the fortieth bag, her stomach made a sound she'd never heard for the first time. Not "grr." Not hungry. Not "not enough." Very low, very muffled, from the very bottom of the stomach all the way up to the throat, like the cello's lowest string drawn slowly with a bow for a full beat—"mmmm"—not crying. The stomach saying "I'm here." She put the bread in her hand back in the wok. Not not eating—but confirming. Wanted to wait a minute, see if that signal was real. A minute passed. Her stomach didn't growl again. Not no sound—but no longer the meaning of "growling." The empty abdominal cavity had already been filled with enough flour and oil and eggs, stomach muscle no longer needed to push empty-stomach signals up the esophagus. Smooth muscle went from spasm to comfortable peristalsis—the kind of peristaltic sound where normal people's intestines push food down after eating, not grr. But a very fine, even "ssss" micro-sound. She placed her palm on her belly—the arc rounder than yesterday. Fuller than any day. The kind of fullness she had in the Giant Country, after breakfast, before Mom chased her out the door to school, patting her own belly. She looked down at that arc—looked a long time. Then she looked up at you. Tenth second—she didn't count today. Because her prefrontal cortex no longer needed to count to a certain number of seconds to confirm she was still here. She just moved her hand from her belly, flipped her palm over—up. Waiting for you to put your hand on it. That gesture wasn't "give me another bread." It was "I'm here." You placed your hand in her palm—steadier than yesterday, because her blood sugar had recovered at least half the energy her eight-year-old prefrontal cortex needed to function normally. Then she said one sentence—this time not "full." Two words: "Today. Enough."

You looked up at her. Tears in her eyes but not about to cry. That kind of tears—after ten full days of hunger, finally swallowing the last flatbread, then seeing in that pale-red mark falling through her fingers that tomorrow would have more than today. You pushed her hand back—not she didn't want the bread. Was telling you to finish it. Because this bread was the last. From the fifty-seventh bag—the last 1/80 of the eighty bags not yet baked. The bread Granny had split in two with barely a free hand and shoved in the wok, then turned the gas to minimum, watching that bread slowly puff up in her own belly. Zhou Ji flipped it for her. The spatula went in flat. Came out flat, a layer of caramel on the spatula—Granny had secretly put a spoonful of white sugar at the wok bottom. She didn't tell you. She knew. She licked that caramel layer off the spatula herself. Then watched Xiaoman swallow the last bread. Then put the spatula back by the bowl. Today it was Zhou Ji's turn to wipe the wok bottom—he already knew after scraping, dry cloth twice to prevent rust. This was the second housework skill his mom taught him. The first was flat in, diagonal out when flipping. Neither skill he'd learned in Lvgreen's office.

Evening. You sat at Xiaoman's feet—that nail seam on her left toe cracked on day one was now completely covered by new nail. New nail slightly thinner than old, edge not yet worn smooth, her toe curled slightly when you accidentally touched it—not surprise today. Tickle. After blood sugar returned to normal, neurons recovered the sensation of tickle before pain. Toe curled, released, curled again—she wanted you to touch it again. You touched again. Her toe pulled back—not dodging, waiting for you to touch a third time. You didn't. You just leaned against it, iPad on your knees. WeChat balance—¥0.00. All spent. Watch still had $692.37. Tomorrow you won't need Mom to see "supermarket spending," because tomorrow you just need to use the first WeChat transfer to say hello to the supermarket owner, then keep moving flour. Program updated to version five: 1. Flour 2. Oil 3. Eggs 4. Xiaoman carries herself 5. Granny doesn't need to stop.

Huangdou lay beside, tail wagging—his tail wag frequency this year was at least fourteen times higher than the Teddy next door tied to the balcony barking all day. He yawned, no raw-dough taste in his mouth—because today he was fed a milk cookie you specifically asked the supermarket owner for. The owner gave you one—you didn't eat it. Gave it to Huangdou. Huangdou's tail stopped for a second when he ate the cookie—it was too hard, needed back teeth to chew. Chewed. Then tail wagged faster than before. Because the cookie was sweet. The sweetness stayed between his teeth after the cookie was gone—he licked the side of that upper-left fourth premolar with his tongue, several times. Stopped. Licked again. Stopped. You weren't much good at calculating today either. But you knew the flatbread in Xiaoman's stomach was being slowly pushed down by her stomach muscles, layer by layer broken down by pancreatic enzymes and bile, slowly flowing from duodenum to jejunum to ileum—those maltoses and fatty acids finally rose in her blood to a level where the prefrontal cortex could gently push aside the amygdala that had been sleeping in the passenger seat for ten good days—push it out the car door, close the door, tighten the parking brake, pull the phone out of your pocket and look. Only one word on it: "Full."

============================================================

## Chapter 44: Many Forks Today

**Day 10 · Evening**

After Xiaoman was full, the world suddenly went very quiet.

Not quiet outside.

There were still cars outside the parking lot.

Far away someone was still honking.

Behind the wall, someone's range hood hummed, a sparrow perched on the rusty iron railing, chirping every few seconds.

It was her body that went quiet.

That voice that had followed her since she arrived was gone.

No empty rumble.

No stomach wall contracting in waves.

No part of her body constantly telling her:

not enough.

need more.

more.

She sat in the abandoned parking lot against the wall.

Legs stretched out in front.

Light-blue skirt spread along the concrete, edges stirred by the evening breeze.

She placed one hand on her belly.

Warm under the palm.

Full.

Then—

grr.

Xiaoman looked down.

She didn't move.

Two seconds passed.

Glk.

Another sound.

This wasn't the old sound.

When her stomach used to growl, Xiaoman would be afraid.

Because she knew what came after that sound.

No strength.

Dizziness.

Can't stand up.

Then hungrier and hungrier.

But today's sound was strange.

Thick.

Slow.

Like something was moving house inside her body.

She placed her other hand on it too.

"Miaowu."

No answer.

"Miaowu?"

Still no answer.

Xiaoman looked up.

Miaowu, who'd been sitting three meters away calculating something, was gone.

The iPad was still there.

Screen lit.

Calculator stopped at:

**254.50 ÷**

Nothing after it.

Xiaoman looked left and right.

No.

Then she heard a very faint "scrape, scrape" sound from the right.

Miaowu was squatting on the concrete.

Holding a small stick.

The stick tip inserted into a crack.

She was following the crack slowly forward.

Crack went left, she went left.

Crack went right, she went right.

Reached a fork.

She stopped.

Squatted.

Started looking.

Xiaoman watched for a while.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"My stomach's growling."

"Yeah."

Miaowu didn't turn around.

"Why?"

"Digesting."

"What's digesting?"

This time, Miaowu stopped.

She stood up.

Thought for a while.

"It means the food you ate becomes something your body can use."

Xiaoman looked down at her belly.

"So it's working?"

"Yeah."

"Why didn't it make this much noise before?"

"There wasn't much before."

Xiaoman didn't ask more.

She gently touched her belly.

Grr.

This time, she smiled.

"It's really working."

Miaowu had squatted back down.

Three cracks.

Left one wide.

Right one short.

Middle one extra thin.

She decided to take the middle one first.

Had just taken two steps.

"Burp—"

Whoosh.

A warm gust blew from behind.

Miaowu's hair flew forward all at once.

The stick rolled half a meter.

A light plastic wrapper on the ground flipped twice in the wind.

Miaowu stopped.

Turned.

Xiaoman had both hands covering her mouth.

Eyes round.

"Sorry."

Miaowu looked at her.

"You burped."

"Yeah..."

"Burping when you're full."

"I know."

After Xiaoman said it, she felt she actually didn't know.

At least she hadn't been this full in a very long time.

She slowly lowered her hands.

After a while.

Mouth corners lifted on their own.

"I've burped four today."

"Yeah."

Miaowu picked up the stick.

Kept walking.

Xiaoman watched her.

Today's Miaowu was a little different from usual.

Not completely different.

She was still Miaowu.

Call her, she'd answer.

Tell her to come, she usually came.

Tell her not to touch the wok, she really wouldn't.

But today—

There were more paths.

Before, Miaowu was like a little cart running on fixed rails.

Wake up.

Take medicine.

Eat breakfast.

Go out.

Find Xiaoman.

Do things.

Go home.

Step by step.

Very orderly.

Today, beside the rails, forks had suddenly grown.

This morning, she'd been carrying a small bag toward the parking lot.

An ad sheet blew up from a wall corner.

She stopped.

Watched the ad fall.

Blow up again.

Fall again.

Then followed it for over ten meters.

Granny called from behind:

"Miaowu, drop that off first."

Miaowu stopped immediately.

"Oh."

Turned.

Continued on the original route.

Noon, she'd been going to get the iPad.

Halfway.

A sparrow landed on the iron railing.

Miaowu looked up.

Sparrow hopped once.

She stepped forward.

Sparrow hopped again.

She stepped forward again.

Xiaoman finally reached out, blocking in front of her.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"Weren't you getting that?"

Miaowu looked along her hand.

Saw the iPad.

"Oh."

Then walked over and got it.

Now too.

She'd been calculating.

A black beetle crawled across the screen.

So the calculator stopped at:

254.50 ÷

Xiaoman watched for a while.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"Did you take your medicine today?"

The stick stopped.

"Not taking it."

"Why not?"

"None left."

"Medicine none left?"

"Yeah."

"When?"

"This morning."

She said it flat.

Like saying oil was gone.

Flour was gone.

Battery was dead.

Xiaoman frowned.

"Then tomorrow?"

"None."

"Day after tomorrow?"

"None."

"..."

Xiaoman felt this question should be serious.

But Miaowu had already walked on.

Crack ended.

A small red ladybug ahead.

Miaowu squatted down.

Started following.

Ladybug forward.

She forward.

Ladybug climbed onto a small light-blue cloth.

Miaowu walked over too.

Xiaoman happened to shift her body a little sideways.

Skirt hem piled a large fold on the ground.

Ladybug crawled in.

Miaowu lay down.

Looked.

Then crawled in too.

Xiaoman was stretching at the time.

She pushed shoulders back first.

Both hands lifted up.

Ten fingers spread in the sunset.

Chest took in a big breath.

"Ah————"

A very long yawn.

Wind dispersed from her mouth.

Dust on the ground rolled a circle.

After yawning, water squeezed from the corner of her eye.

Looked down.

Miaowu was gone.

"Miaowu?"

"Yeah."

Voice from below.

Xiaoman looked down.

"Where are you?"

"Here."

"Where?"

"In your skirt."

Xiaoman froze.

"What are you doing in there?"

"The ladybug came in."

"Oh..."

Xiaoman looked down at her skirt hem.

Couldn't see anything.

Only a small piece of cloth occasionally moving slightly.

She thought.

"Miaowu, come out."

Inside went quiet for a second.

"Okay."

Miaowu turned around.

Started walking toward the light.

Xiaoman had just breathed a sigh of relief.

Inside her belly suddenly moved again.

Grrrr—

She looked down.

A feeling completely different from just burping slowly emerged.

Xiaoman's expression stopped.

She sat up a little.

Felt.

Then her face started reddening.

"Wait!"

Miaowu inside the skirt stopped immediately.

One foot lifted.

Stopped moving.

Xiaoman:

"..."

She herself didn't know why she'd shouted wait just now.

Next second.

She was more nervous.

"No, come out."

Miaowu put her foot down.

Kept walking.

Xiaoman stared at the skirt hem.

She had no idea where Miaowu was.

Only knew some fold occasionally moved.

"Slower!"

Inside immediately slowed.

One step.

Pause.

Another step.

Xiaoman:

"..."

"Not that slow either."

Inside stopped completely.

Xiaoman closed her eyes.

She suddenly realized.

Miaowu had done every sentence.

Wait.

She stopped.

Come out.

She walked.

Slower.

She slowed.

Not that slow.

This sentence didn't tell her how fast she should go.

So she stopped there, waiting for the next instruction.

Xiaoman's face got redder and redder.

She propped both hands on the ground.

Slowly went from sitting to squatting.

She pulled one leg back first.

Then the other.

Nineteen-meter body lowered little by little.

Skirt hem dragged on the ground with the motion.

Scrape—

Scrape—

Two giant feet settled.

Knees bent.

She squatted down.

The ground made a soft thud.

Xiaoman lowered her body.

Both hands on knees.

Face closer to the ground.

Like the first day.

The first day she'd squatted down like this too.

Because Miaowu was too small.

She had to squat down to see her clearly.

Only the first day, she squatted to ask:

"Can I touch you?"

Today, she squatted down because she found she had to look at this tiny person.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"You keep walking toward the light now."

"Okay."

"Don't stop."

"Okay."

"Stop after you come out."

"Okay."

Skirt hem started moving gently.

Miaowu didn't stop this time.

There was a thread beside.

She glanced at it.

Kept walking.

Ladybug crawled past from the other side.

Her eyes followed.

Feet kept walking.

Finally.

Skirt edge moved.

A head poked out.

Xiaoman's eyes lit up immediately.

"Miaowu."

Miaowu kept walking out.

Shoulders out.

Half body out.

Then—

Pfft.

A muffled sound.

Xiaoman's whole body froze.

Miaowu stopped too.

She stood at the skirt edge.

Two seconds passed.

Her nose twitched slightly.

Xiaoman's face was already red to her ears.

Miaowu said:

"It smells."

Xiaoman:

"..."

"It was growling just now too."

"..."

Miaowu looked up.

"It was you—"

"Miaowu!"

"Yeah."

"Out."

Miaowu walked out immediately.

Xiaoman pulled the skirt hem to herself.

Miaowu stood outside.

Looking at her.

"Why is your face red?"

Xiaoman looked down.

"Don't know."

"But you know."

"I don't know right now!"

Miaowu stood for a while.

Didn't keep asking.

Because a ladybug happened to crawl out from the skirt edge.

She turned and chased it.

"Miaowu!"

Xiaoman called quickly.

Miaowu stopped.

"Yeah."

"Don't crawl in there again."

"Okay."

Then she changed direction.

Kept chasing the ladybug.

Xiaoman squatted in place.

Stunned for a few seconds.

Finally smiled.

Very quietly.

After smiling, she felt her belly move gently again.

She immediately stopped smiling.

Looked down.

Waited three seconds.

Nothing new happened.

She breathed a sigh of relief.

---

Evening light got lower and lower.

After Xiaoman was full, she got sleepy.

Really sleepy.

Not the old kind of hungry weakness.

Her eyelids would fall on their own.

Body warm.

Belly slowly working inside.

Occasionally a very light burp.

She didn't say sorry every time anymore.

But Miaowu got busier and busier.

She walked across a parking line.

Found the line was long.

Started walking along it.

Walked to the end.

Turned.

Walked back.

Xiaoman leaned against the wall watching her.

First lap.

Second.

Third.

After the seventh lap, Xiaoman asked:

"Why are you walking back and forth?"

"There's a line here."

"Oh."

This answer made Xiaoman feel very reasonable.

After a while.

Miaowu stopped walking the white line.

Because there was a bottle cap by the road.

She squatted down.

Poked it.

Cap spun.

Whirrrr—

Stopped.

Poked again.

Whirrrr—

Stopped.

She repeated.

Xiaoman closed her eyes for a while.

Opened again.

Cap still spinning.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"Have you been wanting to do things all day?"

Miaowu thought for a second.

"No."

"Then why do you keep moving?"

"Because there are things here."

Xiaoman looked around.

Concrete cracks.

Bottle cap.

Bugs.

Leaves.

White line.

Stones.

Plastic paper blown by wind.

There were things everywhere here.

She suddenly felt the problem was kind of big.

She stood up.

Miaowu immediately looked up.

Nineteen-meter Xiaoman became a mountain again.

Then she stepped forward.

Miaowu's eyes followed the rolling bottle cap.

Also stepped forward.

Xiaoman stepped again.

Miaowu still didn't look at her.

Xiaoman stopped.

Then slowly squatted down again.

This time.

She squatted in front of Miaowu.

Knees bent.

Body lowered.

Finally both hands on knees.

Giant face came down bit by bit.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

Miaowu was still looking at the cap.

Xiaoman reached out one finger.

Blocked the cap.

Miaowu saw her finger.

Then looked up.

Finally looked at her.

"Your medicine is gone."

"Yeah."

"So you've been like this all day?"

Miaowu didn't know.

She didn't answer.

Xiaoman didn't know how to ask either.

She wanted to say:

don't run off.

But what was "run off"?

She wanted to say:

stay near me.

But how near was "near"?

She wanted to say:

don't go where I can't see you.

But before she squatted down just now, she couldn't even see under her own skirt hem.

Xiaoman thought for a long time.

Then reached out her index finger.

Fingertip touched concrete dust.

Scrape—

She started drawing a circle.

A very big circle.

Went around the left.

Past the abandoned pillar.

Came back around.

Finally connected.

About twenty meters in diameter.

Miaowu stood inside.

Xiaoman pointed at the line.

"When I can't see you, don't walk out of this circle."

Miaowu looked down.

Complete.

Closed.

Boundary clear.

"Okay."

Xiaoman finally felt she'd made herself clear.

She went to the side to collect things.

Came back ten minutes later.

Miaowu was still in the circle.

Hadn't gone out at all.

But she was walking along the circumference.

Step.

Step.

Step.

The concrete ground already had layer after layer of dense little shoe prints.

Xiaoman stood there.

Looked for a long time.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"Have you been walking this whole time?"

"Yeah."

"Why?"

"Can't go out."

"..."

Xiaoman looked down at the circle she'd drawn.

Seemed right.

Hadn't gone out indeed.

She squatted down.

This time squatted lower than before.

One hand placed inside the circle.

Palm up.

"Climb on."

Miaowu stopped.

Walked up directly.

Didn't ask why.

Xiaoman lifted her up.

Slowly set her beside her.

Then sat down.

Crossed her legs.

Used her own arms and skirt to enclose a smaller area.

"Today you stay here."

Miaowu asked:

"Where is here?"

Xiaoman pointed around.

"In here."

Miaowu looked.

There was a boundary.

"Okay."

Then she stood up.

Started walking along Xiaoman's arm.

Step.

Step.

Step.

Xiaoman watched her.

Didn't stop her.

At least for now.

She could see her.

The sun was almost down.

Sunlight on top of the wall.

Light slowly turned from orange to red.

Wind blew.

Xiaoman yawned again.

She lay on her side.

Head on her own arm.

Other arm still enclosing Miaowu outside.

Miaowu kept walking along that arm-made boundary.

Lap.

Two.

Three.

Xiaoman's eyes got heavier and heavier.

But she kept watching.

She suddenly remembered the first day.

Back then.

She didn't know where she could walk.

Didn't know what she could touch.

Didn't know what to do if something broke.

Didn't know where the next meal was after being hungry.

It was always Miaowu telling her.

Now.

Miaowu's medicine was gone.

She herself didn't even know what to do tomorrow.

Miaowu walked to Xiaoman's wrist.

There was a very shallow skin line there.

She stopped.

Started looking.

Xiaoman opened her eyes a crack.

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"Don't get lost."

"Okay."

Miaowu kept walking forward.

Didn't walk out of her arms.

Xiaoman watched for a while.

Suddenly felt.

Maybe tomorrow.

She should go ask someone.

Ask Granny.

Ask that high school student.

Ask Miaowu's parents.

Find out why the medicine was gone.

Before, all her problems were solved by Miaowu.

Tomorrow.

Maybe it was her turn.

Xiaoman slowly closed her eyes.

Inside her belly came a very soft:

Grr.

This time.

She didn't open her eyes.

Miaowu was still by her hand.

Step.

Step.

Walking.

For the first time, nothing in the parking lot needed to be solved immediately.

No one was hungry.

No one was thirsty.

Nothing needed washing right now.

And no next meal whose location was unknown.

So those questions that hunger had blocked before.

Finally, one by one.

Walked over from far away.

---

============================================================

## Part Six: Miia Arrives

## Chapter 45: The Medicine Comes Back

**Day 11**

The next morning, you didn't come to the parking lot by yourself.

You sat on the back of Mom's electric scooter.

Helmet tilted a little.

Two hands didn't hold Mom's waist, but gripped the metal handrails on either side of the back seat.

Mom rode slowly.

Because you'd been wiggling the whole way.

At the red light, you put your toe on the edge of the footrest.

Light turned green.

Mom just started off.

You suddenly turned around.

A sprinkler truck was playing music behind you.

You kept watching.

Until Mom said:

"Miaowu, sit properly."

You turned back.

Sat properly.

Ten seconds later, a pigeon flew overhead.

You looked up again.

Mom glanced at you in the rearview mirror.

Didn't speak.

At the parking lot entrance, Xiaoman was already waiting.

She actually hadn't slept well last night.

Not from hunger.

Because for the first time she started worrying about you.

She woke very early.

Sat there.

Watched the entrance.

Looked twenty-three times.

On the twenty-fourth, you appeared.

Xiaoman stood up immediately.

Boom.

Then immediately squatted back down.

She'd gotten used to this motion these past two days.

Stood up because she was happy.

Squatted down because you were too small.

"Miaowu!"

Your Mom parked.

You got off.

Didn't go to Xiaoman first.

Because Mom said:

"Take your medicine first."

You stood still.

Mom took a new yellow pill bottle out of her bag.

Unscrewed it.

Two white tablets.

Round.

With a score line in the middle.

You reached out.

Took them.

Swallowed dry.

Motion exactly like always.

Xiaoman squatted ten-plus meters away.

Watching very carefully.

"Is this it?"

She asked.

Your Mom looked up.

Neck tilted back so far it ached a little.

"...Yes."

This was the first time she really stood in front of Xiaoman.

Before she'd heard about her from Miaowu.

Heard from neighbors.

Seen on phone.

Seen from far away through a window.

But now.

This nineteen-meter girl was squatting right in front of her.

A shallow sleep crease still on her face.

Hair sticking up on one side.

Skirt hem dusted.

Looked nothing like the "unknown giant lifeform" on TV news.

Just a kid who'd woken up and hadn't combed her hair.

Xiaoman looked down.

Both hands on her knees.

Very nervous.

"Auntie."

Voice pressed very low.

Still stirred your Mom's hair a little.

"I'm sorry."

Your Mom was stunned.

"What?"

"Miaowu bought me a lot of things."

Xiaoman said.

"Spent a lot of money."

"And she ran herself tired."

"And yesterday she had no medicine."

"She kept walking."

"I didn't watch her well."

You'd already walked over.

Squatted down.

Watching an ant carry a small crumb of bread from nowhere.

Mom looked at you.

Then at Xiaoman.

Then said:

"The medicine isn't gone because of you."

Xiaoman looked up.

"The bottle at home just ran out."

"Doctor's refill was delayed a day yesterday."

"It's not your fault."

Xiaoman didn't speak.

She seemed to need a little time to take this in.

After a while.

She nodded.

"Oh."

Then she looked at you.

You were still watching the ant.

This morning's medicine had just been swallowed.

Of course it wouldn't work like a switch "snap."

You still followed the ant three meters.

Mom called:

"Miaowu."

You stopped.

"Come back."

You came back.

Xiaoman watched.

She finally confirmed one thing.

Yesterday you hadn't suddenly become another person.

Today wasn't that after taking the medicine you'd turn back into "correct Miaowu."

You'd always been you.

Just sometimes.

Your paths were many.

Sometimes fewer.

---

Noon.

Xiaoman suddenly asked:

"Miaowu."

"Yeah."

"What if the medicine runs out again later?"

You were sorting stones by size.

"Tell Mom."

"Then?"

"Mom handles it."

"What if Mom isn't there?"

You stopped.

Thought.

"Tell Dad."

"What if Dad isn't there either?"

You kept thinking.

A long time.

"Tell Dr. Zhang."

Xiaoman nodded.

She memorized these three answers carefully.

Mom.

Dad.

Dr. Zhang.

This was the first time she learned your "help order."

Before.

You'd been learning hers.

Hungry, find food.

Dirty, find water.

Scared, don't move.

Now.

She started memorizing yours too.

============================================================

## Chapter 46: Artificial Rain

**Day 12**

The shower problem was rediscovered at noon.

Not because Xiaoman was dirty.

But because the weather forecast said:

Seven days ahead.

Sunny.

Granny stood under the awning.

Looking at her phone.

Then looked up at Xiaoman.

"No rain for seven days."

Xiaoman looked up too.

Sky blue as freshly washed.

Not one rain-capable cloud.

She looked down and sniffed her arm.

Said nothing.

Granny stuffed her phone back in her apron pocket.

"Can't wait for heaven to give you a bath."

So.

That day a very strange construction crew appeared in the parking lot.

No construction permit.

No blueprints.

No engineers.

There were:

Zhou Ji.

Delivery guy.

High school student.

Supermarket owner.

Granny.

Three bundles of water hose.

Two abandoned scaffolding poles.

A small fire pump borrowed from who-knew-where.

And you.

You were in charge of watching.

Version one.

Everyone connected a hose high up.

Turned on.

Whoosh.

A column of water fell from four meters up.

Xiaoman stood under it.

Water only hit her knees.

Everyone looked up.

Xiaoman looked down.

Hose continued:

Splash splash splash.

Delivery guy said:

"...This only washes shins."

Version two.

Tied the hose to a taller abandoned light pole.

Seven meters.

Opened.

Water finally reached around Xiaoman's waist.

But pressure too low.

By the time it fell it was a droopy curtain.

Xiaoman stood in it.

After half a minute.

Her left skirt was wet in a strip.

Right side still dry.

Xiaoman:

"..."

Granny:

"Think again."

Version three.

Zhou Ji connected the fire pump.

Six hoses.

Two nozzles.

Scaffolding raised.

Water first pumped to the roof of the abandoned warehouse beside the parking lot, then sprayed out from above.

First startup.

Rrrrrrrr——

Pump on.

Hoses instantly swelled.

Delivery guy yelled:

"It's on!"

Whoooooooooosh!!!

The parking lot suddenly had rain.

Not one stream.

A whole sheet.

Countless water droplets fell from over ten meters up, forming a silver-white curtain in the sunset.

Xiaoman stood under it.

Stunned.

Water first hit the top of her head.

Black long hair sank down bit by bit.

Stuck to her shoulders.

Then face.

Neck.

Arms.

Skirt.

Legs.

She stood there.

Both eyes slowly curved.

"It really is raining."

She said.

Then spun in a circle.

That spin was too happy.

Skirt hem flung a whole ring of water out.

Whoosh—!

Everyone standing nearest got soaked.

Granny head to shoes.

Delivery guy's helmet dripping.

The instruction manual in Zhou Ji's hand turned to mush.

You stood in place.

School uniform clinging.

Hair hanging wet.

Xiaoman stopped.

"Sorry!"

You looked down at your sleeve.

Lifted it.

Water dripped along your elbow.

"Clothes are wet."

"I know..."

"Need to change."

"I know..."

Xiaoman covered her face.

But she was laughing.

Everyone was laughing too.

Finally Granny put the bar soap in a plastic basket.

Hung it on a rope to Xiaoman's hand.

Xiaoman, for the first time on a day with no rain.

Really took a bath.

Water drained out the parking lot ditch.

Didn't flow into the abandoned pool.

Everyone had dug a new shallow trench on purpose.

Because everyone knew now:

That side is toilet.

This side is shower.

Don't mix.

Evening.

Xiaoman sat in the wind drying her hair.

Too much hair.

Forty minutes later inside still wet.

You sat beside her.

Holding an ordinary hair dryer.

Blowing on one hair.

Whoooooosh——

Five minutes.

Xiaoman looked down.

"Did it work?"

You touched it.

"This one's dry."

Xiaoman looked at all the hair on her head.

Silence.

Finally you decided:

Let the wind do it.

============================================================

## Chapter 47: Hide and Seek

**Day 13**

The first person to suggest hide-and-seek was Xiaoman.

Because this day.

There was nothing to do.

Really nothing.

No one hungry.

Nothing broken.

No rain.

No medicine running out.

No one crying.

Xiaoman sat in the parking lot.

Suddenly said:

"Let's play hide and seek."

You were arranging stones.

Looked up.

"What are the rules?"

"One person seeks."

"Others hide."

"How long?"

"Found counts."

"Where can you hide?"

"Anywhere."

This was an imprecise rule.

But you'd played before.

Played at school.

So you knew.

Round one.

Xiaoman hides.

Everyone closes eyes.

Counts to a hundred.

Nineteen-meter Xiaoman ran to hide behind the abandoned warehouse.

She squatted down.

Shrunk her body very small.

Very serious.

She thought she was hiding very well.

The problem was.

Her head stuck above the warehouse roof.

Both eyes still peeking out.

The delivery guy had already seen her by eighty.

The high school student saw too.

Granny hadn't closed her eyes at all.

Only you.

Finished counting to a hundred.

Turned.

Started looking.

You walked left.

Passed the warehouse.

Didn't look up.

Because there was an ant line on the ground.

You followed the ants.
